Part: Models and bounds (96)

1. What is a MIP model (38)

mip-f-001 · level facile · notes
An optimisation model has a linear objective and linear constraints; some variables are binary, some integer and some continuous. Which class does it belong to?
  1. MILP (mixed integer linear programming)
  2. LP (linear programming)
  3. ILP (integer linear programming)
  4. BIP (binary integer programming)
Explanation. The domains distinguish the classes: all continuous is LP, all integer ILP, all binary BIP; a model with integer (or binary) and continuous variables together is a MILP.
mip-f-002 · level facile · notes
**Maximisation** problem. $\zmilp$ is the optimum of the MILP, $\zlp$ that of the linear relaxation with only $x \ge 0$, $\zlpp$ that with $x \le 1$ as well, $\zdual$ the optimum of the dual of the relaxation. $\bar x$ is feasible for the MILP, $\bar\pi$ for the dual. Which chain is correct?
  1. $\lb(\bar x) \le \zmilp \le \zlp \le \zlpp = \zdual \le \ub(\bar\pi)$
  2. $\lb(\bar x) \le \zdual \le \zmilp \le \zlpp \le \ub(\bar\pi)$
  3. $\lb(\bar x) \le \zmilp \le \zlpp \le \zlp = \zdual \le \ub(\bar\pi)$
  4. $\lb(\bar\pi) \le \zdual = \zlp \le \zlpp \le \zmilp \le \ub(\bar x)$
Explanation. In a maximisation the feasible solution gives the pessimistic side (a lower bound) and the dual lies on the side of the relaxation (an upper bound); adding $x \le 1$ restricts the relaxation and in a maximisation cannot raise it: $\zlpp \le \zlp$.
mip-f-003 · level facile · notes
Problem $\max\ x_1 + x_2$ with $2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 3$ and $x_1, x_2 \in \{0,1\}$. The linear relaxation (binaries replaced by $0 \le x \le 1$) has $(3/4, 3/4)$ among its optimal solutions. What does rounding that point up give?
  1. $(1,0)$, which is the integer optimum
  2. $(0,0)$, feasible with value $0$
  3. $(1,1)$, which is the integer optimum
  4. $(1,1)$, which violates the constraint
Explanation. Rounding up gives $(1,1)$ and $2+2 = 4 > 3$: it is infeasible. Rounding can produce infeasible points, and when it produces feasible ones there is no guarantee on their value.
mip-f-004 · level facile · notes
In a timetabling model, $x_i \in \{0,1\}$ equals $1$ if job $i$ is scheduled and $I$ is the set of jobs occupying the same time slot. The sentence "at most one job per slot" is a constraint of which kind?
  1. Set covering: $\sum_{i \in I} x_i \ge 1$
  2. Set packing: $\sum_{i \in I} x_i \le 1$
  3. Set covering: $\sum_{i \in I} x_i \le |I| - 1$
  4. Set partitioning: $\sum_{i \in I} x_i = 1$
Explanation. "At most one" is a set packing ($\le 1$); "exactly one" is a partitioning ($= 1$), "at least one" a covering ($\ge 1$).
mip-f-005 · level facile · notes
In a covering model $x_j \in \{0,1\}$ equals $1$ if post $j$ is active and $S_i$ is the set of posts covering zone $i$. The constraint "zone $i$ is covered" is $\sum_{j \in S_i} x_j \ge 1$. Of which logical expression is it the direct translation?
  1. An OR clause of the negations $\lnot x_j$, $j \in S_i$
  2. An OR clause of the literals $x_j$, $j \in S_i$
  3. An AND conjunction of the literals $x_j$, $j \in S_i$
  4. An implication $x_j \Rightarrow x_k$ for every pair $j, k \in S_i$
Explanation. The covering asks that at least one of the literals be true: it is the clause $\bigvee_{j \in S_i} x_j$ written as a sum $\ge 1$. The OR of the negations would say "at least one post switched off".
mip-f-006 · level facile · notes
In a minimisation problem only $\ub = 10$ (value of a feasible solution found by a heuristic) and $\lb = 7$ (value of a feasible dual solution) are known. The certified gap is $(\ub - \lb)/|\ub|$. What is it?
  1. $42.9\%$
  2. $70\%$
  3. $30\%$
  4. $0\%$
Explanation. $(10 - 7)/10 = 30\%$. It guarantees that the optimum lies between $7$ and $10$, not that it is close to either end; $42.9\%$ comes from dividing by $\lb$ instead of $\ub$.
mip-f-007 · level facile · notes
MILP with binary variables, **minimisation** problem. $\zlp$ is the optimum of the relaxation with only $x \ge 0$, $\zlpp$ that with $x \le 1$ as well, $\zmilp$ the integer optimum. Which relation always holds?
  1. $\zlp = \zlpp \le \zmilp$
  2. $\zlpp \le \zlp \le \zmilp$
  3. $\zlp \le \zlpp \le \zmilp$
  4. $\zmilp \le \zlpp \le \zlp$
Explanation. Every relaxation enlarges the feasible set, and the one without $x \le 1$ enlarges it more: in a minimisation the value can only go down, hence $\zlp \le \zlpp \le \zmilp$. Equality between the two relaxations holds only in special cases.
mip-f-008 · level facile · notes
MILP with binaries $x$. $\zlp$ is the relaxation with only $x \ge 0$, $\zlpp$ that with $x \le 1$ as well. When do the two certainly coincide?
  1. When the MILP has a unique optimal integer solution
  2. When the other constraints already imply $x \le 1$, such as $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$
  3. When the problem is a maximisation and the profits are positive
  4. When all the variables have a positive cost in the objective
Explanation. The second relaxation adds the constraints $x \le 1$; if they are already implied, for instance by an assignment constraint with $x \ge 0$, adding them does not change the feasible set.
mip-f-009 · level teorema · notes
**Minimisation** MILP, with $\zlp$ the optimum of its linear relaxation and $\zmilp$ the integer optimum. Which statement about the feasible solutions of the dual of the relaxation is correct?
  1. Each is worth at least $\zmilp$: it is an upper bound on the integer optimum
  2. If the relaxation has a finite optimum, the optimal dual is worth $\zmilp$
  3. Each gives a bound on $\zmilp$ only if it is optimal for the dual
  4. Each is worth at most $\zlp$, hence at most $\zmilp$: it is a lower bound
Explanation. By weak duality every feasible dual solution is worth at most $\zlp$, and $\zlp \le \zmilp$: it is a lower bound, optimistic like the relaxation. The pessimistic bound comes only from a feasible solution of the MILP.
mip-f-010 · level teorema · notes
A **primal bound** is the value of a solution used to bound the integer optimum $\zmilp$. Which statement is correct?
  1. In a maximisation, the value of every feasible integer solution is a lower bound on $\zmilp$
  2. In a maximisation, the value of every feasible integer solution is an upper bound on $\zmilp$
  3. A solution satisfying the linear constraints with a fractional component gives a primal bound
  4. The optimal solution of the linear relaxation always gives a primal bound
Explanation. A primal bound comes from a feasible solution of the MILP, integrality included: in a maximisation it is worth at most the optimum, hence it is a lower bound. A fractional solution is feasible only for the relaxation.
mip-f-011 · level ipotesi · notes
Minimisation MILP with feasible set $X$ and linear relaxation with feasible set $\hat X$ (same objective). To prove $\zlp \le \zmilp$, what is needed?
  1. That $X \subseteq \hat X$: every solution of the MILP is feasible for the relaxation
  2. That the constraint matrix be totally unimodular
  3. Strong duality between the MILP and the dual of its relaxation
  4. That the relaxation have at least one integer optimal solution
Explanation. The relaxation optimises the same objective over a larger set, $X \subseteq \hat X$: the minimum over a larger set cannot be higher. Nothing else is needed; the other assumptions would serve to prove equality.
mip-f-012 · level ipotesi · notes
Minimisation problem with integer optimum $\zmilp$. To state $7 \le \zmilp \le 10$ without solving the MILP, what is enough?
  1. A feasible integer solution of value $10$ and a feasible solution of the dual of the relaxation of value $7$
  2. A possibly fractional solution satisfying the linear constraints, of value $10$, and a feasible dual of value $7$
  3. A dual solution of value $7$ in complementary slackness with an integer solution of value $10$
  4. An optimal solution of the dual of the relaxation of value $7$ and strong duality between MILP and dual
Explanation. The pessimistic side comes from a feasible solution of the MILP, integrality included; the optimistic side from any feasible dual solution, thanks to weak duality, which holds with no further assumption.
mip-f-152 · level facile · notes
Minimisation problem: a heuristic finds a solution of value $\ub = 22$ and the solver certifies the optimum $\zmilp = 20$. The gap of the heuristic is $|\ub - \zmilp| / |\zmilp|$. What is it?
  1. $2\%$
  2. $9.1\%$
  3. $10\%$
  4. $110\%$
Explanation. $|22 - 20| / 20 = 2/20 = 10\%$. Dividing by $\ub$ would give $9.1\%$, which is another measure; $2$ is the absolute difference, not the relative gap.
mip-f-153 · level facile · notes
Every job $i \in I$ must be assigned to **exactly one** of the machines $m \in \{1, \dots, k\}$, with $x_{im} \in \{0,1\}$ equal to $1$ if $i$ goes on $m$. Which family of constraints expresses it?
  1. $\sum_{m=1}^{k} x_{im} \le 1$ for every $i \in I$ (set packing)
  2. $\sum_{i \in I} x_{im} = 1$ for every machine $m$ (set partitioning)
  3. $\sum_{m=1}^{k} x_{im} = 1$ for every $i \in I$ (set partitioning)
  4. $\sum_{m=1}^{k} x_{im} \ge 1$ for every $i \in I$ (set covering)
Explanation. "Exactly one machine per job" is a partitioning over the job rows: the sum over $m$ equals $1$ for every $i$. Summing over $i$ would instead say "exactly one job per machine".
mip-f-154 · level facile · notes
During branch-and-bound on a **maximisation** problem, the solver knows the best integer solution found, of value $40$, and a bound from the relaxations, of value $46$. What can be said about the optimum $\zmilp$?
  1. $\zmilp = 46$, because it is the value of the relaxation
  2. $\zmilp = 40$, because the solution is integer
  3. $40 \le \zmilp$ only: the bound from the relaxations is not valid until the search closes
  4. $40 \le \zmilp \le 46$
Explanation. In a maximisation the integer solution is the pessimistic side ($\lb = 40$) and the relaxation bound the optimistic side ($\ub = 46$). The solver works to narrow the interval, and stops when the two sides coincide within the tolerance.
mip-f-155 · level teorema · notes
The linear relaxation of a MILP is solved and its integer variables are rounded to the nearest integer. Which statement is correct?
  1. The rounded point is at most as far from the integer optimum as the relaxation is
  2. The rounded point is optimal when the relaxation has a unique optimal solution
  3. The rounded point may be infeasible, and if it is feasible its value has no guarantee of being close to the optimum
  4. The rounded point is always feasible, but it may not be optimal
Explanation. Rounding may violate the constraints (as $(3/4, 3/4) \to (1,1)$ on $2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 3$), and even when it gives a feasible point it says nothing about its quality: this is why verified heuristics and bounds are needed.
mip-f-188 · level facile · notes
Binary knapsack: $\max \sum_j p_j x_j$ with $\sum_j w_j x_j \le c$ and $x_j \in \{0,1\}$, where $p_j$ is the value and $w_j$ the weight of item $j$, $c$ the capacity. Which are the data and which the variables?
  1. Data $w_j$, $c$; variables $x_j$ and $p_j$
  2. Data $p_j$, $w_j$, $c$; variables $x_j$
  3. Data $p_j$, $w_j$; variables $x_j$ and $c$
  4. Data $x_j$; variables $p_j$, $w_j$, $c$
Explanation. The data are the numbers known before deciding (values, weights, capacity); the variables are what the decision maker controls, here which items to take.
mip-f-189 · level facile · notes
Minimisation problem solved with Gurobi. The best integer solution has `ObjVal` $= 50$ and the dual bound is `ObjBound` $= 46$. The reported gap is `MIPGap` $= |\text{ObjVal} - \text{ObjBound}| / |\text{ObjVal}|$. What is it?
  1. $8\%$
  2. $92\%$
  3. $8.7\%$
  4. $4\%$
Explanation. $|50 - 46| / 50 = 4/50 = 8\%$. Dividing by the bound gives $8.7\%$; $4$ is the absolute gap, not the relative one; $92\%$ is the ratio $46/50$.
mip-f-300 · level facile · notes
A linear optimisation model has two families of variables: $x_j \ge 0$ continuous (quantities produced) and $y_j \in \{0,1\}$ (plant $j$ open or not). All constraints and the objective are linear. Which class does it belong to?
  1. BIP, because the model contains binary variables
  2. ILP, because one integer variable is enough to make it integer
  3. LP, because objective and constraints are all linear
  4. MILP, because some variables are integer (binary) and others continuous
Explanation. The class is decided by the domains: all continuous LP, all integer ILP, all binary BIP, a mix MILP. Here continuous and binary variables coexist, so MILP; linearity of objective and constraints is shared by all four classes.
mip-f-301 · level facile · notes
In an integer linear programming model one wants to express «each job goes to exactly one machine», «machine $m$ works no more than $a_m$ hours» and «at least $12$ units are needed». Which type of linear constraint corresponds, in order, to each requirement?
  1. $\ge$ sense, $\le$ sense, equality
  2. Equality, $\ge$ sense, $\le$ sense
  3. $\le$ sense, equality, $\ge$ sense
  4. Equality, $\le$ sense, $\ge$ sense
Explanation. An exact assignment is an equality, a capacity to respect a $\le$ constraint, a requirement to satisfy a $\ge$ constraint.
mip-f-302 · level facile · notes
Problem $\max\ x_1 + x_2$ with $2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 3$ and $x_1, x_2 \in \{0,1\}$. The linear relaxation (binaries replaced by $0 \le x \le 1$) returns $(3/4, 3/4)$. What do rounding up and rounding down this point give?
  1. Up $(1,1)$, feasible with value $2$; down $(0,0)$, with value $0$
  2. Up $(1,0)$, the integer optimum; down $(0,0)$, with value $0$
  3. Up $(1,1)$, infeasible; down $(0,0)$, feasible with value $0$
  4. Up $(1,1)$, infeasible; down $(1,0)$, the integer optimum
Explanation. $(1,1)$ gives $2 + 2 = 4 > 3$ and violates the constraint; $(0,0)$ is feasible but worth $0$, while the integer optimum is $1$. From this point neither direction finds the optimum.
mip-f-303 · level ipotesi · notes
Problem $\max\ x_1 + x_2$ with $2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 3$, $x \in \{0,1\}^2$: $\zlpp = 3/2$ (relaxation with $0 \le x \le 1$) and $\zmilp = 1$ (integer optimum). A student concludes: «the gap $1/2$ is the fault of rounding; a smarter rounding closes it». Which observation dismantles the conclusion?
  1. The gap disappears if the relaxation without bounds is used, that is, with $x \ge 0$ only
  2. Rounding down always closes the gap in maximisation problems with $\le$ constraints
  3. No feasible integer point is worth more than $1$: the gap is between the two problems, it does not depend on how one rounds
  4. The gap is $1/2$ only because the solver returned $(1/2, 1)$ instead of $(3/4, 3/4)$
Explanation. $\zmilp = 1$ is the largest value over the feasible integer points: no rounding, however smart, can exceed it, so $3/2 - 1$ is a jump between relaxation and integer problem. The relaxation without bounds is not tighter (it is at least $3/2$), and all points of the optimal segment have the same value.
mip-f-304 · level teorema · notes
**Minimisation** problem with $\ub > 0$ the value of a feasible integer solution and $\lb$ the value of a feasible solution of the dual of the relaxation. The certified gap is $(\ub - \lb)/\ub$. What does a gap of $5\%$ guarantee?
  1. That the integer optimum lies in $[\lb, \ub]$, so the solution is at most $5\%$ of $\ub$ away from the optimum
  2. That the integer optimum is at most $5\%$ below $\lb$
  3. That the integer optimum coincides with the midpoint between $\lb$ and $\ub$
  4. That the integer optimum is closer to $\ub$ than to $\lb$
Explanation. The two bounds enclose the optimum: $\lb \le \zmilp \le \ub$. Hence $\ub - \zmilp \le \ub - \lb = 0.05\,\ub$. Where the optimum lies inside the interval is unknown: the gap guarantees the interval, not closeness to an endpoint.
mip-f-305 · level facile · notes
Logical clause $x_1 \lor x_2 \lor x_3$ with $x_j \in \{0,1\}$ ($1$ = true). Which of the three classical patterns — set partitioning ($\sum x = 1$), set packing ($\sum x \le 1$), set covering ($\sum x \ge 1$) — is its direct translation?
  1. None: an OR needs one constraint for every pair of variables
  2. Set covering: $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \ge 1$
  3. Set packing: $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 1$
  4. Set partitioning: $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = 1$
Explanation. The OR is true when at least one literal is true: that is exactly «at least one», the covering. Partitioning would impose exactly one (excluding two true literals), packing at most one.
mip-f-306 · level facile · notes
While solving a **minimisation** MILP, the solver knows the best integer solution found (value $80$) and, for one part of the search space, a relaxation bound equal to $83$. What can it do with that part?
  1. Discard it only if the bound were below $80$
  2. Nothing: a relaxation bound does not allow discarding integer solutions
  3. Discard it without exploring: none of its solutions can be worth less than $83 > 80$
  4. Explore it first, because its bound is the highest
Explanation. In a minimisation the relaxation gives an optimistic bound: in that part no solution goes below $83$, worse than the solution to beat ($80$), so the part is discarded.
mip-f-307 · level teorema · notes
Two formulations of the same **maximisation** MILP, with the same integer points: the relaxation of the first is worth $120$, that of the second $104$; the integer optimum is $100$. What is expected from the solver?
  1. With the second the integer optimum may differ from $100$
  2. With the first the solver closes sooner, because its bound is higher
  3. No difference: the solver works on the integer points, which are the same
  4. With the second the dual bound starts closer to the optimum, so in general less space remains to be explored
Explanation. The dual bound comes from the relaxation: the closer it is to the integer optimum (tighter formulation), the less work remains to close the gap. The integer optimum is the same because the integer points are the same.
mip-f-308 · level facile · notes
Minimisation problem: a heuristic gives $\ub = 0$ and a hand-built dual gives $\lb = -4$. How is the gap between the two bounds reported, following the course convention?
  1. As $(\ub - \lb)/|\lb| = 100\%$
  2. As an infinite gap, so the bounds are useless
  3. With the absolute difference $\ub - \lb = 4$, because the denominator $|\ub|$ is zero
  4. As $0\%$, because $\ub = 0$
Explanation. The relative gap divides by $|\ub|$; if it is zero it is not written and the absolute difference is reported, here $0 - (-4) = 4$.
mip-f-309 · level ipotesi · notes
Timetabling model: $x_i \in \{0,1\}$ equals $1$ if job $i$ is scheduled, $I_f$ is the set of jobs occupying slot $f$. One writes $\sum_{i \in I_f} x_i = 1$ for every slot, but the text only asks that no slot contains two jobs. What happens?
  1. The model becomes a covering, which is the right choice for timetables
  2. The model is too restrictive: it forces every slot to be filled and may become infeasible or lose the optimum; the packing $\le 1$ was needed
  3. The model is too loose: it allows two jobs in the same slot
  4. Nothing: equality and $\le 1$ are equivalent with binary variables
Explanation. «Not two jobs» is at most one, a packing. The equality adds «at least one», which the text does not ask: it cuts feasible solutions (empty slots), so it may worsen the optimum or make the model infeasible.
mip-f-310 · level teorema · notes
**Maximisation** MILP with binary variables: $\zmilp$ integer optimum, $\zlpp$ relaxation with $0 \le x \le 1$, $\zlp$ relaxation with $x \ge 0$ only, $\zdual$ optimum of the dual of the latter, $\ub(\bar\pi)$ value of any feasible dual solution. Which chain is correct?
  1. $\ub(\bar\pi) \le \zdual = \zlp \le \zlpp \le \zmilp$
  2. $\zmilp \le \zlpp \le \zlp \le \ub(\bar\pi) \le \zdual$
  3. $\zmilp \le \zlpp \le \zlp = \zdual \le \ub(\bar\pi)$
  4. $\zmilp \le \zlp \le \zlpp = \zdual \le \ub(\bar\pi)$
Explanation. Dropping constraints in a maximisation does not lower the optimum: $\zmilp \le \zlpp \le \zlp$. By strong duality $\zlp = \zdual$, and the dual is a minimisation, so each of its feasible solutions is worth at least $\zdual$.
mip-f-500 · level facile · notes
Model: $\max 5y_1 + 4y_2 + 6y_3$ with $y_1 + y_2 + y_3 \le 2$, $y_1 + y_3 \ge 1$, $y_k \in \{0,1\}$ ($y_k = 1$ if project $k$ is funded). What does the constraint $y_1 + y_3 \ge 1$ express, and what is the model's class?
  1. At least one of projects 1 and 3; MILP
  2. At most one of projects 1 and 3; BIP
  3. At least one of projects 1 and 3; BIP
  4. Exactly one of projects 1 and 3; ILP
Explanation. A $\ge 1$ constraint on binaries is a covering («at least one»). All variables are binary, so the model is a BIP (a special case of ILP, but BIP is the most precise class).
mip-f-501 · level facile · notes
MILP: $\max 3x_1 + 8x_2 - 10y$ with $x_1 + x_2 = 6$, $x_2 - 4y \le 0$, $x_1 \ge 1$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$, $y \in \{0,1\}$ ($y = 1$ if the plant is active). What does the constraint $x_2 - 4y \le 0$ impose?
  1. The plant is active only if $x_2 = 4$
  2. The second product is made only with the plant off
  3. At least $4$ units of the second product are made
  4. The second product is made only with the plant active, and at most $4$ units
Explanation. With $y = 0$ it leaves $x_2 \le 0$; with $y = 1$ it leaves $x_2 \le 4$. It is an activation link with the capacity as coefficient.
mip-f-502 · level facile · notes
Problem $\max x_1 + x_2$ with $2x_1 + 2x_2 \le 3$, $x \in \{0,1\}^2$. The relaxation with $0 \le x \le 1$ has infinitely many optimal solutions, among them $(1, 1/2)$. What does rounding $(1, 1/2)$ down give?
  1. $(1, 1)$, infeasible
  2. $(1, 0)$, feasible but not optimal
  3. $(0, 0)$, feasible with value $0$
  4. $(1, 0)$, feasible with value $1$: the integer optimum
Explanation. Down $1/2 \to 0$: $(1, 0)$ is worth $1 = \zmilp$. From $(3/4, 3/4)$ instead rounding down gives $(0, 0)$: the outcome depends on the starting point, there is no «right» direction.
mip-f-503 · level facile · notes
**Maximisation** problem: a heuristic finds $\lb = 45$, the solver certifies the integer optimum $\zmilp = 50$. What is the heuristic gap $|\lb - \zmilp| / |\zmilp|$?
  1. $90\%$
  2. $11.1\%$
  3. $10\%$
  4. $5\%$
Explanation. $5/50 = 10\%$. Dividing by the heuristic value gives $11.1\%$; $5$ is the absolute gap.
mip-f-504 · level facile · notes
Assignment with $x_{jm} \in \{0,1\}$ ($1$ if job $j$ goes on machine $m$). «Every job goes on one machine, and every machine receives at most one job»: which patterns appear, in order?
  1. Set covering and set partitioning
  2. Set partitioning ($\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$) and set packing ($\sum_j x_{jm} \le 1$)
  3. Set packing and set covering
  4. Set partitioning in both cases
Explanation. «Exactly one machine» per job is a partitioning; «at most one job» per machine is a packing.
mip-f-505 · level ipotesi · notes
Gurobi stops on the time limit on a minimisation MILP with `ObjVal` $= 120$ and `ObjBound` $= 110$. A student writes «the optimum is $115$, halfway between the two». What is wrong?
  1. Nothing: without further information the midpoint is the correct estimate
  2. The bounds only guarantee $110 \le \zmilp \le 120$: where the optimum lies in the interval is unknown
  3. The optimum is certainly $110$, because it is the dual bound
  4. The optimum is certainly $120$, because it is a solution found
Explanation. When the optimum is not reached, the only certainty is the sandwich between the best solution and the dual bound. No interior point is privileged.
mip-f-530 · level facile · notes
Minimisation ILP: $\min 4x_1 + 7x_2$ with $2x_1 + 3x_2 \ge 12$, $x_1 + x_2 \le 5$, $x_1, x_2 \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ (lots bought from two suppliers). Is the solution $(x_1, x_2) = (3, 2)$ feasible? What is it worth?
  1. Yes, it is worth $24$
  2. No: it violates $x_1 + x_2 \le 5$
  3. Yes, it is worth $26$
  4. No: it violates $2x_1 + 3x_2 \ge 12$
Explanation. $2 \cdot 3 + 3 \cdot 2 = 12 \ge 12$ and $3 + 2 = 5 \le 5$: feasible, value $4 \cdot 3 + 7 \cdot 2 = 26$. It is an upper bound on the optimum, because the problem is a minimisation.
mip-f-531 · level facile · notes
Minimisation MILP with binaries; $\zlp$ is the relaxation with $x \ge 0$ only, $\zlpp$ the one with $0 \le x \le 1$. Why is the relaxation an **optimistic** bound?
  1. Because the solver solves it first
  2. Because it always lies above the integer optimum, in every problem
  3. Because its solutions are always integer
  4. It drops constraints: it optimises over a set containing all integer solutions, so it cannot do worse than the integer optimum
Explanation. Every integer solution stays feasible for the relaxation: in a minimisation the relaxation lies below (or at) the integer optimum, in a maximisation above. In both cases on the «better» side.
mip-f-532 · level facile · notes
Minimisation problem: $\ub = 80$ from a heuristic, $\lb = 72$ from a hand-built dual, integer optimum unknown. What is the certified gap $(\ub - \lb)/|\ub|$?
  1. $11.1\%$
  2. $90\%$
  3. $10\%$
  4. $8$
Explanation. $(80 - 72)/80 = 8/80 = 10\%$. Dividing by $\lb$ gives $11.1\%$; $8$ is the absolute gap; $90\%$ is $\lb/\ub$.

2. Relaxations, duality and bounds (58)

mip-f-013 · level facile · notes
One writes the dual of a **minimisation** linear programme (the primal). A primal constraint of direction $\ge$ corresponds in the dual to a variable $\pi_i$ with which sign?
  1. $\pi_i \in \{0,1\}$
  2. $\pi_i \le 0$
  3. $\pi_i$ free
  4. $\pi_i \ge 0$
Explanation. In the conversion table, for a minimisation primal: a $\ge$ constraint gives $\pi_i \ge 0$, a $\le$ constraint gives $\pi_i \le 0$, an equality gives a free $\pi_i$.
mip-f-014 · level facile · notes
Lot sizing, **minimisation** primal: $x_t$ is the production of period $t$, $s_t$ the stock at the end of the period, $d_t$ the demand. What is the sign of the dual variable of the balance constraint $x_t + s_{t-1} - s_t = d_t$?
  1. Free
  2. It depends on the sign of $d_t$
  3. $\ge 0$
  4. $\le 0$
Explanation. An equality constraint corresponds to a free dual variable, whatever the direction of the objective and whatever the right-hand side.
mip-f-015 · level facile · notes
**Minimisation** primal $\min \sum_j c_j x_j$. A **free** primal variable $x_j$ (no sign constraint) corresponds in the dual to a constraint of which kind?
  1. An equality $= c_j$
  2. An inequality $\le c_j$
  3. An inequality $\ge c_j$
  4. No dual constraint
Explanation. A variable $x_j \ge 0$ gives a dual constraint $\le c_j$; a free variable gives an equality constraint $= c_j$.
mip-f-016 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost assignment of jobs $j$ to machines $m$: $x_{jm}$ assigns, $t_{jm}$ is the time, $a_m$ the availability. What is the sign of the dual variable $\pi_m$ of the constraint $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m$?
  1. $\pi_m \ge 0$
  2. always $\pi_m = 0$
  3. $\pi_m$ free
  4. $\pi_m \le 0$
Explanation. In a minimisation primal a $\le$ constraint gives a non-positive dual variable. This is why setting $\bar\pi = 0$ (the "zero out and saturate" recipe) is always feasible for those constraints.
mip-f-017 · level facile · notes
MILP with integer optimum $\zmilp$ and relaxation (with bounds) of value $\zlpp$. What is a **valid inequality**?
  1. An inequality satisfied by all the feasible integer solutions; if it reduces $\zlpp$ it is a cut
  2. An inequality that always reduces $\zmilp$ and leaves the relaxation unchanged
  3. An inequality satisfied by the optimal solution of the relaxation, which therefore leaves $\zlpp$ unchanged
  4. An inequality satisfied by at least one optimal integer solution; if it reduces $\zlpp$ it is a cut
Explanation. Valid means satisfied by every feasible integer solution, hence it does not change $\zmilp$. A constraint that cuts off some feasible solutions but not all the optimal ones preserves optimality, and is not valid.
mip-f-018 · level facile · notes
Binary knapsack: weights $w = (5, 4, 3, 3)$, capacity $9$, i.e. $5x_1 + 4x_2 + 3x_3 + 3x_4 \le 9$ with $x_j \in \{0,1\}$. Which of the following is a **valid** inequality (satisfied by every feasible solution)?
  1. $x_3 + x_4 \le 1$
  2. $x_1 + x_2 \le 1$
  3. $x_2 + x_3 + x_4 \le 1$
  4. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 2$
Explanation. $\{1,2,3\}$ is a cover ($5+4+3 = 12 > 9$), so the three items do not fit together and $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 2$ is valid. The others cut off feasible solutions: items 3 and 4 fit together ($6 \le 9$), and so do items 1 and 2 ($9 \le 9$).
mip-f-019 · level facile · notes
**Maximisation** MILP. A constructive heuristic returns a feasible integer solution; a feasible solution of the dual of the relaxation is built by hand. Which bounds do they give on the integer optimum?
  1. Both a lower bound
  2. The heuristic an upper bound, the dual a lower bound
  3. The heuristic a lower bound, the dual an upper bound
  4. Both an upper bound
Explanation. The name depends on the direction, the role does not: the feasible solution is on the pessimistic side (in a maximisation, below the optimum), the dual on the optimistic side (above).
mip-f-020 · level facile · notes
Minimisation covering model with linear relaxation $\zlp = 15/2$ and integer optimum $\zmilp = 10$. Gurobi reports `ObjBound = 10` (the final dual bound) and `NodeCount = 0` (no node explored). What does one conclude?
  1. The written model has an exact relaxation, and the hand-made dual can also reach $10$
  2. The linear relaxation of the written model is worth $10$, so the stated value $15/2$ is wrong
  3. The solver closed the gap at the root with presolve, cuts and heuristics; the relaxation of the written model is read with `relax()`
  4. The solver found the optimum without using bounds, and `ObjBound` equals `ObjVal` by construction
Explanation. `ObjBound` is not the root relaxation of the written model: the solver strengthens it on its own. The relaxation that matters for the hand-made bounds is that of the written model, and it is obtained with `relax()`.
mip-f-021 · level media · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack with capacity $b$. With $b = 9$ the integer optimum is $17$ and the optimal dual variable of the capacity constraint in the linear relaxation is $7/4$; with $b = 10$ the integer optimum is still $17$. By how much does the integer optimum really change going from $b = 9$ to $b = 10$?
  1. By $0$
  2. By $1$
  3. By $17/9$
  4. By $7/4$
Explanation. The integer optimum stays $17$: the true change is zero, while the LP dual promises $7/4$ per unit of capacity. LP duals are not the marginal prices of the MILP; the only correct answer is to solve the MILP again.
mip-f-022 · level media · notes
Multi-resource maximisation knapsack: $\max \sum_j p_j x_j$ with $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j \le b_i$ for every resource $i$. A dual solution is built by pricing **one** resource $i$ only, with $\bar v_i = \max_j p_j / a_{ij}$, and the others at zero. With $b_i = 10$ and the ratios $p_j/a_{ij}$ equal to $5/3,\ 9/4,\ 7/3,\ 2,\ 2$, which bound is obtained?
  1. $\ub = 20$
  2. $\lb = 70/3$
  3. $\ub = 70/3$
  4. $\ub = 45/2$
Explanation. The largest ratio is $7/3$ and every dual constraint $\sum_i a_{ij} v_i \ge p_j$ is satisfied by the single term $a_{ij}\bar v_i$: the solution is feasible and, in a maximisation, gives $\ub = b_i \bar v_i = 10 \cdot 7/3 = 70/3$. With a smaller ratio the dual would be infeasible.
mip-f-023 · level teorema · notes
Minimisation primal $\min \sum_j c_j x_j$ and its dual $\max \sum_i b_i \pi_i$; $\bar x$ and $\bar\pi$ are feasible solutions of the two problems. Which statement is **weak duality**?
  1. For every pair of feasible solutions, $\sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i \ge \sum_j c_j \bar x_j$
  2. If both solutions are optimal, $\sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i \le \sum_j c_j \bar x_j$
  3. For every pair of feasible solutions, $\sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i = \sum_j c_j \bar x_j$
  4. For every pair of feasible solutions, $\sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i \le \sum_j c_j \bar x_j$
Explanation. Weak duality holds for every feasible pair, with no further assumption, and gives a lower bound from any feasible dual solution, even a hand-made one. Equality between the optima is strong duality.
mip-f-024 · level teorema · notes
MILP with optimum $\zmilp$, linear relaxation with optimum $\zlp$ and dual of the relaxation with optimum $\zdual$. Which statement is correct?
  1. If the MILP has a finite optimum, $\zdual = \zmilp$
  2. If the relaxation has a finite optimum, $\zdual = \zlp$
  3. The MILP has a linear dual whose optimum coincides with $\zmilp$
  4. $\zdual < \zlp$ whenever the MILP has integer variables
Explanation. Strong duality holds between the relaxation and its dual, and serves as a check on the computations. Between the MILP and a linear programme it does not hold in general: the jump between $\zlp$ and $\zmilp$ is precisely what is missing.
mip-f-025 · level teorema · notes
Two formulations $A$ and $B$ of the same integer problem; $X_A$ and $X_B$ are the polyhedra of their linear relaxations. When is $B$ **stronger** than $A$?
  1. When they admit the same integer points and $X_B \subseteq X_A$
  2. When they admit the same integer points and $X_A \subseteq X_B$
  3. When they admit the same integer points and $B$ has more constraints than $A$
  4. When they admit the same integer points and the solver solves $B$ faster
Explanation. The comparison is done in two steps: same integer set, then inclusion of the polyhedra. Stronger means neither faster nor with more rows: strength is proved, speed is measured.
mip-f-026 · level ipotesi · notes
Minimisation MILP. $\bar\pi$ is a hand-made solution of the dual of the linear relaxation, of value $\sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i$. To use that value as a lower bound on $\zmilp$, what is needed?
  1. That $\bar\pi$ be dual feasible, weak duality, and the fact that the relaxation contains the solutions of the MILP
  2. That $\bar\pi$ satisfy complementary slackness with an optimal solution of the MILP, and weak duality
  3. That $\bar\pi$ be dual feasible, and strong duality between the MILP and the dual of the relaxation
  4. That $\bar\pi$ be dual optimal, strong duality, and the fact that the relaxation contains the solutions of the MILP
Explanation. Dual feasibility suffices: by weak duality $\sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i \le \zlp$ and, since the relaxation contains the solutions of the MILP, $\zlp \le \zmilp$. Optimality is not needed; checking every dual constraint is.
mip-f-027 · level ipotesi · notes
Assignment model with binaries $x_{jm}$ and constraints $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$ for every $j$. $\zlp$ is the relaxation with only $x \ge 0$, $\zlpp$ that with $x \le 1$ as well. To prove $\zlp = \zlpp$, what is needed?
  1. That those constraints, with $x \ge 0$, already imply $x_{jm} \le 1$
  2. That the matrix of the model be totally unimodular
  3. Strong duality between the two linear relaxations
  4. That the MILP have a unique optimal integer solution
Explanation. With $x \ge 0$ and a sum equal to $1$ every term is at most $1$: the second relaxation adds constraints that are already implied, so it has the same feasible set and the same optimum.
mip-f-028 · level ipotesi · notes
A constraint is added to a MILP. To declare it a **valid inequality**, and not merely a constraint that preserves optimality (that keeps at least one optimal solution), what must be proved?
  1. That it is satisfied by at least one optimal integer solution
  2. That it is satisfied by the optimal solution of the relaxation
  3. That it reduces the value of the linear relaxation
  4. That it is satisfied by all the feasible integer solutions
Explanation. Valid means satisfied by every feasible integer solution. If it cuts off some feasible solution but keeps at least one optimum, the constraint preserves optimality and must be declared as such.
mip-f-156 · level facile · notes
Two-resource maximisation knapsack: $\max \sum_j p_j x_j$ with $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j \le b_i$, $i = 1, 2$, and $b = (10, 12)$. Dual recipe: price **one** resource $i$ only with $\bar v_i = \max_j p_j / a_{ij}$ and the other at zero; the bound is $b_i \bar v_i$. The largest ratios are $7/3$ for resource 1 and $2$ for resource 2. Which resource should be priced, and with which bound?
  1. Resource 2, $\ub = 20$
  2. Resource 1, $\lb = 70/3$
  3. Resource 2, $\ub = 24$
  4. Resource 1, $\ub = 70/3$
Explanation. Both choices are feasible and give an upper bound (maximisation): $10 \cdot 7/3 = 70/3 \approx 23.3$ and $12 \cdot 2 = 24$. One keeps the lower, $70/3$. $20$ multiplies the ratio of resource 2 by the $b$ of resource 1.
mip-f-157 · level facile · notes
Minimisation problem, solved with a heuristic, a hand-made dual and the solver: $\ub = 12$ (heuristic), $\lb = 9$ (hand-made dual), $\zlp = 10$, $\zmilp = 11$. Which of these numbers is inconsistent with the others?
  1. $\lb = 9$, because it must coincide with $\zlp$
  2. $\zlp = 10$, because it must coincide with $\zmilp$
  3. $\ub = 12$, because it must coincide with $\zmilp$
  4. None: $9 \le 10 \le 11 \le 12$ is the expected chain
Explanation. In a minimisation $\lb \le \zlp \le \zmilp \le \ub$ holds: the hand-made dual need not be optimal, the heuristic need not find the optimum and the relaxation need not be exact. A number outside this chain would signal a mistake.
mip-f-190 · level facile · notes
Minimisation problem $\min \{f(x) : x \in X\}$. Which of the following problems is a **relaxation**, that is, has an optimum no larger than that of the given problem?
  1. $\max \{f(x) : x \in X\}$, that is, the same set with the opposite sense
  2. $\min \{f(x) + 1 : x \in X\}$, that is, the same set with the objective raised
  3. $\min \{f(x) : x \in \hat X\}$ with $\hat X \subseteq X$, for example by adding a constraint
  4. $\min \{f(x) : x \in \hat X\}$ with $X \subseteq \hat X$, for example by dropping a constraint
Explanation. A relaxation has the same objective and a larger feasible set: the minimum over a larger set cannot be higher. Adding a constraint gives a restriction, which in a minimisation cannot lower the optimum.
mip-f-191 · level facile · notes
Minimisation linear programme $\min 3x_1 + 5x_2$ with $x_1 + x_2 \ge 4$, $x_1 + 3x_2 \ge 6$, $x \ge 0$. Its dual is $\max 4\pi_1 + 6\pi_2$ with $\pi_1 + \pi_2 \le 3$, $\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 \le 5$, $\pi \ge 0$. What does the dual solution $\bar\pi = (2, 1)$ give?
  1. $\lb = 14$, because $\bar\pi$ is dual feasible
  2. No bound, because $\bar\pi$ violates a dual constraint
  3. $\lb = 3$, the sum of the components of $\bar\pi$
  4. $\ub = 14$, because $\bar\pi$ is dual feasible
Explanation. Check: $2 + 1 = 3 \le 3$ and $2 + 3 = 5 \le 5$, so $\bar\pi$ is feasible and is worth $4 \cdot 2 + 6 \cdot 1 = 14$. In a minimisation a feasible dual gives a lower bound (weak duality).
mip-f-192 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost covering: $\min \sum_j c_j x_j$ with $\sum_{j \in S_i} x_j \ge 1$ for every zone $i$ ($S_i$ = teams covering zone $i$) and $x_j \in \{0,1\}$. What is the dual of the relaxation with only $x \ge 0$?
  1. $\max \sum_i \pi_i$ with $\sum_{i : j \in S_i} \pi_i \le c_j$ for every $j$, $\pi \ge 0$
  2. $\max \sum_i \pi_i$ with $\sum_{i : j \in S_i} \pi_i \le c_j$ for every $j$, $\pi$ free
  3. $\max \sum_i \pi_i$ with $\sum_{i : j \in S_i} \pi_i \ge c_j$ for every $j$, $\pi \ge 0$
  4. $\min \sum_i \pi_i$ with $\sum_{i : j \in S_i} \pi_i \le c_j$ for every $j$, $\pi \ge 0$
Explanation. Minimisation primal with $\ge$ constraints: maximisation dual with $\pi_i \ge 0$, one per zone; to each variable $x_j \ge 0$ corresponds a dual constraint $\le c_j$ summing the $\pi_i$ of the zones covered by $j$.
mip-f-193 · level facile · notes
A covering model (minimisation) is closed by Gurobi with `NodeCount = 0` (no branching). Switching off the solver's presolve, cuts and heuristics (`Presolve = Cuts = Heuristics = 0`) the same model needs $5$ nodes. What can be concluded?
  1. The written model becomes stronger, because it needs more nodes to close the gap
  2. The relaxation of the written model gets worse, because switching off cuts weakens it
  3. The optimum does not change: how hard a model is depends on the solver too, not on the model alone
  4. The optimum changes, because without cuts the solver solves a different model
Explanation. Presolve, cuts and heuristics are tools of the solver: they change the work needed, not the model nor its optimum. The relaxation of the written model (the one of `relax()`) stays the same.
mip-f-194 · level teorema · notes
Maximisation MILP with a capacity constraint $\sum_j w_j x_j \le b$. $\tilde v$ is the optimal dual variable of that constraint in the linear relaxation. Which statement about moving from $b$ to $b + 1$ is correct?
  1. $\zmilp$ grows at least by $\tilde v$, because the relaxation lies above the integer optimum
  2. $\zmilp$ grows exactly by $\tilde v$, as in linear programming
  3. $b \tilde v$ is still an upper bound, but the change in $\zmilp$ is obtained only by solving the MILP again
  4. $\zmilp$ grows at most by $\tilde v$, and the difference is a rounding error
Explanation. The LP marginal price does not describe the integer optimum, which changes in jumps (by $0$ or by more than $\tilde v$). What remains true of the dual is its use as a bound; the true change is $\zmilp(b + 1) - \zmilp(b)$, to be computed.
mip-f-195 · level facile · notes
Two formulations $A$ and $B$ of the same integer problem admit the same integer points, and the polyhedra of their relaxations satisfy $X_B \subseteq X_A$. $B$ has more constraints and on the test instance the solver solves it more slowly. Which conclusion is correct?
  1. Neither is stronger, because they have the same integer points
  2. $A$ is stronger than $B$, because the solver solves it faster
  3. $A$ is stronger than $B$, because it has fewer constraints and a larger relaxation
  4. $B$ is stronger than $A$: strength is proved with the polyhedra, speed is measured
Explanation. Stronger means a contained relaxation, $X_B \subseteq X_A$, with the same integer points. A stronger formulation has fewer nodes but more rows, so it can be slower: they are two different properties.
mip-f-196 · level facile · notes
In the course's bound protocol, for every MILP the dual of a relaxation is written by hand and a feasible solution of it is built. Of which relaxation?
  1. Of the relaxation strengthened with the valid inequalities
  2. Of the relaxation without the bounds, with the binaries replaced by $x \ge 0$
  3. Of the relaxation with the bounds, with the binaries replaced by $0 \le x \le 1$
  4. Of the root relaxation computed by the solver after presolve and cuts
Explanation. The hand-made dual is that of $\mathit{LP}$, without the bounds: it has fewer primal constraints, hence fewer dual variables. The relaxation with the bounds, $\mathit{LP}^+$, is computed by the solver with `relax()`.
mip-f-311 · level teorema · notes
**Minimisation** MILP with binaries. $\zlp$: relaxation with $x \ge 0$ only; $\zlpp$: with $0 \le x \le 1$; $z(\mathit{LP}^{++})$: like $\zlpp$ plus some valid inequalities. Which chain always holds?
  1. $\zlp \le \zlpp \le z(\mathit{LP}^{++}) \le \zmilp$
  2. $\zmilp \le z(\mathit{LP}^{++}) \le \zlpp \le \zlp$
  3. $\zlpp \le \zlp \le z(\mathit{LP}^{++}) \le \zmilp$
  4. $\zlp \le \zlpp \le \zmilp \le z(\mathit{LP}^{++})$
Explanation. Each step adds constraints satisfied by all integer solutions: the feasible set shrinks without losing integer points, so in a minimisation the value can only rise, staying below $\zmilp$.
mip-f-312 · level facile · notes
In the course protocol the dual is written by hand for the relaxation **without** bounds ($x \in \{0,1\}$ becomes $x \ge 0$), not for the one with $0 \le x \le 1$. Why?
  1. It has fewer constraints, so the dual has fewer variables and is simpler to build by hand
  2. It always gives a tighter bound than the one with bounds
  3. It is the relaxation that Gurobi solves with `relax()`
  4. It is the only one of the two for which weak duality holds
Explanation. Without the constraints $x \le 1$ the dual loses one family of variables. The bound is equal to or weaker than the one with bounds (not tighter); `relax()` keeps the bounds; weak duality holds for both.
mip-f-313 · level teorema · notes
Minimisation linear programme $\min \sum_j c_j x_j$ (the primal) with finite optimum, and its dual $\max \sum_i b_i \pi_i$. What does **strong duality** state, and what is it used for in the course?
  1. The dual optimum coincides with the integer optimum of the MILP whose relaxation is the primal
  2. The two optima coincide; it serves as a check: the optimum of the hand-written dual must equal the primal optimum
  3. The two optima differ by at most the gap; it serves to estimate `MIPGap`
  4. Every feasible dual solution is worth as much as the primal optimum; it serves to build exact bounds by hand
Explanation. Strong duality: with finite optimum, $z(D(\mathit{LP})) = \zlp$. Arbitrary feasible dual solutions are worth at most $\zlp$ (weak duality), and between the MILP and the dual of the relaxation the jump $\zmilp - \zlp$ remains in general.
mip-f-314 · level ipotesi · notes
A student writes: «by strong duality the optimum of the dual of the relaxation equals $\zmilp$, so an optimal dual solution certifies the integer optimum». What is wrong?
  1. Nothing: strong duality holds for any programme, integer ones included
  2. Strong duality ties the dual to the relaxation: $z(D) = \zlp$, which in general differs from $\zmilp$; the MILP has no linear dual
  3. Strong duality holds only for maximisation problems
  4. Strong duality requires the dual solution to be integer
Explanation. The dual being written is that of the linear relaxation. Between the MILP and any linear programme strong duality does not hold in general: the jump $\zmilp - \zlp$ is exactly what is missing.
mip-f-315 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost assignment with availabilities: $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$ for every job $j$ (dual $\mu_j$ free) and $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m$ for every machine $m$ (dual $\pi_m \le 0$); dual constraints $\mu_j + t_{jm}\pi_m \le c_{jm}$. The «zero and saturate» recipe sets $\bar\pi = 0$. Which value of $\bar\mu_j$ is the largest feasible one?
  1. $\bar\mu_j = \min_m c_{jm}$
  2. $\bar\mu_j = \min_m c_{jm} / t_{jm}$
  3. $\bar\mu_j = \max_m c_{jm}$
  4. $\bar\mu_j = \sum_m c_{jm}$
Explanation. With $\pi = 0$ the dual constraints become $\mu_j \le c_{jm}$ for every $m$: the largest value is the minimum of the row costs. The bound is $\sum_j \min_m c_{jm}$: «every job costs at least its minimum cost».
mip-f-316 · level ipotesi · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack $\max \sum_j p_j x_j$ with $\sum_j w_j x_j \le b$. Dual of the relaxation without bounds: $\min b\,v$ with $w_j v \ge p_j$ for every $j$, $v \ge 0$. A student picks $\bar v = \min_j p_j/w_j$ and reports $\ub = b\,\bar v$. What is wrong?
  1. The right bound is $\bar v / b$, not $b\,\bar v$
  2. $\bar v$ violates the dual constraints of the items with higher ratio: the bound holds only with $\bar v = \max_j p_j / w_j$
  3. In a maximisation the dual gives a lower bound, not an upper bound
  4. Nothing: any value of $v$ gives an upper bound by weak duality
Explanation. Weak duality holds for **feasible** dual solutions: one needs $v \ge p_j/w_j$ for every $j$, that is, $v \ge \max_j p_j/w_j$. With the minimum ratio at least one constraint is violated and $b\,\bar v$ is not a bound.
mip-f-317 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost covering: four teams with cost $c = (6, 6, 8, 8)$, six zones, and zone $i$ is covered by the teams $S_i$: $\{1,2\}, \{2,3\}, \{1,3\}, \{1,4\}, \{2,4\}, \{3,4\}$. The dual of the relaxation has $\pi_i \ge 0$ per zone and one constraint per team: the sum of the $\pi_i$ of the zones it covers does not exceed its cost. What does $\bar\pi_i = 1$ for every zone give?
  1. It is feasible and gives $\lb = 28$, the sum of the costs
  2. It is feasible and gives $\ub = 6$
  3. It is infeasible, because team 1 receives $6$
  4. It is feasible (every team covers $3$ zones, $3 \le 6$) and gives $\lb = 6$
Explanation. Every team appears in three zones, so it receives $3 \le c_j$: feasible. The value is $\sum_i \bar\pi_i = 6$ and, in a minimisation, a feasible dual gives a lower bound.
mip-f-318 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost covering, with $x_j \in \{0,1\}$ team chosen and constraints $\sum_{j \in S_i} x_j \ge 1$ for every zone $i$. Writing the dual of the relaxation (minimisation primal), what sign has the dual variable $\pi_i$ of a zone and what sense has the dual constraint of a team $j$?
  1. $\pi_i \ge 0$; constraint $\sum_{i : j \in S_i} \pi_i \le c_j$
  2. $\pi_i \le 0$; constraint $\sum_{i : j \in S_i} \pi_i \le c_j$
  3. $\pi_i$ free; constraint $\sum_{i : j \in S_i} \pi_i = c_j$
  4. $\pi_i \ge 0$; constraint $\sum_{i : j \in S_i} \pi_i \ge c_j$
Explanation. Table for a minimisation primal: $\ge$ constraint → dual variable $\ge 0$; variable $x_j \ge 0$ → dual constraint of sense $\le c_j$.
mip-f-319 · level facile · notes
Multi-resource knapsack, **maximisation**: $\max \sum_j p_j x_j$ with $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j \le b_i$ for every resource $i$ and $x_j \in \{0,1\}$. In the dual of the relaxation without bounds, what sign do the variables $\pi_i$ have and what sense do the dual constraints have?
  1. $\pi_i \ge 0$, constraints $\sum_i a_{ij} \pi_i \le p_j$, objective $\min \sum_i b_i \pi_i$
  2. $\pi_i \ge 0$, constraints $\sum_i a_{ij} \pi_i \ge p_j$, objective $\min \sum_i b_i \pi_i$
  3. $\pi_i$ free, constraints $\sum_i a_{ij} \pi_i = p_j$, objective $\min \sum_i b_i \pi_i$
  4. $\pi_i \le 0$, constraints $\sum_i a_{ij} \pi_i \le p_j$, objective $\max \sum_i b_i \pi_i$
Explanation. In a maximisation the senses flip: $\le$ constraint → $\pi_i \ge 0$, variable $x_j \ge 0$ → dual constraint $\ge p_j$, and the dual is a minimisation. Each of its feasible solutions gives an upper bound.
mip-f-320 · level teorema · notes
**Maximisation** MILP. A heuristic returns a feasible integer solution $\bar x$; a feasible solution $\bar\pi$ of the dual of the relaxation is built by hand. Which pair of statements is correct?
  1. $\bar x$ gives $\ub(\bar x) \ge \zmilp$ and $\bar\pi$ gives $\lb(\bar\pi) \le \zmilp$
  2. Both give an upper bound, because the problem is a maximisation
  3. $\bar x$ gives $\lb(\bar x) \le \zmilp$ and $\bar\pi$ gives $\ub(\bar\pi) \ge \zmilp$
  4. $\bar x$ gives $\lb(\bar x)$ and $\bar\pi$ gives another $\lb$, on the same side
Explanation. In a maximisation a feasible solution is worth at most the optimum (lower bound, pessimistic side); the dual of the relaxation is a minimisation and each of its feasible solutions is worth at least $\zlp \ge \zmilp$ (upper bound).
mip-f-321 · level facile · notes
Binary knapsack with constraint $5x_1 + 4x_2 + 3x_3 + 3x_4 \le 9$. A set $S$ of items is a **cover** if $\sum_{j \in S} w_j > 9$; then $\sum_{j \in S} x_j \le |S| - 1$ is valid. Which of the following sets is a **minimal** cover (removing any item it is no longer a cover)?
  1. $\{3, 4\}$
  2. $\{1, 2, 3, 4\}$
  3. $\{1, 2\}$
  4. $\{1, 3, 4\}$
Explanation. $\{1,3,4\}$ weighs $11 > 9$; removing one item leaves $8$, $8$ or $6$, all $\le 9$. $\{1,2\}$ weighs $9$, it does not exceed the capacity; $\{1,2,3,4\}$ is a cover but not minimal; $\{3,4\}$ weighs $6$.
mip-f-322 · level facile · notes
One wants to prove that formulation $B$ of an integer problem is **stronger** than formulation $A$. Which are the two required steps?
  1. (1) $X_A \subseteq X_B$; (2) the relaxation of $B$ is worth more than that of $A$
  2. (1) $B$ has the same integer optimum as $A$; (2) $B$ has fewer variables
  3. (1) $A$ and $B$ have exactly the same integer points; (2) the relaxation polyhedra satisfy $X_B \subseteq X_A$
  4. (1) $B$ has fewer constraints than $A$; (2) $B$ is solved faster by Gurobi
Explanation. Without the same integer set nothing is being compared; then strength is the inclusion of the polyhedra. Speed is not proved, it is measured, and a stronger formulation often has more rows.
mip-f-323 · level ipotesi · notes
The disaggregated formulation $x_{ij} \le y_j$ is stronger than the aggregated one $\sum_i x_{ij} \le n\,y_j$. A student concludes: «so with Gurobi the disaggregated one is always faster». What is missing in the reasoning?
  1. The disaggregated one admits extra integer points, so it must be compared with another formulation
  2. The disaggregated one is not stronger: the two formulations have the same relaxation
  3. Nothing: a stronger formulation explores fewer nodes, so it is always faster
  4. Strength concerns the relaxation and is proved; speed also depends on the cost of each node (more rows) and must be measured
Explanation. Stronger means a tighter relaxation, hence typically fewer nodes; but the disaggregated one has many more rows and each node costs more. What is proved is strength; speed is measured.
mip-f-324 · level facile · notes
On a covering model (minimisation) the relaxation of the written model is worth $15/2$, the integer optimum $10$. Gurobi closes with `NodeCount = 0`. With `Presolve = Cuts = Heuristics = 0` the same model needs $5$ nodes. What does the comparison teach?
  1. `NodeCount = 0` means that the solver did not solve the relaxation
  2. Difficulty is not a property of the model alone: the solver's presolve, cuts and heuristics close the gap already at the root
  3. The relaxation of the written model is worth $10$, not $15/2$
  4. With the parameters set to zero Gurobi finds a different optimum
Explanation. The same model gives the same optimum, but the work changes with the solver's tools. The relaxation used in the hand-made bounds is still that of the written model ($15/2$), obtained with `relax()`.
mip-f-325 · level facile · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack with capacity $b$: with $b = 9$ the integer optimum is $17$, with $b = 10$ it is still $17$; the optimal dual of the capacity constraint in the linear relaxation is $7/4$ for both. By how much does the integer optimum really increase from $b = 9$ to $b = 10$?
  1. $0$
  2. $1$
  3. $17/9$
  4. $7/4$
Explanation. The true change is obtained by solving the MILP again: $17 - 17 = 0$. The LP dual ($7/4$) is the marginal price of the relaxation, not of the integer problem, whose response changes in jumps.
mip-f-326 · level teorema · notes
**Maximisation** MILP with a capacity constraint $\sum_j w_j x_j \le b$. One wants to know how much the integer optimum grows by adding one unit of capacity. Which procedure gives the correct answer?
  1. Read the optimal dual variable of the constraint in the linear relaxation
  2. Solve the MILP again with $b + 1$ and compute $\zmilp(b + 1) - \zmilp(b)$
  3. Multiply the relative gap by $b$
  4. Read the reduced cost of the variable with the highest ratio $p_j/w_j$
Explanation. The dual of the relaxation is still a bound, but as a marginal price of the MILP it is unreliable: the integer problem responds in jumps and has no closed formula. The only correct answer is to solve again.
mip-f-327 · level facile · notes
In the course's bound protocol, for a **minimisation** problem the final table reports $\ub$ (heuristic), $\lb$ (hand-built dual), $\zlp$ and $\zlpp$ (relaxations without and with the bounds $0 \le x \le 1$), $\zmilp$ (integer optimum) and the gap. Which ordering of the first five values must result, if everything is correct?
  1. $\ub \le \zmilp \le \zlpp \le \zlp \le \lb$
  2. $\lb \le \zlpp \le \zlp \le \zmilp \le \ub$
  3. $\zlp \le \lb \le \zlpp \le \zmilp \le \ub$
  4. $\lb \le \zlp \le \zlpp \le \zmilp \le \ub$
Explanation. $\lb$ comes from a feasible dual of the relaxation without bounds, so it is at most $\zlp$; then $\zlp \le \zlpp \le \zmilp$ (more constraints in a minimisation) and $\ub$, the value of a feasible solution, lies above the optimum.
mip-f-328 · level ipotesi · notes
In the bound protocol, a heuristic for a minimisation problem returns a vector that satisfies all linear constraints but has an integer component equal to $2.5$. Can its value be reported as $\ub$?
  1. No, because in a minimisation a feasible solution gives a lower bound
  2. Yes: it satisfies all linear constraints, so the value is an upper bound
  3. Yes, but only after rounding it up, which always stays feasible
  4. No: it is feasible for the relaxation, not for the MILP; the primal bound also requires integrality
Explanation. The feasibility check concerns constraints, bounds **and** integrality. A fractional point is worth as much as a relaxation point, which may lie below $\zmilp$. Rounding may violate the constraints.
mip-f-329 · level facile · notes
**Maximisation** primal $\max \sum_j c_j x_j$. A primal constraint of sense $\le$ and a primal variable $x_j \ge 0$ correspond in the (minimisation) dual to:
  1. a variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ and a constraint of sense $\ge c_j$
  2. a variable $\pi_i \le 0$ and a constraint of sense $\le c_j$
  3. a free variable $\pi_i$ and an equality constraint
  4. a variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ and a constraint of sense $\le c_j$
Explanation. For a maximisation primal the minimisation table flips: $\le$ constraint → $\pi_i \ge 0$; variable $\ge 0$ → dual constraint $\ge c_j$.
mip-f-330 · level teorema · notes
A constraint is added to a MILP. In which case can it be called a **cut**?
  1. If it is satisfied by all feasible integer solutions and reduces the value of the relaxation with bounds
  2. If it is satisfied by at least one optimal solution, even if it cuts feasible ones
  3. If it changes neither the integer optimum nor that of the relaxation
  4. If it removes at least one optimal integer solution
Explanation. A cut is a valid inequality (it excludes no feasible integer point, so $\zmilp$ does not change) that worsens $\zlpp$, that is, brings the relaxation closer to the integer optimum. A constraint that keeps only some optimum preserves optimality but is not valid.
mip-f-350 · level facile · notes
One wants to report the value $\zlpp$ of the linear relaxation (with bounds) of the model **as written**. Why can't the dual bound that Gurobi shows at the root while solving the MILP be used?
  1. The root bound is always weaker than $\zlpp$, because the solver has not started working yet
  2. The root bound coincides with $\zlp$, the relaxation without bounds
  3. The root bound includes the solver's presolve and cuts, so it may be tighter than the relaxation of the written model; `relax()` is needed
  4. Gurobi shows no bound at the root
Explanation. The solver strengthens the model before and during the root: its bound may already equal the integer optimum. The relaxation of the written model is obtained by solving `m.relax()`.
mip-f-506 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost covering: elements with cost $c = (4, 3, 5, 3)$, zones $1, 2, 3$ covered respectively by $\{1, 2\}$, $\{2, 4\}$, $\{1, 3\}$. «Constructive on the constraints» dual recipe: scan the zones in order, raise $\pi_i$ to the minimum residual among the elements covering it, and subtract $\pi_i$ from the residuals of those elements. Which solution is obtained?
  1. $\bar\pi = (3, 0, 4)$, $\lb = 7$
  2. $\bar\pi = (3, 3, 4)$, $\lb = 10$
  3. $\bar\pi = (4, 3, 5)$, $\lb = 12$
  4. $\bar\pi = (3, 0, 1)$, $\lb = 4$
Explanation. Zone 1: residuals $4, 3$ → $\pi_1 = 3$, residuals $(1, 0, 5, 3)$. Zone 2: elements 2 and 4, residuals $0, 3$ → $\pi_2 = 0$. Zone 3: elements 1 and 3, residuals $1, 5$ → $\pi_3 = 1$. Value $4$, a lower bound because feasible by construction.
mip-f-507 · level facile · notes
Minimisation primal with an **equality** constraint $\sum_j a_{ij} x_j = b_i$ (for example «each job on exactly one machine»). Which dual variable corresponds to it?
  1. A $\pi_i \ge 0$
  2. A $\pi_i \le 0$
  3. None: equalities have no dual
  4. A free $\pi_i$, with no sign constraint
Explanation. An equality is equivalent to two inequalities of opposite sense, with duals of opposite sign: their difference is a free variable.
mip-f-508 · level facile · notes
Knapsack $5x_1 + 4x_2 + 3x_3 + 3x_4 \le 9$, values $(10, 7, 6, 4)$: the relaxation with bounds has solution $\tilde x = (1, 1/4, 1, 0)$. The cover cut for $S = \{1, 2, 3\}$ is $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 2$. What happens at $\tilde x$?
  1. It is satisfied with slack
  2. It cannot be evaluated: $\tilde x$ is fractional
  3. It is violated ($9/4 > 2$): adding it cuts off $\tilde x$ and strengthens the relaxation
  4. It is satisfied with equality, so it is useless
Explanation. $1 + 1/4 + 1 = 9/4 > 2$. The cut is valid (every integer solution respects it) and separates $\tilde x$: with the four covers the relaxation drops from $71/4$ to $69/4$.
mip-f-509 · level facile · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack with capacity $b$: $\zmilp(10) = 17$, $\zmilp(11) = 20$, and the optimal dual of the capacity constraint in the linear relaxation is $7/4$. By how much does the integer optimum really grow from $b = 10$ to $b = 11$?
  1. $7/4$
  2. $0$
  3. $3$
  4. $20/11$
Explanation. The true change is $20 - 17 = 3$, more than the LP dual: the integer problem responds in jumps, sometimes $0$ (from $9$ to $10$), sometimes more than the marginal price.
mip-f-510 · level teorema · notes
Minimisation MILP; $\bar\pi$ is a solution of the dual of the relaxation built by hand with one of the course recipes (zero and saturate, constructive on constraints, best ratio). What is the only property that makes the bound $\sum_i b_i \bar\pi_i \le \zmilp$ valid?
  1. The feasibility of $\bar\pi$ for the dual, checked constraint by constraint
  2. The fact that the recipe starts from $\bar\pi = 0$
  3. The optimality of $\bar\pi$ for the dual
  4. The integrality of the components of $\bar\pi$
Explanation. Weak duality requires only feasibility. A non-optimal dual solution gives a weaker but valid bound; an infeasible one gives nothing.
mip-f-526 · level facile · notes
Activation with $n = 3$ objects and one resource: aggregated formulation $A$: $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 3y$; disaggregated $B$: $x_i \le y$ for every $i$ (all binary). Which relaxation does the fractional point $x = (1, 0, 0)$, $y = 1/3$ belong to?
  1. To both: the two formulations have the same relaxation
  2. Only to that of $B$
  3. Only to that of $A$: in $B$ it violates $x_1 \le y$, so $X_B \subsetneq X_A$
  4. To neither
Explanation. In $A$: $1 \le 3 \cdot 1/3 = 1$, feasible. In $B$: $x_1 = 1 > 1/3 = y$, excluded. Same integer points, $B$'s polyhedron strictly contained: $B$ is stronger.
mip-f-527 · level teorema · notes
Two formulations $A$ and $B$ of a **minimisation** MILP, with the same integer points and $X_B \subseteq X_A$ (relaxation polyhedra). What is the relation between the relaxation values $z_A$ and $z_B$?
  1. $z_A \le z_B \le \zmilp$: $B$'s relaxation gives a bound at least as good
  2. $z_A = z_B$, because the integer points are the same
  3. No general relation
  4. $z_B \le z_A \le \zmilp$
Explanation. Minimising over a smaller set cannot give a lower value: $z_A \le z_B$; both stay below $\zmilp$ because they contain all integer points.
mip-f-533 · level facile · notes
**Maximisation** MILP $\max\{f(x) : x \in X\}$ and a problem $\max\{f(x) : x \in \hat X\}$ with $X \subseteq \hat X$. What is the relation between the two optima?
  1. They are equal
  2. No relation
  3. The optimum over $\hat X$ is smaller than or equal
  4. The optimum over $\hat X$ is greater than or equal: it is a relaxation, hence an upper bound
Explanation. In a maximisation, optimising over a larger set cannot give a lower value: the relaxation gives an upper bound. In minimisations the inequality flips.
mip-f-534 · level facile · notes
Minimisation primal with a **free** variable $x_j$ (no sign constraint) and cost $c_j$. Which dual constraint corresponds to it?
  1. A constraint $\sum_i a_{ij}\pi_i \ge c_j$
  2. An equality constraint $\sum_i a_{ij}\pi_i = c_j$
  3. A constraint $\sum_i a_{ij}\pi_i \le c_j$
  4. No constraint: the variable disappears
Explanation. Variable $\ge 0$ → dual constraint $\le c_j$; free variable → equality. The sign of the primal variables decides the sense of the dual constraints.
mip-f-535 · level facile · notes
Two-resource knapsack, maximisation: $\max \sum_j p_j x_j$ with $\sum_j a_{1j} x_j \le 10$, $\sum_j a_{2j} x_j \le 12$. In the dual ($\min 10\pi_1 + 12\pi_2$, $a_{1j}\pi_1 + a_{2j}\pi_2 \ge p_j$, $\pi \ge 0$) the solution $\bar\pi = (2, 1)$ is feasible. Which bound does it give?
  1. $\ub = 32$
  2. $\ub = 3$
  3. $\ub = 22$
  4. $\lb = 32$
Explanation. $10 \cdot 2 + 12 \cdot 1 = 32$; the dual of a maximisation is a minimisation, and each of its feasible solutions is worth at least the optimum: upper bound.
mip-f-536 · level facile · notes
Minimisation linear programme with a feasible solution $\bar x$ of value $40$ and a feasible dual solution $\bar\pi$ of value $40$. What is concluded?
  1. Nothing without solving
  2. Only $\bar x$ is optimal
  3. Both are optimal: by weak duality no primal solution is worth less than $40$ and no dual more than $40$
  4. The problem is unbounded
Explanation. Weak duality gives $\sum_i b_i\pi_i \le \sum_j c_j x_j$ for every feasible pair: equal values close the problem, and it is the optimality certificate of the linear programme.
mip-f-537 · level facile · notes
Bound protocol for a **maximisation** problem: the heuristic gives $40$, the hand-built dual $55$, the relaxations $\zlp = 52$ (without bounds) and $\zlpp = 50$ (with $0 \le x \le 1$), the integer optimum $45$. Which line contains an error?
  1. $\zlpp$ should exceed $\zlp$
  2. No error: $40 \le 45 \le 50 \le 52 \le 55$ is the expected order in a maximisation
  3. The hand-built dual should be at most $\zlp$
  4. The heuristic should exceed the optimum
Explanation. In a maximisation: feasible solution $\le \zmilp \le \zlpp \le \zlp \le$ feasible dual (which is a minimisation and is worth at least $\zlp$). All values respect the chain.

Part: Gurobi and heuristics (98)

3. From the model to Python/Gurobi (54)

mip-f-029 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy (the Python interface of Gurobi) one declares the dual variable of an equality constraint, which must be allowed to take negative values. Which call is correct?
  1. `addVar()` with no arguments
  2. `addVar(vtype=GRB.BINARY)`
  3. `addVar(lb=-GRB.INFINITY)`
  4. `addVar(ub=GRB.INFINITY)`
Explanation. A continuous variable has `lb = 0` by default: without `lb=-GRB.INFINITY` the model is silently wrong. `GRB.BINARY` already implies `lb = 0` and `ub = 1`, and an infinite `ub` is already the default.
mip-f-030 · level facile · notes
Gurobi, minimisation problem. After `optimize()` one reads `Status = TIME_LIMIT` (time limit reached) and `SolCount` $\ge 1$ (at least one integer solution found). `ObjVal` is the value of the best solution, `ObjBound` the dual bound. What can be reported?
  1. `ObjBound` $\le \zmilp \le$ `ObjVal`
  2. $\zmilp =$ `ObjBound`
  3. $\zmilp =$ `ObjVal`
  4. Nothing: without `OPTIMAL` no number is meaningful
Explanation. With the time limit and at least one solution one reports an interval; saying "the optimum is `ObjVal`" would be false. With `SolCount = 0` instead there is nothing to report.
mip-f-031 · level facile · notes
Gurobi, minimisation problem, with the parameter `SolutionLimit = 1` (stop at the first integer solution). The solver stops with `Status = 10`, `ObjVal = 12` (value of the solution) and `ObjBound = 10` (dual bound). What is correct to say about the optimum $\zmilp$?
  1. It lies between $10$ and $12$
  2. It is $12$
  3. The model is infeasible
  4. It is $10$
Explanation. The solver has a solution of value $12$ and a bound of $10$: the only safe statement is the interval $10 \le \zmilp \le 12$.
mip-f-032 · level facile · notes
Gurobi considers a variable integer within the tolerance `IntFeasTol` ($10^{-5}$). A binary `x` comes back with value `x.X` $= 0.9999999997$. How does one check in Python code whether it equals $1$?
  1. `if x.X > 0.5`
  2. `if x.X >= 1`
  3. `if round(x.X, 12) == 1`
  4. `if x.X == 1`
Explanation. "Integer" means integer within the tolerance: comparisons are made with a margin, and for a binary the threshold $0.5$ is enough. Exact comparison, even after rounding to many digits, fails on this value.
mip-f-033 · level facile · notes
Travelling salesman on $n$ cities, MTZ formulation: $x_{ij} = 1$ if the tour goes from $i$ to $j$, and $u_i \in [1, n-1]$ is the position of city $i \ne 1$ along the tour. What does the constraint $u_i - u_j + n\, x_{ij} \le n - 1$ impose when $x_{ij} = 1$?
  1. $u_i = u_j$: the two cities have the same position
  2. $u_i \ge u_j + 1$: the position of $i$ follows that of $j$
  3. $u_i - u_j \le n - 1$, which forbids nothing
  4. $u_j \ge u_i + 1$: the position of $j$ follows that of $i$
Explanation. With $x_{ij} = 1$ what remains is $u_i - u_j \le -1$. With $x_{ij} = 0$ it becomes $u_i - u_j \le n - 1$, always true because the $u$ lie between $1$ and $n - 1$. A subtour avoiding city $1$ would need ever-increasing positions that close on themselves.
mip-f-034 · level facile · notes
Bin packing: items of weight $w = (5, 4, 3, 3)$, bins of capacity $c = 7$; $x_{jb} = 1$ if item $j$ goes into bin $b$, $y_b = 1$ if $b$ is used, constraints $\sum_j w_j x_{jb} \le c\, y_b$, objective $\min \sum_b y_b$. What is the value of the linear relaxation?
  1. $2$
  2. $4$
  3. $15/7$
  4. $3$
Explanation. The relaxation buys fractions of a bin and drops to $\sum_j w_j / c = 15/7 \approx 2.14$: it does not know that a bin opens whole. The integer optimum is $3$.
mip-f-035 · level facile · notes
Assignment with $3$ jobs and $3$ machines: $x_{jm} \in \{0,1\}$ for every pair, one "assign" constraint $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$ per job and one availability constraint $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m$ per machine. How many variables and how many constraints (domains excluded) does the model have?
  1. $9$ variables and $3$ constraints
  2. $9$ variables and $9$ constraints
  3. $9$ variables and $6$ constraints
  4. $6$ variables and $9$ constraints
Explanation. One variable per job-machine pair ($3 \cdot 3 = 9$), one "assign" constraint per job and one availability constraint per machine ($3 + 3 = 6$).
mip-f-036 · level facile · notes
Makespan on identical machines: $d_j$ is the duration of job $j$, $x_{jm} = 1$ if $j$ goes on machine $m$, and the model is $\min z$ with $\sum_j d_j x_{jm} - z \le 0$ for every machine. Which modelling technique is it?
  1. Max-min: $z$ goes up to the load of the least loaded machine
  2. A big-M switching off the unused machines
  3. Min-max: $z$ goes down to the load of the busiest machine
  4. A set covering constraint on the machines
Explanation. The load rows keep $z$ above every load and the minimisation squeezes it onto the largest: it is the min-max technique.
mip-f-037 · level teorema · notes
Gurobi stops when the relative gap between `ObjVal` (best solution) and `ObjBound` (dual bound) falls below the parameter `MIPGap`, by default $10^{-4}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. With the default the solver may declare `OPTIMAL` before reaching the exact optimum
  2. `ObjBound` at the end of the solve is the value of the root relaxation
  3. The `Runtime` of two models with different settings tells which formulation is stronger
  4. `OPTIMAL` guarantees the exact optimum, whatever the value of `MIPGap`
Explanation. The solver stops when the relative gap falls below `MIPGap`: on instances with large values it is worth lowering it. `ObjBound` is not the root relaxation, and times obtained with different settings are not comparable.
mip-f-038 · level ipotesi · notes
After `optimize()` in Gurobi one wants to write in the report "the optimum of the MILP is `ObjVal`". Which check is necessary and sufficient?
  1. That `SolCount` is at least $1$
  2. That `ObjVal` is an integer
  3. That `Status` equals `OPTIMAL`
  4. That `NodeCount` equals $0$
Explanation. Only with `Status = OPTIMAL` is the optimum proved. With `SolCount` $\ge 1$ but a limit reached one reports the interval between `ObjBound` and `ObjVal`; `NodeCount` and the integrality of the value say nothing.
mip-f-158 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, `r = m.relax()` returns a copy of model `m` in which binary variables become continuous in $[0,1]$ and integer ones continuous above their `lb`. What does one get with `r.optimize()` and `r.ObjVal`?
  1. The final dual bound `ObjBound` of the integer model `m`
  2. The value of the best integer solution of `m`
  3. The value of the root relaxation after the solver's cuts and presolve
  4. The value of the linear relaxation of the written model, with the bounds of the binaries
Explanation. `relax()` builds the relaxation of the model as written: it is the number to compare with the hand-made bounds. The relaxation strengthened by the solver and `ObjBound` are something else.
mip-f-159 · level facile · notes
Gurobi returns `Status = TIME_LIMIT` and `SolCount = 0`: the time limit expired without any integer solution being found. What can be reported about the optimum of the MILP?
  1. That the model is infeasible
  2. That the optimum is `ObjBound`
  3. Nothing: neither a solution nor, in general, a useful bound
  4. That the optimum lies between `ObjBound` and `ObjVal`
Explanation. Without solutions there is no pessimistic side, and the time limit does not prove infeasibility (that requires `Status = INFEASIBLE`). Not even a gap is written.
mip-f-160 · level facile · notes
Bin packing with $n = 5$ items and $k = 5$ available bins: binaries $x_{jb}$ (item $j$ in bin $b$) and $y_b$ (bin used), constraints $\sum_b x_{jb} = 1$ per item and $\sum_j w_j x_{jb} \le c\, y_b$ per bin. How many variables and how many constraints (domains excluded)?
  1. $30$ variables and $30$ constraints
  2. $30$ variables and $10$ constraints
  3. $25$ variables and $10$ constraints
  4. $30$ variables and $5$ constraints
Explanation. The $x_{jb}$ are $5 \cdot 5 = 25$ and the $y_b$ are $5$: $30$ variables. The constraints are $5$ assignment and $5$ capacity constraints: $10$.
mip-f-161 · level teorema · notes
Travelling salesman with $x_{ij} = 1$ if the tour goes from $i$ to $j$, and only the constraints "leave every city once" and "enter every city once". Which statement is correct?
  1. They describe exactly the Hamiltonian tours, and the MTZ constraints only strengthen the relaxation
  2. They admit only tours through city $1$, so subtours are impossible
  3. They admit subtours only if the distances are not symmetric
  4. They admit solutions made of several disjoint subtours, which the MTZ constraints exclude
Explanation. Leaving and entering every city once describes a set of disjoint cycles, not necessarily a single one. The position variables $u_i$ of the MTZ formulation forbid the cycles that do not touch city $1$.
mip-f-197 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy (the Python interface of Gurobi) one writes `x = m.addVars(4, 3, vtype=GRB.BINARY)`. How many variables are created and how are they indexed?
  1. $7$ binaries, `x[0]`, …, `x[6]`
  2. $12$ binaries, `x[i, j]` with `i` in $0, \dots, 3$ and `j` in $0, \dots, 2$
  3. $12$ binaries, `x[i, j]` with `i` in $1, \dots, 4$ and `j` in $1, \dots, 3$
  4. $4$ binaries, each with domain $\{0, 1, 2\}$
Explanation. `addVars(4, 3)` creates the $4 \times 3$ matrix; Python indices start from zero, while in the written models they start from one.
mip-f-198 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy `x = m.addVars(n, k, vtype=GRB.BINARY)` assigns job $j$ to machine $h$. Which statement writes the family "every job on exactly one machine", $\sum_h x_{jh} = 1$ for every $j$?
  1. `m.addConstrs(x.sum('*', j) == 1 for j in range(n))`
  2. `m.addConstrs(x.sum(j, '*') == 1 for j in range(1, n))`
  3. `m.addConstr(x.sum(j, '*') == 1 for j in range(n))`
  4. `m.addConstrs(x.sum(j, '*') == 1 for j in range(n))`
Explanation. `addConstrs` adds a family from a generator; `x.sum(j, '*')` sums over the dummy machine index. Summing over the first index counts the jobs of a machine; `range(1, n)` skips job $0$.
mip-f-199 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy a signed deviation $d \in \mathbb{R}$, which must be allowed to be negative, is declared with `d = m.addVar()`, without arguments. What happens?
  1. $d$ is free by default, so the model is correct
  2. $d$ becomes binary, because that is the default type
  3. $d$ gets `lb = 0` by default and the solved model is a different one, without any warning
  4. Gurobi raises an error, because the domain is missing
Explanation. A continuous variable has `lb = 0` by default: a variable that must be allowed to be negative is declared with `lb=-GRB.INFINITY`, otherwise the model is silently wrong.
mip-f-200 · level facile · notes
In the course code the model is built by a function `modello(t, c, a)` that receives the data as arguments, instead of reading them from global variables. Why?
  1. Because the solver then solves the model faster
  2. So that the same function serves for the base instance and for all the variants
  3. To avoid the difference between indices starting from zero and from one
  4. Because gurobipy cannot read global variables
Explanation. It is one of the course's three writing rules (with one `addConstrs` per family and all variables declared together): data as arguments make the function reusable on different instances.
mip-f-201 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, before `r = m.relax()` (which returns a copy of `m` with the integer variables made continuous) the course code calls `m.update()`. Why?
  1. `relax()` copies the model, and the pending modifications must be applied first
  2. `update()` declares the binaries continuous, and `relax()` just copies them
  3. `update()` resets the solver parameters, which would otherwise pass to the copy
  4. `update()` solves the integer model, and `relax()` needs a model already solved
Explanation. gurobipy applies modifications lazily: added variables and constraints enter the model only with `update()` or `optimize()`. Without it, the copy made by `relax()` might not contain them.
mip-f-202 · level facile · notes
Makespan on identical machines: $4$ jobs of duration $d = (3, 4, 5, 6)$ on $k = 2$ machines; binaries $x_{jm}$ (job $j$ on machine $m$), one continuous $z$, constraints $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$ per job and $\sum_j d_j x_{jm} - z \le 0$ per machine, $\min z$. How many variables and how many constraints (domains excluded)?
  1. $9$ variables and $6$ constraints
  2. $9$ variables and $4$ constraints
  3. $8$ variables and $6$ constraints
  4. $9$ variables and $8$ constraints
Explanation. Variables: $4 \cdot 2 = 8$ binaries plus $z$, that is $9$. Constraints: $4$ assignment plus $2$ load, that is $6$. Forgetting $z$ gives $8$; counting one load constraint per pair gives $8$.
mip-f-203 · level facile · notes
In the course protocol, the solution of the constructive heuristic goes through `assert ammissibile(m, ...)` before its value becomes a primal bound. What does that check verify?
  1. Only the linear constraints, even with fractional components
  2. Constraints, variable bounds and integrality
  3. That the solution is optimal for the MILP
  4. That strong duality holds with the hand-written dual
Explanation. Only a solution satisfying constraints, bounds **and** integrality is feasible for the MILP, and only then is its value a primal bound.
mip-f-204 · level facile · notes
The course protocol always follows the same order of steps for every problem. Which one?
  1. Data, LP and dual, model, MIP, heuristic and check, table
  2. Data, model, heuristic and check, LP and dual, MIP, table
  3. Data, model, MIP, heuristic and check, LP and dual, table
  4. Model, data, MIP, table, heuristic and check, LP and dual
Explanation. First the two hand-made bounds (heuristic, then dual), then the solver: the MIP serves to judge the bounds, not the other way round. The table gathers all the numbers at the end.
mip-f-205 · level facile · notes
