The licence included in the pip package of `gurobipy` allows models up to $2000$ variables and $2000$ constraints. What happens if a model exceeds the limit?
  1. Gurobi raises an explicit error when `optimize()` is called
  2. Gurobi solves the linear relaxation instead of the MILP
  3. Gurobi solves only the first $2000$ variables and returns a partial optimum
  4. Gurobi returns `Status = TIME_LIMIT` without solutions
Explanation. The *size-limited* licence never silently produces a wrong result: beyond the limit `optimize()` fails with an error. The course instances are all far below the limit.
mip-f-206 · level facile · notes
In the course code, every number quoted in the notes (bounds, optima, gaps) comes from a CSV produced by the problem's script. What does `verifica_numeri.py` do?
  1. It compares, with an `assert`, every number quoted in the text with the value in the CSV
  2. It solves every MILP again with another solver and compares the optima
  3. It rewrites the numbers of the text by copying them from the CSV
  4. It checks that every model fits within the licence limit
Explanation. A number exists in one place only, the CSV; the notes, the site and the check read it from there, and the `assert`s flag every disagreement.
mip-f-207 · level ipotesi · notes
In gurobipy, `r = m.relax()` followed by `r.optimize()`. To report `r.ObjVal` as $\zlpp$, the linear relaxation with the bounds of the written model `m`, what is needed?
  1. That `m` was solved before, so that `relax()` inherits its solution
  2. That `m` is updated with `update()` before `relax()` and that `r` ends with `Status = OPTIMAL`
  3. That the integer model `m` ends with `NodeCount = 0`
  4. That `r.ObjVal` equals `m.ObjBound` at the end of the solve
Explanation. `r` is a copy of `m` with the integers made continuous: the copy must contain the whole model (hence `update()`) and its LP must be solved to optimality. `ObjBound` is something else: it can include cuts and branching.
mip-f-331 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy (the Python interface of Gurobi) a model `m` contains the variables `x`. Which statement sets the objective «maximise $\sum_j p_j x_j$»?
  1. `m.setObjective(gp.quicksum(p[j] * x[j] for j in J), GRB.MAXIMIZE)`
  2. `m.optimize(gp.quicksum(p[j] * x[j] for j in J), GRB.MAXIMIZE)`
  3. `m.addConstr(gp.quicksum(p[j] * x[j] for j in J), GRB.MAXIMIZE)`
  4. `m.setObjective(max(p[j] * x[j] for j in J))`
Explanation. `setObjective(expression, sense)` sets the objective; `quicksum` builds the linear sum. `addConstr` adds constraints, Python's `max` does not build a linear expression, `optimize()` takes no arguments.
mip-f-332 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, after `m.optimize()` with `m.Status == GRB.OPTIMAL`, how are the optimal value and the value of variable `x[2]` read?
  1. `m.Status` and `x[2].VarName`
  2. `m.getObjective()` and `x[2].LB`
  3. `m.ObjBound` and `x[2].Start`
  4. `m.ObjVal` and `x[2].X`
Explanation. `ObjVal` is the value of the best (here optimal) solution and a variable's `X` attribute its value. `ObjBound` is the dual bound, `Start` a starting value, `LB` the lower bound.
mip-f-333 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy the four variable classes of the course are declared: binary, non-negative integer, non-negative continuous, free continuous. Which declaration corresponds to the **non-negative integer**?
  1. `m.addVar(vtype=GRB.BINARY, ub=GRB.INFINITY)`
  2. `m.addVar(vtype=GRB.INTEGER)`
  3. `m.addVar(lb=0)`
  4. `m.addVar(vtype=GRB.INTEGER, lb=-GRB.INFINITY)`
Explanation. The default lower bound is $0$, so `vtype=GRB.INTEGER` suffices. With `lb=-GRB.INFINITY` it would be a free integer; `addVar(lb=0)` is continuous.
mip-f-334 · level ipotesi · notes
In gurobipy the absolute value $|u - v|$ is linearised with a variable $d$ and the constraints $d \ge u - v$, $d \ge v - u$; the signed difference $s = u - v$ instead must be allowed to be negative. A student declares both with `m.addVar()`. Which of the two is declared wrongly?
  1. $d$: an absolute value must be declared with `lb=-GRB.INFINITY`
  2. Neither: `addVar()` creates free variables
  3. $s$: without `lb=-GRB.INFINITY` the lower bound is $0$ and negative values are forbidden
  4. Both: `addVar()` without arguments creates a binary variable
Explanation. `addVar()` creates a continuous variable with $\mathrm{lb} = 0$. For $d \ge |u - v| \ge 0$ that is fine; for $s$, which may be negative, `lb=-GRB.INFINITY` is needed, otherwise the model cuts solutions silently.
mip-f-335 · level facile · notes
In the course code the model is written «one family per block». What does that mean?
  1. Each constraint family goes into a different Gurobi model
  2. Each family of variables or constraints of the mathematical model corresponds to a single statement (`addVars`, `addConstrs`), with a comment naming it
  3. Variables and constraints are written in the same block, mixed, to save lines
  4. Each constraint is written with a separate `addConstr` statement, one per line
Explanation. The one-to-one match between the families of the written model and the code blocks makes the code checkable line by line against the model.
mip-f-336 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy `x = m.addVars(n, k, vtype=GRB.BINARY)` assigns job $j$ to machine $h$, with durations `t[j, h]` and availabilities `a[h]`. Which statement writes the family «machine $h$ does not exceed its availability», one constraint per machine?
  1. `m.addConstrs(gp.quicksum(t[j, h] * x[j, h] for j in range(n)) <= a[h] for h in range(k))`
  2. `m.addConstrs(t[j, h] * x[j, h] <= a[h] for j in range(n) for h in range(k))`
  3. `m.addConstrs(gp.quicksum(t[j, h] * x[j, h] for h in range(k)) <= a[h] for j in range(n))`
  4. `m.addConstr(gp.quicksum(t[j, h] * x[j, h] for j in range(n) for h in range(k)) <= sum(a))`
Explanation. One constraint per machine: the outer generator runs over $h$, the sum over $j$. The second is a single aggregated constraint, the third one constraint per pair, the fourth sums over the wrong index.
mip-f-337 · level facile · notes
Gurobi, minimisation problem with `MIPGap` $= 10^{-4}$ (default): the solver stops as soon as $|\text{ObjVal} - \text{ObjBound}|/|\text{ObjVal}| \le 10^{-4}$. With `ObjVal` $= 20000$, which value of `ObjBound` is enough to stop with `Status = OPTIMAL`?
  1. $19800$
  2. $19990$
  3. $19998$
  4. $19980$
Explanation. One needs $20000 - \text{ObjBound} \le 20000 \cdot 10^{-4} = 2$, that is, $\text{ObjBound} \ge 19998$. That is why «optimal» for Gurobi means optimal within the tolerance.
mip-f-338 · level ipotesi · notes
A course script checks a Gurobi solution with `assert x[j].X == 1` for a binary, and the assert fails even though the solver returned `Status = OPTIMAL`. What is the most likely cause, and the fix?
  1. `X` is the relaxation value: read `x[j].Xn` instead
  2. The value is $1$ only within `IntFeasTol` (for example $0.9999999$): compare `round(x[j].X)` or use a tolerance
  3. Binaries in gurobipy are `True`/`False`: write `x[j].X is True`
  4. The solver returned an infeasible solution: rerun it with `MIPGap = 0`
Explanation. Gurobi deems a variable integer within the integrality tolerance: the floating value may be $0.9999999997$. Exact comparison fails; round or use a tolerance.
mip-f-339 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy `r = m.relax()` produces the linear relaxation of model `m`. What do binary variables become and which of the two course relaxations is obtained?
  1. Continuous in $[0,1]$: one obtains $\zlpp$, the relaxation with bounds
  2. Continuous in $[0, +\infty)$: one obtains $\zlp$, the relaxation without bounds
  3. They disappear: `relax()` fixes them to $0$
  4. They stay binary, but the solver stops branching
Explanation. `relax()` removes the integer type keeping the bounds: a binary becomes continuous in $[0,1]$, that is, the relaxation with bounds $\zlpp$.
mip-f-340 · level facile · notes
Bin packing: items of weight $w = (6, 5, 4, 3, 2)$ in containers of capacity $c = 10$. The lower bound $\lceil \sum_j w_j / c \rceil$ says that at least how many containers are needed?
  1. $1$
  2. $2$
  3. $3$
  4. $5$
Explanation. $\sum_j w_j = 20$ and $20/10 = 2$: at least $2$ containers are needed. Here they suffice: $\{6, 4\}$ and $\{5, 3, 2\}$, so the optimum is $2$.
mip-f-341 · level teorema · notes
Makespan on $k$ identical machines, jobs of duration $d_j$, model $\min z$ with $\sum_j d_j x_{jm} \le z$ for every machine and $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$ for every job. Which value is always a lower bound on the optimum $\zmilp$?
  1. $\sum_j d_j$
  2. $\sum_j d_j / k - \max_j d_j$
  3. $\max\{\sum_j d_j / k,\ \max_j d_j\}$
  4. $\min_j d_j$
Explanation. The average load $\sum_j d_j / k$ cannot exceed the maximum load, and the longest job lies entirely on one machine: both are lower bounds, hence also their maximum. $\sum_j d_j$ is an upper bound (everything on one machine).
mip-f-342 · level ipotesi · notes
TSP on $n$ cities with the MTZ formulation: $u_i - u_j + (n-1)x_{ij} \le n - 2$ for every arc $(i,j)$ with $i, j \ne 1$, and $u_i \in [1, n-1]$ the position of city $i$ in the tour. Why do the constraints **exclude** city $1$?
  1. Because subtours can never pass through city 1
  2. Because city 1 always has position $u_1 = n$
  3. To save constraints: including it would give the same model
  4. The tour must return to the start: with city 1 included the Hamiltonian cycle would be forbidden too
Explanation. Along a cycle using arcs $(i,j)$ the constraints impose $u_j \ge u_i + 1$; summing them over a cycle gives a contradiction. Excluding city 1 forbids only the cycles that do not touch it, that is, subtours, and the full tour survives.
mip-f-343 · level facile · notes
In the course protocol every problem produces the table $\ub$ (heuristic), $\lb$ (hand-built dual), $\zlp$ and $\zlpp$ (relaxations without and with the bounds $0 \le x \le 1$), $\zmilp$ (integer optimum), gap. Where does the value $\zlp$ (relaxation without bounds) come from in the code?
  1. From the model of the relaxation without bounds, or from the hand-written dual, solved with Gurobi: by strong duality the two values coincide
  2. From `m.ObjBound` after optimising the MILP
  3. From the constructive heuristic, as the value of its solution
  4. From `m.relax()`, which also drops the upper bounds of the binaries
Explanation. `relax()` keeps the bounds and gives $\zlpp$; `ObjBound` is the solver's final bound; the heuristic gives $\ub$. The value $\zlp$ is the optimum of the relaxation without bounds, equal by strong duality to the optimum of the hand-written dual: this is what the `assert` checks.
mip-f-344 · level facile · notes
In the course protocol, why does the hand-built dual solution go through a feasibility check (every dual constraint and every sign) before its value enters the table?
  1. Because the dual value must coincide with the integer optimum
  2. Because Gurobi rejects non-integer dual solutions
  3. Because only a feasible dual solution gives a valid bound by weak duality
  4. Because the hand-built dual is always optimal and only needs confirming
Explanation. Weak duality holds for feasible dual solutions: a solution violating even one dual constraint gives no bound. A hand-built solution is in general not optimal.
mip-f-345 · level facile · notes
The course code also runs on Colab: each chapter has a notebook opened from the badge at the top of the page. What does the first cell of each notebook do?
  1. It installs `gurobipy` and downloads the three common Python modules of the course
  2. It activates the academic Gurobi licence from the portal
  3. It solves all the course models and saves the CSV files
  4. It runs `verifica_numeri.py` on all chapters
Explanation. The first cell prepares the environment: it installs the pip package `gurobipy` (with the bundled size-limited licence, enough for the course instances) and downloads the common modules; the following cells build and solve the chapter's models.
mip-f-346 · level facile · notes
From the course's `python/` folder, after creating the virtual environment and installing `gurobipy pandas matplotlib`, which command runs all chapters and notebooks at once?
  1. `python3 fam07_1_assegnamento.py`
  2. `python3 esegui_tutti.py`
  3. `pip install esegui_tutti`
  4. `python3 verifica_numeri.py`
Explanation. `esegui_tutti.py` runs all scripts and notebooks; `verifica_numeri.py` checks the quoted numbers with `assert`s; `fam07_1_assegnamento.py` runs a single problem.
mip-f-511 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, what is the difference between `m.addConstr(...)` and `m.addConstrs(...)`?
  1. `addConstrs` adds variables and constraints together
  2. They are the same statement under two names
  3. `addConstr` adds linear constraints, `addConstrs` quadratic ones
  4. `addConstr` adds a single constraint; `addConstrs` one per element of a generator (a family)
Explanation. `addConstrs(expr for i in I)` creates one constraint per $i$, indexed like the generator: it is how a family is written in one statement.
mip-f-512 · level facile · notes
Gurobi, after `optimize()`: `Status = INFEASIBLE`. What can be stated, and what cannot be read?
  1. The model is unbounded
  2. The solver found no solutions within the time, but they might exist
  3. The written model has no feasible solutions; `ObjVal` does not exist and the status must be read before any value
  4. `ObjVal` holds the value of the relaxation
Explanation. `INFEASIBLE` is a proof (for the written model) that no solutions exist. It differs from `TIME_LIMIT` with `SolCount = 0`, where nothing is known. In both cases reading `ObjVal` raises an error.
mip-f-513 · level facile · notes
Travelling salesman on $n = 6$ cities, MTZ formulation: $x_{ij}$ binary for every directed arc $(i, j)$ with $i \ne j$, and a position variable $u_i$ for every city other than 1. How many variables does the model have?
  1. $41$
  2. $36$
  3. $30$
  4. $35$
Explanation. Directed arcs: $6 \cdot 5 = 30$; positions: $5$. Total $35$. ($36$ would also count arcs from a city to itself.)
mip-f-514 · level teorema · notes
**Minimisation** MILP with binaries; `r = m.relax()` and `r.optimize()` give $\zlpp$, while the hand-written dual (relaxation without bounds) has optimum $\zlp$. Which relation must result, if everything is correct?
  1. $\zmilp \le \zlpp \le \zlp$
  2. $\zlp = \zlpp$ always
  3. $\zlp \le \zlpp \le \zmilp$
  4. $\zlpp \le \zlp \le \zmilp$
Explanation. `relax()` keeps the bounds $x \le 1$, so it solves a problem with more constraints than the relaxation without bounds: in a minimisation the value can only rise, staying below the integer optimum.
mip-f-528 · level facile · notes
Bin packing in gurobipy: $x_{jb} = 1$ if item $j$ goes into container $b$, $y_b = 1$ if the container is used, capacity $c$. Which constraint links capacity and use of container $b$?
  1. $\sum_j w_j x_{jb} \le c$
  2. $\sum_b x_{jb} \le y_b$
  3. $\sum_j w_j x_{jb} \le c\, y_b$
  4. $x_{jb} \le c\, y_b$
Explanation. The aggregated constraint does two things: it respects the capacity of a used container and, with $y_b = 0$, forbids placing items in it. Without $y_b$ the model would not count the used containers.
mip-f-529 · level facile · notes
Makespan on identical machines: $\min z$ with $\sum_j d_j x_{jm} \le z$ for every machine $m$. Why at the optimum does $z$ equal exactly the load of the busiest machine?
  1. Because there is a constraint $z = \max_m \sum_j d_j x_{jm}$
  2. $z$ lies above every load by the constraints and the objective pushes it down: it stops at the maximum load
  3. It is not true: $z$ may stay larger than the maximum load
  4. Because $z$ is declared integer
Explanation. It is the maximum variable: the constraints give $z \ge$ every load, minimisation brings it to equality with the largest. The $\max$ cannot be written directly in a linear model.
mip-f-538 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, which statement creates an empty model and which solves it?
  1. `m = gp.Env()` and `m.solve()`
  2. `m = gp.Model()` and `m.run()`
  3. `m = GRB.Model()` and `gp.optimize(m)`
  4. `m = gp.Model()` and `m.optimize()`
Explanation. `gp.Model()` creates the model, to which variables, constraints and objective are added; `optimize()` solves it, and only afterwards are `Status`, `ObjVal` and the `X` values read.
mip-f-539 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, how is a binary variable `y` declared?
  1. `y = m.addVar(lb=0, ub=1)`
  2. `y = m.addVar(vtype='bool')`
  3. `y = m.addVar(vtype=GRB.BINARY)`
  4. `y = m.addVar(vtype=GRB.INTEGER, lb=-1, ub=1)`
Explanation. `GRB.BINARY` imposes values in $\{0,1\}$. `lb=0, ub=1` without a type gives a continuous variable in $[0,1]$, that is, its relaxation.
mip-f-540 · level facile · notes
Gurobi, maximisation problem: `Status = OPTIMAL`, `ObjVal` $= 120$, `ObjBound` $= 120.005$, with default `MIPGap` $10^{-4}$. What is reported?
  1. $\zmilp = 120.005$
  2. $\zmilp = 120.0025$, the midpoint
  3. $120 \le \zmilp \le 120.005$, optimality not proved
  4. $\zmilp = 120$ (optimal within the tolerance)
Explanation. With `OPTIMAL` one reports `ObjVal`; the residual gap ($\approx 0.004\%$) is below the tolerance, which is why the solver stopped.
mip-f-541 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy one solves `r = m.relax()` on an assignment model with constraints $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$, and gets an integer solution. What does it mean?
  1. The relaxation with bounds has an integer optimum, so $\zlpp = \zmilp$ and that solution is optimal for the MILP too
  2. That the model was written wrongly
  3. Nothing: the MILP must be solved anyway to get a bound
  4. That `relax()` did not remove integrality
Explanation. If the relaxation optimum is feasible for the integer problem (integrality included), it is optimal for it too. In assignment models this often happens, because of the matrix structure.
mip-f-542 · level facile · notes
In the course protocol, which step comes **before** solving the MIP with the solver?
  1. Heuristic with feasibility check, then hand-written dual and relaxations
  2. The figures and notebooks
  3. The bound table
  4. Computing the heuristic gap
Explanation. Order: data → model → heuristic and check → LP and dual → MIP → table → figures and notebooks. The heuristic gap needs the optimum, so it comes after the MIP.
mip-f-543 · level teorema · notes
Gurobi, minimisation problem, `Status = TIME_LIMIT`, `SolCount` $\ge 1$, `ObjVal` $= 75$, `ObjBound` $= 70$. Which statement is proved?
  1. $\zmilp \ge 75$
  2. $\zmilp = 70$
  3. $70 \le \zmilp \le 75$
  4. $\zmilp = 75$
Explanation. The solution found is feasible (upper bound in a minimisation), the dual bound is a lower bound: the optimum lies between the two, but which value it takes is not proved.
mip-f-544 · level teorema · notes
Bin packing: containers of capacity $c$, items of weight $w_j$. Which value is always a lower bound on the minimum number of containers?
  1. the number of items
  2. $\lfloor \sum_j w_j / c \rfloor - 1$
  3. $\max_j w_j$
  4. $\lceil \sum_j w_j / c \rceil$
Explanation. The used containers carry at most $c$ each: at least $\sum_j w_j / c$ containers are needed, and the number is integer. The number of items is an upper bound (one per container).

4. Constructive heuristics (44)

mip-f-039 · level facile · notes
A constructive heuristic, applied to a **maximisation** MILP with optimum $\zmilp$, returns a feasible integer solution of value $16$. How is the result written correctly?
  1. $\zmilp = 16$
  2. $\zlp \le 16$
  3. $\zmilp \le \ub = 16$
  4. $\lb = 16 \le \zmilp$
Explanation. In a maximisation a feasible solution is worth at most the optimum: it is a lower bound. Calling the result of a heuristic on a maximisation $\ub$ is the commonest sign mistake.
mip-f-040 · level facile · notes
Assignment of jobs to machines with limited availability. The next-fit heuristic (fills one machine at a time and never goes back) cannot place the last job and answers "no solution found". What does one conclude?
  1. That the problem is infeasible and the model must be corrected
  2. That an improvement heuristic is needed to prove infeasibility
  3. Nothing about the feasibility of the problem: the failure concerns the heuristic
  4. That the linear relaxation is infeasible
Explanation. A constructive heuristic is myopic and never goes back. To prove that a model is infeasible one needs the solver (`Status = INFEASIBLE`) or a proof.
mip-f-041 · level facile · notes
Bin packing: items to be placed, in the given order, into bins of fixed capacity. What distinguishes the next-fit heuristic from first-fit?
  1. First-fit opens a new bin for every item; next-fit fills the current one as long as it can
  2. Next-fit first sorts the items by decreasing weight; first-fit takes them in the given order
  3. Next-fit chooses the bin left fullest; first-fit the first bin where the item fits
  4. Next-fit looks only at the current bin and never goes back; first-fit tries all the open bins in order
Explanation. Next-fit closes a bin as soon as an item does not fit; first-fit puts the item into the first open bin where it fits. Choosing the fullest bin is best-fit; both scan the items in the given order.
mip-f-042 · level facile · notes
Uncapacitated lot sizing (choose in which periods to produce to cover the demand, with setup and holding costs). What is the relation between the "least unit cost" rule and the Wagner–Whitin procedure?
  1. Wagner–Whitin is the hand-made dual of least unit cost, and gives a lower bound on it
  2. Least unit cost is a heuristic, and its value is only a bound; Wagner–Whitin is an exact algorithm
  3. They are two names for the same heuristic, which picks setups at the lowest average unit cost
  4. Least unit cost is exact on this model, Wagner–Whitin is an improvement heuristic
Explanation. Wagner–Whitin solves uncapacitated lot sizing to optimality by dynamic programming, in polynomial time; least unit cost decides one setup at a time and may stop above the optimum.
mip-f-043 · level facile · notes
Makespan on $3$ identical machines (minimise the largest load): LPT gives loads $(11, 8, 8)$, while the optimum is $9$. Local search with the single move "shift one job to another machine" stops at $11$. What does one conclude?
  1. It proved that the linear relaxation is worth $11$
  2. It found a better lower bound than the elementary one
  3. It proved that $11$ is the optimal makespan for this instance
  4. It found a local optimum, which is still only an upper bound on the optimal makespan
Explanation. Local search returns a feasible solution, hence an upper bound in a minimisation, and nothing else. Here reaching $9$ needs a swap move between two machines.
mip-f-044 · level facile · notes
Makespan: seven jobs of duration $t = (5, 5, 4, 4, 3, 3, 3)$ on $k = 3$ identical machines, minimising the load of the busiest machine. What is the elementary bound $\max(\max_j t_j,\ \sum_j t_j / k)$?
  1. $11$
  2. $27$
  3. $9$
  4. $5$
Explanation. $\max_j t_j = 5$ and $\sum_j t_j / k = 27/3 = 9$: the bound is $9$, and here it is exactly the optimum, while LPT gives $11$.
mip-f-045 · level facile · notes
Binary knapsack with values $p = (10, 7, 6, 4)$, weights $w = (5, 4, 3, 3)$, capacity $9$. The heuristic scanning by decreasing $p_j/w_j$ takes items 1 and 3 (value $16$); the optimum takes 1 and 2 (value $17$). Why does the heuristic fail?
  1. The heuristic actually picks by increasing weight, not by ratio
  2. The heuristic violates the capacity and the solution must be repaired
  3. Item 2 has the highest ratio and the heuristic discards it
  4. After items 1 and 3 there is a residual capacity that no remaining item can use
Explanation. After items 1 and 3 the residual is $1$ and stays unused, while 1 and 2 fill the knapsack exactly. In a maximisation $\lb = 16 \le \zmilp = 17$.
mip-f-046 · level facile · notes
Travelling salesman on $n$ cities: the nearest neighbour heuristic (always moves to the closest unvisited city) is run from all $n$ starting cities and the shortest tour is kept. What does one obtain?
  1. A certificate of optimality when two different starts give the same tour
  2. A lower bound on the length of the optimal tour, because the minimum is taken
  3. An upper bound that is in general better, at $n$ times the cost: still a bound from one side only
  4. The optimal tour, because every city has been tried as a start
Explanation. It is the simplest form of multi-start: many feasible solutions, keeping the best. It is still a feasible solution, hence an upper bound; whether it is optimal can only be known some other way.
mip-f-047 · level teorema · notes
Makespan: $n$ jobs of duration $t_j$ on $k$ identical machines, minimising the largest load. LPT sorts the jobs by decreasing duration and puts each on the least loaded machine. Which statement is correct?
  1. LPT is optimal when the durations are all different from each other
  2. With $k = 2$ machines LPT always finds the optimal assignment
  3. The makespan of any assignment, LPT included, is at least $\max(\max_j t_j,\ \sum_j t_j / k)$
  4. The makespan of LPT is always at most $\sum_j t_j / k$
Explanation. The machine receiving the longest job works at least $\max_j t_j$, and the busiest works at least the average $\sum_j t_j / k$: the bound holds for every feasible solution. LPT remains a heuristic.
mip-f-048 · level teorema · notes
A constructive heuristic for a MILP builds a solution one decision at a time, without going back. Which statement is correct?
  1. A solution satisfying the linear constraints with some fractional component still gives a primal bound
  2. It always returns a feasible solution, possibly of poor quality
  3. Its value is always within $50\%$ of the integer optimum
  4. If it ends with a solution satisfying constraints, bounds and integrality, its value is a primal bound; if it fails it gives no bound
Explanation. The heuristic can get stuck halfway; when it ends with a feasible solution of the MILP, integrality included, that is the pessimistic side of the sandwich. It proves nothing about quality.
mip-f-049 · level ipotesi · notes
Minimisation MILP. To declare that the value of a hand-built solution is an upper bound on the integer optimum, what must be checked?
  1. That the solution is close to the optimal solution of the relaxation
  2. That the solution is a local optimum for local search
  3. That the solution satisfies the constraints, the variable bounds and integrality
  4. That the solution satisfies the linear constraints only, possibly with fractional components
Explanation. The feasibility check is not a formality: a solution with a fractional component is feasible for the relaxation, not for the MILP, and its value is not a primal bound.
mip-f-050 · level ipotesi · notes
Bin packing (minimum number of bins of capacity $7$): items of total weight $21$, and the best-fit heuristic packs them into $3$ bins. To conclude without a solver that $3$ is optimal, what is needed?
  1. The fact that best-fit is exact for bin packing when the weights are integer
  2. The elementary bound $\lceil 21/7 \rceil = 3$, valid for every solution, together with the feasible best-fit solution
  3. The linear relaxation with continuous $y_b$ being equal to $3$, together with the best-fit solution
  4. That next-fit and first-fit also find a solution with $3$ bins
Explanation. Best-fit gives $\ub = 3$; the total weight forces at least $\lceil 21/7 \rceil = 3$ bins, i.e. $\lb = 3$. The two bounds coincide and the optimum is proved.
mip-f-162 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost set covering: teams are chosen to cover all the zones. The constructive heuristic always picks the team with the smallest ratio $c_j / \text{new}(j)$, where $\text{new}(j)$ is the number of still uncovered zones that $j$ covers. Teams: A costs $6$ and covers $3$ uncovered zones, B costs $4$ and covers $1$, C costs $5$ and covers $2$. Which is picked first?
  1. B (smallest cost)
  2. A (ratio $2$)
  3. None: ties are broken at random
  4. C (ratio $5/2$)
Explanation. The ratios are $6/3 = 2$, $4/1 = 4$ and $5/2$: A wins. The criterion is the cost per newly covered zone, not the absolute cost, which would pick B.
mip-f-163 · level facile · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack: values $p = (12, 9, 8)$, weights $w = (6, 5, 4)$, capacity $10$. The heuristic scans the items by decreasing $p_j / w_j$ and takes every item that still fits. What value does it return?
  1. $21$
  2. $20$
  3. $17$
  4. $12$
Explanation. The ratios are $2$, $1.8$ and $2$: with equal ratios the smaller index comes first, so the order is 1, 3, 2. Item 1 fits (residual $4$) and so does item 3 (residual $0$): value $12 + 8 = 20$, a lower bound. Here it is also the optimum.
mip-f-164 · level facile · notes
Lot sizing with setup $f = 50$ and holding $h = 1$ per unit and period; demands $d = (20, 10, 30)$. The least unit cost rule, starting from period 1, computes the average cost per unit covering $1$, $2$ or $3$ periods. What is it when covering the first **two** periods?
  1. $60$
  2. $5/3$
  3. $2$
  4. $5/2$
Explanation. One produces $20 + 10 = 30$ units in period 1; the $10$ of period 2 stay one period in stock. Cost $(50 + 1 \cdot 10)/30 = 60/30 = 2$. $5/3$ forgets the holding; $60$ is the total cost, not the cost per unit.
mip-f-165 · level teorema · notes
Local search on a minimisation MILP: starting from a feasible solution, a move is applied as long as a better neighbour exists. Which statement is correct?
  1. It always stops at the global optimum, if the move is rich enough
  2. It stops at a local optimum with respect to that move, which is an upper bound but in general not the optimum
  3. The value where it stops is a lower bound, because no neighbour is better
  4. It stops at the global optimum when it starts from the constructive heuristic's solution
Explanation. "No better neighbour" holds only within the neighbourhood defined by the move: the solution remains feasible, hence an upper bound in a minimisation, and proving its optimality needs a bound from the other side.
mip-f-208 · level facile · notes
Travelling salesman, nearest neighbour heuristic: from the starting city one moves each time to the nearest unvisited city, and at the end returns to the start. Why is the last arc often the longest of the tour?
  1. Because the heuristic chooses the longest arc to close the tour
  2. It is forced: one returns to the start whatever the distance, without choosing it
  3. Because the return arc counts twice in the tour length
  4. Because the cities left for last are always the farthest from each other
Explanation. The heuristic is myopic: it chooses the arcs one at a time without looking ahead, and the closing arc is not chosen, it is suffered.
mip-f-209 · level facile · notes
Three jobs of duration $(3, 3, 2)$ go on two machines with availability $(5, 3)$ minutes. Next-fit (fills one machine at a time, in order, never going back) puts job 1 on machine 1, job 2 on machine 2, and has nowhere to put job 3. Is the problem feasible?
  1. No: next-fit explores all the assignments before failing
  2. Yes, but only by splitting job 3 between the two machines
  3. No: the total duration $8$ exceeds the total availability
  4. Yes: jobs 2 and 3 on machine 1 ($5$) and job 1 on machine 2 ($3$)
Explanation. The total duration is $8 = 5 + 3$, and the assignment $\{2, 3\} \to 1$, $\{1\} \to 2$ fills it exactly. The failure of a myopic heuristic is information about the heuristic, not about the problem.
mip-f-210 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost set covering: teams A, B, C, D cost $4, 3, 5, 3$ and cover zones A $\{1,3,4\}$, B $\{1,2,5\}$, C $\{2,3,6\}$, D $\{4,5,6\}$. The heuristic chooses each time the team with minimum ratio cost / new zones covered (equal ratios: the first in alphabetical order), until everything is covered. What cost does it obtain?
  1. $8$
  2. $10$
  3. $11$
  4. $6$
Explanation. Step 1: ratios $4/3$, $1$, $5/3$, $1$: B is chosen. Step 2: A covers $\{3,4\}$ ($2$), C $\{3,6\}$ ($5/2$), D $\{4,6\}$ ($3/2$): D is chosen. Step 3: zone 3 is left, A ($4$) beats C ($5$). Cost $3 + 3 + 4 = 10$; B and D alone ($6$) leave zone 3 uncovered.
mip-f-211 · level facile · notes
Among the course's constructive heuristics (next-fit, first-fit and best-fit for bin packing, LPT for the makespan, covering, ratio rule for the knapsack, nearest neighbour for the TSP, least unit cost for lot sizing), which gives a **lower** bound on the optimum?
  1. Best-fit for bin packing, because it fills the containers best
  2. None: a heuristic always gives an upper bound
  3. LPT for the makespan, because the makespan is a maximum of the loads
  4. The ratio rule for the knapsack, because the knapsack is a maximisation problem
Explanation. A feasible solution gives the pessimistic side: an upper bound in a minimisation, a lower bound in a maximisation. The knapsack is the only maximisation problem in the list; the makespan is minimised.
mip-f-212 · level facile · notes
On the instances of the heuristics chapter, best-fit (bin packing) and the covering heuristic find the optimum (gap $0\%$), while next-fit has gap $66.7\%$. What could be known **before** solving the MILP?
  1. That next-fit would be wrong, because it looks at a single container
  2. Nothing about the gap: without a bound on the other side $0\%$ and $67\%$ are told apart only afterwards
  3. That best-fit would be optimal, because it is the most accurate rule
  4. That the gap would be zero for all the heuristics of a minimisation problem
Explanation. A heuristic alone says how much a realisable solution costs, not how much is being lost: this is why the course always asks for two bounds, a primal and a dual one.
mip-f-351 · level teorema · notes
A constructive heuristic for a **minimisation** MILP stops with the message «no solution found». Which conclusion is correct?
  1. The MILP is infeasible, because the heuristic tried all possibilities
  2. None about the problem: the heuristic is myopic; infeasibility is proved by the solver (`INFEASIBLE`) or by a proof
  3. The primal bound is $+\infty$ and the gap is $0\%$
  4. The linear relaxation is infeasible
Explanation. The heuristic decides one thing at a time and never goes back: it may get stuck even on feasible instances. The failure is information about the heuristic, not about the problem.
mip-f-352 · level facile · notes
Bin packing: items of weight $w = (4, 4, 5, 3, 2, 3)$, in the given order, into containers of capacity $C = 7$. **First-fit** puts each item in the first open container where it fits, otherwise opens a new one. How many containers does it use?
  1. $6$
  2. $4$
  3. $5$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $4 \to$ b1; $4 \to$ b2; $5 \to$ b3; $3 \to$ b1 (filled to $7$); $2 \to$ b2 ($6$); $3$ fits nowhere (residuals $0, 1, 2$) $\to$ b4. Four containers; next-fit uses $5$, best-fit $3$.
mip-f-353 · level facile · notes
Bin packing, **best-fit** heuristic on the filling: each item goes, among the open containers where it fits, into the one with the smallest residual space after insertion (ties: smallest index); if it fits nowhere a new one is opened. Open containers with residuals $(3, 2, 5)$; an item of weight $2$ arrives. Where does it go?
  1. Into a new container
  2. Into container 2, which is left with residual $0$
  3. Into container 3, the one with most space
  4. Into container 1, the first where it fits
Explanation. It fits in all three, with final residuals $1$, $0$, $3$: the minimum is container 2. First-fit would choose container 1, worst-fit container 3.
mip-f-354 · level facile · notes
Makespan on $k = 2$ identical machines, jobs of duration $t = (2, 3, 4, 5)$. **List scheduling** in the given order (each job on the least loaded machine, ties: smallest index) gives one makespan; LPT (same rule, jobs by decreasing duration) another. What are they, in order?
  1. List scheduling $8$, LPT $8$
  2. List scheduling $8$, LPT $7$
  3. List scheduling $9$, LPT $7$
  4. List scheduling $7$, LPT $7$
Explanation. Given order: $2 \to$ M1, $3 \to$ M2, $4 \to$ M1 (loads $6, 3$), $5 \to$ M2 (loads $6, 8$): makespan $8$. LPT: $5 \to$ M1, $4 \to$ M2, $3 \to$ M2 ($5, 7$), $2 \to$ M1 ($7, 7$): makespan $7$, equal to the bound $\sum t / k = 7$, hence optimal.
mip-f-355 · level ipotesi · notes
LPT for the makespan on identical machines sorts the jobs by **decreasing** duration and puts each on the least loaded machine. If the sorting is removed (list scheduling in the given order), what is lost?
  1. Nothing: the least-loaded rule gives the same result in any order
  2. Feasibility: without sorting some jobs remain unassigned
  3. The guarantee that the result is optimal, which LPT always has
  4. Long jobs may come last onto already loaded machines, and the makespan worsens: the decreasing order is essential
Explanation. Without sorting the solution stays feasible but long jobs placed at the end unbalance the loads. LPT is not always optimal anyway: on $t = (5,5,4,4,3,3,3)$, $k = 3$ it gives $11$ against $9$.
mip-f-356 · level facile · notes
Set covering, cheapest-completion heuristic: at each step the element $j$ with minimum **cost per new zone** $c_j / \text{new}(j)$ is chosen, where $\text{new}(j)$ counts the still uncovered zones that $j$ covers (ties: smallest index). At the first step the costs are $c = (4, 3, 5, 3)$ and the new zones $(3, 3, 3, 2)$. Which element is chosen?
  1. Element 2 (ratio $1$)
  2. Element 1 (covers more zones at equal cost)
  3. Element 4 (minimum cost $3$, higher index)
  4. Element 4 (ratio $3/2$)
Explanation. The ratios are $4/3$, $1$, $5/3$, $3/2$: the minimum is element 2. The criterion is the cost per new zone, not the absolute cost (where 2 and 4 would tie).
mip-f-357 · level ipotesi · notes
Constructive covering heuristic (minimum cost): at some step uncovered zones remain, but no element not yet chosen covers any of them ($\text{new}(j) = 0$ for every $j$). What must the heuristic do, and why?
  1. Return «no solution found»: with no new zones the ratio $c_j/\text{new}(j)$ is undefined and the cover cannot be completed
  2. Return the partial solution as a primal bound
  3. Restart from the first step with a different order, until it finds a cover
  4. Choose the minimum-cost element, which covers something anyway
Explanation. The partial solution leaves zones uncovered: it is infeasible and gives no bound. The failure case must be declared in the pseudocode.
mip-f-358 · level facile · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack: values $p = (10, 7, 6, 4)$, weights $w = (5, 4, 3, 3)$, capacity $C = 9$. The heuristic scans the items by decreasing $p_j/w_j$ (ties: smallest index) and takes every item that fits in the residual. Which bound is obtained, and of which type?
  1. $\ub = 16$ (items 1 and 3)
  2. $\lb = 16$ (items 1 and 3)
  3. $\ub = 17$ (items 1 and 2)
  4. $\lb = 17$ (items 1 and 2)
Explanation. Ratios $2$, $7/4$, $2$, $4/3$: order 1, 3, 2, 4. It takes 1 (residual $4$) and 3 (residual $1$), then nothing fits: value $16$. In a maximisation a feasible solution gives a lower bound; the optimum is $17$.
mip-f-359 · level teorema · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack with a single capacity constraint. The heuristic by decreasing ratio $p_j/w_j$ gives $\lb$; the dual of the relaxation with $\bar v = \max_j p_j/w_j$ gives $\ub = C\,\bar v$; $\zmilp$ is the integer optimum. Which statement is always true?
  1. $\ub$ always coincides with the optimum of the relaxation
  2. $\lb \le \zmilp \le \ub$, but neither is in general equal to the optimum
  3. The ratio heuristic is always optimal on the binary knapsack
  4. $\ub - \lb$ never exceeds the value of the lightest item
Explanation. The first is a lower bound (feasible solution), the second an upper bound (feasible dual). The heuristic may fail (unused residual) and $C\bar v$ is in general higher than $\zlp$.
mip-f-360 · level facile · notes
Travelling salesman on five cities, symmetric distances: $d_{12} = 5$, $d_{13} = 2$, $d_{14} = 2$, $d_{15} = 9$, $d_{23} = 4$, $d_{24} = 3$, $d_{25} = 4$, $d_{34} = 4$, $d_{35} = 7$, $d_{45} = 7$. Nearest neighbour starts from city 2 (ties: smallest index) and finally returns to the start. How long is the tour?
  1. $16$
  2. $18$
  3. $19$
  4. $25$
Explanation. From 2 the nearest is 4 ($3$); from 4 city 1 ($2$); from 1 city 3 ($2$); city 5 remains ($7$); back to 2 ($4$). Total $3 + 2 + 2 + 7 + 4 = 18$, which here is the optimum; starting from 1 one gets $25$.
mip-f-361 · level facile · notes
Travelling salesman: nearest neighbour is run from **all** $n$ starting cities and the shortest tour is kept (multi-start). What is obtained?
  1. The TSP optimum, because all starts have been tried
  2. A lower bound, because the minimum of the tours is taken
  3. An upper bound better than or equal to that of a single start, at the cost of $n$ runs; it remains a one-sided bound
  4. The same tour from every start, because the rule is deterministic
Explanation. Every tour is feasible, so the best one is still only an upper bound (minimisation). Different starts give different tours; trying all starts is not the same as enumerating all tours.
mip-f-362 · level facile · notes
Uncapacitated lot sizing: demands $d = (20, 10, 30, 40, 10)$, setup cost $f = 50$, holding cost $h = 1$ per unit and period. The least unit cost rule, from period 1, compares the average cost per unit of covering $1$ period ($50/20$) or $2$ periods ($(50 + 10)/30$). Which does it choose, and with what average cost?
  1. $3$ periods, average cost $1.5$
  2. $1$ period, average cost $2.5$
  3. $2$ periods, average cost $2$
  4. $2$ periods, average cost $2.5$
Explanation. One period: $50/20 = 2.5$. Two periods: the $10$ units of period 2 are held one period, cost $50 + 10 = 60$ over $30$ units, that is $2$. (Three periods: $50 + 10 + 60 = 120$ over $60$, again $2$: ties keep the smaller number.)
mip-f-363 · level teorema · notes
Uncapacitated lot sizing. What is the difference between the least unit cost rule and the Wagner–Whitin procedure?
  1. Wagner–Whitin is an exact algorithm (dynamic programming) for this model; least unit cost is a heuristic and gives only an upper bound
  2. They are the same procedure under two names
  3. Wagner–Whitin gives a lower bound, least unit cost an upper bound
  4. Wagner–Whitin is a faster heuristic; least unit cost is exact
Explanation. Wagner–Whitin solves uncapacitated lot sizing to optimality in polynomial time; the least unit cost rule looks at one launch at a time and may fail (on the course example $200$ against $170$).
mip-f-364 · level facile · notes
Makespan on $3$ identical machines, loads $L = (11, 8, 8)$ with machine 1 holding jobs $(5, 3, 3)$. Local search uses only the move «move one job to another machine» and accepts only moves that reduce the makespan. What happens?
  1. No move improves: moving a job of $3$ brings another machine to $11$; the search stops at $11$
  2. Local search always finds the optimum $9$ with move operations
  3. Moving a job of $3$ brings the makespan to $9$
  4. Moving the job of $5$ brings the makespan to $13$, then it goes down to $9$
Explanation. Removing a $3$ lowers machine 1 to $8$, but the receiving machine rises to $11$: the makespan does not decrease. Moving the $5$ gives $13$. To reach $9$ a swap move is needed.
mip-f-365 · level ipotesi · notes
Minimisation MILP. A local search starts from a heuristic solution ($\ub = 30$) and stops at a local optimum of value $26$. A student writes «$\zmilp = 26$, because no move improves». What is missing to claim it?
  1. A lower bound equal to $26$ (for example from a feasible dual or from the solver): the local optimum gives only $\ub = 26$
  2. Checking that the solution is integer: if so, it is optimal
  3. Nothing: a local optimum is always global in MILPs
  4. Repeating the local search with the same move
Explanation. Local search returns a feasible solution, that is, a bound on the pessimistic side. Having stopped does not mean having arrived: a bound from the other side matching it is needed.
mip-f-366 · level facile · notes
Two machines with availabilities $(5, 3)$ and three jobs of duration $(3, 3, 2)$, all to be assigned. Which assignment proves the problem feasible, even though next-fit fails?
  1. Jobs 1 and 2 on machine 1, job 3 on machine 2
  2. None: the failure of next-fit proves that none exists
  3. Jobs 1 and 3 on machine 2, job 2 on machine 1
  4. Jobs 2 and 3 on machine 1 ($5$), job 1 on machine 2 ($3$)
Explanation. $3 + 2 = 5 \le 5$ and $3 \le 3$: feasible. The others violate an availability ($6 > 5$, $5 > 3$). The failure of the heuristic says nothing about the existence of solutions.
mip-f-367 · level facile · notes
An assignment MILP with availabilities: the constructive heuristic fails. Which tool can **prove** that the model is infeasible?
  1. A second constructive heuristic with a different order, if it fails too
  2. The solver, returning `Status = INFEASIBLE`, or a mathematical proof
  3. Local search starting from the partial solution
  4. The linear relaxation, if it has a fractional solution
Explanation. Heuristics, even several, do not prove non-existence: they are myopic. A feasible fractional relaxation proves nothing about the integer problem; the solver (or an argument) is needed.
mip-f-368 · level facile · notes
In the course's overview of heuristics, next-fit on bin packing uses $5$ containers when the optimum is $3$. What is the heuristic gap $|\ub - \zmilp|/|\zmilp|$?
  1. $66.7\%$
  2. $2\%$
  3. $33.3\%$
  4. $40\%$
Explanation. $(5 - 3)/3 = 2/3 \approx 66.7\%$. Dividing by $\ub$ would give $40\%$; $33.3\%$ is the gap of first-fit ($4$ containers).
mip-f-369 · level facile · notes
In the course's overview of heuristics, the ratio heuristic on the knapsack (maximisation) finds $16$ and the optimum is $17$. How is the result written correctly?
  1. $\lb = 16 \le \zmilp = 17$, gap $5.9\%$
  2. $\ub = 17$, gap $0\%$
  3. $\ub = 16 \ge \zmilp$, gap $5.9\%$
  4. $\lb = 16$, gap $6.25\%$
Explanation. In a maximisation a feasible solution gives a lower bound. Gap with respect to the optimum: $(17 - 16)/17 \approx 5.9\%$; dividing by $16$ gives $6.25\%$.
mip-f-515 · level teorema · notes
Bin packing with containers of capacity $C$ and items of total weight $W$. Best-fit returns $k$ containers and $k = \lceil W / C \rceil$. What can be concluded?
  1. Best-fit is always optimal
  2. Nothing: the solver is needed to confirm
  3. The solution is optimal only if all containers are exactly full
  4. The solution is optimal: $\lceil W/C \rceil$ is a lower bound and the solution attains it
Explanation. No solution uses fewer than $\lceil W/C \rceil$ containers; a feasible solution matching it closes the sandwich: $\lb = \ub$. The containers need not be full.
mip-f-516 · level ipotesi · notes
TSP, nearest neighbour: a student claims «the rule picks the shortest available arc at each step, so the tour is optimal». Which observation refutes it?
  1. The rule is optimal only with symmetric distances
  2. The tour obtained may visit a city twice
  3. The rule does not pick the shortest arc, but the longest
  4. Locally best choices do not guarantee the best tour: the final return arc is forced and may be very long
Explanation. The heuristic is myopic: on the course instance the tour from 1 is $25$ long against the optimum $18$, and the last arc ($5 \to 1$, length $9$) is the one that costs.
mip-f-548 · level teorema · notes
Makespan on identical machines, LPT on $t = (5, 5, 4, 4, 3, 3, 3)$ and $k = 3$: $\ub = 11$; bound $\max(\max_j t_j, \sum_j t_j/k) = 9$, which is the optimum. Which general conclusion is correct?
  1. LPT gives a feasible solution but is not always optimal; the elementary bound may coincide with the optimum
  2. The elementary bound is never attained
  3. If LPT is not optimal, the problem is infeasible
  4. LPT is always optimal with identical machines
Explanation. LPT is a heuristic: here it misses by $22.2\%$. The bound $\max(\max t, \sum t/k)$ is exact in this case, but one knows it only after finding a solution of value $9$.
mip-f-549 · level teorema · notes
In the course's overview of heuristics some find the optimum (gap $0\%$) and others do not (up to $67\%$). What can be said **before** solving the MILP?
  1. That the best heuristic is always best-fit
  2. Nothing about quality: a heuristic alone gives only a one-sided bound; a bound from the other side is needed to measure what is lost
  3. That heuristics with gap $0\%$ are those with more steps
  4. That the heuristic gap is computed from its value
Explanation. The heuristic gap needs the optimum; without it, a dual bound is needed for the certified gap. That is why the course always asks for two bounds.

Part: Modelling techniques (108)

5. Logic and binary variables (43)

mip-f-051 · level facile · notes
In Boolean logic a literal is a variable or its negation. When is an expression in **conjunctive normal form** (CNF)?
  1. When every variable appears in a single clause
  2. When it is an OR of terms, each an AND of literals
  3. When it is an AND of clauses, each an OR of literals
  4. When it contains no negated variable
Explanation. A clause is an OR of literals and CNF is an AND of clauses: it is the form from which one linear constraint per clause is written. An OR of ANDs is the disjunctive normal form.
mip-f-052 · level facile · notes
Binary variables $x_1, x_2, x_3$ ($1$ = true). Which linear constraint is satisfied exactly by the assignments that make the clause $x_1 \lor \lnot x_2 \lor x_3$ true?
  1. $x_1 - x_2 + x_3 \ge 0$
  2. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \ge 1$
  3. $x_1 - x_2 + x_3 \le 1$
  4. $x_1 - x_2 + x_3 \ge 1$
Explanation. Every OR becomes a $+$, the negative literal $\lnot x_2$ becomes $1 - x_2$, the clause becomes $\ge 1$: $x_1 + 1 - x_2 + x_3 \ge 1$, that is $x_1 - x_2 + x_3 \ge 0$.
mip-f-053 · level facile · notes
Binary variables. The clause $\lnot x_1 \lor \lnot x_6 \lor x_7$ translates into $(1 - x_1) + (1 - x_6) + x_7 \ge 1$. Which of the following is an equivalent form of the same constraint?
  1. $x_1 + x_6 - x_7 \ge 1$
  2. $x_7 - x_1 - x_6 \ge 1$
  3. $x_1 + x_6 + x_7 \le 2$
  4. $x_1 + x_6 - x_7 \le 1$
Explanation. Moving the constants to the right gives $-x_1 - x_6 + x_7 \ge -1$, and multiplying by $-1$ gives $x_1 + x_6 - x_7 \le 1$: it is the same constraint, more readable when there are two or more negative literals.
mip-f-054 · level facile · notes
Binary variables. For the implication $x_a \Rightarrow x_b$ the constraint $x_b - x_a \ge 0$ has already been written. How many more constraints are needed to also impose the contrapositive $\lnot x_b \Rightarrow \lnot x_a$?
  1. One, $x_a - x_b \ge 0$
  2. Two, one per literal
  3. One, $x_a + x_b \le 1$
  4. None: it has the same clause $\lnot x_a \lor x_b$
Explanation. $\lnot x_b \Rightarrow \lnot x_a$ is $x_b \lor \lnot x_a$ (by double negation), the same clause as $x_a \Rightarrow x_b$: the contrapositive is not a second constraint.
mip-f-055 · level facile · notes
Binary variables $x_1, x_2, x_3$. "At most one out of three" is written with the three clauses $x_1 + x_2 \le 1$, $x_1 + x_3 \le 1$, $x_2 + x_3 \le 1$, or with $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 1$. What is the difference, also in the linear relaxation ($0 \le x \le 1$)?
  1. Same binary solutions, but the single constraint is tighter in the relaxation: $(1/2, 1/2, 1/2)$ satisfies the clauses and violates it
  2. Same binary solutions, but the three clauses are tighter in the relaxation because they are more constraints
  3. Different binary solutions: the single constraint excludes assignments that the three clauses allow
  4. Same binary solutions and same relaxation: the two forms are interchangeable in the continuum too
Explanation. On binary assignments the two forms coincide; in the continuum the cardinality constraint cuts off points the clauses allow. When counting is possible, count.
mip-f-056 · level facile · notes
Five binary variables $x_i$, $i \in I$. "At least $2$ out of $5$" is written $\sum_{i \in I} x_i \ge 2$. In CNF the same constraint asks that every subset of $4$ variables contain a true one. How many clauses would be needed?
  1. $32$
  2. $1$
  3. $10$
  4. $5$
Explanation. "At least $p$ out of $|I|$" in CNF asks that every subset of $|I| - p + 1$ elements contain a true one: $\binom{5}{4} = 5$ clauses, against a single cardinality constraint. $10 = \binom{5}{2}$ counts the pairs.
mip-f-057 · level facile · notes
A Boolean expression is **unsatisfiable** if no assignment of the variables makes it true. Which of the following is?
  1. $(x_a \lor x_b) \land (x_c \lor x_d) \land \lnot x_e$
  2. $(x_a \lor x_b) \land (\lnot x_a \lor x_b) \land (x_a \lor \lnot x_b)$
  3. $\lnot x_a \lor \big((x_b \lor x_c) \land (x_d \lor x_e)\big)$
  4. $(x_a \lor x_b) \land (\lnot x_a \lor x_b) \land (x_a \lor \lnot x_b) \land (\lnot x_a \lor \lnot x_b)$
Explanation. Each of the four assignments of $(x_a, x_b)$ falsifies one of the four clauses. Without the last clause $x_a = x_b = 1$ works.
mip-f-058 · level facile · notes
Boolean variables $x_a, x_b, x_c$. "At least two out of $a, b, c$" is written $(x_a \land x_b) \lor (x_a \land x_c) \lor (x_b \land x_c)$. What is its conjunctive normal form?
  1. $x_a \land x_b \land x_c$
  2. $(x_a \lor x_b) \land (x_a \lor x_c) \land (x_b \lor x_c)$
  3. $(x_a \lor x_b \lor x_c) \land (\lnot x_a \lor \lnot x_b \lor \lnot x_c)$
  4. $x_a \lor x_b \lor x_c$
Explanation. Distributivity gives three pairwise clauses: every pair must contain at least one true element, which is exactly "at least two out of three".
mip-f-059 · level facile · notes
Project selection: $x_p \in \{0,1\}$ equals $1$ if project $p$ is chosen. "If project 4 is chosen then at least two out of 1, 2, 3". Which **single** constraint translates it exactly on binary solutions?
  1. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 - x_4 \ge 1$
  2. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \ge 2$
  3. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \ge 2x_4$
  4. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 2x_4$
Explanation. With $x_4 = 1$ it asks for at least two projects, with $x_4 = 0$ it says nothing: the same binary solutions as the three CNF clauses, in a single constraint that is stronger in the relaxation. The third asks for one project only.
mip-f-060 · level facile · notes
For Boolean variables $x_a, x_b$, which law states the equivalence $\lnot(x_a \land x_b) \iff \lnot x_a \lor \lnot x_b$?
  1. The absorption law
  2. The distributive law
  3. The double negation law
  4. De Morgan's law
Explanation. It is De Morgan's law; its other form is $\lnot(x_a \lor x_b) \iff \lnot x_a \land \lnot x_b$. Double negation is $\lnot\lnot x \iff x$.
mip-f-061 · level facile · notes
Boolean variables. Why can the implication $(x_a \land x_b) \Rightarrow x_c$ **not** be replaced by $(x_a \Rightarrow x_c) \land (x_b \Rightarrow x_c)$?
  1. With $x_a = 1$, $x_b = 0$, $x_c = 0$ the original is true and the replacement false: the replacement cuts off allowed solutions
  2. With $x_a = x_b = 0$, $x_c = 1$ the original is false and the replacement true: the replacement admits forbidden solutions
  3. With $x_a = x_b = x_c = 1$ the original is false and the replacement true: the replacement admits forbidden solutions
  4. With $x_a = x_b = 1$, $x_c = 0$ the original is true and the replacement false: the replacement cuts off allowed solutions
Explanation. With a false antecedent $x_a \land x_b$ the original is true, but $x_a \Rightarrow x_c$ is false: the conjunction of the two implications is strictly stronger. Splitting is allowed only with a disjunction in the antecedent.
mip-f-062 · level facile · notes
Binary variables; every CNF clause becomes one linear constraint. How many constraints are needed for $x_a \lor x_b \Rightarrow x_c$, and how many for $x_a \Rightarrow x_b \lor x_c$?
  1. One each
  2. Two the first, one the second
  3. Two each
  4. One the first, two the second
Explanation. The first is $(\lnot x_a \lor x_c) \land (\lnot x_b \lor x_c)$: two clauses. The second is the single clause $\lnot x_a \lor x_b \lor x_c$, i.e. $x_b + x_c - x_a \ge 0$.
mip-f-063 · level media · notes
Boolean variables. The implication $(x_1 \lor x_4) \land x_6 \Rightarrow x_2 \land (x_5 \lor x_7)$ is brought to CNF with $p \Rightarrow q \equiv \lnot p \lor q$, De Morgan and distributivity. How many clauses (and hence linear constraints) are obtained?
  1. $6$
  2. $3$
  3. $4$
  4. $2$
Explanation. Negating the antecedent gives $(\lnot x_1 \lor \lnot x_6) \land (\lnot x_4 \lor \lnot x_6)$, the consequent is $x_2 \land (x_5 \lor x_7)$; distributing the OR yields $2 \cdot 2 = 4$ clauses, for instance $\lnot x_1 \lor \lnot x_6 \lor x_5 \lor x_7$.
mip-f-064 · level teorema · notes
Which equivalence between Boolean expressions holds for every $x_a, x_b, x_c \in \{0,1\}$?
  1. $x_a \Rightarrow (x_b \lor x_c) \iff (x_a \Rightarrow x_b) \land (x_a \Rightarrow x_c)$
  2. $(x_a \land x_b) \Rightarrow x_c \iff (x_a \Rightarrow x_c) \land (x_b \Rightarrow x_c)$
  3. $x_a \Rightarrow x_b \iff x_b \Rightarrow x_a$
  4. $(x_a \lor x_b) \Rightarrow x_c \iff (x_a \Rightarrow x_c) \land (x_b \Rightarrow x_c)$
Explanation. A disjunctive antecedent splits, and so does a conjunctive consequent. A conjunctive antecedent does not, nor does a disjunctive consequent, and an implication is not equivalent to its converse.
mip-f-065 · level teorema · notes
A Boolean expression in the variables $x_1, \dots, x_n$ is translated into a system of linear constraints on the same binary variables. When is the translation **correct**?
  1. When every binary assignment making the expression true satisfies all the constraints
  2. When every binary assignment satisfying all the constraints makes the expression true
  3. When the optimal solution of the linear relaxation makes the expression true
  4. When, for every binary assignment, the expression is true if and only if all the constraints are satisfied
Explanation. Both directions are needed: no true assignment excluded and no false one admitted. With few variables it is checked by enumerating the $2^n$ assignments, which is a proof by cases.
mip-f-066 · level ipotesi · notes
Binary variables $x_1, x_2, x_3$. One wants to prove that $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 1$ and the three clauses $x_i + x_j \le 1$ ($i < j$) have the same binary solutions but different linear relaxations. What is enough?
  1. Enumerating the $8$ binary assignments and exhibiting a fractional point, such as $(1/2, 1/2, 1/2)$, that satisfies the clauses and violates the single constraint
  2. Enumerating the $8$ binary assignments and noting that the clauses outnumber the single constraint
  3. Applying weak duality to the two relaxations and comparing their optimal values
  4. Exhibiting a fractional point that satisfies the single constraint and violates the clauses, without enumerating
Explanation. Enumeration proves equality on binary points; for the difference in the relaxations one continuous point admitted by one formulation and not by the other is enough, and that point lies on the clauses' side.
mip-f-067 · level ipotesi · notes
Boolean variables. To prove that $(x_a \land x_b) \Rightarrow x_c$ is **not** equivalent to $(x_a \Rightarrow x_c) \land (x_b \Rightarrow x_c)$, what is needed?
  1. An assignment making the first true and the second false, such as $x_a = 1$, $x_b = 0$, $x_c = 0$
  2. Applying De Morgan to the contrapositive of the first expression
  3. An assignment making the first false and the second true, such as $x_a = x_b = 1$, $x_c = 0$
  4. Showing that the two expressions have the same number of true assignments
Explanation. One counterexample is enough. It cannot be an assignment making the first false and the second true: the second is stronger, so such an assignment does not exist (with $x_a = x_b = 1$, $x_c = 0$ both are false).
mip-f-068 · level ipotesi · notes
Binary variables. To translate $x_a \Rightarrow x_b \land x_c$ into the two constraints $x_b - x_a \ge 0$ and $x_c - x_a \ge 0$, which results are used?
  1. $p \Rightarrow q \equiv \lnot p \lor q$, distributivity $x \lor (y \land z) \iff (x \lor y) \land (x \lor z)$ and one constraint per clause
  2. $p \Rightarrow q \equiv \lnot p \lor q$, De Morgan $\lnot(y \land z) \iff \lnot y \lor \lnot z$ and one constraint per clause
  3. Distributivity $x \land (y \lor z) \iff (x \land y) \lor (x \land z)$, double negation and one constraint per clause
  4. The contrapositive $\lnot q \Rightarrow \lnot p$, absorption $x \lor (x \land y) \iff x$ and one constraint per clause
Explanation. The implication becomes $\lnot x_a \lor (x_b \land x_c)$, distributivity of OR over AND turns it into $(\lnot x_a \lor x_b) \land (\lnot x_a \lor x_c)$, and every clause gives a constraint.
mip-f-166 · level facile · notes
Binary variables $x_1, x_2$. "Exactly one of the two" (exclusive OR) is true only for $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$. Which linear constraint translates it exactly?
  1. $x_1 + x_2 \ge 1$
  2. $x_1 - x_2 = 0$
  3. $x_1 + x_2 \le 1$
  4. $x_1 + x_2 = 1$
Explanation. It is the CNF $(x_1 \lor x_2) \land (\lnot x_1 \lor \lnot x_2)$: "at least one" ($\ge 1$) and "at most one" ($\le 1$) together give the equality. $x_1 - x_2 = 0$ would admit exactly the two excluded cases.
mip-f-167 · level facile · notes
Project knapsack: $\max \sum_p r_p x_p$ with a budget constraint and $x_p \in \{0,1\}$. The logical constraint "projects 2 and 5 are not chosen together" is added. What happens to the integer optimum?
  1. It always goes up, because the constraint excludes an expensive combination
  2. It stays the same or goes up: a logical constraint removes no feasible solutions
  3. It always stays the same, because the logical constraint acts only on the relaxation
  4. It stays the same or goes down: the constraint restricts the feasible set of a maximisation
Explanation. The constraint $x_2 + x_5 \le 1$ removes the solutions choosing both; in a maximisation the optimum over a smaller set cannot increase. It stays the same if an optimum already avoided them.
mip-f-213 · level facile · notes
Boolean expressions in the binary variables $x_a, x_b$ ($1$ = true). Which of the following is true for **every** assignment?
  1. $x_a \land \lnot x_a$
  2. $(x_a \lor x_b) \land \lnot x_b$
  3. $x_a \lor x_b$
  4. $x_a \lor \lnot x_a \lor x_b$
Explanation. $x_a \lor \lnot x_a$ is always true, and adding a literal to a true disjunction keeps it true. $x_a \land \lnot x_a$ is unsatisfiable; the other two are satisfiable but false for $x_a = x_b = 0$.
mip-f-214 · level facile · notes
A Boolean expression in $3$ binary variables has been translated into linear constraints on the same variables. How is the translation **proved** correct?
  1. Enumerate the $8$ assignments and check for each: expression true $\iff$ constraints satisfied
  2. Check the assignments that make the expression true: they must satisfy the constraints
  3. Solve the linear relaxation and check that its optimum makes the expression true
  4. Try one random assignment: if it is consistent, all of them are
Explanation. Correct means "true if and only if the constraints are satisfied" on every assignment: both directions are needed on all $2^3$ cases. Checking only the true assignments lets constraints that are too loose pass.
mip-f-215 · level facile · notes
Maximum-revenue project selection with a budget, $x_p \in \{0,1\}$. Without logical constraints the optimum is $30$; adding six implications translated into clauses, the integer optimum drops to $28$ and the linear relaxation (with $0 \le x \le 1$) of the new model is $29$. Which reading is correct?
  1. The logical constraints restrict the set and lower the optimum; $29$ is an upper bound on $28$
  2. The optimum dropped because the clauses were translated wrongly: a logical constraint removes no value
  3. $29$ is a lower bound on $28$, because the relaxation has fewer constraints
  4. The logical constraints act only on integer solutions, so the relaxation should be $30$
Explanation. In a maximisation adding constraints cannot raise the optimum ($30 \to 28$), and the relaxation lies above the integer optimum ($29 \ge 28$). The clauses cut the relaxation too: $29 < 30$.
mip-f-370 · level facile · notes
Boolean variables $x_a, x_b$ ($1$ = true). Is the expression $x_a \land \lnot x_a$ satisfiable, that is, is there an assignment making it true?
  1. No: for every value of $x_a$ one of the two literals is false
  2. Yes, with $x_a = 0$
  3. It depends on $x_b$
  4. Yes, with $x_a = 1$
Explanation. $x_a$ and $\lnot x_a$ cannot be true together: the expression is unsatisfiable (a contradiction); $x_b$ does not appear in it.
mip-f-371 · level facile · notes
Boolean variables $x_a, x_b$ ($1$ = true). For how many of the four assignments of $(x_a, x_b)$ is the expression $x_a \Rightarrow x_b$ true?
  1. $2$
  2. $3$
  3. $1$
  4. $4$
Explanation. $x_a \Rightarrow x_b$ is equivalent to $\lnot x_a \lor x_b$: it is false only for $x_a = 1$, $x_b = 0$. True in the other three.
mip-f-372 · level facile · notes
Boolean variables. Which of the following expressions is in **conjunctive normal form** (CNF: conjunction of clauses, each clause a disjunction of literals)?
  1. $x_1 \Rightarrow (x_2 \land x_3)$
  2. $\lnot(x_1 \lor x_2)$
  3. $(x_1 \land x_2) \lor x_3$
  4. $(x_1 \lor \lnot x_2) \land (x_2 \lor x_3)$
Explanation. The first is an AND of two ORs of literals. The second is an OR of an AND; the third has a negation over a clause; the fourth contains an implication.
mip-f-373 · level facile · notes
Binary variables ($1$ = true). The clause $x_1 \lor \lnot x_2 \lor \lnot x_3$ is translated with the rule «a positive literal is $x$, a negated one $1 - x$, the sum is $\ge 1$». Which constraint is obtained, with constants on the right?
  1. $x_1 - x_2 - x_3 \ge 0$
  2. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \ge 1$
  3. $x_1 - x_2 - x_3 \ge 1$
  4. $x_1 - x_2 - x_3 \ge -1$
Explanation. $x_1 + (1 - x_2) + (1 - x_3) \ge 1$, that is $x_1 - x_2 - x_3 \ge 1 - 2 = -1$. Only $(0, 1, 1)$ is excluded, giving $-2$.
mip-f-374 · level teorema · notes
A Boolean expression in CNF with $k$ clauses is translated into linear constraints on binary variables with the course rules (one clause, one constraint $\ge 1$). Which property does the translation have?
  1. The binary assignments satisfying the $k$ constraints are exactly those making the expression true
  2. Every solution of the linear relaxation of the constraints makes the expression true
  3. The number of constraints is always $2^n$, with $n$ variables
  4. The translation also admits assignments making the expression false, which the objective then discards
Explanation. A clause is true if and only if at least one of its literals is true, that is, if the sum of the translated literals is at least $1$; the CNF is true if and only if all clauses are. The correspondence holds on binary points, not on fractional ones.
mip-f-375 · level facile · notes
Binary variables ($1$ = true). Which linear constraint translates exactly $x_a \Rightarrow \lnot x_b$ («if $a$ is chosen, $b$ is not»)?
  1. $x_a + x_b \le 1$
  2. $x_a \le x_b$
  3. $x_a + x_b \ge 1$
  4. $x_a - x_b \le 0$
Explanation. $x_a \Rightarrow \lnot x_b$ is equivalent to $\lnot x_a \lor \lnot x_b$: $(1 - x_a) + (1 - x_b) \ge 1$, that is $x_a + x_b \le 1$, excluding only $(1,1)$.
mip-f-376 · level facile · notes
Boolean variables. Which split of an implication into two simpler implications is **valid** (equivalent to the original)?
  1. $(x_a \lor x_b) \Rightarrow x_c \iff (x_a \Rightarrow x_c) \land (x_b \Rightarrow x_c)$
  2. $x_a \Rightarrow (x_b \lor x_c) \iff (x_a \Rightarrow x_b) \land (x_a \Rightarrow x_c)$
  3. $(x_a \land x_b) \Rightarrow x_c \iff (x_a \Rightarrow x_c) \land (x_b \Rightarrow x_c)$
  4. $(x_a \land x_b) \Rightarrow x_c \iff (x_a \Rightarrow x_c) \lor (x_b \Rightarrow x_c) \lor x_c$
Explanation. With a disjunctive antecedent one may split: either one being true requires $x_c$. With a conjunctive antecedent the split is strictly stronger; with a disjunctive consequent the split into AND is too strong.
mip-f-377 · level facile · notes
A translation of a Boolean expression in $4$ binary variables into linear constraints is checked by enumeration. How many assignments must be checked, and what is checked for each?
  1. $16$; that at least one constraint is satisfied when the expression is true
  2. $16$; that the expression is true if and only if all constraints are satisfied
  3. $8$; that the constraints are satisfied
  4. $4$; that every variable appears in at least one constraint
Explanation. With $4$ binaries there are $2^4 = 16$ assignments, and for each one the truth of the expression and the satisfaction of all constraints must coincide: it is the proof by cases.
mip-f-378 · level ipotesi · notes
A student checks the translation of a Boolean expression by verifying only that **every** assignment making the expression true satisfies all constraints. What remains unchecked?
  1. That the assignments making the expression false violate at least one constraint: the constraints might be too loose
  2. That the linear relaxation of the constraints is integral
  3. That the constraints are linear
  4. Nothing: one direction suffices, the other follows
Explanation. An exact translation needs both directions: true ⇒ constraints satisfied (no solution cut) and false ⇒ at least one constraint violated (no wrong solution admitted). The empty system of constraints passes the first check.
mip-f-379 · level facile · notes
Project selection, $x_p \in \{0,1\}$ ($1$ = project chosen). «If project 1 or project 2 is chosen, project 3 must be chosen.» Which translation is exact?
  1. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \ge 1$
  2. $x_3 \ge x_1$ and $x_3 \ge x_2$
  3. $x_1 + x_2 - x_3 \le 1$
  4. $x_3 \ge x_1 + x_2$
Explanation. $(x_1 \lor x_2) \Rightarrow x_3$ splits into two implications, each a constraint $x_3 \ge x_i$. $x_3 \ge x_1 + x_2$ forbids $x_1 = x_2 = 1$; $x_1 + x_2 - x_3 \le 1$ translates the antecedent with AND.
mip-f-380 · level facile · notes
Binary variables ($1$ = true). «Exactly two of $x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4$.» Which constraint translates it in one go?
  1. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + x_4 = 2$
  2. $x_1 + x_2 = 1$ and $x_3 + x_4 = 1$
  3. $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + x_4 \ge 2$
  4. $x_1 + x_2 \le 1$ and $x_3 + x_4 \le 1$
Explanation. A cardinality is counted directly. «At least two» also admits three or four; the other two pairs of constraints impose a split between the pairs that the text does not ask (they exclude for example $x_1 = x_2 = 1$).
mip-f-381 · level teorema · notes
Project selection with **maximum** revenue and a budget. Some logical constraints are added (implications translated exactly). Which relation links the optimum with the logical constraints, $z_L$, to the one without, $z_0$?
  1. $z_L \le z_0$: logical constraints restrict the feasible set
  2. $z_L \ge z_0$: logical constraints guide the solver towards better solutions
  3. $z_L = z_0$: exactly translated implications cut no solutions
  4. No general relation
Explanation. Adding constraints to a maximisation cannot raise the optimum. Translating exactly means not cutting solutions that respect the logic, but those violating it are excluded.
mip-f-382 · level ipotesi · notes
In a project selection model the condition «if project 2 is chosen, then project 5 is chosen» is translated as $x_2 = x_5$. What is the effect of the mistake?
  1. None: with binaries $x_2 = x_5$ is equivalent to $x_2 \le x_5$
  2. It allows $x_2 = 1$, $x_5 = 0$, which the logic forbids
  3. It makes the model infeasible in every case
  4. It also forbids $x_5 = 1$, $x_2 = 0$, which the logic allows: the model is tighter and may lose the optimum
Explanation. The implication is $x_2 \le x_5$. The equality adds the reverse direction $x_5 \Rightarrow x_2$, which the text does not ask: it cuts feasible solutions, so in a maximisation the optimum may drop.
mip-f-383 · level facile · notes
Each binary variable is associated with a subset of a universe $U$: $x_a = 1$ means «the chosen element lies in $A$». Which set operation corresponds to $x_a \land x_b$?
  1. The complement $\overline{A}$
  2. The union $A \cup B$
  3. The intersection $A \cap B$
  4. The difference $A \setminus B$
Explanation. AND is true when the element lies in both sets: intersection. OR corresponds to union, negation to complement.
mip-f-384 · level facile · notes
With the correspondence between logic and sets ($\land \leftrightarrow \cap$, $\lor \leftrightarrow \cup$, $\lnot \leftrightarrow$ complement), which set identity corresponds to De Morgan's law $\lnot(x_a \lor x_b) \iff \lnot x_a \land \lnot x_b$?
  1. $A \cup (A \cap B) = A$
  2. $\overline{A \cup B} = \overline{A} \cap \overline{B}$
  3. $\overline{A \cup B} = \overline{A} \cup \overline{B}$
  4. $\overline{A \cap B} = \overline{A} \cap \overline{B}$
Explanation. The negation of an OR becomes the complement of a union, equal to the intersection of the complements. The second and third mix the two laws; the last is absorption.
mip-f-385 · level facile · notes
Logic and sets: with $x_a = 1$ if the chosen element lies in $A$, absorption $x_a \lor (x_a \land x_b) \iff x_a$ corresponds to which identity?
  1. $A \cup (A \cap B) = A \cup B$
  2. $A \cup (A \cap B) = A \cap B$
  3. $A \cap (A \cup B) = B$
  4. $A \cup (A \cap B) = A$
Explanation. $A \cap B \subseteq A$, so joining it with $A$ adds nothing. The dual absorption is $A \cap (A \cup B) = A$, not $B$.
mip-f-386 · level teorema · notes
Distributivity $x_a \land (x_b \lor x_c) \iff (x_a \land x_b) \lor (x_a \land x_c)$ can be proved by enumerating the $8$ assignments or with Venn diagrams. Which set identity expresses it?
  1. $\overline{A \cap (B \cup C)} = \overline{A} \cup \overline{B} \cup \overline{C}$
  2. $A \cap (B \cup C) = (A \cap B) \cup (A \cap C)$
  3. $A \cup (B \cap C) = (A \cap B) \cup (A \cap C)$
  4. $A \cap (B \cup C) = (A \cup B) \cap (A \cup C)$
Explanation. AND becomes intersection, OR union: intersection distributes over union. The third is the other distributivity written wrongly; the fourth gets the complement of $B \cup C$ wrong.
mip-f-517 · level ipotesi · notes
Binary variables $x_1, x_2, x_3$. «At most one of three» is written with three clauses ($x_1 + x_2 \le 1$, $x_1 + x_3 \le 1$, $x_2 + x_3 \le 1$) or with $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 1$. A student concludes «they are equivalent in every respect». What is overlooked?
  1. Nothing: they are equivalent also in the relaxation
  2. The aggregated constraint admits $(1, 1, 0)$
  3. They have the same binary solutions, but not the same relaxation: $(1/2, 1/2, 1/2)$ satisfies the clauses and violates the aggregated constraint
  4. They do not have the same binary solutions: the clauses admit $(1, 1, 0)$
Explanation. On binary points they coincide; in the relaxation the aggregated form is tighter. When one can count, one counts: it is shorter and stronger.
mip-f-550 · level ipotesi · notes
Binary variables. A student translates $x_a \Rightarrow x_b$ as $x_a \ge x_b$. What is wrong?
  1. An equality is needed
  2. The direction is reversed: $x_a \ge x_b$ translates $x_b \Rightarrow x_a$; the required implication is $x_b \ge x_a$
  3. Nothing: the two forms are equivalent
  4. Two constraints are needed
Explanation. $x_a \Rightarrow x_b$ excludes only $(1, 0)$: $x_b - x_a \ge 0$. The student's constraint excludes $(0, 1)$ instead.
mip-f-551 · level ipotesi · notes
Binary variables. The clause $x_1 \lor \lnot x_2$ is translated by a student as $x_1 - x_2 \ge 1$. What happens?
  1. The constraint is too tight: it admits only $(1, 0)$, while the clause is also true at $(0, 0)$ and $(1, 1)$; the right constant is $0$
  2. The constraint is infeasible
  3. The constraint is too loose
  4. Nothing: it is the correct translation
Explanation. $x_1 + (1 - x_2) \ge 1$ gives $x_1 - x_2 \ge 0$. Forgetting to move the negated literal's constant to the right cuts assignments that the clause admits.

6. Links between variables (65)

mip-f-069 · level facile · notes
Activation link: $n$ objects $i$, $m$ resources $j$, $x_{ij} = 1$ if object $i$ uses resource $j$, $y_j = 1$ if the resource is activated. Disaggregated form $x_{ij} \le y_j$ for every pair, aggregated form $\sum_i x_{ij} \le n\, y_j$ for every resource. Which statement is correct?
  1. The disaggregated form has $n\,m$ constraints and admits fewer binary solutions than the aggregated one
  2. The aggregated form has $n\,m$ constraints and a tighter relaxation; the disaggregated one has $m$
  3. The two forms have the same number of constraints and the same linear relaxation
  4. The disaggregated form has $n\,m$ constraints and a tighter relaxation; the aggregated one has $m$
Explanation. On binary points the two forms are equivalent; in the relaxation the disaggregated one is stronger, because it prevents activating the resource for a mere fraction $1/n$. More rows, tighter relaxation.
mip-f-070 · level facile · notes
Activation link $x_{ij} \le y_j$ ($x_{ij} = 1$ if $i$ uses resource $j$, $y_j = 1$ if $j$ is activated), in a minimisation with activation cost $f_j\, y_j$ in the objective. What can be said about "if resource $j$ is activated then somebody uses it"?
  1. It holds in every optimal solution even when $f_j = 0$
  2. It is not imposed by the constraints; if $f_j > 0$ it holds in every optimal solution
  3. It is imposed by the constraints $x_{ij} \le y_j$ in every feasible solution
  4. It holds in no optimal solution, whatever $f_j$ is
Explanation. $y_j = 1$ with all $x_{ij} = 0$ is feasible. With $f_j > 0$ switching the resource off strictly reduces the cost: "in every optimum". With $f_j = 0$ one can only say "there exists an optimum with $y_j = 0$".
mip-f-071 · level facile · notes
Fixed cost: plant $j$ produces the continuous quantity $q_j \ge 0$, at most the capacity $C_j$, and pays $f_j$ if it is open ($y_j = 1$). The link is written $q_j \le K\, y_j$. Which coefficient $K$ should be chosen?
  1. The sum of the capacities of all the plants
  2. $1$, because $y_j$ is binary
  3. The capacity $C_j$, derived from the data
  4. Any large number, for instance $100$
Explanation. The right coefficient is the smallest upper bound on the quantity when the plant is open: the capacity. A "large enough" big-M is valid but weakens the relaxation considerably; $K = 1$ would cut off the solutions with $q_j > 1$.
mip-f-072 · level facile · notes
Minimum lot: with the binary $y_j$ one wants the quantity $q_j$ to be **semicontinuous**, i.e. $q_j \in \{0\} \cup [\ell, c_j]$, with threshold $\ell$ and capacity $c_j \ge \ell$. Which pair of constraints imposes it?
  1. $q_j \le c_j\, y_j$ and $q_j \ge 0$
  2. $q_j \ge \ell\, y_j$ and $q_j \le c_j$
  3. $\ell \le q_j$ and $q_j \le c_j$
  4. $\ell\, y_j \le q_j$ and $q_j \le c_j\, y_j$
Explanation. With $y_j = 0$ one gets $0 \le q_j \le 0$, with $y_j = 1$ one gets $\ell \le q_j \le c_j$. Without $\ell y_j$ the threshold is missing, without $c_j y_j$ one can produce with the plant off, and $\ell \le q_j$ forbids zero.
mip-f-073 · level facile · notes
Model with fixed cost ($q_j \le c_j y_j$, $y_j \in \{0,1\}$). Adding the minimum lot $q_j \ge \ell\, y_j$ raises the integer optimum from $44$ to $49$, but the linear relaxation ($0 \le y_j \le 1$) stays $112/3$. Why?
  1. In the relaxation it suffices to lower $y_j$ to $q_j/\ell$: the threshold constraint never bites in the continuum
  2. Because the threshold constraint is not valid and the solver ignores it in the relaxation
  3. Because the threshold $\ell$ exceeds the capacity and the constraint is redundant
  4. Because the linear relaxation keeps the $y_j$ integer
Explanation. With $y_j$ free in $[0,1]$ the constraint is met with $y_j \le q_j/\ell$, and the fixed cost already pushes $y_j$ towards $q_j/c_j \le q_j/\ell$: the whole effect of the threshold falls on integrality.
mip-f-074 · level facile · notes
