Integer count: $q \ge 0$ units go into boxes of capacity $c$, and $w$ is the number of boxes. How does one impose $w \ge \lceil q/c \rceil$ in a linear model?
  1. With $w \le q/c + 1$ and $w$ integer
  2. With $w = \lceil q/c \rceil$ written in the constraint
  3. With $w \ge q/c$ and $w$ continuous
  4. With $q \le c\, w$ and $w$ declared integer
Explanation. $\lceil \cdot \rceil$ is not linear and cannot be written in a constraint: $q \le c\, w$ gives $w \ge q/c$ and integrality raises $w$ to the ceiling. The gap between $q/c$ and $\lceil q/c \rceil$ comes entirely from integrality.
mip-f-075 · level facile · notes
Maximum variable: $x_j \in \{0,1\}$ selects element $j$ of duration $t_j \ge 0$; the model minimises $z$ with $z \ge t_j x_j$ for every $j$ and $z \ge 0$. In which case can one **not** conclude that in every optimum $z = \max_{j : x_j = 1} t_j$?
  1. When the durations $t_j$ are all distinct
  2. When $z$ also appears in another constraint that wants it large
  3. When no $x_j$ equals $1$
  4. When the problem has several optimal solutions
Explanation. The optimality step lowers $z$ to the maximum: if another constraint wants it large, lowering it may violate that constraint, and only $z \ge \max$ remains. With no $x_j = 1$ the convention $\max \emptyset = 0$ holds, guaranteed by $z \ge 0$.
mip-f-076 · level facile · notes
Five jobs of total duration $21$ are split between two workers. Three fairness models: min-max (minimise the largest load), max-min (maximise the smallest load) and range (minimise max minus min). All choose the loads $(11, 10)$, with optimal values $11$, $10$ and $1$. What follows?
  1. Min-max is off by $10$ units with respect to the range model
  2. With more than two workers the three models would always choose the same solution
  3. The three values describe the same solution and are not comparable with each other
  4. The range is the best model, because it has the lowest optimal value
Explanation. Comparing "$z = 11$" of a min-max with "$z = 1$" of a range means nothing: they measure different things. And with more than two workers min-max and max-min in general choose different splits.
mip-f-077 · level facile · notes
Continuous variables $u, v$ and a constant $k > 0$. How is the constraint $|u - v| \le k$ imposed in a linear model?
  1. It cannot: the absolute value needs a non-linear model
  2. With $u - v \le k$ and $v - u \le k$, with no binary variables
  3. With the single constraint $u - v \ge -k$
  4. With a binary $b$ and a big-M, as for a disjunction
Explanation. $|u - v| \le k$ is the intersection of two half-planes, a convex set: two linear constraints suffice. It is the case $|u - v| \ge k$ that needs a binary.
mip-f-078 · level facile · notes
Continuous variables $u, v$. One wants to **minimise** $|u - v|$ with a linear model. Which formulation is correct?
  1. A variable $d \ge 0$ with $d \ge u - v$ and $d \ge v - u$, and $\min d$
  2. No auxiliary variable: $\min (u - v)$
  3. A binary $b$ and a variable $d$ with $d \ge u - v - Mb$, and $\min d$
  4. A variable $d \ge 0$ with $d \le u - v$ and $d \le v - u$, and $\min d$
Explanation. The two constraints impose $d \ge |u - v|$ and minimisation, if $d$ appears nowhere else, brings equality. In the objective an absolute value to be minimised costs two constraints and no binary.
mip-f-079 · level facile · notes
Conditional constraint: with $x \in \{0,1\}^3$, the constraint $3x_1 + 4x_2 + 5x_3 \le 6$ must hold only if the binary $y$ equals $1$, and it is written $3x_1 + 4x_2 + 5x_3 \le 6 + M(1 - y)$. What happens using $M = 5$ instead of $M = 6$?
  1. The model becomes infeasible for every value of $y$
  2. With $y = 0$ what remains is $3x_1 + 4x_2 + 5x_3 \le 11$, which excludes $x = (1,1,1)$: the integer set changes
  3. Nothing on the integer set: with $M = 5$ only the linear relaxation gets worse
  4. The relaxation improves and the integer set stays exactly the same
Explanation. The maximum of $3x_1 + 4x_2 + 5x_3$ is $12$, so the switched-off constraint needs $M \ge 12 - 6 = 6$. With $M = 5$ the switched-off constraint still cuts off $(1,1,1)$, and the model answers another question.
mip-f-080 · level facile · notes
Conditional constraint $3x_1 + 4x_2 + 5x_3 \le 6 + M(1 - y)$, with binary $x, y$ and objective $\max\ x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + y$: the smallest valid $M$ is $6$. What changes using $M = 1000$?
  1. Nothing, neither on the integer set nor on the linear relaxation
  2. The relaxation gets stronger, because the switched-off constraint no longer bites
  3. The integer set stays the same, the relaxation gets weaker (from $3$ to almost $4$)
  4. The integer set changes and the integer optimum rises from $3$ to $4$
Explanation. A valid $M$ larger than necessary is improvable: same integer set, weaker relaxation, because lowering $y$ slightly is enough to switch the constraint almost entirely off. $M$ is always computed from the data.
mip-f-081 · level facile · notes
Sequencing $n$ jobs on one machine: $s_{ij} = 1$ if $i$ precedes $j$, with $s_{ij} + s_{ji} = 1$ for every pair, and one big-M precedence constraint on the completion times for every ordered pair $(i, j)$, $i \ne j$. How many constraints does each family have?
  1. $n(n-1)$ order and $n(n-1)/2$ precedence
  2. $n(n-1)$ each
  3. $n(n-1)/2$ each
  4. $n(n-1)/2$ order and $n(n-1)$ precedence
Explanation. The order is imposed once per unordered pair ($i < j$), the precedence once per ordered pair ($i \ne j$), because each direction has its own constraint to switch on or off.
mip-f-082 · level facile · notes
Class of three jobs with binaries $x_j$ (job performed) and $y$ (class complete), maximisation problem. There are only the constraints $y \le x_j$, no constraint for the direction "all performed $\Rightarrow y = 1$", and the bonus of $y$ in the objective is $0$. What may happen at the optimum?
  1. One may get $y = 0$ with all $x_j = 1$: $y$ is no longer a faithful indicator
  2. The optimal value changes with respect to the model with both directions
  3. In every optimum $y = 1$ if all $x_j = 1$, because the objective imposes it
  4. The model becomes infeasible, because one direction of the link is missing
Explanation. Without a bonus the exchange argument fails: raising $y$ does not improve the objective. The optimal value stays the same, but $y$ no longer says "class complete"; if $y$ appears in other constraints, the second direction must be written.
mip-f-083 · level facile · notes
"At least $p$ different types": $q_j$ is the quantity of type $j$, $y_j = 1$ if the type is active, with $q_j \le c_j y_j$ and $\sum_j y_j \ge p$, but **without** the minimum lot $q_j \ge \ell\, y_j$ ($\ell > 0$). What is wrong?
  1. The count is met by switching on indicators with $q_j = 0$: the condition no longer says anything
  2. Nothing: the activation constraint $q_j \le c_j y_j$ suffices to give the count a meaning
  3. The linear relaxation becomes exact and the integrality of the $y_j$ is no longer needed
  4. The model becomes infeasible whenever $p$ exceeds the number of profitable types
Explanation. Without a threshold a solution with $y_j = 1$ and $q_j = 0$ is feasible. Counting types works only if every type switched on really produces something: activation and minimum lot go together.
mip-f-084 · level facile · notes
An integer variable $v \in \{0, \dots, 2^p - 1\}$ is rewritten with the binary expansion $v = \sum_{k=0}^{p-1} 2^k b_k$, with $b_k \in \{0,1\}$. What is its effect on the linear relaxation?
  1. No strengthening: with $b_k \in [0,1]$ it covers the whole interval $[0, 2^p - 1]$, like $0 \le v \le 2^p - 1$
  2. It always strengthens it, because it adds $p$ binary variables to the model
  3. It makes it exact, because every value of $v$ has a single binary representation
  4. It makes it infeasible, because fractional $b_k$ do not represent integers
Explanation. The expansion serves to reformulate, not to strengthen, for instance when another part of the model needs binary indicators of the bits. In the continuum fractional $b_k$ produce every value of the interval.
mip-f-085 · level facile · notes
Soft constraint: the target $a'x = \beta$ is written $a'x + s^- - s^+ = \beta$ with deviations $s^-, s^+ \ge 0$ and penalties $\pi^- s^- + \pi^+ s^+$ in the minimisation objective, $\pi^-, \pi^+ > 0$. What holds in every optimum?
  1. The two deviations are equal, $s^- = s^+$
  2. At least one of the two deviations is zero, and $s^- + s^+ = |a'x - \beta|$
  3. $s^-$ measures how much $a'x$ exceeds the target $\beta$
  4. Both deviations are zero, because the penalties are positive
Explanation. If both were positive, subtracting their minimum from both would keep the constraint satisfied and strictly reduce the objective. $s^-$ measures how much one is below the target, $s^+$ how much above.
mip-f-086 · level facile · notes
A piecewise linear and **convex** cost function $g(q)$, to be minimised, is modelled as a convex combination $\sum_k \lambda_k$ of its breakpoints. Are the segment binaries and the adjacency constraints (only two consecutive positive $\lambda$) also needed?
  1. Yes, always: without adjacency the model mixes distant breakpoints and gets the cost wrong
  2. Yes, because without segment binaries the model becomes infeasible
  3. No: the lower convex envelope coincides with $g$, and minimisation picks adjacent $\lambda$ by itself
  4. No, but only if the breakpoints are equally spaced and the slopes integer
Explanation. With convex $g$ and a minimisation the model without binaries is already correct. With non-convex $g$, for instance with quantity discounts, adjacency is mandatory.
mip-f-087 · level facile · notes
**Non-convex** piecewise linear cost (quantity discounts), modelled with the $\lambda_k$ of the breakpoints. Without segment binaries $w_t$ and adjacency the optimum is $117/4$; with binaries and adjacency the integer optimum is $33$. What is the linear relaxation (with $0 \le w_t \le 1$) of the model with adjacency?
  1. $117/4$: with fractional $w_t$ adjacency does not bite
  2. $33$, like its integer optimum
  3. More than $33$, because the model is tighter
  4. A value strictly between $117/4$ and $33$
Explanation. Adjacency changes the integer set, hence the optimum, but not the strength of the relaxation: with fractional $w_t$ the model mixes the breakpoints again. Two things not to be confused.
mip-f-088 · level facile · notes
Two plants with fixed cost, quantity $q_j$ and opening $y_j \in \{0,1\}$. With the link $q_j \le C_j y_j$ ($C_j$ capacity) the relaxation is $112/3$; with $q_j \le 100\, y_j$ and $q_j \le C_j$ separately it is $1059/50$; the integer optimum is $44$ in both. What does one conclude?
  1. Same integer set, but the big-M gives a tighter relaxation because it has one more constraint
  2. Different integer sets: the big-M admits solutions that the capacity link excludes
  3. Same relaxation and same integer set: the two values differ by rounding
  4. Same integer set, but the coefficient equal to the capacity gives a much tighter relaxation
Explanation. Both forms are valid and have the same integer optimum; the big-M lets the model buy tiny fractions of a plant ($y_j = q_j/100$) and the relaxation collapses.
mip-f-089 · level teorema · notes
A property of an optimal solution is proved by an exchange argument: a solution violating it is modified, staying feasible, and the objective is compared. Which statement is correct?
  1. If the exchange does not worsen the objective the property holds in every optimum, because no optimum can violate it
  2. If the exchange strictly improves the objective the property holds in every optimum; if it leaves it unchanged it holds only in some optimum
  3. A property proved by exchange holds in every feasible solution, not only in the optima
  4. If the variable's coefficient in the objective is zero, the exchange proves the property in every optimum
Explanation. The strength of the conclusion depends on the sign of the coefficient: a strict improvement gives "in every optimum", an unchanged objective gives at most "there exists an optimum". Writing "in every optimum" with a merely non-negative coefficient is the commonest mistake.
mip-f-090 · level teorema · notes
Activation with binaries $x_{ij}$ (object $i$ uses resource $j$, $i = 1, \dots, n$) and $y_j$ (resource active): disaggregated form $x_{ij} \le y_j$, aggregated form $\sum_i x_{ij} \le n\, y_j$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The aggregated form implies the disaggregated one for every $x \ge 0$ and every $y \in [0,1]$
  2. The disaggregated form does not imply the aggregated one, not even on binary points
  3. They are equivalent on fractional points too, so they have the same linear relaxation
  4. On binary points they are equivalent; the direction "aggregated $\Rightarrow$ disaggregated" uses binarity and fails outside binary points
Explanation. Summing the disaggregated constraints over $i$ gives the aggregated one, always. The opposite direction uses $y_j \in \{0,1\}$ and $x_{ij} \le 1$: and this is exactly what makes the two forms differ in the relaxation.
mip-f-091 · level teorema · notes
Continuous variables $u, v$ and a constant $k > 0$. Which statement about the constraint $|u - v| \ge k$ is correct?
  1. It is written with a variable $d$ and the constraints $d \ge u - v$, $d \ge v - u$ and $d \ge k$
  2. The set it describes is not convex, so no system of linear constraints without integer variables describes it
  3. It is written with the two linear constraints $u - v \ge k$ and $v - u \ge k$, with no integer variables
  4. It is convex like $|u - v| \le k$, because the absolute value is a convex function
Explanation. It is the complement of a strip: a disjunction, which requires a binary and a big-M. Imposing both constraints would give $0 \ge 2k$, infeasible; and $d \ge |u - v|$ with $d \ge k$ does not say that $|u - v|$ is large.
mip-f-092 · level teorema · notes
Alldiff: $n$ objects must receive distinct values among $n$ values. With $x_{iv} = 1$ if object $i$ receives value $v$, it is written as a double set partitioning: $\sum_v x_{iv} = 1$ for every object and $\sum_i x_{iv} = 1$ for every value. Which statement is correct?
  1. The formulation is correct only if the binary expansion of the values is added
  2. The values turn out distinct only in optimal solutions, not in all feasible ones
  3. The relaxation is as weak as that of a big-M, and must be strengthened with cuts
  4. The matrix is that of assignment, totally unimodular: the vertices of the relaxation are integer
Explanation. "Every object one value" and "every value to one object only" impose a bijection in every feasible solution; total unimodularity makes integrality free, i.e. $\zlpp = \zmilp$ for every linear objective.
mip-f-093 · level ipotesi · notes
Maximum variable: binaries $x_j$, durations $t_j \ge 0$, variable $z \ge 0$ with the constraints $z \ge t_j x_j$ for every $j$. To prove that in every optimum $z = \max_{j : x_j = 1} t_j$, what is needed?
  1. The constraints $z \ge t_j x_j$, a strictly positive coefficient of $z$ in a maximisation, and $z \ge 0$
  2. The constraints $z \ge t_j x_j$, a non-negative coefficient of $z$ in a minimisation, and $z$ absent from every other constraint
  3. The constraints $z \ge t_j x_j$, a strictly positive coefficient of $z$ in a minimisation, and $z$ absent from every other constraint
  4. The constraints $z \ge t_j x_j$ and $z$ absent from every other constraint, whatever the coefficient of $z$
Explanation. These are the three steps: the constraint gives $z \ge \max$, optimality lowers $z$ (with a strictly positive coefficient in a minimisation and $z$ absent elsewhere), the synthesis gives equality in every optimum.
mip-f-094 · level ipotesi · notes
Activation with $x_{ij} \in \{0,1\}$ ($i = 1, \dots, n$) and $y_j$. To prove that the aggregated form $\sum_i x_{ij} \le n\, y_j$ implies the disaggregated one $x_{ij} \le y_j$, what is needed?
  1. That $y_j$ be binary (cases $y_j = 0$ and $y_j = 1$) and that $x_{ij} \le 1$, because the $x$ are binary
  2. That the activation cost of resource $j$ be strictly positive
  3. Only that $x_{ij} \ge 0$, whatever the value of $y_j$ in $[0,1]$
  4. That the matrix of the aggregated constraint be totally unimodular
Explanation. With $y_j = 0$ the aggregated form gives $\sum_i x_{ij} \le 0$, hence every $x_{ij} = 0$; with $y_j = 1$ it suffices that $x_{ij} \le 1$. Outside binary points the direction fails: $y_j = 1/n$ with a single $x_{ij} = 1$ satisfies the aggregated form.
mip-f-095 · level ipotesi · notes
Conditional constraint $a'x \le b + M(1 - y)$: it must hold when the binary $y$ equals $1$ and be switched off when it equals $0$. To prove that a given value of $M$ is **valid**, what is needed?
  1. That $M \ge \max a'x - b$ over the points allowed by the other constraints, usually computed from the variable bounds, which must be finite
  2. That the linear relaxation with that value of $M$ have the same optimum as the MILP
  3. That $y$ be binary, whatever the value of $M$, as long as it is positive
  4. That $M$ be larger than all the coefficients appearing in the model
Explanation. With $y = 0$ the constraint must be satisfied by every point the other constraints allow: $M \ge \max a'x - b$ is needed. Without finite bounds on the variables no finite $M$ is guaranteed valid.
mip-f-096 · level ipotesi · notes
Sequencing on one machine: $\kappa_i$ is the completion of job $i$ of duration $t_i$, $s_{ij} = 1$ if $i$ follows $j$, and $\kappa_i \ge \kappa_j + t_i - M(1 - s_{ij})$. To prove that $M = \sum_h t_h$ is valid, what is needed?
  1. $\kappa_i \ge t_i$ and the horizon $\kappa_j \le \sum_h t_h$ on the solutions among which the optimum is sought
  2. That $M$ be at least the largest duration $\max_h t_h$ and that the durations be integer
  3. Only $s_{ij} + s_{ji} = 1$, i.e. that every pair is ordered one way
  4. Only $\kappa_i \ge t_i$, i.e. that no job ends before its own duration
Explanation. With $s_{ij} = 0$ one has $\kappa_j + t_i - M \le \sum_h t_h + t_i - \sum_h t_h = t_i \le \kappa_i$. Without an upper bound on the $\kappa_j$ no finite $M$ is valid: the horizon must be declared.
mip-f-097 · level ipotesi · notes
Complete-class indicator: binaries $x_1, \dots, x_p$ and $y$, with $y \le x_j$ for every $j$. One wants to omit the constraint $y \ge \sum_j x_j - (p - 1)$. What is needed so that in every optimum $y = 1$ when all $x_j = 1$?
  1. That the $x_j$ have positive revenues in the objective, whatever the bonus of $y$
  2. That $y$ appear in the objective of a maximisation with bonus $v \ge 0$ and in no other constraint
  3. That $y$ appear in a constraint counting the complete classes, with bonus $v > 0$
  4. That $y$ appear in the objective of a maximisation with bonus $v > 0$ and in no other constraint
Explanation. With $v > 0$ and $y$ absent elsewhere, raising $y$ to $1$ stays feasible and strictly increases the objective. With $v = 0$, or if $y$ appears in other constraints, the second direction must be written.
mip-f-098 · level ipotesi · notes
Soft constraint $a'x + s^- - s^+ = \beta$ with $s^-, s^+ \ge 0$ and penalties $\pi^- s^- + \pi^+ s^+$ in a minimisation. To prove that in every optimum at least one of the two deviations is zero, what is needed?
  1. Penalties $\pi^-, \pi^+ \ge 0$ and the fact that subtracting the same value from $s^-$ and $s^+$ leaves the difference $s^- - s^+$ unchanged
  2. That the deviations $s^-, s^+$ be declared binary, so that only one can be switched on
  3. That the hard constraint $a'x = \beta$ have at least one feasible solution
  4. Penalties $\pi^-, \pi^+ > 0$ and the fact that subtracting the same value from $s^-$ and $s^+$ leaves the difference $s^- - s^+$ unchanged
Explanation. If $s^- = \sigma > 0$ and $s^+ = \tau > 0$, removing $\min(\sigma, \tau)$ from both keeps the constraint and reduces the objective by $(\pi^- + \pi^+) \min(\sigma, \tau) > 0$: a contradiction. With zero penalties the reduction is not strict.
mip-f-216 · level facile · notes
Integer counting: $q = 37$ units go into boxes of capacity $c = 10$, with $q \le c\, w$ and the number of boxes $w$ minimised. What is $w$ at the optimum of the linear relaxation, where $w$ is continuous?
  1. $\tfrac{10}{37}$
  2. $\tfrac{37}{10}$
  3. $4$
  4. $3$
Explanation. In the continuous problem the minimum pushes $w$ down to $q/c = 37/10$. The ceiling $\lceil 37/10 \rceil = 4$ comes only from the integrality of $w$; $3$ boxes are not enough.
mip-f-217 · level facile · notes
Fairness among $K$ workers: $\ell_k$ is the load of worker $k$ (a linear expression of the assignments). Which model **maximises the minimum load**?
  1. A variable $u$, the constraints $u \le \ell_k$ for every $k$ and $\min u$
  2. A variable $u$, the constraints $u \ge \ell_k$ for every $k$ and $\max u$
  3. A variable $u$, the constraint $u = \sum_k \ell_k / K$ and $\max u$
  4. A variable $u$, the constraints $u \le \ell_k$ for every $k$ and $\max u$
Explanation. $u \le \ell_k$ keeps $u$ below every load and the maximum pushes it onto the smallest: this is the max-min. With $u \ge \ell_k$ and $\max u$ the problem is unbounded; with $\min u$, $u$ decreases without limit.
mip-f-218 · level facile · notes
"At least $p = 2$ different types": $q_j$ is the quantity of type $j \in \{1,2,3\}$, $y_j$ the binary of active type, with $5 y_j \le q_j \le 20 y_j$ and $y_1 + y_2 + y_3 \ge 2$. Which solution is feasible?
  1. $q = (5, 7, 0)$, $y = (1, 1, 0)$
  2. $q = (25, 5, 0)$, $y = (1, 1, 0)$
  3. $q = (3, 8, 0)$, $y = (1, 1, 0)$
  4. $q = (10, 0, 0)$, $y = (1, 1, 0)$
Explanation. An active type must have a quantity between the threshold $5$ and the capacity $20$. $q_2 = 0$ with $y_2 = 1$ violates the threshold (it is exactly the "empty" count the threshold prevents); $3 < 5$; $25 > 20$.
mip-f-219 · level facile · notes
In the map of the techniques linking variables, each technique has something to declare when used. What must be declared for a big-M conditional constraint, $a'x \le b + M(1 - y)$ with $y$ binary?
  1. The value of $M$, computed from the data
  2. That $M$ is any large number
  3. That the integrality of $y$ realises a rounding up
  4. The sign of the coefficient of $y$ in the objective
Explanation. An $M$ too small cuts off solutions, one too large weakens the relaxation: it must be computed from the variable bounds and declared. The sign in the objective is declared for activation, the rounding for integer counting.
mip-f-387 · level facile · notes
Fixed cost: plant $j$ produces $q_j \ge 0$, with capacity $C_j = 40$, and pays the fixed cost $f_j$ if open ($y_j = 1$). The link is $q_j \le C_j y_j$. Why not write $q_j \le M y_j$ with $M = 10^6$?
  1. There is no difference: the value of $M$ does not matter if $M \ge C_j$
  2. Because $q_j \le M y_j$ no longer imposes that a closed plant produces nothing
  3. With a huge $M$ the relaxation can keep $y_j = q_j/M$ almost zero, paying a tiny fraction of the fixed cost: the bound worsens
  4. Because with $M = 10^6$ the model becomes infeasible
Explanation. Both constraints are correct on integer points, but in the relaxation $y_j \ge q_j/M$: with $M = C_j$ the plant pays at least $f_j q_j / C_j$, with a huge $M$ almost nothing. The right coefficient is the capacity.
mip-f-388 · level teorema · notes
Fixed cost, minimisation problem: $\min \sum_j (c_j q_j + f_j y_j)$ with $q_j \le C_j y_j$, $q_j \ge 0$, $y_j \in \{0,1\}$, $f_j > 0$. In an optimal solution, what holds for a plant with $q_j = 0$?
  1. $y_j = 1$: a plant must be open to be able to produce in the future
  2. $y_j = 0$: opening it would cost $f_j > 0$ with no advantage
  3. $y_j$ may equal $0$ or $1$ indifferently
  4. $y_j = q_j / C_j$
Explanation. The constraint only imposes $q_j > 0 \Rightarrow y_j = 1$. The reverse direction ($q_j = 0 \Rightarrow y_j = 0$) is imposed by the objective: with $f_j > 0$ closing an unused plant lowers the cost.
mip-f-389 · level facile · notes
Minimum lot: $q_j$ must be $0$ or lie in $[\ell, c_j]$, with $\ell = 5$ and $c_j = 20$; $y_j \in \{0,1\}$. With the constraints $\ell y_j \le q_j \le c_j y_j$, which values of $q_j$ are allowed with $y_j = 1$ and with $y_j = 0$?
  1. With $y_j = 1$: $0 \le q_j \le 20$; with $y_j = 0$: $0 \le q_j \le 5$
  2. With $y_j = 1$: only $q_j = 5$ or $q_j = 20$; with $y_j = 0$: only $q_j = 0$
  3. With $y_j = 1$: $5 \le q_j \le 20$; with $y_j = 0$: only $q_j = 0$
  4. With $y_j = 1$: $5 \le q_j \le 20$; with $y_j = 0$: $q_j \le 5$
Explanation. With $y_j = 0$ the two constraints become $0 \le q_j \le 0$; with $y_j = 1$ they become $5 \le q_j \le 20$. The domain is the union of a point and an interval: a semicontinuous variable.
mip-f-390 · level ipotesi · notes
Minimum lot with $\ell y_j \le q_j \le c_j y_j$, $y_j \in \{0,1\}$, in a minimum-cost problem with fixed cost $f_j > 0$. A student removes the constraint $\ell y_j \le q_j$, claiming that «the objective already pushes $y_j$ to zero». What is lost?
  1. The integrality of $y_j$
  2. Nothing: the objective also imposes the threshold
  3. The threshold: with $y_j = 1$ the values $0 < q_j < \ell$ remain allowed, that is, lots smaller than the minimum
  4. The link «closed plant, no production»
Explanation. The objective pushes $y_j$ to zero when $q_j = 0$, but does not prevent producing a positive quantity below the threshold with the plant open: that direction is given only by $\ell y_j \le q_j$.
mip-f-391 · level facile · notes
Integer count: pieces of size $a = (4, 7, 5)$, all to be shipped ($x_i = 1$), in boxes of capacity $c = 6$; $w \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ is the number of boxes, with $\sum_i a_i x_i \le c\,w$, and $w$ is minimised. What is $w$ at the optimum of the model, and in the linear relaxation?
  1. $w = 3$; in the relaxation $w = 8/3$
  2. $w = 3$; in the relaxation $w = 3$
  3. $w = 2$; in the relaxation $w = 8/3$
  4. $w = 16$; in the relaxation $w = 16/6$
Explanation. $\sum_i a_i = 16$, so $w \ge 16/6 = 8/3$: the relaxation stops there, the integer rises to $\lceil 8/3 \rceil = 3$. It is a rounding up. (The model counts the total capacity, not the packing of the pieces.)
mip-f-392 · level teorema · notes
Integer count: $w \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ with the single constraint $Q \le c\,w$ ($Q > 0$ fixed quantity, $c > 0$ capacity), minimising a positive unit cost for $w$. What holds at the optimum?
  1. $w = \lfloor Q/c \rfloor$
  2. $w = \lfloor Q/c \rfloor + 1$
  3. $w = Q/c$
  4. $w = \lceil Q/c \rceil$
Explanation. The constraint gives $w \ge Q/c$ and $w$ integer, so $w \ge \lceil Q/c \rceil$; the objective pushes $w$ to the smallest feasible value. $\lfloor Q/c \rfloor + 1$ fails when $Q/c$ is an integer.
mip-f-393 · level facile · notes
Maximum variable: $x_j \in \{0,1\}$ chooses activity $j$ of duration $t_j$, and $z$ should equal the duration of the longest chosen activity. The constraints are $z \ge t_j x_j$ for every $j$ and $z \ge 0$. In which type of objective does $z$ equal the maximum exactly at the optimum?
  1. When $z$ appears with a positive coefficient in an objective to be minimised
  2. Only if the objective is $\min \sum_j t_j x_j$
  3. In any objective: the constraints already fix it
  4. When $z$ appears with a positive coefficient in an objective to be maximised
Explanation. The constraints give $z \ge \max_j t_j x_j$; only an objective pushing $z$ down (minimising it) brings it to equality. Maximising it, $z$ would grow without limit.
mip-f-394 · level facile · notes
Maximum variable $z \ge t_j x_j$, with durations $t = (4, 9, 6)$ and choice $x = (1, 0, 1)$, in a problem minimising $z$ plus other terms not depending on $z$. What is $z$ in an optimal solution with this choice?
  1. $4$
  2. $9$
  3. $6$
  4. $10$
Explanation. The constraints give $z \ge 4$, $z \ge 0$, $z \ge 6$: the smallest feasible value is $6$, the longest duration among the chosen activities. $9$ belongs to an unchosen activity; $10$ is the sum.
mip-f-395 · level facile · notes
Fairness between two workers: loads $\ell_1 = 10$ and $\ell_2 = 6$ in a given assignment. In the three course models — min-max ($\min z$, $z \ge \ell_k$), max-min ($\max u$, $u \le \ell_k$), difference ($\min z - u$ with both) — what are $z$, $u$ and $z - u$ at the optimum **for this fixed assignment**?
  1. $z = 10$, $u = 6$, $z - u = 4$
  2. $z = 10$, $u = 10$, $z - u = 0$
  3. $z = 16$, $u = 0$, $z - u = 16$
  4. $z = 8$, $u = 8$, $z - u = 0$
Explanation. With fixed loads $z$ drops to the maximum load ($10$), $u$ rises to the minimum ($6$), and the difference is $4$. The value $8$ is the average, which none of the three models computes.
mip-f-396 · level ipotesi · notes
Fairness: $\ell_k$ is the load of resource $k$. One wants to minimise the spread between maximum and minimum load with $\min (z - u)$. A student writes only the constraints $z \ge \ell_k$ for every $k$, forgetting $u \le \ell_k$. What happens?
  1. $u$ is no longer tied to the loads and grows without limit: $z - u$ goes to $-\infty$ and the model is unbounded
  2. The model still minimises the spread, because $u$ settles at the minimum load
  3. The model becomes a min-max
  4. The model is infeasible
Explanation. The difference model needs both groups of constraints: $z$ is pushed down and held by $z \ge \ell_k$, $u$ is pushed up and held only by $u \le \ell_k$. Without them, $u$ has no upper limit.
mip-f-397 · level facile · notes
Absolute value in a minimisation objective: $|u - v|$ is minimised by introducing $d \ge 0$ with $d \ge u - v$ and $d \ge v - u$, and minimising $d$. With $u = 7$ and $v = 3$ fixed, what is $d$ at the optimum?
  1. $-4$
  2. $10$
  3. $4$
  4. $0$
Explanation. The constraints give $d \ge 4$ and $d \ge -4$; minimising, $d$ drops to the larger of the two, $4 = |7 - 3|$. The linearisation works because the absolute value is minimised.
mip-f-398 · level ipotesi · notes
Conditional constraint $a'x \le b + M(1 - y)$, with $y$ binary and $x$ within its bounds. A student chooses $M$ **smaller** than the maximum of $a'x - b$ over the bounds. What may happen?
  1. With $y = 0$ the constraint is not fully switched off and cuts feasible solutions, perhaps the optimal one
  2. The model becomes unbounded
  3. Nothing: a smaller $M$ only gives a tighter relaxation
  4. With $y = 1$ the constraint no longer holds
Explanation. $M$ must be at least $\max (a'x - b)$ over the bounds so that with $y = 0$ every $x$ satisfies it. Below that value the switched-off constraint still cuts points: the model is wrong, not just tighter.
mip-f-399 · level facile · notes
Sequencing $n$ jobs on one machine: $s_{ij} = 1$ if $j$ precedes $i$, with $s_{ij} + s_{ji} = 1$ for $i < j$ and $\kappa_i \ge \kappa_j + t_i - M(1 - s_{ij})$ for $i \ne j$. With $n = 5$ jobs, how many variables $s_{ij}$ and how many big-M constraints are there?
  1. $20$ variables and $10$ big-M constraints
  2. $20$ variables and $20$ big-M constraints
  3. $25$ variables and $20$ big-M constraints
  4. $10$ variables and $10$ big-M constraints
Explanation. One $s_{ij}$ and one big-M constraint per ordered pair with $i \ne j$: $n(n-1) = 20$. The constraints $s_{ij} + s_{ji} = 1$ are instead one per unordered pair, $\binom{5}{2} = 10$.
mip-f-400 · level teorema · notes
Sequencing on one machine with no idle time, jobs of duration $t_j$. The constraint $\kappa_i \ge \kappa_j + t_i - M(1 - s_{ij})$ must be switched off when $s_{ij} = 0$. Which value of $M$ suffices for every pair?
  1. $M = \max_j t_j$, the duration of the longest job
  2. $M = t_i + t_j$, the sum of the two durations of the pair
  3. $M = \sum_j t_j$, the total duration: no completion exceeds this value
  4. $M = n$, the number of jobs
Explanation. With no idle time every $\kappa$ lies in $[t_j, \sum t]$, so $\kappa_j + t_i - \kappa_i \le \sum_j t_j$: with that $M$ the switched-off constraint is always satisfied. Smaller values such as $\max t_j$ may cut sequences.
mip-f-401 · level facile · notes
If and only if: binary $y$ must equal $1$ exactly when $x_1 = x_2 = x_3 = x_4 = 1$ (binaries). With the constraints $y \le x_j$ for every $j$, which constraint completes the link?
  1. $y \ge x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + x_4 - 4$
  2. $y \ge x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + x_4 - 3$
  3. $y \ge x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + x_4$
  4. $y \le x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + x_4 - 3$
Explanation. The constraint $y \ge \sum_j x_j - (p - 1)$ with $p = 4$ forces $y = 1$ only if all equal $1$ (sum $4$, right side $1$); with at least one at zero the right side is $\le 0$. With $-4$ the right side never exceeds $0$.
mip-f-402 · level teorema · notes
Link «$y = 1$ if and only if all binaries $x_1, \dots, x_p$ equal $1$», with $y \le x_j$ for every $j$. In a **maximisation** problem where $y$ has a positive coefficient in the objective (a bonus), can the constraint $y \ge \sum_j x_j - (p-1)$ be omitted?
  1. Yes: the objective pushes $y$ to $1$ as soon as allowed, so the missing direction is imposed by the optimisation
  2. Yes, but only if the problem is a minimisation
  3. No: without that constraint the model becomes unbounded
  4. No: without that constraint $y$ may equal $1$ even with some $x_j = 0$
Explanation. $y \le x_j$ forbids the bonus if an element is missing. The reverse direction («all at one ⇒ bonus») is guaranteed at the optimum by the positive coefficient in a maximisation: one direction is enough.
mip-f-403 · level facile · notes
Counting distinct types: $q_j \ge 0$ is the quantity of type $j \in \{1, \dots, 5\}$, $y_j$ the indicator, with $q_j \le c_j y_j$, $q_j \ge \ell y_j$ and $\sum_j y_j \ge 3$. What does the model impose?
  1. That at least $3$ types are produced, each in quantity at least $\ell$
  2. That at most $3$ types are produced
  3. That exactly $3$ types are produced
  4. That the total quantity is at least $3\ell$, however distributed
Explanation. Each $y_j = 1$ forces $q_j \ge \ell$, and the counting constraint wants at least three of them: at least three types present significantly. The total quantity alone is not enough (it could all lie in one type).
mip-f-404 · level ipotesi · notes
«At least $p$ distinct types» with $q_j \le c_j y_j$, $\sum_j y_j \ge p$. A student omits the threshold $q_j \ge \ell y_j$. Which unwanted solution becomes feasible?
  1. $\sum_j y_j > p$, that is, more types than required
  2. $y_j = 0$ with $q_j > 0$: a type produced without being counted
  3. $y_j = 1$ with $q_j = 0$: an «active» type without production, satisfying the count without producing anything
  4. None: $q_j \le c_j y_j$ suffices on its own
Explanation. $q_j \le c_j y_j$ forbids producing without activating, not activating without producing. Without the threshold the count is satisfied by indicators at $1$ with zero quantities: both thresholds are needed.
mip-f-405 · level facile · notes
Binary expansion: an integer variable $v \in \{0, 1, \dots, 100\}$ is rewritten as $v = \sum_{k=0}^{p-1} 2^k b_k$ with $b_k \in \{0,1\}$. What is the smallest number $p$ of binaries representing all allowed values?
  1. $8$
  2. $100$
  3. $6$
  4. $7$
Explanation. With $p$ binaries one represents $0, \dots, 2^p - 1$: with $6$ one reaches $63$, with $7$ one reaches $127 \ge 100$. (The constraint $v \le 100$ is then needed to exclude the excess values.)
mip-f-406 · level facile · notes
Alldiff: $4$ objects must receive $4$ pairwise distinct values; $p_{iv} = 1$ if object $i$ receives value $v$, with $\sum_v p_{iv} = 1$ for every object and $\sum_i p_{iv} = 1$ for every value. How many binary variables and how many constraints does the formulation have?
  1. $8$ binaries and $8$ constraints
  2. $4$ binaries and $6$ constraints
  3. $16$ binaries and $8$ constraints
  4. $16$ binaries and $16$ constraints
Explanation. One binary per object–value pair, $4 \cdot 4 = 16$; one constraint per object and one per value, $4 + 4 = 8$. It is an assignment.
mip-f-407 · level facile · notes
Soft constraint: the period demand, $\beta = 50$, is written $a'x + s^- - s^+ = 50$ with $s^-, s^+ \ge 0$ and penalty $\pi^- s^- + \pi^+ s^+$ ($\pi^-, \pi^+ > 0$) in a minimisation objective. If the solution provides $a'x = 42$, what are the deviations at the optimum?
  1. $s^- = 8$, $s^+ = 8$
  2. $s^- = 8$, $s^+ = 0$
  3. $s^- = 58$, $s^+ = 50$
  4. $s^- = 0$, $s^+ = 8$
Explanation. The constraint gives $s^- - s^+ = 8$; minimising positive penalties keeps only the necessary deviation: $s^- = 8$ (the target is undershot by $8$), $s^+ = 0$.
mip-f-408 · level facile · notes
A planning model with every period's demand as a hard constraint $a'x = \beta$ turns out infeasible (insufficient capacity). What does the soft-constraint technique offer?
  1. Removing the demand constraint, which makes the model feasible at no cost
  2. Replacing the constraint with $a'x + s^- - s^+ = \beta$ and penalising the deviations: the model becomes feasible and tells how much and where the target is missed
  3. Making the integer variables continuous
  4. Increasing $\beta$ until the model becomes feasible
Explanation. A soft constraint can be violated by paying: the optimal solution balances cost and penalty and measures the violation with $s^-$, $s^+$. Dropping it altogether would lose the target.
mip-f-409 · level facile · notes
Piecewise linear cost with nodes $q = (0, 10, 20)$ and values $g = (0, 50, 80)$. With the convex combination of the nodes ($q = \sum_k q_k \lambda_k$, $g = \sum_k g_k \lambda_k$, $\sum_k \lambda_k = 1$) and adjacency (only the $\lambda$ of the two nodes of the chosen segment positive), what is $g$ for $q = 14$?
  1. $62$
  2. $56$
  3. $80$
  4. $70$
Explanation. $q = 14$ lies in the second segment: $\lambda_1 = 0.6$, $\lambda_2 = 0.4$, so $g = 0.6 \cdot 50 + 0.4 \cdot 80 = 62$. ($70$ uses the slope of the first segment, $56$ combines nodes $0$ and $20$.)
mip-f-410 · level teorema · notes
Piecewise linear cost modelled with node weights $\lambda_k$. In which case can the segment binaries $w_t$ and the adjacency constraints be omitted without changing the optimum?
  1. Always: the $\lambda$ are already a convex combination
  2. Never: without binaries the model is not linear
  3. When the function is concave and is minimised
  4. When the function is convex and is minimised: the optimum uses adjacent nodes on its own
Explanation. Minimising a convex function, mixing non-adjacent nodes gives a value above the graph, never convenient. With quantity discounts (non-convex) the relaxation would choose the chord below the graph: the binaries are needed.
mip-f-411 · level facile · notes
Map of the variable-linking techniques: a plant with a fixed cost, capacity and continuous quantity which, if open, must produce at least a minimum threshold. Which two techniques are combined?
  1. Alldiff and binary expansion
  2. Maximum variable and absolute value
  3. Fixed cost ($q_j \le C_j y_j$) and minimum lot ($q_j \ge \ell y_j$)
  4. If and only if and precedences
Explanation. The continuous quantity tied to opening is the fixed cost; the threshold «if produced, at least $\ell$» is the minimum lot, with the same binary $y_j$.
mip-f-412 · level facile · notes
Map of techniques: facing a new link between variables, which of these questions is **not** one of those to ask?
  1. How strong is the relaxation of the chosen formulation?
  2. Which direction of the link does the constraint impose and which (possibly) the objective?
  3. How many lines of code are needed in gurobipy?
  4. What is the smallest valid coefficient (the capacity, the minimum $M$)?
Explanation. For every link one declares what the constraint imposes and what the objective does, chooses the tightest valid coefficient and assesses the strength of the relaxation; code length is not a modelling criterion.
mip-f-518 · level teorema · notes
Absolute value: $d \ge u - v$, $d \ge v - u$ linearises $d = |u - v|$ in which case?
  1. Only if $u \ge v$
  2. When $d$ is maximised
  3. Always, in any objective
  4. When $d$ is minimised (or has a positive coefficient in a minimisation): at the optimum $d$ drops to the larger of the two
Explanation. The constraints give $d \ge |u - v|$; only the downward push of the objective brings equality. To maximise $|u - v|$, or impose $|u - v| \ge k$, a binary is needed.
mip-f-519 · level ipotesi · notes
Binary expansion of an integer variable $v \in \{0, \dots, 100\}$ with $7$ binaries: $v = \sum_{k=0}^{6} 2^k b_k$. A student omits the constraint $v \le 100$. What happens?
  1. The values from $101$ to $127$ become feasible, which the problem does not allow
  2. The model becomes infeasible
  3. Negative values become feasible
  4. Nothing: with $7$ binaries one reaches exactly $100$
Explanation. With $7$ binaries one represents $0, \dots, 127$: the constraint $v \le 100$ is needed to exclude the excess values.
mip-f-545 · level facile · notes
Activation: $x_{ij} = 1$ if object $i$ uses resource $j$, $y_j = 1$ if the resource is active, $n$ objects. With the aggregated form $\sum_i x_{ij} \le n\,y_j$, how many constraints are needed for $m$ resources, and how many with the disaggregated $x_{ij} \le y_j$?
  1. $n$ and $n\,m$
  2. $n\,m$ and $m$
  3. $m$ and $n\,m$
  4. $m$ and $m$
Explanation. Aggregated: one constraint per resource. Disaggregated: one per object–resource pair. More rows, but a stronger relaxation.
mip-f-546 · level facile · notes
Big-M disjunction: at least one of $g_1(x) \le 0$ and $g_2(x) \le 0$ must hold; with a binary $y$ one writes $g_1(x) \le M_1(1 - y)$ and $g_2(x) \le M_2\,y$. What happens with $y = 1$?
  1. The first constraint is active, the second switched off (if $M_2$ is large enough)
  2. Both are switched off
  3. The second is active, the first switched off
  4. Both constraints are active
Explanation. With $y = 1$: $g_1(x) \le 0$ (active) and $g_2(x) \le M_2$, satisfied by every $x$ within the bounds if $M_2 \ge \max g_2$. The binary chooses which constraint must hold.
mip-f-547 · level facile · notes
Sequencing on one machine: $\kappa_j \ge t_j$ for every job $j$ ($\kappa_j$ completion, $t_j$ duration). What does this constraint express?
  1. The machine works one job at a time
  2. Every job finishes within its duration
  3. All jobs start together
  4. No job finishes before having worked at least its duration, starting from $0$
Explanation. The first job of the sequence finishes no earlier than its duration; the others are pushed later by the big-M precedence constraints.

Part: The problems (164)

Fifteen numerical models (62)

mip-f-099 · level facile · notes
An eight-seat van: four groups offer a price to ride, and $x_j \in \{0,1\}$ equals $1$ if group $j$ is accepted. Rule: "if group 2 is accepted, group 4 must be accepted too". Which constraint translates it?
  1. $x_2 + x_4 \ge 1$
  2. $x_2 + x_4 \le 1$
  3. $x_2 - x_4 \le 0$
  4. $x_4 - x_2 \le 0$
Explanation. The implication $x_2 \Rightarrow x_4$ is the clause $\lnot x_2 \lor x_4$, i.e. $x_4 - x_2 \ge 0$, also written $x_2 - x_4 \le 0$. The opposite direction would say "if 4 then 2".
mip-f-100 · level facile · notes
Bus lines: four lines $j$ to be given to three companies $i$ with costs $c_{ij}$; each line to one company, each company at most two lines, minimum cost. The lowest cost offered for each line is $(1, 2, 3, 1)$. With the "zero out and saturate" dual recipe ($\bar\alpha_j = \min_i c_{ij}$, capacity duals at zero), which bound is obtained?
  1. $\ub = 7$
  2. $\ub = 3$
  3. $\lb = 7$
  4. $\lb = 3$
Explanation. The recipe gives $1 + 2 + 3 + 1 = 7$: "every line costs at least its lowest offer". It is a lower bound because the problem is a minimisation; the optimum is $9$, because the company capacities prevent taking all the minima.
mip-f-101 · level facile · notes
Relay: three strokes $s$ and four swimmers $n$, $x_{sn} = 1$ if $n$ swims stroke $s$; constraints $\sum_n x_{sn} = 1$ per stroke and $\sum_s x_{sn} \le 1$ per swimmer, minimum total time. Why does the linear relaxation have the same optimum as the integer problem?
  1. Because one swimmer stays out and frees one degree of freedom
  2. Because the hand-made dual solution is optimal for the dual
  3. The matrix is that of an assignment, totally unimodular: the vertices of the relaxation are integer
  4. Because the swimmers' times are all integers
Explanation. The matrix is totally unimodular and integrality costs nothing. This stops being true as soon as a constraint with different coefficients is added, for instance a capacity.
mip-f-102 · level facile · notes
Hub-and-spoke: the minimum number of hub cities is chosen so that every city is within $1000$ miles of a hub (set covering). In the dual $\pi_c = 1$ is given to Atlanta, Denver and Seattle and $0$ to the others, giving $\lb = 3$. What does it say, in words?
  1. The three cities are pairwise far apart: no hub can serve two of them, so at least three hubs are needed
  2. Three hubs suffice to serve all the cities, so the optimum is three
  3. Every hub can serve at most three cities, so there are at least three hubs
  4. Atlanta, Denver and Seattle must be chosen as hubs in every optimal solution
Explanation. The feasible dual prices mutually distant cities: each hub covers at most one of them, so there are at least three hubs. That three suffice is shown by a feasible solution, with $\ub = 3$.
mip-f-103 · level facile · notes
Funds bought in lots: $x_1, \dots, x_4 \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ are the lots bought of the four funds. Rule: "the lots of the second fund do not exceed $50\%$ of the total number of lots". How is it written in linear form?
  1. $x_2 \le 50$
  2. $-x_1 + x_2 - x_3 - x_4 \le 0$
  3. $x_2 - x_1 - x_3 - x_4 \ge 0$
  4. $2x_2 \le x_1 + x_3 + x_4 + 1$
Explanation. From $x_2 \le \tfrac12 (x_1 + x_2 + x_3 + x_4)$, multiplying by $2$ and moving everything to the left, one gets $-x_1 + x_2 - x_3 - x_4 \le 0$.
mip-f-104 · level facile · notes
Funds bought in lots (maximisation, budget $100$): a candidate dual solution $(\bar\alpha, \bar\beta) = (5/32, 1/8)$ gives, on the first dual constraint $12\alpha - \beta \ge 2$, the value $12 \cdot 5/32 - 1/8 = 7/4$. What does one conclude?
  1. It is feasible and gives the upper bound $100 \cdot 5/32$
  2. It is feasible, but the bound it gives is weak
  3. It is not a feasible dual solution, and its value is not a bound
  4. It suffices that the other dual constraints hold: one violated constraint does not count
Explanation. $7/4 < 2$: the first constraint is violated by $1/4$. A dual value can be read as a bound only after checking all the constraints, one by one.
mip-f-105 · level facile · notes
Seminars: the student chooses sessions $(s, k)$ (seminar $s$, slot $k$) with liking $p_{sk}$, $x_{sk} \in \{0,1\}$, and must attend **exactly** two: $\sum_{s,k} x_{sk} = 2$. Maximisation problem. What is the sign of the dual variable $\gamma$ of that constraint?
  1. $\gamma \ge 0$
  2. Free
  3. $\gamma \in \{0,1\}$
  4. $\gamma \le 0$
Explanation. An equality constraint gives a free dual variable. The shortest recipe sets everything to zero except $\bar\gamma = \max_{s,k} p_{sk}$, and gives $\ub = 2 \max_{s,k} p_{sk}$.
mip-f-106 · level facile · notes
Queens on a $4 \times 4$ board: $x_{ij} = 1$ if there is a queen on square $(i,j)$, and on every row, column and diagonal one imposes $\sum x_{ij} \le 1$. Why are the diagonals made of a single square not written?
  1. Because they would make the solution with a queen in the corner infeasible
  2. Because a queen in a corner cannot attack any other queen
  3. Because those squares are already counted among the column constraints
  4. They would give $x_{ij} \le 1$ on a variable that is already binary: a row that forbids nothing
Explanation. What remains are the $5 + 5$ diagonals with at least two squares: with $4$ rows and $4$ columns they make $18$ set packing constraints, without losing any solution.
mip-f-107 · level facile · notes
CNC machine: $x_i = 1$ if operation $i$ is performed, $y_j = 1$ if tool $j$ is mounted; the operation needs **all** the tools of its kit $T_i$. How many link constraints are needed for operation $i$, and which?
  1. One only: $\sum_{j \in T_i} y_j \ge x_i$
  2. None: the limit on the number of tools suffices
  3. $|T_i|$, one per tool: $x_i \le y_j$ for every $j \in T_i$
  4. One only: $x_i \le \sum_{j \in T_i} y_j$
Explanation. $x_i \Rightarrow \bigwedge_{j \in T_i} y_j$ has a conjunction in the consequent: it costs one constraint per tool. The sum (second and third options, which are the same constraint) would say "at least one tool".
mip-f-108 · level facile · notes
Four indivisible jobs of total duration $18$ are split between two workers, with loads $w_1, w_2$. The min-max model ($\min \max(w_1, w_2)$) is worth $9$, the range model ($\min |w_1 - w_2|$) $0$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The range model is wrong, because a load cannot be $0$
  2. The two models have different optimal solutions, because the values differ
  3. Same optimal solutions and different optimal values: $9$ is the largest load, $0$ the difference
  4. Min-max is off by $9$ units, because the optimal difference is $0$
Explanation. With a constant total, $\max(w_1, w_2) = 9 + |w_1 - w_2|/2$: minimising one is minimising the other. Reporting $9$ as the difference, or $0$ as the largest load, is a mistake.
mip-f-109 · level facile · notes
Music school timetable: $x_{dti} = 1$ if on day $d$, hour $t$, there is a lesson of instrument $i$, and $y_{di} = 1$ if instrument $i$ is present on day $d$. With the link $x_{dti} \le y_{di}$ alone, the constraint "at least two instruments per day" $\sum_i y_{di} \ge 2$ achieves nothing. What must be added?
  1. A big-M on the number of hours of each instrument
  2. $\sum_i y_{di} \le 2$, to fix the count at two
  3. $x_{dti} \ge y_{di}$ for every hour $t$ of the day
  4. $y_{di} \le \sum_t x_{dti}$, the opposite direction of the link
Explanation. With one direction only it suffices to set $y_{di} = 1$ with no lesson to meet the count. When a constraint counts indicators, the link must go both ways. $x_{dti} \ge y_{di}$ for every $t$ would force a lesson in every hour.
mip-f-110 · level facile · notes
Emergency room shifts: every shift pattern covers $1$ on its four full-time days and $1/2$ on the half-shift day; $x_j$ is the number of staff assigned to pattern $j$ and every day has a minimum requirement. Which model is it?
  1. A linear programme: the variables can be continuous without changing the optimum
  2. A set packing with coefficients $1$ and $1/2$ and binary variables
  3. A set partitioning with binary variables, one pattern per day
  4. A set covering with coefficients $1$ and $1/2$ and integer, non-binary variables
Explanation. Every day must have at least its requirement covered: $\ge$ constraints with coefficients $1$, $1/2$ or $0$; the variables count people, so they are integer but not binary.
mip-f-111 · level teorema · notes
Minimisation problem whose linear relaxation is exact, $\zlp = \zmilp = 9$, while a hand-made dual solution is worth $7$. Which statement is correct?
  1. An exact relaxation also makes every constructive heuristic optimal
  2. If a dual solution is worth less than $\zlp$, it is infeasible
  3. That the relaxation is exact says nothing about the quality of a particular hand-made dual solution
  4. If the relaxation is exact, every feasible dual solution is worth $\zmilp$
Explanation. The hand-made dual solution is feasible but not optimal for the dual: it gives $\lb = 7 \le \zlp = 9$. By strong duality a dual of value $9$ exists, but the hand-made recipe is not bound to find it.
mip-f-112 · level ipotesi · notes
Vehicles with a minimum quantity: $x_j \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ units of type $j$, binary $y_j$ (type produced), minimum $q_j$ and maximum $M_j$. To conclude that $x_j$ is **semi-integer**, i.e. $x_j = 0$ or $q_j \le x_j \le M_j$, what is needed?
  1. The constraint $x_j \le M_j y_j$ alone, with binary $y_j$ and integer $x_j$
  2. The constraint $x_j \le M_j y_j$ and a positive unit profit for type $j$
  3. Both constraints $x_j \ge q_j y_j$ and $x_j \le M_j y_j$, with binary $y_j$ and integer $x_j$
  4. The constraint $x_j \ge q_j y_j$ alone, with binary $y_j$ and integer $x_j$
Explanation. With $y_j = 0$ the two constraints give $x_j = 0$, with $y_j = 1$ they give $q_j \le x_j \le M_j$. Without the first the minimum quantity disappears; without the second a type could be produced with $y_j = 0$.
mip-f-168 · level facile · notes
Queens on a $5 \times 5$ board: $x_{ij} = 1$ if there is a queen on $(i,j)$, and one constraint $\sum x_{ij} \le 1$ is written for every row, column and diagonal **with at least two squares** (in each of the two directions there are $2 \cdot 5 - 3$ of them). How many constraints in all?
  1. $18$
  2. $28$
  3. $10$
  4. $24$
Explanation. Rows and columns are $5 + 5 = 10$; the diagonals with at least two squares are $7$ per direction, $14$ in all: $24$ constraints. $28$ also counts the $4$ single-square diagonals, $18$ is the count for the $4 \times 4$ board.
mip-f-169 · level facile · notes
Eight-seat van, four groups of $2$, $3$, $4$ and $5$ people offering $30$, $50$, $80$ and $70$ euros; at most two groups, and "if 2 is accepted then 4 is accepted". What is the maximum revenue?
  1. $120$
  2. $110$
  3. $130$
  4. $150$
Explanation. The pairs within eight seats are $\{1,2\}$, $\{1,3\}$, $\{1,4\}$, $\{2,4\}$, while $\{3,4\}$ is excluded ($9 > 8$). $\{1,2\}$ breaks the rule; $\{1,3\}$ is worth $110$, $\{1,4\}$ $100$, $\{2,4\}$ $120$. $130$ and $150$ are disallowed pairs.
mip-f-170 · level facile · notes
Aircraft to order: customer $j$ orders up to $D_j$ aircraft; $x_j \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ is how many are built and $y_j = 1$ if the fixed set-up cost $f_j$ is paid. Which link imposes "no set-up, no production", with the tightest coefficient?
  1. $x_j \le 1000\, y_j$
  2. $y_j \le x_j$
  3. $x_j \le D_j\, y_j$
  4. $x_j \le D_j + y_j$
Explanation. With $y_j = 0$ the constraint gives $x_j = 0$, with $y_j = 1$ it leaves $x_j \le D_j$, which holds anyway: the coefficient is the customer's order, read from the data. Any large number is valid but weakens the relaxation.
mip-f-171 · level facile · notes
Shoes, production and workers: $y_t$ workers in month $t$, each working $160$ hours; one pair needs $4$ hours. Which constraint links the production $x_t$ (pairs) to the workers?
  1. $x_t \le 160\, y_t$, with integer $y_t$
  2. $160\, x_t \le 4\, y_t$, with integer $y_t$
  3. $4\, x_t = 160\, y_t$, with continuous $y_t$
  4. $4\, x_t \le 160\, y_t$, with integer $y_t$
Explanation. The hours required, $4 x_t$, do not exceed the hours available, $160 y_t$: each worker makes at most $40$ pairs a month. Workers are counted, so $y_t$ is integer; the equality would force all hours to be used.
mip-f-220 · level facile · notes
Relay: three strokes (backstroke, breaststroke, butterfly) to be given to different swimmers, chosen among four, minimising the total time. Times in seconds, rows = strokes, columns = Carlo, Paolo, Fabio, Marco: backstroke $(37, 32, 33, 37)$, breaststroke $(43, 33, 42, 34)$, butterfly $(33, 28, 38, 30)$. The heuristic scans the strokes in the given order and assigns each to the fastest free swimmer. What time does it obtain?
  1. $93$
  2. $95$
  3. $99$
  4. $96$
Explanation. Backstroke to Paolo ($32$), breaststroke to Marco ($34$, the fastest among those left), butterfly to Carlo ($33$): $\ub = 99$. $93$ takes the minimum of each row and uses Paolo three times; $95$ is the optimum, which the heuristic does not find.
mip-f-221 · level facile · notes
Hub-and-spoke: $y_j = 1$ if city $j$ becomes a hub, and city $i$ is served if there is a hub in $S_i$, the set of cities within $1000$ miles of $i$ ($i$ included). The model is a set covering with a symmetric coefficient matrix. Why symmetric?
  1. Because "within $1000$ miles" is a symmetric relation: $j \in S_i \iff i \in S_j$
  2. Because there are as many candidate hubs as cities, and every square matrix is symmetric
  3. Because every city covers itself, and the diagonal is made of $1$s
  4. Because all the hub costs are equal to $1$
Explanation. The entry in row $i$ and column $j$ is $1$ if $j \in S_i$; the distance between two cities is the same both ways, so $j \in S_i$ if and only if $i \in S_j$. Square does not mean symmetric.
mip-f-222 · level facile · notes
Vehicles with minimum quantity: $1000$ tonnes of steel and $2000$ hours available. The large minivan needs $8$ tonnes and $55$ hours per unit, with minimum quantity $5$. In the link $x_5 \le M_5 y_5$ ($x_5$ integer units, $y_5$ binary) $M_5$ is the maximum producible of that type alone. What is it?
  1. $37$
  2. $125$
  3. $36$
  4. $5$
Explanation. $M_5 = \min\{\lfloor 1000/8 \rfloor, \lfloor 2000/55 \rfloor\} = \min\{125, 36\} = 36$: the hours decide. $125$ looks at steel only; $37$ rounds up and admits a unit that cannot be produced; $5$ is the minimum threshold.
mip-f-223 · level facile · notes
Aircraft on order: $x_j \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ aircraft for customer $j$, $y_j = 1$ if the set-up $f_j$ is paid, with $x_j \le D_j y_j$ and $-f_j y_j$ in the maximisation objective. Customer 3 has $f_3 = 0$ and $D_3 = 5$. What can be said of $y_3$?
  1. It equals $0$ in every optimal solution, because its coefficient is zero
  2. It equals $1$ in every feasible solution, even with $x_3 = 0$
  3. With $x_3 > 0$ it can be $0$, because the customer pays no set-up
  4. With $x_3 > 0$ the constraint forces $y_3 = 1$; with $x_3 = 0$ it can be $0$ or $1$ without changing the objective
Explanation. The link says $y_3 = 0 \Rightarrow x_3 = 0$, that is $x_3 > 0 \Rightarrow y_3 = 1$. With $f_3 = 0$ nothing pushes $y_3$ to $0$ when $x_3 = 0$: both choices give the same value.
mip-f-224 · level facile · notes
Seminars: $x_{sk} = 1$ if the student attends seminar $s \in \{1,2,3\}$ in time slot $k \in \{1,2,3,4\}$ (consecutive slots). Which family imposes "never two consecutive hours of lessons"?
  1. $\sum_s (x_{sk} + x_{s,k+1}) \le 1$ for $k = 1, 2, 3$
  2. $\sum_s x_{sk} \le 1$ for every slot $k$
  3. $\sum_s (x_{sk} + x_{s,k+1}) \ge 1$ for $k = 1, 2, 3$
  4. $x_{sk} + x_{s,k+1} \le 1$ for every seminar $s$ and $k = 1, 2, 3$
Explanation. Two consecutive slots $k$ and $k+1$ may contain at most one lesson, of any seminar. The single-seminar constraint would allow different seminars in adjacent slots; $\sum_s x_{sk} \le 1$ only forbids two lessons in the same slot.
mip-f-225 · level facile · notes
CNC machine, maximum profit, tool magazine of $k = 4$ tools. Operations (tools required, profit): 1 $\{1,3,4,5\}$, $2000$; 2 $\{1,2,6\}$, $1500$; 3 $\{1,2,3,5\}$, $1800$; 4 $\{2,4,5\}$, $1700$; 5 $\{5,6\}$, $800$. Dual recipe: the profit of each operation $i$ is spread equally over its tools, $\bar\beta_{ij} = p_i / |T_i|$, and $\bar\alpha = \max_j \sum_{i : j \in T_i} \bar\beta_{ij}$; the bound is $k \bar\alpha$. What is it?
  1. $\ub = \tfrac{23000}{3}$
  2. $\ub = 7800$
  3. $\ub = 2000$
  4. $\ub = 5800$
Explanation. The sums per tool are largest on tool 5: $500 + 450 + \tfrac{1700}{3} + 400 = \tfrac{5750}{3}$, and $4 \cdot \tfrac{5750}{3} = \tfrac{23000}{3}$. $7800$ is the sum of the profits, $5800$ uses tool 1 ($4 \cdot 1450$), which is not the maximum; $2000$ is the heuristic's lower bound.
mip-f-226 · level facile · notes
Shoe factory, maximum revenue. Per pair: boots leather $850$ g and price $150$, minimum $100$ pairs; loafers $600$ g, $120$, minimum $200$; walking shoes $700$ g, $130$, minimum $150$. Available leather $120\,000$ g (hours and nails are never binding). The heuristic scans the types by price per gram of leather, decreasing, and produces of each the maximum possible, provided it reaches the minimum. What revenue does it obtain?
  1. $22\,230$
  2. $24\,000$
  3. $420\,000$
  4. $21\,150$
Explanation. Ratios $3/17$, $1/5$, $13/70$: loafers first, $120\,000/600 = 200$ pairs, and the leather runs out: $200 \cdot 120 = 24\,000$. Sorting by price gives $141$ boots ($21\,150$); $22\,230$ is $171$ walking shoes; $420\,000$ ignores the leather.
mip-f-227 · level ipotesi · notes
Shoe factory (maximum revenue, leather, hours and nails constraints, minimum thresholds): the heuristic produces $200$ loafers, revenue $24\,000$; the dual solution that prices only leather, $1/5$ euro per gram, is worth $120\,000 \cdot 1/5 = 24\,000$. To conclude **without a solver** that $24\,000$ is the optimum, what is needed?
  1. That the dual solution is the lowest among the three recipes (leather, hours, nails)
  2. That the loafers solution uses up exactly the available leather
  3. That the loafers solution is feasible and integer and that the dual solution is feasible
  4. That loafers have the highest price per gram of leather
Explanation. A feasible integer solution gives $\lb = 24\,000$, a feasible dual gives $\ub = 24\,000$ (weak duality): they coincide, so the optimum is certified. Being the smallest of the three recipes is not enough without dual feasibility.
mip-f-228 · level teorema · notes
Balancing indivisible jobs between two workers, with loads $w_1, w_2$ and constant total $w_1 + w_2 = q$. Which identity links the min-max objective to the difference objective?
  1. $\max(w_1, w_2) = |w_1 - w_2|$
  2. $\max(w_1, w_2) = q - |w_1 - w_2|$
  3. $\max(w_1, w_2) = q/2 + |w_1 - w_2|/2$
  4. $\max(w_1, w_2) = q/2 - |w_1 - w_2|/2$
Explanation. The larger of the two is the average plus half the difference. With constant $q$ the two objectives have the same optimal solutions, but different optimal values, which are not compared.
mip-f-229 · level facile · notes
Emergency room shifts: pattern 2 works full time on Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday and Friday, half time on Saturday, and rests Sunday and Monday. A full day costs $100$ euro on weekdays, $110$ on Saturday, $130$ on Sunday; at half time the day's cost is halved. How much does pattern 2 cost per worker?
  1. $465$
  2. $510$
  3. $400$
  4. $455$
Explanation. Four full weekdays, $400$, plus half of Saturday, $55$: $455$. $510$ pays Saturday in full; $400$ forgets the half shift; $465$ halves Sunday instead of Saturday.
mip-f-230 · level facile · notes
Shoes, production and workers, a **minimisation** problem: the hours constraint of month $t$ is $160\, y_t - 4\, x_t \ge 0$ ($y_t$ workers, $x_t$ pairs). In the dual of the relaxation, what sign has the variable $\beta_t$ of that constraint?
  1. $\beta_t$ free
  2. $\beta_t \in \{0, 1\}$
  3. $\beta_t \le 0$
  4. $\beta_t \ge 0$
Explanation. In a minimisation primal a $\ge$ constraint gives a non-negative dual variable; the equalities (inventory and workforce balances) give free variables.
mip-f-231 · level facile · notes
Music school timetable: $2$ days $d$, $2$ hours $t$ per day, $2$ instruments $i$; $x_{dti} = 1$ if instrument $i$ is taught at hour $t$ of day $d$, $y_{di} = 1$ if instrument $i$ appears on day $d$. The link must be written in both directions: $x_{dti} \le y_{di}$ and $y_{di} \le \sum_t x_{dti}$. How many link constraints in total?
  1. $8$
  2. $16$
  3. $4$
  4. $12$
Explanation. The first direction has one constraint per triple $(d, t, i)$: $2 \cdot 2 \cdot 2 = 8$; the second one per pair $(d, i)$: $4$. Total $12$. With the first direction only ($8$) the constraint "at least two instruments per day" is empty.
mip-f-420 · level facile · notes
Van with $8$ seats, four groups of $2$, $3$, $4$, $5$ people offering $30$, $50$, $80$, $70$ euro (accepted whole or not at all); at most two groups; if group 2 is accepted group 4 is accepted too; revenue is maximised. The heuristic scans the groups by decreasing offer and accepts a group only if all constraints stay satisfied (implication included). Which bound is obtained?
  1. $\ub = 110$ (groups 1 and 3)
  2. $\lb = 120$ (groups 2 and 4)
  3. $\lb = 110$ (groups 1 and 3)
  4. $\lb = 150$ (groups 3 and 4)
Explanation. Order $3, 4, 2, 1$: 3 fits ($4$ seats); 4 does not ($9 > 8$); 2 would force 4 (three groups); 1 fits ($6$ seats). Value $110$: in a maximisation it is a lower bound. The optimum, $120$, takes groups 2 and 4.
mip-f-421 · level facile · notes
Van with $8$ seats, four groups of $2$, $3$, $4$, $5$ people offering $30$, $50$, $80$, $70$ euro (accepted whole or not at all); at most two groups; if group 2 is accepted group 4 is accepted too; revenue is maximised. In the dual of the relaxation ($\alpha$ for seats, $\beta$ for the number of groups, $\gamma$ for the implication, all $\ge 0$; objective $\min 8\alpha + 2\beta$) one sets $\bar\beta = \bar\gamma = 0$. Which minimum $\bar\alpha$ is feasible and which bound does it give?
  1. $\bar\alpha = 14$, $\ub = 112$
  2. $\bar\alpha = 20$, $\lb = 160$
  3. $\bar\alpha = 20$, $\ub = 160$
  4. $\bar\alpha = 50/3$, $\ub = 400/3$
Explanation. The dual constraints become $2\alpha \ge 30$, $3\alpha \ge 50$, $4\alpha \ge 80$, $5\alpha \ge 70$: one needs $\alpha \ge \max(15, 50/3, 20, 14) = 20$. Value $8 \cdot 20 = 160$, an upper bound because the primal is a maximisation: «a seat is worth as much as the group paying best for it».
mip-f-422 · level facile · notes
Van with $8$ seats, four groups of $2$, $3$, $4$, $5$ people offering $30$, $50$, $80$, $70$ euro (accepted whole or not at all); at most two groups; if group 2 is accepted group 4 is accepted too; revenue is maximised. The optimum is $120$ (groups 2 and 4). Removing one constraint at a time, which of the two logical constraints really «costs», and how much?
  1. Neither: without one or the other the optimum stays $120$
  2. The implication: without it the optimum rises to $130$ (groups 2 and 3)
  3. Both cost $10$ euro each
  4. The two-group limit: without it the optimum rises to $150$
Explanation. Without the limit on the number of groups the optimum stays $120$: no triple fits in $8$ seats with higher revenue. Without the implication one can take 2 and 3 ($7$ seats, $130$). The implication costs $10$ euro.
mip-f-423 · level facile · notes
Bus lines: three companies, four lines, costs (rows companies, columns lines) $(10, 4, 9, 7)$, $(1, 2, 3, 10)$, $(8, 9, 10, 1)$; each line to exactly one company, each company at most two lines; cost is minimised. The heuristic scans the lines in the given order and assigns each to the cheapest company among those not yet saturated. Which bound is obtained?
  1. $\lb = 13$
  2. $\ub = 9$
  3. $\ub = 7$
  4. $\ub = 13$
Explanation. Line 1 to company 2 ($1$), line 2 again to 2 ($2$, now saturated), line 3 to 1 ($9$), line 4 to 3 ($1$): $13$, an upper bound in a minimisation. The optimum is $9$.

Which bound is obtained, and why is it not attainable?

  2. $\ub = 93$; in a minimisation the dual gives an upper bound

mip-f-426 · level teorema · notes
Relay: three strokes to be given to three different swimmers chosen among four, minimising the total time. Times (rows backstroke, breaststroke, butterfly; columns Carlo, Paolo, Fabio, Marco): $(37, 32, 33, 37)$, $(43, 33, 42, 34)$, $(33, 28, 38, 30)$. The linear relaxation (binaries replaced by $x \ge 0$) is worth $95$, exactly the integer optimum. Why is the relaxation exact here?
  1. Because there are more swimmers than strokes
  2. Because the times are integer
  3. The constraint matrix is the assignment one, totally unimodular: all vertices of the relaxation are integer
  4. Because the problem is a minimisation
Explanation. With a totally unimodular matrix and integer right-hand sides every vertex of the polyhedron is integer, so the relaxation optimum is already integer. Integer data alone are not enough: adding a capacity with coefficients other than $0$ and $1$ loses the property.

  1. Atlanta, Los Angeles, San Francisco
  2. Atlanta, Los Angeles
  3. Chicago, Los Angeles, Seattle
  4. Atlanta, Denver, Seattle

Hub-and-spoke (minimum number of hubs, every city within $1000$ miles of a hub): Atlanta covers Atlanta, Chicago, Houston, New York; Chicago and New York cover Atlanta, Chicago, New York; Denver covers Denver and Los Angeles; Houston covers Atlanta and Houston; Los Angeles covers Denver, Los Angeles, San Francisco; San Francisco covers Los Angeles, San Francisco, Seattle; Seattle covers San Francisco and Seattle. In the dual of the relaxation ($\pi_i \ge 0$ per city, and for every candidate hub the sum of the $\pi_i$ of the cities it covers $\le 1$) one sets $\bar\pi = 1$ for Atlanta, Denver and Seattle and $0$ elsewhere. Why is this solution feasible, and what does it prove?
  1. It is infeasible: Atlanta appears in four sets
  2. The three cities are the largest; it gives $\lb = 3$, but the optimum could be $2$
  3. Every hub covers at least one of the three; it gives $\ub = 3$
  4. No hub covers two of those three cities; it gives $\lb = 3$, which with the $3$-hub heuristic proves optimality
Explanation. The sets of hubs covering Atlanta, Denver and Seattle are disjoint, so every dual constraint receives at most $1$. The value $3$ is a lower bound: with a $3$-hub solution, $\lb = \ub = 3$.

  3. $(2, 5, 2, 4)$

mip-f-449 · level ipotesi · notes
Music school timetable: Monday and Tuesday, two hours each; $2$ hours of guitar and $2$ of violin must be placed; every day must have lessons of at least two different instruments. What is true of this argument?

  2. It is an upper bound, not a lower bound

mip-f-460 · level facile · notes

mip-f-520 · level teorema · notes

  1. The integer optimum is actually $7$

  3. That the relaxation is exact says nothing about the quality of a particular hand-built dual solution
  4. The relaxation cannot be exact if the hand-built dual is worth $7$

7. Assignment and scheduling (31)

mip-f-113 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost assignment with availability: $x_{jm} = 1$ if job $j$ goes on machine $m$; every job on one machine, and $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m$ for every machine. Why are there no links between variables to prove in this model?
  1. Because the assignment constraints are equalities
  2. It has a single family of variables, and availability is a capacity constraint, not an implication
  3. Because all the costs $c_{jm}$ are strictly positive
  4. Because the constraint matrix is totally unimodular
Explanation. Links connect different families of variables. A capacity constraint compares required minutes with available minutes: the relation is the inequality itself. With different times $t_{jm}$ the matrix is not even totally unimodular.
mip-f-114 · level facile · notes
Machines with a fixed cost: $x_{jm} = 1$ if job $j$ goes on machine $m$, $y_m = 1$ if the machine is used (cost $c_m > 0$), $t_{jm}$ times, $a_m$ availability. What does the constraint $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m y_m$ impose?
  1. Every used machine performs at most one job at a time
  2. Every job goes on the used machine of minimum cost, within its availability
  3. If a machine performs jobs then it is used, and a used machine does not exceed its availability
  4. If a machine is used then it performs at least one job, within its availability
Explanation. A single constraint acts as link and capacity. The direction "used $\Rightarrow$ performs jobs" is not imposed: it follows from optimality because $c_m > 0$.
mip-f-115 · level facile · notes
Parallel jobs: the jobs assigned to the same machine start together, and the time of machine $m$ is the **maximum** of the times $t_{jm}$ of its jobs; the sum of the machine times is minimised. With binary $x_{jm}$, how is it linearised?
  1. With a continuous $y_m$, the constraints $y_m \le t_{jm} x_{jm}$ for every job, and $\min \sum_m y_m$
  2. With a continuous $y_m$, the constraints $y_m \ge t_{jm} x_{jm}$ for every job, and $\min \sum_m y_m$
  3. With a continuous $y_m$, the constraint $y_m \ge \sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm}$, and $\min \sum_m y_m$
  4. With a continuous $y_m$, the constraint $y_m = \max_j t_{jm} x_{jm}$, and $\min \sum_m y_m$
Explanation. It is the maximum variable: the constraints give $y_m \ge \max_j t_{jm} x_{jm}$ and the objective, with a positive coefficient, brings equality. The $\max$ is not linear; the sum would give the time of jobs in series.
mip-f-116 · level facile · notes
Parallel jobs, with $y_m \ge t_{jm} x_{jm}$ and $\min \sum_m y_m$. With $\bar t_m = \max_j t_{jm}$ the constraints $\bar t_m \sum_j x_{jm} - y_m \ge 0$ are added, forcing $y_m = 0$ when the machine is empty. What are they?
  1. Valid inequalities, satisfied by every feasible solution, that strengthen the relaxation
  2. Redundant constraints, already implied by the maximum constraints $y_m \ge t_{jm} x_{jm}$
  3. Constraints that preserve optimality but are not valid: the model admits $y_m > 0$ on an empty machine
  4. Constraints that make the model infeasible when a machine stays empty
Explanation. A feasible solution with $y_m > 0$ on an empty machine violates them, so they are not valid; but every optimum satisfies them, because there $y_m = 0$.
mip-f-117 · level facile · notes
One machine, jobs split into classes with setup: $x_j = 1$ if job $j$ is performed, $y_c = 1$ if class $c$ is activated (cost $f_c \ge 0$, possibly zero, and time $s_c$), maximisation problem. In which form does "if no job of the class is performed, the class is not activated" hold?
  1. It holds in every optimal solution, because the setup consumes time
  2. It is imposed by the constraints $x_j \le y_c$ in every feasible solution
  3. There exists an optimum in which it holds, because $f_c$ may be zero
  4. It holds in no optimal solution
Explanation. Setting $y_c = 0$ stays feasible, frees $s_c$ minutes and does not worsen the objective because $f_c \ge 0$; with $f_c = 0$ the exchange does not strictly improve, so the correct conclusion is the weaker one.
mip-f-118 · level facile · notes
Classes with setup: $\mathscr{J}_c$ is the set of jobs of class $c$. Instead of the links $x_j \le y_c$, $j \in \mathscr{J}_c$, the aggregated form $\sum_{j \in \mathscr{J}_c} x_j \le |\mathscr{J}_c|\, y_c$ is used. What changes?
  1. Different integer set, weaker relaxation
  2. Nothing, not even in the linear relaxation
  3. Same integer set, stronger relaxation
  4. Same integer set, weaker relaxation
Explanation. It is the aggregated activation: equivalent on binary points, weaker in the relaxation ($y_c = 1/|\mathscr{J}_c|$ suffices for one job), with fewer rows.
mip-f-119 · level facile · notes
One machine, jobs in classes: $x_j = 1$ if job $j$ is performed, and the binary $z$ must equal $1$ if jobs of at least two different classes are performed. Which constraint imposes "$x_j = 1$ and $x_i = 1$, with $i, j$ of different classes, $\Rightarrow z = 1$"?
  1. $z \le x_j + x_i - 1$
  2. $x_j + x_i - z \le 1$
  3. $x_j + x_i + z \le 2$
  4. $x_j + x_i - 2z \ge 0$
Explanation. The implication $x_j \land x_i \Rightarrow z$ is the clause $\lnot x_j \lor \lnot x_i \lor z$, i.e. $x_j + x_i - z \le 1$: one constraint per mixed pair.
mip-f-120 · level facile · notes
Total tardiness on one machine: $s_{ji} = 1$ if $j$ precedes $i$, with $s_{ji} + s_{ij} = 1$ and big-M precedences. Why can the transitivity inequalities $s_{ji} + s_{ik} - s_{jk} \le 1$ be added?
  1. They are needed to make the big-M $M = \sum_j t_j$ valid in the precedence constraints
  2. They are valid, because a cycle of precedences is impossible, and they cut off the $1/2$ solutions of the relaxation
  3. They preserve optimality but are not valid: they cut off some feasible sequences
  4. They are implied by the order constraints $s_{ji} + s_{ij} = 1$, so they change nothing
Explanation. Every feasible sequence respects transitivity ($j$ before $i$ and $i$ before $k$ give $j$ before $k$): the inequalities are valid. In the relaxation, where $s = 1/2$ half switches off the precedences, they are cuts.
mip-f-121 · level facile · notes
Total tardiness: $\kappa_j$ is the completion of job $j$ and $d_j$ its due date. With $\tau_j \ge \kappa_j - d_j$, $\tau_j \ge 0$ and $\min \sum_j \tau_j$, what holds for $\tau_j$?
  1. The constraints give $\tau_j \ge \max\{0, \kappa_j - d_j\}$, and the optimum brings equality
  2. The constraints already give $\tau_j = \max\{0, \kappa_j - d_j\}$ in every feasible solution
  3. A binary with big-M is needed to write the maximum of $0$ and $\kappa_j - d_j$
  4. $\tau_j$ becomes negative when the job finishes early
Explanation. The link is the inequality itself; equality comes from the objective: lowering $\tau_j$ to the maximum stays feasible and reduces the sum. A big-M would be needed only to have the maximum in a "$\le$" constraint.
mip-f-122 · level teorema · notes
Classes with a bonus: $\mathscr{J}_c$ are the jobs of class $c$, $y_c = 1$ gives a bonus $v_c > 0$ in a maximisation, and there are only the constraints $x_j \ge y_c$ for $j \in \mathscr{J}_c$ ($y_c$ appears only there). Which statement is correct?
  1. "$y_c = 1 \Rightarrow$ class complete" is imposed by the constraints; "class complete $\Rightarrow y_c = 1$" holds in every optimum
  2. Both directions are imposed by the constraints $x_j \ge y_c$ in every feasible solution
  3. Neither direction is guaranteed, by the constraints or by optimality
  4. "Class complete $\Rightarrow y_c = 1$" holds only in some optimum, because the bonus is not enough
Explanation. The constraint gives one direction in every feasible solution; for the other, raising $y_c$ to $1$ with the class complete stays feasible and strictly increases the objective by $v_c > 0$: "in every optimum".
mip-f-123 · level ipotesi · notes
Machines with a fixed cost (minimisation): $y_m = 1$ if the machine is used, cost $c_m$, link $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m y_m$. To prove that **in every optimum** a used machine performs at least one job, what is needed?
  1. $c_m > 0$ and the fact that setting $y_m = 0$ on an empty machine keeps the link satisfied ($0 \le 0$)
  2. $c_m \ge 0$ and the fact that setting $y_m = 0$ on an empty machine keeps the link satisfied ($0 \le 0$)
  3. The assignment constraints and total unimodularity of the matrix
  4. The link constraint alone, which holds in every feasible solution
Explanation. It is the exchange argument: switching off an empty machine stays feasible and reduces the cost by $c_m$. Strict positivity is needed for "in every optimum"; with $c_m = 0$ one would only get "there exists an optimum".
mip-f-124 · level ipotesi · notes
One machine with jobs in classes: the binary $z = 1$ reduces the availability by $u > 0$ minutes and is forced by $x_j + x_i - z \le 1$ for every mixed pair; $z$ does not appear in the objective. To prove that **there exists an optimum** with $z = 0$ when the performed jobs lie in a single class, what is needed?
  1. That $u > 0$ and that the machine availability be saturated at the optimum
  2. That setting $z = 0$ stays feasible (it frees $u$ minutes, no mixed pair is active) and that $z$ does not appear in the objective
  3. The constraint $x_j + x_i - z \le 1$ read backwards, as "$z = 1 \Rightarrow$ mixed pair"
  4. That $z$ have a strictly positive coefficient in the maximisation objective
Explanation. The exchange stays feasible and leaves the objective unchanged, because $z$ does not appear in it: the conclusion is "there exists an optimum", not "in every optimum".
mip-f-125 · level ipotesi · notes
Total tardiness on one machine, with precedences $\kappa_i \ge \kappa_j + t_i - M(1 - s_{ji})$ and $\kappa_i \ge t_i$; the model does not impose $\kappa_j \le M$. What is needed to state that $M = \sum_j t_j$ switches off the precedences on all the solutions among which the optimum is sought?
  1. That every job meet its due date, $\kappa_j \le d_j$
  2. That the optimal total tardiness be zero
  3. That there exists an optimum without idle time, on which every $\kappa_j \le \sum_j t_j$, together with $\kappa_i \ge t_i$
  4. That the binaries $s_{ji}$ satisfy the transitivity inequalities
Explanation. If the machine stops, the following jobs can be brought forward and no tardiness gets worse: there exists an optimum without idle time, and there $\kappa_j + t_i - M \le t_i \le \kappa_i$.
mip-f-172 · level facile · notes
