Selection of jobs with revenue $r_j$ and machines with fixed cost $c_m$, to be maximised (revenues minus costs of the machines used). A heuristic chooses jobs and machines and gets a profit of $20$; the hand-made dual of the relaxation is worth $34$. How is the result written?
  1. $\zmilp \le 20$
  2. $20 \le \zmilp \le 34$
  3. $\zmilp \ge 34$
  4. $34 \le \zmilp \le 20$
Explanation. In a maximisation the heuristic's solution is the pessimistic side (lower bound) and the dual the optimistic side (upper bound). The integer optimum of that instance is $25$.
mip-f-173 · level facile · notes
Job selection (revenue $r_j$, time $t_j$) on machines with fixed cost $c_m$ and availability $a_m$, to be maximised. A job has $r_j < \min_m c_m$ and fits on no machine already switched on for other jobs. What can be said?
  1. It must go on the cheapest machine, which remains profitable
  2. It must be performed anyway, because every job has a positive revenue
  3. Performing it never pays: it would require switching on a machine that costs more than it earns
  4. It makes the problem infeasible, because no machine can host it
Explanation. Switching on a machine just for that job adds $r_j - c_m < 0$ to the profit. The jobs are not mandatory ($\sum_m x_{jm} \le 1$): leaving it out is feasible and better.
mip-f-174 · level facile · notes
Job selection: every job $j$ is performed on **at most** one machine, $\sum_m x_{jm} \le 1$, instead of exactly one. Why is this constraint "$\le$" in the maximisation problem with revenues $r_j > 0$?
  1. Because the jobs are optional: one chooses which to perform, and an unprofitable job stays out
  2. Because the dual requires non-negative variables for those constraints
  3. Because with "$=$" the linear relaxation would become integer
  4. Because positive revenues already push every job onto a machine
Explanation. The problem decides which jobs to perform: with "$= 1$" every job would become mandatory and the model would answer another question. The sign of the dual is a consequence, not the reason.
mip-f-232 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost assignment with availability: three jobs of duration $(3, 2, 4)$ (the same on every machine), two machines with availability $(5, 6)$; costs $c_{jm}$ per job: $(2, 5)$, $(1, 3)$, $(1, 6)$. The heuristic takes the jobs in order and puts each on the cheapest machine where it still fits. What cost does it obtain?
  1. $9$
  2. $4$
  3. $10$
  4. $8$
Explanation. Job 1 on machine 1 (cost $2$, residual $2$); job 2 on machine 1 (cost $1$, residual $0$); job 3 no longer fits on machine 1 and goes to machine 2 (cost $6$): $\ub = 9$. $4 = 2 + 1 + 1$ ignores availabilities: it is the dual bound "every job at its minimum cost".
mip-f-233 · level teorema · notes
Minimum-cost assignment with availability ($\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$ per job, $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m$ per machine). The dual of the relaxation has free $\mu_j$, $\pi_m \le 0$ and the constraints $\mu_j + t_{jm} \pi_m \le c_{jm}$, objective $\sum_j \mu_j + \sum_m a_m \pi_m$. Which statement is correct?
  1. For every $\bar\pi \le 0$, $\bar\mu_j = \min_m (c_{jm} - t_{jm} \bar\pi_m)$ is feasible and gives a lower bound
  2. For every $\bar\pi \le 0$, $\bar\mu_j = \max_m (c_{jm} - t_{jm} \bar\pi_m)$ is feasible and gives a lower bound
  3. For every $\bar\pi \ge 0$, $\bar\mu_j = \min_m (c_{jm} - t_{jm} \bar\pi_m)$ is feasible and gives a lower bound
  4. Only $\bar\pi = 0$ with $\bar\mu_j = \min_m c_{jm}$ gives a feasible dual
Explanation. Once $\bar\pi \le 0$ is fixed (the sign required by a $\le$ constraint in a minimisation), the largest $\mu_j$ with $\mu_j \le c_{jm} - t_{jm}\bar\pi_m$ for every $m$ is the minimum over $m$. With the maximum some constraint is violated; $\bar\pi = 0$ is just the "zero and saturate" case.
mip-f-461 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost assignment with availabilities: $x_{jm} = 1$ if job $j$ goes on machine $m$, time $t_{jm}$, availability $a_m$, constraint $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m$. If $t_{jm} > a_m$ for some pair, what can be done before solving?
  1. Fix $x_{jm} = 1$, because the job must be assigned anyway
  2. Fix $x_{jm} = 0$: the model shrinks and the relaxation does not worsen
  3. Increase $a_m$ up to $t_{jm}$
  4. Nothing: the availability constraint already excludes it, also in the relaxation
Explanation. No integer solution can use that pair; in the relaxation, however, $x_{jm}$ could equal $a_m / t_{jm} < 1$. Fixing it to zero removes a variable without losing integer solutions, and can only strengthen the relaxation.
mip-f-462 · level facile · notes
Machines with a fixed usage cost: $x_{jm} = 1$ if job $j$ goes on machine $m$, $y_m = 1$ if the machine is used (cost $c_m$), times $t_{jm}$, availability $a_m$. The model uses the aggregated constraint $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m y_m$. What does it impose on its own?
  1. Only that a switched-off machine does not work
  2. That every switched-on machine performs at least one job
  3. Both the availability of a switched-on machine and that a switched-off machine performs no jobs
  4. Only the availability: a separate constraint is needed for the switched-off machine
Explanation. With $y_m = 1$ it is the availability constraint; with $y_m = 0$ it becomes $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le 0$, so no job (positive times). That a switched-on machine really works is imposed by the objective, not by the constraint.
mip-f-463 · level teorema · notes
Machines with fixed cost: model with the aggregated constraint $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m y_m$. The disaggregated constraints $x_{jm} \le y_m$ are added for every pair. What is their effect?
  1. They lower the value of the relaxation, because they add variables
  2. They do not change the integer optimum (they are implied on integer points) but raise the value of the relaxation
  3. They change the integer optimum, because they forbid feasible assignments
  4. They change nothing, neither the optimum nor the relaxation
Explanation. On integer points $x_{jm} = 1$ with $y_m = 0$ is already forbidden by the aggregated constraint; in the relaxation, however, the aggregated one allows a small $y_m$ with a large $x_{jm}$. The disaggregated ones cut those fractional points: stronger formulation, same integer set.
mip-f-464 · level facile · notes
Job selection with fixed-cost machines (maximum profit = revenues of the jobs performed minus the costs $c_m$ of the machines used). Job $j$ has revenue $r_j$ smaller than $\min_m c_m$. In which case is performing it never worthwhile?
  1. When the machine is already on for other jobs
  2. When it would be the only job on its machine: its revenue does not pay for switching on
  3. Always: a low-revenue job is never performed
  4. Never: every performed job increases profit
Explanation. Alone on a machine it would bring $r_j - c_m < 0$. On a machine already on for other jobs the fixed cost is already paid and the job adds $r_j > 0$, if there is time.
mip-f-465 · level facile · notes
Machines performing jobs in parallel: all jobs assigned to machine $m$ start together, and the machine's time is that of its longest job; the sum of the machine times is minimised. With $x_{jm} = 1$ if $j$ goes on $m$ and $y_m \ge 0$ the machine time, which constraint defines $y_m$?
  1. $y_m = \max_j t_{jm}$
  2. $y_m \ge \sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm}$
  3. $y_m \le t_{jm} x_{jm}$ for every job $j$
  4. $y_m \ge t_{jm} x_{jm}$ for every job $j$
Explanation. It is a maximum variable: $y_m$ lies above every time of the assigned jobs, and minimising it brings it down to the longest. The sum would describe jobs in series; $\max_j t_{jm}$ ignores which jobs are assigned.
mip-f-466 · level ipotesi · notes
Parallel machines (minimum sum of times $y_m$, with $y_m \ge t_{jm} x_{jm}$). With $\bar t_m = \max_j t_{jm}$ the constraints $\bar t_m \sum_j x_{jm} - y_m \ge 0$ are added, forcing $y_m = 0$ on an empty machine. How should they be classified?
  1. They are valid inequalities, because no optimal solution violates them
  2. They are cuts: they reduce the relaxation without removing integer solutions
  3. They are wrong: they exclude the optimal solution
  4. They are not valid (the model admits $y_m > 0$ on an empty machine) but preserve optimality, because the objective brings $y_m$ to zero anyway
Explanation. A valid inequality removes no feasible integer solution; these constraints remove the solutions with $y_m > 0$ on an empty machine, feasible but never optimal. They remove only non-optimal solutions: they preserve optimality.
mip-f-467 · level facile · notes
Class setups on one machine (maximum profit): $x_j = 1$ if job $j$ is performed, $y_c = 1$ if class $c$ is activated, with setup cost $f_c$ and setup time $s_c$; availability $a$. Which pair of constraints models activation and capacity?
  1. $\sum_{j \in \mathscr{J}_c} x_j = y_c$, and $\sum_j t_j x_j + \sum_c s_c y_c \le a$
  2. $y_c \le x_j$ for every $j$ in class $c$, and $\sum_j t_j x_j \le a$
  3. $x_j \le y_c$, and $\sum_j t_j x_j \le a + \sum_c s_c y_c$
  4. $x_j \le y_c$ for every $j$ in class $c$, and $\sum_j t_j x_j + \sum_c s_c y_c \le a$
Explanation. A job is performed only if its class is active; the setup consumes time, so it adds to the job times in the capacity constraint. The equality would impose a single job per class; the last one gives time away instead of consuming it.
mip-f-468 · level teorema · notes
Class setups (maximum profit, $x_j \le y_c$ for the jobs of class $c$, setup cost $f_c \ge 0$). The constraint $\sum_{j \in \mathscr{J}_c} x_j \ge y_c$ («an activated class performs at least one job») is added. What can be said?
  1. It is necessary: without it the model is wrong
  2. It is not valid (the model admits an activated empty class) but it preserves optimality: activating an empty class costs without yielding
  3. It changes the optimum, because it forbids optimal solutions
  4. It is valid and strengthens the relaxation
Explanation. An activated class without jobs is feasible in the model, so the constraint is not valid; but it is never better than the same solution with $y_c = 0$, so at least one optimal solution survives.
mip-f-469 · level ipotesi · notes
One machine, jobs divided into classes (maximum revenue). The availability drops by $u > 0$ minutes **if and only if** jobs of at least two classes are performed; the binary $z$ equals $1$ when the reduction applies. Which of the two directions must be imposed by a constraint, and why?
  1. Both, always, because it is an if and only if
  2. «Two classes used ⇒ $z = 1$»: the objective prefers $z = 0$ (more time), so optimisation does not guarantee this direction
  3. «$z = 1$ ⇒ two classes used»: without it the solver would set $z = 1$ to gain time
  4. Neither: the objective imposes both directions
Explanation. The reduction is a penalty: left free, $z$ would stay at $0$. So the constraint forcing it to $1$ when two classes are active is needed; the reverse direction is guaranteed at the optimum, because $z = 1$ for no reason only removes time.
mip-f-470 · level facile · notes
Total tardiness on one machine: $s_{ij} = 1$ if $j$ precedes $i$ (with $s_{ij} + s_{ji} = 1$). What role do the transitivity inequalities $s_{ji} + s_{ik} - s_{jk} \le 1$ («if $i$ precedes $j$ and $k$ precedes $i$, then $k$ precedes $j$») play?
  1. They are valid (a cycle of precedences is impossible in a sequence) and cut fractional solutions of the relaxation
  2. They are necessary: without them the model admits integer cyclic sequences
  3. They preserve optimality but are not valid
  4. They worsen the relaxation
Explanation. Every real sequence respects them, so they are valid; in the relaxation the $s$ may equal $1/2$ and these inequalities cut those points. With the big-M constraints on completions integer cycles are already excluded.
mip-f-521 · level ipotesi · notes
Assignment with availabilities and constraints $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$. A student adds $x_{jm} \le 1$ «to strengthen the relaxation». What is obtained?
  1. An infeasible model
  2. A different integer optimum
  3. A stronger relaxation, because points with $x_{jm} > 1$ are removed
  4. Nothing: those constraints are implied by the assignment with $x \ge 0$, so the relaxation does not change
Explanation. With $x \ge 0$ and sum $1$ on every row each $x_{jm}$ is already at most $1$: the constraints are valid but redundant also in the relaxation.
mip-f-552 · level ipotesi · notes
Total tardiness on one machine: $\tau_j \ge \kappa_j - d_j$ and $\tau_j \ge 0$, minimising $\sum_j \tau_j$. A student omits $\tau_j \ge 0$. What happens?
  1. Early jobs would get $\tau_j < 0$ and «offset» the delays: net lateness would be minimised, not total tardiness
  2. Nothing: $\tau_j$ is non-negative at the optimum anyway
  3. The model becomes infeasible
  4. The maximum tardiness is minimised
Explanation. Tardiness is $\max\{0, \kappa_j - d_j\}$: both constraints are needed, and minimisation brings $\tau_j$ to the larger of the two. Without the zero, earliness becomes a reward.
mip-f-553 · level ipotesi · notes
Job selection on fixed-cost machines (maximum profit): $x_{jm} = 1$ if job $j$ runs on machine $m$, with $\sum_m x_{jm} \le 1$. A student writes $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$. What is the effect?
  1. It weakens the relaxation without changing the optimum
  2. It allows a job on two machines
  3. None: with binaries it is the same
  4. It makes all jobs mandatory: the problem no longer chooses which to perform and the optimum may worsen (or the model become infeasible)
Explanation. «At most one machine» leaves the choice of not performing the job; the equality removes it. It is the «all jobs mandatory» variant, which costs profit.

8. Location and covering (18)

mip-f-126 · level facile · notes
Capacitated facility location: $x_l = 1$ if site $l$ is installed (capacity $u_l$, cost $i_l > 0$), $y_{lc} \ge 0$ are the litres shipped from $l$ to customer $c$. The constraint $u_l x_l - \sum_c y_{lc} \ge 0$ does two things. Which ones?
  1. It meets the customers' demand and imposes the capacity of the site
  2. It imposes the capacity of the site and forces every installed site to ship something
  3. It links shipments to installation (nothing leaves a closed site) and imposes the capacity of the site
  4. It meets the customers' demand and links shipments to installation
Explanation. With $x_l = 0$ it gives $\sum_c y_{lc} \le 0$, with $x_l = 1$ it gives $\sum_c y_{lc} \le u_l$: aggregated activation with the right coefficient. That an open site ships something follows from optimality, since $i_l > 0$.
mip-f-127 · level facile · notes
p-median: at most $k$ open sites ($x_l = 1$), every customer $c$ assigned to a site ($y_{lc} = 1$), the sum of distances is minimised; there are no opening costs. The link $x_l \ge y_{lc}$ is imposed. What can be said about the direction "open site $\Rightarrow$ serves at least one customer"?
  1. It follows from the CNF of $y_{lc} \Rightarrow x_l$, which gives both directions
  2. It holds in every optimum, because the distances are strictly positive
  3. It is neither imposed nor guaranteed by optimality: nothing discourages an unused open site
  4. It is imposed by the constraint $\sum_l x_l \le k$ on the number of sites
Explanation. The CNF of $y_{lc} \Rightarrow x_l$ only gives $x_l \ge y_{lc}$. Without an opening cost the exchange "close an empty site" does not improve the objective.
mip-f-128 · level facile · notes
Signal coverage: $x_l = 1$ if site $l$ is installed ($m$ candidate sites), $y_c = 1$ if customer $c$ is covered, and $\mathscr{L}_c$ are the sites giving $c$ a strong signal. What does $\sum_{l \in \mathscr{L}_c} x_l + (m - 1)\, y_c \le m$ impose when $y_c = 1$?
  1. At most $m - 1$ strong sites installed for customer $c$
  2. No strong site installed for customer $c$
  3. At most one strong site installed for customer $c$
  4. At least one strong site installed for customer $c$
Explanation. With $y_c = 1$ what remains is $\sum_{l \in \mathscr{L}_c} x_l \le 1$ (not too much interference); with $y_c = 0$ it is $\le m$, which forbids nothing.
mip-f-129 · level facile · notes
Hubs with maximum cost: $y_j = 1$ if hub $j$ is active, $x_{ij} = 1$ if terminal $i$ goes to $j$, and $z_j \ge c_{ij} x_{ij}$ is the hub's largest connection cost, minimised. With $M_j = \max_i c_{ij}$, the added constraint $z_j \le M_j y_j$ is:
  1. A valid inequality that strengthens the linear relaxation
  2. Not valid (the model admits $z_j > 0$ with $y_j = 0$), but it preserves optimality
  3. Necessary for the model to compute the cost correctly
  4. Redundant, because implied by the maximum constraints $z_j \ge c_{ij} x_{ij}$
Explanation. Minimising $z_j$, an optimum sets it to zero anyway when the hub is off: the constraint cuts off feasible solutions but no optimum.
mip-f-130 · level facile · notes
Hubs with capacity $k$ terminals: the aggregated link is $\sum_i x_{ij} \le k\, y_j$. The disaggregated links $x_{ij} \le y_j$ are **added** as well. What happens to the integer optimum and to the linear relaxation (with bounds)?
  1. The integer optimum goes up, the relaxation does not change
  2. Neither the integer optimum nor the relaxation changes
  3. Both go up, because the model has more constraints
  4. The integer optimum does not change, the relaxation goes up
Explanation. The disaggregated links are implied by the aggregated constraint on integer points, not in the relaxation: adding them leaves the optimum untouched and strengthens the relaxation (in a minimisation, raises it).
mip-f-131 · level facile · notes
p-median without opening costs: the constraint is "at most $k$ open sites", $\sum_l x_l \le k$, with $k$ not exceeding the number of candidate sites. What changes by writing "exactly $k$"?
  1. Nothing on the optimal value: opening one more site lengthens no distance
  2. The problem becomes infeasible, because the extra sites stay empty
  3. The linear relaxation becomes integer
  4. The optimal value always gets worse, because useless sites are opened
Explanation. Without opening costs an extra site can only shorten the distances (every customer picks the closest), so there exists an optimum with $k$ open sites: the two forms give the same value.
mip-f-132 · level teorema · notes
Hubs with activation costs $f_j \ge 0$ (possibly zero), link $\sum_i x_{ij} \le k\, y_j$, minimisation problem. Which statement about the direction "activated hub $\Rightarrow$ serves at least one terminal" is correct?
  1. It is imposed by the aggregated activation constraint in every feasible solution
  2. It holds in every optimum whatever $f_j \ge 0$ is, because an empty hub is useless
  3. It is not imposed by the constraints; if $f_j > 0$ it holds in every optimum, if $f_j = 0$ only in some optimum
  4. It never holds, because the maximum cost $z_j$ may stay positive
Explanation. $y_j = 1$ with all $x_{ij} = 0$ is feasible. The exchange "switch off an empty hub" strictly improves only if $f_j > 0$: the strength of the conclusion depends on the sign of the coefficient.
mip-f-133 · level ipotesi · notes
Signal coverage, maximisation: $y_c = 1$ if customer $c$ is covered, with profit $p_c$; two link constraints impose "$y_c = 1 \Rightarrow$ enough signal and not too much interference". To prove that if both conditions hold then $y_c = 1$ **in every optimum**, what is needed?
  1. That the customer have at least one candidate site with a strong signal
  2. The constraint $\sum_l x_l \le k$ on the number of installed sites
  3. $p_c > 0$ and the fact that $y_c$ appears only in those two constraints, which stay satisfied when it is raised
  4. $p_c \ge 0$ and the fact that $y_c$ appears only in those two constraints, which stay satisfied when it is raised
Explanation. Raising $y_c$ to $1$ stays feasible (both conditions hold) and strictly increases the objective by $p_c > 0$. With $p_c = 0$ one would only get "there exists an optimum".
mip-f-175 · level facile · notes
p-median (minimisation, at most $k$ sites, every customer to its assigned open site): a feasible dual solution is obtained by giving each customer the value $\bar\mu_c = \min_l d_{lc}$, the distance to the closest site overall. With minimum distances per customer $3$, $6$ and $4$, which bound is obtained?
  1. $\ub = 13$
  2. $\lb = 6$
  3. $\lb = 13$
  4. $\lb = 4$
Explanation. The value is $3 + 6 + 4 = 13$: "every customer travels at least its minimum distance", as if all sites were open. In a minimisation it is a lower bound.
mip-f-176 · level facile · notes
p-median with $k = 2$ and three candidate sites; site-customer distances $d_{lc}$, rows by site: site 1 $(5, 6, 10)$, site 2 $(3, 12, 9)$, site 3 $(10, 9, 4)$. Sites 1 and 2 are opened and every customer goes to the closest open one. What value is obtained?
  1. $13$
  2. $15$
  3. $18$
  4. $21$
Explanation. Customer 1: $\min(5, 3) = 3$; customer 2: $\min(6, 12) = 6$; customer 3: $\min(10, 9) = 9$. Total $18$, an upper bound. Opening 1 and 3 gives $\min(5,10) + \min(6,9) + \min(10,4) = 5 + 6 + 4 = 15$, which is the optimum.
mip-f-177 · level facile · notes
Capacitated facility location (minimisation): site $l$ with installation cost $i_l$ and capacity $u_l$, transport $t_{lc}$ per litre. The dual recipe "spreads" the fixed cost over the capacity: $\bar\mu_l = i_l / u_l$. With $i_l = 60$ and $u_l = 50$, how much does it cost per litre, in that recipe, to serve a customer from site $l$ with $t_{lc} = 4$?
  1. $4$
  2. $26/5$
  3. $6/5$
  4. $64$
Explanation. Every litre pays the transport plus its share of the fixed cost: $t_{lc} + \bar\mu_l = 4 + 60/50 = 26/5$. The dual value of the customer is the minimum of this sum over the sites.
mip-f-178 · level facile · notes
Capacitated facility location: the demand $d_c$ of customer $c$ must be met exactly, with continuous shipments $y_{lc} \ge 0$ from the sites $l$. Which constraint expresses it?
  1. $\sum_l y_{lc} \le d_c$ for every customer $c$
  2. $\sum_l y_{lc} = d_c$ for every customer $c$
  3. $\sum_c y_{lc} = d_c$ for every site $l$
  4. $y_{lc} = d_c\, x_l$ for every pair $(l, c)$
Explanation. One sums over all the sites serving customer $c$: the total received is its demand. The last option would force every open site to serve the whole demand of every customer.
mip-f-179 · level ipotesi · notes
p-median: besides the links $x_l \ge y_{lc}$ one considers $\sum_c y_{lc} \le n\, x_l$ ($n$ customers). To declare it a **valid inequality** weaker than the disaggregated links, what must be shown?
  1. That it is obtained by summing the links $y_{lc} \le x_l$ over the customers, and that a fractional point satisfies it while violating one of them
  2. That the relaxation with it coincides with the relaxation with the disaggregated links
  3. That it is satisfied by at least one optimal solution, even if it cuts off other feasible ones
  4. That it cuts off the optimal solution of the linear relaxation and keeps at least one integer optimum
Explanation. A sum of valid constraints, hence valid; and $y_{l1} = 1$, $x_l = 1/n$ satisfies it but violates $y_{l1} \le x_l$: in the relaxation it is weaker.
mip-f-234 · level teorema · notes
Capacitated location: $x_l = 1$ if site $l$ is installed (capacity $u_l$), $y_{lc} \ge 0$ litres shipped from $l$ to customer $c$, with $\sum_l y_{lc} = d_c$ and $u_l x_l - \sum_c y_{lc} \ge 0$. Which statement about the constraint $y_{lc} \le d_c x_l$ is correct?
  1. It is valid only if every site has capacity $u_l \ge \sum_c d_c$
  2. It is redundant even in the relaxation, because implied by the capacity constraint
  3. It preserves the optimum but is not valid: it cuts off feasible shipments above $d_c$
  4. It is valid: with $x_l = 0$ it follows from $\sum_c y_{lc} \le 0$, with $x_l = 1$ from the demand; it can strengthen the relaxation
Explanation. It holds on every integer solution: closed site, no shipment; open site, $y_{lc} \le \sum_{l'} y_{l'c} = d_c$. In the continuous problem a fractional $x_l$ can satisfy the aggregated capacity and violate $y_{lc} \le d_c x_l$: it is a cut.
mip-f-471 · level facile · notes
Coverage with signal and interference (maximum profit): a customer $c$ is covered if the total signal of the installed sites is at least $t = 5$. Customer 5 receives from the three candidate sites the signals $1$, $0$, $2$. What can be done before solving?
  1. Open all three sites to cover it
  2. Lower the threshold $t$ for that customer
  3. Nothing: the solver will find out by itself
  4. Fix its coverage variable to zero: even opening all sites it receives $3 < 5$
Explanation. A data check: the maximum possible signal is $1 + 0 + 2 = 3 < 5$, so the customer can never be covered and its variable can be fixed to zero, without changing the optimum.
mip-f-472 · level facile · notes
Coverage with signal and interference: a customer is covered if, among other things, **at most one** installed site generates a signal $\ge b$ for it; $\mathscr{L}_c$ is the set of sites generating a signal $\ge b$ for $c$, $x_l = 1$ if site $l$ is installed, $w_c = 1$ if $c$ is covered. For which customers is the interference constraint redundant?
  1. For none: the constraint is always needed
  2. For those with $|\mathscr{L}_c| \ge 2$
  3. For those with $|\mathscr{L}_c| \le 1$: they cannot receive two strong signals
  4. For those with the lowest profit
Explanation. If at most one site can give a strong signal, «at most one strong site installed» is always true and the constraint cuts nothing: it can be omitted for those customers.
mip-f-473 · level ipotesi · notes
Hubs with capacity and maximum connection cost (minimisation): $y_j = 1$ if hub $j$ is active, $z_j \ge c_{ij} x_{ij}$ is the hub's maximum connection cost, and the objective sums activation costs and the $z_j$. With $M_j = \max_i c_{ij}$ the constraint $z_j \le M_j y_j$ is added. How should it be classified?
  1. It is a valid inequality
  2. It preserves optimality but is not valid: the model admits $z_j > 0$ with $y_j = 0$, which is never convenient when minimising
  3. It excludes the optimum
  4. It is necessary for the model to be correct
Explanation. $z_j$ is pushed down by the objective: with the hub off it is $0$ at the optimum. The constraint removes feasible solutions with $z_j > 0$ and the hub off (so it is not valid), but none of them is optimal.
mip-f-522 · level teorema · notes
Capacitated location (minimum cost): $x_l = 1$ if site $l$ is installed, $y_{lc} \ge 0$ litres sent from $l$ to customer $c$, with $\sum_c y_{lc} \le u_l x_l$ and $\sum_l y_{lc} = d_c$. The constraint $y_{lc} \le d_c x_l$ is valid. Why is it not needed for correctness?
  1. Because it is false: a customer may receive more than its demand
  2. Because it makes the model infeasible
  3. It is implied by the two constraints together on integer points: with the site closed nothing leaves, and no customer receives more than its demand
  4. Because the $y$ are binary
Explanation. With $x_l = 0$ the capacity zeroes the site's flows; with $x_l = 1$ demand limits each flow to $d_c$. In the relaxation it may still strengthen (disaggregated form).

9. Production planning (15)

mip-f-134 · level facile · notes
Lot sizing with a fixed cost over $4$ periods: demands $d = (30, 20, 40, 10)$, initial stock $r_0 = 0$, required final stock $r_n = 0$; $x_t$ is the production and $y_t = 1$ if production is set up in period $t$. What is the smallest $M_3$ in the constraint $x_3 \le M_3\, y_3$ that cuts off no optimal solution?
  1. $100$
  2. $40$
  3. $70$
  4. $50$
Explanation. At an optimum one never produces more than the remaining demand: $M_3 = d_3 + d_4 + r_n = 40 + 10 = 50$. A larger value is valid but weakens the relaxation; a smaller one may cut off optimal solutions.
mip-f-135 · level facile · notes
Lot sizing: $x_t \ge 0$ is the production of period $t$, $y_t = 1$ if set up (fixed cost $q_t > 0$), with the constraint $x_t \le M_t\, y_t$, minimisation problem. What holds?
  1. $x_t = 0 \Rightarrow y_t = 0$ is imposed; $y_t = 0 \Rightarrow x_t = 0$ holds in every optimum
  2. $y_t = 0 \Rightarrow x_t = 0$ is imposed; $x_t = 0 \Rightarrow y_t = 0$ holds in every optimum
  3. Both directions are imposed by the constraint $x_t \le M_t\, y_t$
  4. $y_t = 0 \Rightarrow x_t = 0$ is imposed; $x_t = 0 \Rightarrow y_t = 0$ holds only in some optimum
Explanation. The constraint says "no setup, no production"; the opposite direction comes from optimality, because setting $y_t = 0$ saves $q_t > 0$. With $q_t = 0$ one would only say "there exists an optimum".
mip-f-136 · level facile · notes
Production and workforce, no layoffs: $m_0$ initial workers, $y_t$ workers in service in month $t$ (first formulation), $z_t$ workers hired at the start of month $t$ (second formulation). Which correspondence links the two formulations?
  1. $y_t = m_0 + \sum_{j=1}^{t} z_j$, i.e. $z_t = y_t - y_{t-1}$ with $y_0 = m_0$
  2. $z_t = y_t + y_{t-1}$ with $y_0 = m_0$
  3. $y_t = z_t$ for every month $t$
  4. $y_t = m_0 + z_t$ for every month $t$
Explanation. The workers of a month are the initial ones plus all those hired up to that month. $y_t = m_0 + z_t$ would forget the hirings of previous months.
mip-f-137 · level facile · notes
Production and workforce: in the formulation with the workforce $y_t$ (workers in service) the ban on layoffs is $y_t \ge y_{t-1}$. What does it correspond to in the formulation with the hirings $z_t = y_t - y_{t-1}$?
  1. To the constraints on the available working hours
  2. To the non-negativity of the $z_t$: the ban is hidden in the domain
  3. To no constraint: the two formulations are not equivalent
  4. To the inventory balance constraints
Explanation. With $z_t = y_t - y_{t-1}$, the condition $z_t \ge 0$ is exactly $y_t \ge y_{t-1}$. This is why the hiring formulation has fewer constraints.
mip-f-138 · level facile · notes
Vehicles with a minimum lot: type $j$ consumes $a_{ij} \ge 0$ units of resource $i$, available in quantity $b_i$. How is the maximum producible quantity $M_j$ of type $j$ alone, used in $x_j \le M_j y_j$, computed?
  1. $M_j = \max_i \lfloor b_i / a_{ij} \rfloor$ over the resources the type consumes
  2. $M_j = \sum_i \lfloor b_i / a_{ij} \rfloor$ over the resources the type consumes
  3. $M_j = \min_i \lfloor b_i / a_{ij} \rfloor$ over all resources, including those with $a_{ij} = 0$
  4. $M_j = \min \{\lfloor b_i / a_{ij} \rfloor : a_{ij} > 0\}$, over the resources the type consumes only
Explanation. Type $j$ alone is limited by the scarcest resource among those it consumes; a resource with $a_{ij} = 0$ does not limit it, and dividing by zero makes no sense. The maximum or the sum would not be tight upper bounds.
mip-f-139 · level facile · notes
Vehicles: $y_j = 1$ if type $j$ is produced (at least at the minimum lot). The bonus $\bar r > 0$ is earned if at least two types are produced; the binary $z = 1$ gives the bonus in a maximisation. Which constraint imposes "$z = 1 \Rightarrow$ at least two active types"?
  1. $\sum_j y_j \le 2z$
  2. $z \le y_1 + y_2$
  3. $z \ge \sum_j y_j - 1$
  4. $2z \le \sum_j y_j$
Explanation. With $z = 1$ at least two active types are needed, which by the minimum lot are really produced. The opposite direction is not imposed: it follows from optimality because $\bar r > 0$. $z \le y_1 + y_2$ would accept a single type.
mip-f-140 · level teorema · notes
Two formulations of the same problem are equivalent on integer points (there is a correspondence between their integer solutions with the same cost, up to a constant). Which statement is correct?
  1. They do not always have the same linear relaxation: it must be checked case by case
  2. They always have the same objective function, constants included
  3. The one with fewer constraints always has the stronger linear relaxation
  4. They always have the same linear relaxation, because they describe the same problem
Explanation. Equivalence on integers says nothing about the continuum; in some cases the relaxations coincide, but it is a check, not a given. And the objective functions may differ by a constant.
mip-f-141 · level ipotesi · notes
Vehicles: the binary $z$ gives the bonus $\bar r$ in a maximisation, with the single constraint $2z \le \sum_j y_j$. To prove that with at least two active types $z = 1$ **in every optimum**, what is needed?
  1. $\bar r \ge 0$ and the fact that raising $z$ to $1$ keeps $2z \le \sum_j y_j$ satisfied
  2. $\bar r > 0$ and the fact that raising $z$ to $1$ keeps $2z \le \sum_j y_j$ satisfied
  3. That the unit profits $\bar p_j$ of all types be positive
  4. The minimum lot constraints $x_j \ge \bar q_j y_j$ and $\bar r > 0$
Explanation. With two active types raising $z$ stays feasible and strictly increases the objective by $\bar r$. With $\bar r = 0$ the argument fails and $z$ stops being a faithful indicator.
mip-f-180 · level facile · notes
Lot sizing over $n$ periods: $x_t$ production, $s_t$ stock at the end of period $t$, $d_t$ demand, initial stock $r_0$. What is the balance constraint of the first period?
  1. $x_1 - s_1 = d_1 + r_0$
  2. $x_1 + s_1 = d_1 + r_0$
  3. $x_1 - s_1 = d_1 - r_0$
  4. $x_1 = d_1$
Explanation. What comes in ($r_0 + x_1$) equals what goes out ($d_1$) plus what remains ($s_1$): $r_0 + x_1 = d_1 + s_1$, i.e. $x_1 - s_1 = d_1 - r_0$. $x_1 = d_1$ would forbid producing in advance.
mip-f-181 · level facile · notes
Lot sizing with setup $f = 50$ and holding $h = 1$ per unit and period, demands $d = (20, 10)$, no initial stock. Plan A: one setup in period 1 covering both periods. Plan B: one setup per period. What do the two plans cost (excluding the unit production cost, equal for both)?
  1. A $70$, B $100$
  2. A $50$, B $100$
  3. A $60$, B $50$
  4. A $60$, B $100$
Explanation. A pays one setup ($50$) and holds $10$ units for one period ($10$): $60$. B pays two setups: $100$. $50$ forgets the holding, $70$ charges holding on $20$ units instead of $10$.
mip-f-182 · level facile · notes
"Just in time" production: every month exactly the demand is produced and the minimum number of workers needed is hired. With $160$ hours a month per worker and $4$ hours per pair, how many workers are needed for $100$ pairs in a month?
  1. $2$
  2. $25$
  3. $5/2$
  4. $3$
Explanation. One needs $4 \cdot 100 = 400$ hours, and $400/160 = 2.5$: workers are counted, so $\lceil 2.5 \rceil = 3$. The fractional value is what the relaxation would use.
mip-f-183 · level teorema · notes
Lot sizing with a fixed cost: $M_t = \sum_{\tau \ge t} d_\tau + r_n$ in the constraint $x_t \le M_t\, y_t$ is replaced by a larger value, for instance $10 M_t$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The integer optimum does not change and the linear relaxation gets stronger
  2. The integer optimum changes, because larger quantities can be produced
  3. The integer optimum does not change and the linear relaxation gets weaker
  4. The model becomes infeasible for the periods with zero demand
Explanation. A larger valid $M$ neither removes nor adds optimal solutions, but in the relaxation $y_t = x_t/(10 M_t)$ suffices and the fixed cost paid collapses: the bound gets worse.
mip-f-235 · level ipotesi · notes
Production and workforce without layoffs: formulation A uses the workforce $y_t$ with $y_t \ge y_{t-1}$, formulation B the hirings $z_t \ge 0$; $y_0 = m_0$ initial workers. To prove that A and B are equivalent on the integer points, what is needed?
  1. The correspondence $z_t = y_t - y_{t-1}$, one-to-one between integer solutions, preserving feasibility and the cost up to a constant
  2. That the two formulations have the same number of variables and constraints
  3. That the two linear relaxations have the same optimal value
  4. That the hiring cost is zero, so that the two objectives coincide
Explanation. Equivalence on the integer points: to every integer solution of one corresponds a feasible one of the other, with the same cost up to a constant. $z_t \ge 0$ translates exactly $y_t \ge y_{t-1}$. The relaxations are compared separately.
mip-f-474 · level facile · notes
Vehicle production with minimum quantity $\bar q_j$ if type $j$ is produced and big-M: $\bar q_j y_j \le x_j \le M_j y_j$. How is $M_j$ chosen in the course?
  1. The maximum producible quantity of type $j$ alone with the available resources
  2. A large constant equal for all, for example $10^6$
  3. The minimum quantity $\bar q_j$
  4. The maximum producible quantity of the whole plan
Explanation. $M_j = \min_i \lfloor b_i / a_{ij} \rfloor$ (resources permitting) is the tightest value that cuts no solutions: it strengthens the relaxation compared with an arbitrary constant. If $\bar q_j > M_j$ the type would be eliminated.
mip-f-523 · level ipotesi · notes
Production with a diversification bonus: the binary $z$ equals $1$ if at least two types are produced, and brings the bonus $\bar r > 0$ in a maximisation; $y_j = 1$ if type $j$ is produced. A student writes only $2z \le \sum_j y_j$. Is it enough?
  1. No: the constraint allows the bonus with a single type
  2. No: $z$ must be a general integer, not binary
  3. Yes: the constraint forbids the bonus with fewer than two types, and the objective brings $z$ to $1$ as soon as allowed
  4. No: $z \ge \sum_j y_j - 1$ is also needed, otherwise the bonus is never collected
Explanation. The direction «bonus ⇒ at least two types» is imposed by the constraint; the reverse is guaranteed by the positive coefficient of $z$ in a maximisation. One constraint, one binary.

10. Mixed problems (38)

mip-f-142 · level facile · notes
Rewards of a loyalty scheme: every reward $i$ is taken with points only ($x_i = 1$) or with a cash contribution ($y_i = 1$), in at most one way: $x_i + y_i \le 1$. Which statement is correct?
  1. If the reward is not taken with points, then it is taken with a contribution
  2. The constraint forces every reward to be taken in one of the two ways
  3. The constraint admits only the configurations $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$
  4. The constraint forbids only $(1,1)$: $(0,0)$ is allowed too, so $x_i = 0$ does not imply $y_i = 1$
Explanation. It is a set packing: three configurations allowed. Forcing every reward to be taken would require the equality $x_i + y_i = 1$, a partitioning, and the problem would change.
mip-f-143 · level facile · notes
Combinatorial auction: $x_j = 1$ if bid $j$ (on the set of items $B_j$) is accepted; every item is sold at most once. Why is one constraint per item written, $\sum_{j : i \in B_j} x_j \le 1$, instead of $x_j + x_{j'} \le 1$ for every pair of conflicting bids?
  1. Because the pairwise constraints would give a different set of integer solutions
  2. Because the pairwise constraints need auxiliary variables for every conflict
  3. Because the per-item constraint always makes the linear relaxation integer
  4. It has fewer constraints and is tighter: three bids on the same item cannot be worth $1/2$ each
Explanation. The two forms have the same integer points; per item there are as many constraints as items instead of a number growing like the square of the bids, and $x_1 = x_2 = x_3 = 1/2$ satisfies the pairs but not the row.
mip-f-144 · level facile · notes
"Triangle" auction: three items and three bids of profit $1$, each on two different items, so that every pair of bids shares an item. What are the linear relaxation $\zlp$ and the integer optimum $\zmilp$ (maximisation)?
  1. Both $3/2$
  2. $\zlp = 3$ and $\zmilp = 1$
  3. $\zlp = 3/2$ and $\zmilp = 1$
  4. Both $1$
Explanation. With $x = 1/2$ on all three bids every item is sold "once" and the value is $3/2$; any two bids share an item, so the integer optimum accepts only one.
mip-f-145 · level facile · notes
Diet: $x_i$ kilos of food $i$, $y_i = 1$ if the food is in the diet, with $x_i \le d_i y_i$; the diet must use at least $t$ different foods, $\sum_i y_i \ge t$. Why is the minimum lot $x_i \ge c_i y_i$ with $c_i > 0$ also needed?
  1. To make the linear relaxation of the model exact
  2. To respect the upper limits on the intake of the nutrients
  3. Without it, indicators with $x_i = 0$ would be switched on and the variety would be met by foods not eaten
  4. Because otherwise the model is infeasible when $t$ is large
Explanation. The count looks at the indicators, not at the quantities: with $y_i = 1$ the minimum lot guarantees $x_i \ge c_i > 0$. Without it, the cost drops by switching on foods "at zero quantity".
mip-f-146 · level facile · notes
Decorated trees: $x_c \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ counts the trees decorated with configuration $c$, and the binary $z_c$ must equal $1$ only if the configuration is really used (at least $f$ different configurations are required, $\sum_c z_c \ge f$). Which constraint is used?
  1. $z_c \ge x_c$
  2. $z_c \le x_c$, with no big-M
  3. $x_c \le M z_c$, with $M$ the number of trees
  4. $x_c + z_c \le 1$
Explanation. Since $x_c$ is an integer count, $z_c = 1$ directly imposes $x_c \ge 1$. $x_c \le M z_c$ is the link in the other direction, which does not prevent counting empty configurations; $z_c \ge x_c$ would forbid more than one tree.
mip-f-147 · level facile · notes
Shipments in boxes of capacity $w$: $x_{psc}$ units of product $p$ from plant $s$ to customer $c$, and $y_{sc} \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ boxes on the route, with $\sum_p x_{psc} \le w\, y_{sc}$. What does it impose on $y_{sc}$?
  1. $y_{sc} \in \{0, 1\}$: one box per route
  2. $y_{sc} \le \lfloor \sum_p x_{psc} / w \rfloor$
  3. $y_{sc} = \sum_p x_{psc} / w$, possibly fractional
  4. $y_{sc} \ge \lceil \sum_p x_{psc} / w \rceil$: integrality produces the ceiling
Explanation. It is the integer count: the constraint gives $y_{sc} \ge \sum_p x_{psc} / w$ and integrality rounds up. That no empty boxes travel follows from optimality, since the number of boxes is minimised.
mip-f-148 · level facile · notes
Children in summer camps: in every camp $j$ nationality $c$ must have at least as many children as **each** other one, i.e. $n_{cj} \ge n_{ij}$ for every $i \ne c$ ($n_{ij}$ children of nationality $i$ in camp $j$). What is the difference with the single constraint $n_{cj} \ge \sum_{i \ne c} n_{ij}$?
  1. The single constraint is weaker, because it is one instead of many
  2. No difference, for any number of nationalities
  3. The single constraint admits camps in which $c$ is outnumbered by another nationality
  4. With more than two nationalities the single constraint is stronger: it asks that $c$ exceed all the others together
Explanation. The two readings coincide with two nationalities and diverge with more than two, where the single constraint cuts off solutions the statement allows. Fewer constraints do not mean the right model.
mip-f-149 · level facile · notes
Branches between two companies: $x_i = 1$ if branch $i$ goes to company $A$, otherwise to $B$; one minimises $z$, the maximum over the products of the revenue difference between $A$ and $B$ (in absolute value). Why is the linear relaxation worth $0$?
  1. Because the variable $z$ is free and the relaxation drives it to $-\infty$, then to $0$
  2. Because the model has a single family of binary variables
  3. With $x_i = 1/2$ every branch is split in half and the revenues of the two companies coincide on every product
  4. Because the revenues are all non-negative and $z$ is bounded below
Explanation. A fractional solution puts half of every branch in each company and evens everything out. The useful bound, here, is combinatorial: it comes from integrality.
mip-f-150 · level teorema · notes
Tracks onto CDs: $x_{ij} = 1$ if track $i$ (duration $d_i$) goes on CD $j$; one minimises $y - z$ with $y \ge \sum_i d_i x_{ij}$ and $z \le \sum_i d_i x_{ij}$ for every CD $j$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The constraints already impose $y$ equal to the duration of the longest CD
  2. At the optimum $z$ is $0$, because it is bounded only from above
  3. Minimising $y - z$ is equivalent to minimising $y$ alone
  4. The constraints give $y \ge$ largest duration and $z \le$ smallest duration, and at the optimum the equalities hold
Explanation. It is the maximum variable applied twice, in opposite directions: the objective pushes $y$ down and $z$ up. Minimising $y$ alone is the makespan, with different optima.
mip-f-151 · level ipotesi · notes
Books onto shelves: $x_{bs} = 1$ if book $b$ (height $h_b$) goes on shelf $s$, and $y_s$ is the height of the shelf, with $\min \sum_s y_s$. To prove that in every optimum $y_s$ is the height of the tallest book on the shelf, what is needed?
  1. The constraints $y_s \ge h_b\, x_{bs}$, the coefficient $1 > 0$ of $y_s$ in the minimisation and $y_s$ absent from every other constraint
  2. A big-M equal to the largest height and the constraints $y_s \ge h_b\, x_{bs}$
  3. The width constraints of the shelves and the coefficient $1 > 0$ of $y_s$ in the minimisation
  4. The constraints $y_s \ge h_b\, x_{bs}$ and $y_s$ absent from every other constraint, whatever the coefficient
Explanation. The constraint gives $y_s \ge \max_{b : x_{bs} = 1} h_b$; since $y_s$ costs in the objective and appears nowhere else, lowering it to the maximum stays feasible and strictly improves.
mip-f-184 · level facile · notes
Combinatorial auction (maximisation): the heuristic sorts the bids by profit **per item** requested, $p_j / |B_j|$, decreasing (equal ratios in the listed order), and accepts those whose items are still free. Bids: $\{1\}$ for $6$, $\{1,3\}$ for $12$, $\{3,4\}$ for $12$, $\{2,4\}$ for $10$. Which set does it accept?
  1. $\{1,3\}$ and $\{3,4\}$, value $24$
  2. $\{1\}$ and $\{2,4\}$, value $16$
  3. $\{1,3\}$ and $\{2,4\}$, value $22$
  4. $\{1\}$ and $\{3,4\}$, value $18$
Explanation. The ratios are $6$, $6$, $6$ and $5$: with equal ratios the given order is followed. $\{1\}$ and then $\{3,4\}$ are accepted; $\{1,3\}$ and $\{2,4\}$ find items already sold. $\lb = 18$, while the optimum is $22$; the last option sells item 3 twice.
mip-f-185 · level facile · notes
Combinatorial auction (maximisation): in the dual of the relaxation $\lambda_i \ge 0$ is the price of item $i$, with $\sum_{i \in B_j} \lambda_i \ge p_j$ for every bid. The recipe $\bar\lambda_i = \max_{j : i \in B_j} p_j / |B_j|$ is feasible. Why divide by $|B_j|$?
  1. It spreads the bid's profit over its items: it stays feasible and gives a lower upper bound than without dividing
  2. To always obtain the optimal dual solution
  3. Without the division the dual solution would be infeasible
  4. To turn the bound into a lower bound instead of an upper bound
Explanation. $\bar\lambda_i = \max_j p_j$ is also feasible, but it charges a bid's profit to each of its items and inflates the bound. Dividing, $\sum_{i \in B_j} \bar\lambda_i \ge |B_j| \cdot p_j / |B_j| = p_j$ still holds, with lower prices.
mip-f-186 · level facile · notes
Tracks onto two CDs, minimising the duration difference: each track, in the given order, goes on the CD currently shorter (on equal durations on the first). With durations $(10, 7, 6, 5, 4, 3)$, what difference is obtained?
  1. $1$
  2. $3$
  3. $9$
  4. $0$
Explanation. $10 \to$ CD1, $7 \to$ CD2, $6 \to$ CD2 ($13$), $5 \to$ CD1 ($15$), $4 \to$ CD2 ($17$), $3 \to$ CD1 ($18$): durations $18$ and $17$, difference $1$. It is an upper bound; the total $35$ is odd, so $0$ is impossible and $1$ is optimal.
mip-f-187 · level ipotesi · notes
Branches between two companies, $\min z$ with $z \ge$ revenue difference on every product: the linear relaxation is worth $0$. To still obtain a positive lower bound on the integer optimum, what can one rely on?
  1. On the value of the constructive heuristic, which is a lower bound in minimisation problems
  2. On integrality: each branch goes wholly to one company, and product by product exact ties are limited
  3. On a feasible dual solution of the relaxation with a positive value
  4. On strong duality of the relaxation, which gives a bound equal to the integer optimum
Explanation. No feasible dual exceeds $\zlp = 0$; the useful bound is combinatorial and comes from indivisibility. For instance, a product sold by a single branch leaves a difference equal to its whole revenue.
mip-f-236 · level facile · notes
Loyalty programme prizes with $s = 3$ prizes: for each prize $x_i = 1$ (points only) and $y_i = 1$ (points plus contribution), with $x_i + y_i \le 1$ per prize, one points budget constraint and one minimum preference constraint. How many variables and how many constraints (domains excluded)?
  1. $3$ variables and $5$ constraints
  2. $6$ variables and $5$ constraints
  3. $6$ variables and $3$ constraints
  4. $6$ variables and $9$ constraints
Explanation. Two binaries per prize: $6$. Constraints: $3$ mutual exclusion, $1$ budget, $1$ preference, that is $5$. Forgetting budget and preference gives $3$; counting them once per prize gives $9$.
mip-f-237 · level teorema · notes
Diet: $x_i \ge 0$ kilos of food $i$, $y_i \in \{0,1\}$ food in the diet, with $x_i - c_i y_i \ge 0$ and $x_i - d_i y_i \le 0$ (minimum lot $c_i > 0$, cap $d_i$). Which statement is correct?
  1. In every feasible solution $c_i \le x_i \le d_i$, whatever $y_i$
  2. The two constraints make $x_i$ integer, because $y_i$ is binary
  3. If $c_i \le d_i$, in every feasible solution $x_i = 0$ or $c_i \le x_i \le d_i$; if $c_i > d_i$ the constraints force $y_i = 0$
  4. $x_i = 0$ implies $y_i = 1$, because the minimum lot is positive
Explanation. With $y_i = 0$ the constraints give $0 \le x_i \le 0$; with $y_i = 1$ they give $c_i \le x_i \le d_i$, an empty interval if $c_i > d_i$. It is the semicontinuous variable: $x_i$ stays continuous inside the interval.
mip-f-238 · level facile · notes
Decorated trees: $x_c$ trees with configuration $c$, which needs $u_{cl}$ lights of colour $l$; $y_b$ boxes of type $b$, which contains $v_{bl}$ lights of colour $l$. What does the constraint $\sum_b v_{bl}\, y_b - \sum_c u_{cl}\, x_c \ge 0$, written for every colour $l$, say?
  1. The lights of colour $l$ bought are enough for those needed by the decorated trees
  2. The number of boxes is at least the number of trees
  3. Every box bought must be used on at least one tree
  4. The lights of colour $l$ needed are enough to fill the boxes bought
Explanation. The first term counts the lights of colour $l$ in the boxes, the second those mounted on the trees: the non-negative difference says the purchase covers the need, colour by colour.
mip-f-239 · level facile · notes
Shipments in boxes: $k = 2$ products, $n = 2$ plants, $m = 3$ customers. Constraints: demand $\sum_s x_{psc} = d_{pc}$ per product–customer pair, availability $\sum_c x_{psc} \le a_{ps}$ per product–plant pair, capacity $\sum_p x_{psc} \le w\, y_{sc}$ per plant–customer route. How many constraints in total (domains excluded)?
  1. $18$
  2. $10$
  3. $12$
  4. $16$
Explanation. Demand $k m = 6$, availability $k n = 4$, capacity $n m = 6$: $16$. $12$ forgets availability; $18 = 6 + 12$ uses one capacity per triple and forgets availability.
mip-f-240 · level facile · notes
Children among summer camps: $s = 4$ nationalities, $r = 3$ camps; in every camp nationality $c$ must have at least as many children as each other one, with one constraint per pair (other nationality $i \ne c$, camp $j$). How many majority constraints?
  1. $18$
  2. $12$
  3. $9$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $(s - 1)\, r = 3 \cdot 3 = 9$: one constraint for every other nationality in every camp. $12 = s\,r$ includes comparing $c$ with itself; $18$ compares every pair of nationalities; $3$ writes only one per camp.
mip-f-241 · level facile · notes
Books on shelves: $x_{bs} = 1$ if book $b$ (height $h_b$) goes on shelf $s$, $y_s$ height of the shelf, $\min \sum_s y_s$. Why write $y_s \ge h_b\, x_{bs}$ for every pair $(b, s)$ instead of $y_s = \max_b h_b\, x_{bs}$?
  1. Because then $y_s$ can be declared binary
  2. Because $y_s = \max_b h_b x_{bs}$ would give an infeasible model
  3. Because the pairwise constraints already impose equality in every feasible solution
  4. The maximum is not linear; the pairwise constraints express it linearly and the minimum brings $y_s$ to the maximum
Explanation. It is the maximum variable in disaggregated form: the constraints give $y_s \ge$ height of the tallest book on the shelf, and the minimisation objective, where $y_s$ has a positive coefficient, pushes it onto that value.
mip-f-475 · level facile · notes
Rewards with two modes (minimum money): reward $i$ is obtained with $a_i$ points ($x_i = 1$) or with $b_i < a_i$ points plus $c_i$ euro ($y_i = 1$), at most once and in a single mode; available points $p$. Which constraints express mutual exclusion and the points budget?
  1. $x_i + y_i \le 1$ for every $i$, and $\sum_i (a_i x_i + b_i y_i) \le p$
  2. $x_i + y_i \le 2$ for every $i$, and $\sum_i (a_i + b_i)(x_i + y_i) \le p$
  3. $x_i \le y_i$ for every $i$, and $\sum_i (a_i x_i + b_i y_i) \le p$
  4. $x_i + y_i = 1$ for every $i$, and $\sum_i a_i x_i \le p$
Explanation. At most one mode per reward is a packing of two variables; the points spent depend on the chosen mode. The equality would force taking every reward.
mip-f-476 · level facile · notes
Rewards with two modes: points only ($a_i$ points) or $b_i < a_i$ points plus $c_i$ euro; money spent is minimised. If $c_i = 0$ for some reward, what can be done?
  1. Fix that reward to «taken»
  2. Eliminate the mode with contribution, which is useless
  3. Nothing: both modes remain necessary
  4. Eliminate the «points only» mode of that reward: the other costs the same euros and fewer points
Explanation. With $c_i = 0$ the second mode dominates the first (zero euro in both, fewer points): variable $x_i$ can be removed without losing optima. It is not implied that the reward is worth taking.
mip-f-477 · level facile · notes
Diet (minimum cost) with minimum lot and variety: $q_i \ge 0$ kilograms of food $i$, $y_i = 1$ if the food enters the diet, with at least $c_i$ and at most $d_i$ kilograms if chosen, and at least $t$ different foods. Which constraints express this?
  1. $c_i y_i \le q_i \le d_i y_i$ for every $i$, and $\sum_i y_i \ge t$
  2. $c_i \le q_i \le d_i$ for every $i$, and $\sum_i q_i \ge t$
  3. $c_i y_i \le q_i \le d_i y_i$ for every $i$, and $\sum_i y_i \le t$
  4. $q_i \le d_i y_i$ for every $i$, and $\sum_i y_i \ge t$
Explanation. Minimum lot (semicontinuous) plus counting of distinct types, with both thresholds: without $c_i y_i \le q_i$ a «chosen» food could have zero kilograms and count towards variety.
mip-f-478 · level facile · notes
The classical diet problem (Stigler, 1945) — minimum cost with lower and upper bounds on nutrients — is a pure LP. What makes it a MILP in the course version?
  1. The upper bounds on nutrients
  2. The fact that quantities are in kilograms
  3. The minimum lots for the chosen foods and the counting of different foods
  4. The cost per kilogram of the foods
Explanation. Nutrient bounds and costs stay linear and continuous. The two additions requiring binaries are the threshold «if eaten, at least $c_i$ kilograms» and the minimum number of different foods.
mip-f-479 · level facile · notes
Decorated trees (minimum cost): $x_c \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ trees with configuration $c$, which needs $u_{cl}$ lights of colour $l$; $z_b \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ boxes of type $b$, containing $v_{bl}$ lights of colour $l$. Which constraint guarantees enough lights?
  1. $v_{bl} z_b \ge u_{cl} x_c$ for every $b$, $c$, $l$
  2. $\sum_l \sum_b v_{bl} z_b \ge \sum_l \sum_c u_{cl} x_c$
  3. $\sum_b v_{bl} z_b \ge \sum_c u_{cl} x_c$ for every colour $l$
  4. $\sum_b z_b \ge \sum_c x_c$
Explanation. The lights bought of each colour must cover those required of that colour: one constraint per colour. Summing over colours would allow compensating one colour with another.
mip-f-480 · level facile · notes
Decorated trees (minimum cost), with the constraint $\sum_c x_c = q$ on the number of trees to decorate. What would happen writing it as $\le q$ or as $\ge q$?
  1. In both cases the optimum would stay the same
  2. With $\ge q$ the optimum would decorate infinitely many trees
  3. With $\le q$ the optimum would decorate nothing (zero cost); with $\ge q$ nothing would change, because decorating more costs more
  4. With $\le q$ nothing would change; with $\ge q$ the problem would become infeasible
Explanation. In a minimisation with positive costs the objective pushes the number of trees down: with $\le q$ down to zero; with $\ge q$ it stops at $q$, as with the equality.
mip-f-481 · level teorema · notes
Shipping in boxes (minimum number of boxes): $x_{psc} \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ units of product $p$ from plant $s$ to customer $c$, $y_{sc} \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ boxes on the route, with $\sum_p x_{psc} \le w\,y_{sc}$, integer data. Why could the $x$ be continuous without changing the optimum?
  1. They could not: continuous $x$ would always change the optimum
  2. Because the problem is a minimisation
  3. Because the number of boxes is already integer
  4. With $y$ fixed it is still a transportation problem, with a totally unimodular matrix: with integer data it has integer solutions
Explanation. With the boxes fixed, the constraints on the $x$ (availabilities, demands, route capacities) have the transportation structure: vertices are integer, so there is always an integer plan with the same boxes. The $x$ stay integer because the problem speaks of units.
mip-f-482 · level facile · notes
Shipping in boxes of capacity $w = 10$ units, minimum number of boxes; customer demands total $11$ units. The relaxation gives the bound $\sum_{p,c} d_{pc} / w = 11/10$. Which integer lower bound follows?
  1. $11$: one box per unit
  2. $2$: the number of boxes is integer, so one rounds up
  3. $11/10$: a bound is not rounded
  4. $1$: one rounds to the nearest integer
Explanation. In a minimisation with integer-valued objective, $\zmilp \ge 11/10$ implies $\zmilp \ge \lceil 11/10 \rceil = 2$. Rounding down would give a valid but weaker bound.
mip-f-483 · level facile · notes
Summer camps (maximum children accepted): $f_{ij}, g_{ij} \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$ girls and boys of nationality $i$ in camp $j$. In every camp girls must be at least as many as boys. Which constraint expresses this?
  1. $\sum_j f_{ij} \ge \sum_j g_{ij}$ for every nationality $i$
  2. $\sum_{i,j} f_{ij} \ge \sum_{i,j} g_{ij}$
  3. $f_{ij} \ge g_{ij}$ for every nationality $i$ and camp $j$
  4. $\sum_i f_{ij} \ge \sum_i g_{ij}$ for every camp $j$
Explanation. The comparison is per camp, summing all nationalities. Per nationality and camp would be too restrictive; per nationality over all camps, or in total, too loose.
mip-f-484 · level facile · notes
Summer camps (maximum children accepted) with capacity $d_j$ per camp: $15$ and $8$ places. Which immediate upper bound holds on the optimum, regardless of the parity and majority constraints?
  1. $\ub = 23$, the sum of the capacities
  2. $\ub = 46$, twice the capacities (girls and boys)
  3. $\ub = 15$, the capacity of the largest camp
  4. $\lb = 23$
Explanation. No camp takes more than its capacity, so the accepted children are at most $15 + 8 = 23$: it is the bound obtained by pricing only the capacity constraints at $1$.
mip-f-485 · level teorema · notes
Antitrust split of branches into two companies, minimising the maximum revenue difference between them over all products. With **a single** product, which classical problem does it reduce to?
  1. Transportation, with integral relaxation
  2. Number partitioning, NP-complete (one of Karp's twenty-one problems)
  3. The assignment problem, solvable in polynomial time
  4. The fractional knapsack
Explanation. With one product one seeks a bipartition of numbers making the two sums as close as possible: number partitioning. With several products it is the vector version, still NP-hard.
mip-f-486 · level facile · notes
Branch split: $x_i = 1$ if branch $i$ goes to company A, $0$ to B; $v_{ij}$ revenue of branch $i$ with product $j$. The difference between A and B on product $j$ is $D_j = \sum_i v_{ij}(2x_i - 1)$. How is $\max_j |D_j|$ minimised with a linear model?
  1. $\min z$ with $z \le D_j$ and $z \le -D_j$ for every $j$
  2. $\min z$ with $z \ge D_j$ and $z \ge -D_j$ for every product $j$
  3. $\min z$ with $z \ge D_j$ for every $j$
  4. $\min \sum_j D_j$
Explanation. Maximum variable and absolute value together: two constraints per product put $z$ above $|D_j|$, and minimising $z$ brings it down to the maximum. With $z \ge D_j$ alone negative differences would escape.
mip-f-487 · level teorema · notes
Tracks on two CDs, minimising the difference between the duration of the longest and the shortest CD; durations are integer and sum to $35$ minutes. Why is the optimum at least $1$?
  1. Because the linear relaxation is worth $1$
  2. The two durations are integers with an odd sum, so they cannot be equal: the difference is at least $1$
  3. Because each CD must contain at least one track
  4. Because the longest track lasts at least $1$ minute
Explanation. $L_1 + L_2 = 35$ with integer $L_1, L_2$: $L_1 - L_2 = 35 - 2L_2$ is odd, so at least $1$ in absolute value. It is a parity argument, which the relaxation (value $0$) does not see.
mip-f-488 · level facile · notes
Tracks on $m$ CDs (minimum difference between longest and shortest CD, with $z_{\max} \ge L_j$ and $z_{\min} \le L_j$ for every CD). Every CD must contain at least $w_j = 1$ track. Why is the constraint needed, although it never bites at the optimum?
  1. Because without it the model is unbounded
  2. Without it an empty CD (duration $0$) would be feasible and the problem's definition would not be respected, even if it is not convenient at the optimum
  3. It is not needed: it can be dropped without changing the problem
  4. Because it makes the relaxation integral
Explanation. An empty CD has duration $0$ and brings the difference up to the total duration, so it is never optimal; but the problem requires at least one track per CD and the model must say so. On other instances ($w_j > 1$) the constraint may bite.
mip-f-489 · level facile · notes
Books on shelves (minimum sum of heights, identical shelves): swapping the contents of two shelves gives a solution of the same value. How is this symmetry broken on large instances?
  1. By imposing $y_1 \ge y_2 \ge \dots \ge y_m$ on the shelf heights
  2. By adding one more shelf
  3. By fixing every book to a shelf before solving
  4. By making the assignment variables continuous
Explanation. Every solution has a permutation of the shelves with non-increasing heights, so the constraint loses no optimum but removes the symmetric copies the solver would explore uselessly.
mip-f-490 · level facile · notes
Books on shelves: each book $b$ has width $w_b$ and height $h_b$, shelves have width $c$, a shelf's height is that of its tallest book, the sum of the heights is minimised. Compared with classical bin packing, what changes?
  1. Items can be split among several containers
  2. The cost of a container is not fixed but depends on its content (the tallest book)
  3. One maximises instead of minimising
  4. The containers have different capacities
Explanation. As in bin packing items go into containers of capacity $c$, but every shelf costs the maximum height of its books: it is modelled with a maximum variable $y_s \ge h_b x_{bs}$.
mip-f-524 · level teorema · notes
Summer camps (maximum children accepted, integer counting variables, all constraints are sums). The constraint «girls at least as many as boys» and the one «nationality $c$ at least as numerous as any other» are:
  1. Independent: there can be camps respecting one and violating the other
  2. The first implies the second
  3. Equivalent: one implies the other
  4. Both redundant, because the capacity implies them
Explanation. The first compares genders, the second nationalities: a camp with many girls of another nationality respects the first and violates the second, and vice versa. On the course instance the second one bites.
mip-f-525 · level ipotesi · notes
Shipping in boxes: if the costs were per unit transported and there were no boxes, the problem would be a pure transportation problem. What would be lost by removing the boxes from the course model?
  1. The integrality of the units, which become fractional in transportation
  2. Nothing: pure transportation has the same optimum
  3. The availability constraints of the plants
  4. The step cost of whole boxes: exactly what makes the problem integer and hard
Explanation. Without boxes transportation has an integral relaxation and is solved in polynomial time. The integer variables $y_{sc}$ (boxes) introduce the fixed cost per box, which makes the problem a true MILP.

Generated questions (60)

Each generator builds a new instance, with different numbers, at every start: a few examples here.

1. What is a MIP model (5)

Example 1 · mip-g-gap-euristica · level facile · notes
In a minimisation problem the constructive heuristic finds a solution of value $75$, a hand-made dual solution is worth $47$ and the solver certifies the integer optimum $\zmilp = 60$. What is the gap of the heuristic, that is, the relative distance of its value from the optimum, measured with respect to the optimum?
  1. $25.0\%$
  2. $20.0\%$
  3. $37.3\%$
  4. $21.7\%$
Explanation. The gap of the heuristic is measured against the known optimum: $|\ub - \zmilp| / |\zmilp| = 15/60 \approx 25.0\%$. The ratio $(\ub - \lb)/|\ub|$ is instead the certified gap between the two bounds, used when the optimum is unknown.
Example 2 · mip-g-gap-euristica · level facile · notes
In a minimisation problem the constructive heuristic finds a solution of value $69$, a hand-made dual solution is worth $41$ and the solver certifies the integer optimum $\zmilp = 47$. What is the gap of the heuristic, that is, the relative distance of its value from the optimum, measured with respect to the optimum?
  1. $46.8\%$
  2. $31.9\%$
  3. $40.6\%$
  4. $12.8\%$
Explanation. The gap of the heuristic is measured against the known optimum: $|\ub - \zmilp| / |\zmilp| = 22/47 \approx 46.8\%$. The ratio $(\ub - \lb)/|\ub|$ is instead the certified gap between the two bounds, used when the optimum is unknown.
Example 1 · mip-g-divario-certificato · level media · notes
**Maximisation** problem. A constructive heuristic finds a feasible integer solution of value $61$; a feasible solution of the dual of the linear relaxation, built by hand, is worth $108$. The certified gap is $(\ub - \lb)/|\ub|$, with $\ub$ and $\lb$ the upper and lower bound on the integer optimum. What is it (rounded to one decimal)?
  1. $43.5\%$
  2. $77.0\%$
  3. $56.5\%$
  4. $47.0\%$
Explanation. In a maximisation the heuristic gives $\lb = 61$ and the dual $\ub = 108$: $(108 - 61)/108 \approx 43.5\%$. Dividing by $\lb$ is the mistake of swapping the roles of the two bounds; $\lb/\ub$ is the complement, not the gap.
Example 2 · mip-g-divario-certificato · level media · notes
**Minimisation** problem. A constructive heuristic finds a feasible integer solution of value $117$; a feasible solution of the dual of the linear relaxation, built by hand, is worth $104$. The certified gap is $(\ub - \lb)/|\ub|$, with $\ub$ and $\lb$ the upper and lower bound on the integer optimum. What is it (rounded to one decimal)?
  1. $11.1\%$
  2. $12.5\%$
  3. $88.9\%$
  4. $13.0\%$
Explanation. In a minimisation the heuristic gives $\ub = 117$ and the dual $\lb = 104$: $(117 - 104)/117 \approx 11.1\%$. Dividing by $\lb$ is the mistake of swapping the roles of the two bounds; $\lb/\ub$ is the complement, not the gap.
Example 1 · mip-g-arrotonda-bound · level facile · notes
**Maximisation** MILP with all variables integer and integer objective coefficients, so every feasible solution has an integer value. The linear relaxation is worth $\zlp = \tfrac{301}{4}$. What is the tightest bound on the integer optimum $\zmilp$ that follows?
  1. $\ub = 75$
  2. $\ub = 76$
  3. $\ub = \tfrac{301}{4}$
  4. $\ub = 74$
Explanation. In a maximisation $\zmilp \le \zlp$; since $\zmilp$ is integer one rounds down: $\ub = \lfloor \tfrac{301}{4} \rfloor = 75$. Rounding the other way is not a bound; $\zlp$ itself is valid but weaker.
Example 2 · mip-g-arrotonda-bound · level facile · notes
**Minimisation** MILP with all variables integer and integer objective coefficients, so every feasible solution has an integer value. The linear relaxation is worth $\zlp = \tfrac{251}{6}$. What is the tightest bound on the integer optimum $\zmilp$ that follows?
  1. $\lb = 42$
  2. $\lb = 41$
  3. $\lb = \tfrac{251}{6}$
  4. $\lb = 43$
Explanation. In a minimisation $\zlp \le \zmilp$; since $\zmilp$ is integer one rounds up: $\lb = \lceil \tfrac{251}{6} \rceil = 42$. Rounding the other way is not a bound; $\zlp$ itself is valid but weaker.
Example 1 · mip-g-due-rilassamenti · level facile · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack: values $p = (10, 14, 13, 11)$, weights $w = (5, 6, 2, 5)$, capacity $C = 14$. $\zlp$ is the relaxation with $x \ge 0$ only, $\zlpp$ the one with $0 \le x \le 1$. What are $(\zlp, \zlpp)$?
  1. $(91, 40)$
  2. $(40, 91)$
  3. $(91, 38)$
  4. $(48, 40)$
Explanation. Without bounds it pays to fill the knapsack with the item of maximum ratio $p_j/w_j$, even several times: $\zlp = C \max_j p_j/w_j = 91$. With $x \le 1$ items are taken by decreasing ratio and the last one partially: $\zlpp = 40$. In a maximisation $\zlp \ge \zlpp \ge \zmilp$.
Example 2 · mip-g-due-rilassamenti · level facile · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack: values $p = (11, 13, 15, 12)$, weights $w = (6, 7, 2, 7)$, capacity $C = 12$. $\zlp$ is the relaxation with $x \ge 0$ only, $\zlpp$ the one with $0 \le x \le 1$. What are $(\zlp, \zlpp)$?
  1. $(90, \tfrac{67}{2})$
  2. $(\tfrac{67}{2}, 90)$
  3. $(90, 28)$
  4. $(51, \tfrac{67}{2})$
Explanation. Without bounds it pays to fill the knapsack with the item of maximum ratio $p_j/w_j$, even several times: $\zlp = C \max_j p_j/w_j = 90$. With $x \le 1$ items are taken by decreasing ratio and the last one partially: $\zlpp = \tfrac{67}{2}$. In a maximisation $\zlp \ge \zlpp \ge \zmilp$.
Example 1 · mip-g-ilp-due-var · level complessa · notes
Maximisation ILP: $\max 6x_1 + 3x_2$ with $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 22$, $5x_1 + x_2 \le 13$, $x_1, x_2 \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$. What are $\zlp$ (relaxation with continuous $x \ge 0$) and $\zmilp$?
  1. $\zlp = \tfrac{105}{4}$, $\zmilp = 24$
  2. $\zlp = \tfrac{105}{4}$, $\zmilp = 21$
  3. $\zlp = 26$, $\zmilp = 24$
  4. $\zlp = 24$, $\zmilp = \tfrac{105}{4}$
Explanation. The relaxation has its optimum at a vertex: $\zlp = \tfrac{105}{4}$. Enumerating the feasible integer points the optimum is $\zmilp = 24$. Rounding the optimal vertex down gives only $21$, and $\lfloor \zlp \rfloor$ is a bound, not the optimum.
Example 2 · mip-g-ilp-due-var · level complessa · notes
Maximisation ILP: $\max 2x_1 + 8x_2$ with $2x_1 + x_2 \le 28$, $x_1 + 6x_2 \le 26$, $x_1, x_2 \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$. What are $\zlp$ (relaxation with continuous $x \ge 0$) and $\zmilp$?
  1. $\zlp = \tfrac{476}{11}$, $\zmilp = 42$
  2. $\zlp = \tfrac{476}{11}$, $\zmilp = 40$
  3. $\zlp = 43$, $\zmilp = 42$
  4. $\zlp = 42$, $\zmilp = \tfrac{476}{11}$
Explanation. The relaxation has its optimum at a vertex: $\zlp = \tfrac{476}{11}$. Enumerating the feasible integer points the optimum is $\zmilp = 42$. Rounding the optimal vertex down gives only $40$, and $\lfloor \zlp \rfloor$ is a bound, not the optimum.

2. Relaxations, duality and bounds (7)

Example 1 · mip-g-duale-azzera · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost assignment with availability: $n = 4$ jobs, $k = 3$ machines, $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$ per job and $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m$ per machine; the rows of the costs $c_{jm}$, one per job, are (7, 10, 11), (2, 10, 8), (8, 11, 1), (11, 3, 4). The dual of the relaxation has a free $\mu_j$ per job, $\pi_m \le 0$ per machine, constraints $\mu_j + t_{jm} \pi_m \le c_{jm}$ and objective $\max \sum_j \mu_j + \sum_m a_m \pi_m$. Recipe "zero out and saturate": $\bar\pi = 0$ and each $\bar\mu_j$ at the largest feasible value. What is the bound?
  1. $13$
  2. $43$
  3. $24$
  4. $6$
Explanation. With $\bar\pi = 0$ the dual constraints become $\mu_j \le c_{jm}$ for every $m$, so $\bar\mu_j = \min_m c_{jm}$ and the bound is $\sum_j \min_m c_{jm} = 13$: "every job costs at least its minimum cost". In a minimisation it is a lower bound, valid by weak duality.
Example 2 · mip-g-duale-azzera · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost assignment with availability: $n = 3$ jobs, $k = 3$ machines, $\sum_m x_{jm} = 1$ per job and $\sum_j t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m$ per machine; the rows of the costs $c_{jm}$, one per job, are (11, 4, 2), (5, 11, 10), (8, 12, 10). The dual of the relaxation has a free $\mu_j$ per job, $\pi_m \le 0$ per machine, constraints $\mu_j + t_{jm} \pi_m \le c_{jm}$ and objective $\max \sum_j \mu_j + \sum_m a_m \pi_m$. Recipe "zero out and saturate": $\bar\pi = 0$ and each $\bar\mu_j$ at the largest feasible value. What is the bound?
  1. $15$
  2. $34$
  3. $22$
  4. $11$
Explanation. With $\bar\pi = 0$ the dual constraints become $\mu_j \le c_{jm}$ for every $m$, so $\bar\mu_j = \min_m c_{jm}$ and the bound is $\sum_j \min_m c_{jm} = 15$: "every job costs at least its minimum cost". In a minimisation it is a lower bound, valid by weak duality.
Example 1 · mip-g-zaino-sandwich · level media · notes
Binary knapsack: values $p = (4, 4, 6, 14, 11)$, weights $w = (4, 7, 2, 4, 6)$, capacity $C = 11$, to be maximised ($x_j = 1$ if item $j$ is taken). The constructive heuristic scans the items by decreasing $p_j/w_j$ and takes every item that still fits; in the dual of the relaxation ($\min C v$ with $w_j v \ge p_j$, $v \ge 0$) one chooses $\bar v = \max_j p_j / w_j$. Which bounds are obtained?
  1. $\lb = 24$, $\ub = \tfrac{77}{2}$
  2. $\lb = 20$, $\ub = \tfrac{77}{2}$
  3. $\lb = 24$, $\ub = 39$
  4. $\lb = \tfrac{77}{2}$, $\ub = 24$
Explanation. In a maximisation the heuristic gives a lower bound: $\lb = 24$ (one does not stop at the first item that does not fit). The dual gives an upper bound: $\ub = C \cdot \max_j p_j/w_j = \tfrac{77}{2}$, feasible because $w_j \bar v \ge p_j$ for every $j$. In a minimisation the names would swap.
Example 2 · mip-g-zaino-sandwich · level media · notes
Binary knapsack: values $p = (12, 7, 12, 4)$, weights $w = (6, 4, 4, 5)$, capacity $C = 8$, to be maximised ($x_j = 1$ if item $j$ is taken). The constructive heuristic scans the items by decreasing $p_j/w_j$ and takes every item that still fits; in the dual of the relaxation ($\min C v$ with $w_j v \ge p_j$, $v \ge 0$) one chooses $\bar v = \max_j p_j / w_j$. Which bounds are obtained?
  1. $\lb = 19$, $\ub = 24$
  2. $\lb = 12$, $\ub = 24$
  3. $\lb = 19$, $\ub = 35$
  4. $\lb = 24$, $\ub = 19$
Explanation. In a maximisation the heuristic gives a lower bound: $\lb = 19$ (one does not stop at the first item that does not fit). The dual gives an upper bound: $\ub = C \cdot \max_j p_j/w_j = 24$, feasible because $w_j \bar v \ge p_j$ for every $j$. In a minimisation the names would swap.
Example 1 · mip-g-duale-valore · level facile · notes
Linear relaxation of a minimisation MILP: $\min 7x_1 + 7x_2 + 8x_3$ with $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \ge 7$, $2x_1 + x_2 + 2x_3 \ge 10$, $x \ge 0$. The dual has $\pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0$, one constraint per variable ($a_{1j}\pi_1 + a_{2j}\pi_2 \le c_j$) and objective $\max 7\pi_1 + 10\pi_2$. The solution $\bar\pi = (1, 3)$ is feasible. Which lower bound does it give on the integer optimum?
  1. $\lb = 37$
  2. $\lb = 4$
  3. $\lb = 31$
  4. $\lb = 22$
Explanation. By weak duality the dual value $7 \cdot 1 + 10 \cdot 3 = 37$ lies below $\zlp \le \zmilp$. Summing the components or swapping the right-hand sides does not give the dual objective; $\sum_j c_j$ is not a bound.
Example 2 · mip-g-duale-valore · level facile · notes
Linear relaxation of a minimisation MILP: $\min 8x_1 + 15x_2 + 8x_3$ with $3x_1 + 4x_2 + x_3 \ge 3$, $2x_1 + 4x_2 + 3x_3 \ge 10$, $x \ge 0$. The dual has $\pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0$, one constraint per variable ($a_{1j}\pi_1 + a_{2j}\pi_2 \le c_j$) and objective $\max 3\pi_1 + 10\pi_2$. The solution $\bar\pi = (2, 1)$ is feasible. Which lower bound does it give on the integer optimum?
  1. $\lb = 16$
  2. $\lb = 3$
  3. $\lb = 23$
  4. $\lb = 31$
Explanation. By weak duality the dual value $3 \cdot 2 + 10 \cdot 1 = 16$ lies below $\zlp \le \zmilp$. Summing the components or swapping the right-hand sides does not give the dual objective; $\sum_j c_j$ is not a bound.
Example 1 · mip-g-tabella-duale · level media · notes
One writes the dual of a **minimisation** linear programme with 6 constraints, of sense $1$: $\le$, $2$: $\ge$, $3$: $\ge$, $4$: $\le$, $5$: $=$, $6$: $\le$, and variables $x_{1} \ge 0$, $x_{2} \ge 0$, $x_{3} \ge 0$. How many dual variables are, in order, $\ge 0$, $\le 0$ and free?
  1. $(2, 3, 1)$
  2. $(3, 2, 1)$
  3. $(2, 3, 0)$
  4. $(3, 3, 0)$
Explanation. One dual variable per constraint. Minimisation primal: $\ge \to \pi_i \ge 0$, $\le \to \pi_i \le 0$, $= \to$ free. Free primal variables decide the sense of the dual constraints (equalities), not the sign of the dual variables.
Example 2 · mip-g-tabella-duale · level media · notes
One writes the dual of a **minimisation** linear programme with 6 constraints, of sense $1$: $\ge$, $2$: $\le$, $3$: $=$, $4$: $=$, $5$: $\le$, $6$: $=$, and variables $x_{1}$ free, $x_{2} \ge 0$, $x_{3}$ free. How many dual variables are, in order, $\ge 0$, $\le 0$ and free?
  1. $(1, 2, 3)$
  2. $(2, 1, 3)$
  3. $(1, 2, 2)$
  4. $(4, 2, 0)$
Explanation. One dual variable per constraint. Minimisation primal: $\ge \to \pi_i \ge 0$, $\le \to \pi_i \le 0$, $= \to$ free. Free primal variables decide the sense of the dual constraints (equalities), not the sign of the dual variables.
Example 1 · mip-g-copertura-minimale · level media · notes
Binary knapsack with constraint $6x_{1} + 8x_{2} + 8x_{3} + 2x_{4} + 8x_{5} \le 22$. A set $S$ of items is a **cover** if $\sum_{j \in S} w_j > 22$ (then $\sum_{j \in S} x_j \le |S| - 1$ is valid); it is **minimal** if removing any item it is no longer a cover. Which set is a minimal cover?
  1. $\{1, 2, 3, 4\}$
  2. $\{1, 2, 3, 4, 5\}$
  3. $\{2, 5\}$
  4. $\{1, 4, 5\}$
Explanation. $\{1, 2, 3, 4\}$ weighs $24 > 22$ and removing any item it drops to $22$ or less. $\{1, 2, 3, 4, 5\}$ is a cover but not minimal; the other two weigh at most $22$.
Example 2 · mip-g-copertura-minimale · level media · notes
Binary knapsack with constraint $8x_{1} + 4x_{2} + 2x_{3} + 5x_{4} \le 15$. A set $S$ of items is a **cover** if $\sum_{j \in S} w_j > 15$ (then $\sum_{j \in S} x_j \le |S| - 1$ is valid); it is **minimal** if removing any item it is no longer a cover. Which set is a minimal cover?
  1. $\{1, 2, 4\}$
  2. $\{1, 2, 3, 4\}$
  3. $\{1, 3, 4\}$
  4. $\{2, 4\}$
Explanation. $\{1, 2, 4\}$ weighs $17 > 15$ and removing any item it drops to $15$ or less. $\{1, 2, 3, 4\}$ is a cover but not minimal; the other two weigh at most $15$.
Example 1 · mip-g-taglio-violato · level media · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack with values $p = (12, 7, 9, 9)$ and constraint $6x_{1} + 8x_{2} + 3x_{3} + 6x_{4} \le 14$. The relaxation with $0 \le x \le 1$ has optimal solution $\tilde x = (1, 0, 1, \tfrac{5}{6})$. For a minimal cover $S$ ($\sum_{j \in S} w_j > 14$) the cut is $\sum_{j \in S} x_j \le |S| - 1$. Which of these minimal covers gives a cut **violated** by $\tilde x$?
  1. $\{1, 3, 4\}$
  2. $\{1, 2, 3\}$
  3. $\{1, 2, 4\}$
  4. $\{2, 3, 4\}$
Explanation. Sum $\tilde x$ over the items of each cover and compare with $|S| - 1$: only for $\{1, 3, 4\}$ does the sum exceed the limit, so only that cut separates $\tilde x$ and strengthens the relaxation.
Example 2 · mip-g-taglio-violato · level media · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack with values $p = (7, 13, 3, 6)$ and constraint $7x_{1} + 5x_{2} + 6x_{3} + 7x_{4} \le 7$.
