The relaxation with $0 \le x \le 1$ has optimal solution $\tilde x = (\tfrac{2}{7}, 1, 0, 0)$. For a minimal cover $S$ ($\sum_{j \in S} w_j > 7$) the cut is $\sum_{j \in S} x_j \le |S| - 1$. Which of these minimal covers gives a cut **violated** by $\tilde x$?
  1. $\{1, 2\}$
  2. $\{3, 4\}$
  3. $\{2, 4\}$
  4. $\{1, 4\}$
Explanation. Sum $\tilde x$ over the items of each cover and compare with $|S| - 1$: only for $\{1, 2\}$ does the sum exceed the limit, so only that cut separates $\tilde x$ and strengthens the relaxation.
Example 1 · mip-g-zaino-ottimo-gap · level complessa · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack: values $p = (13, 18, 17, 14, 3)$, weights $w = (5, 7, 2, 6, 8)$, capacity $C = 16$. The heuristic by decreasing $p_j/w_j$ (ties: smallest index) gives $\lb$; the dual of the relaxation with $\bar v = \max_j p_j/w_j$ gives $\ub = C\bar v$. What are the optimum $\zmilp$ and the certified gap $(\ub - \lb)/\ub$ (one decimal)?
  1. $\zmilp = 49$, gap $64.7\%$
  2. $\zmilp = 49$, gap $2.0\%$
  3. $\zmilp = 48$, gap $64.7\%$
  4. $\zmilp = 49$, gap $183.3\%$
Explanation. $\lb = 48$, $\ub = 136$, so the certified gap is $\approx 64.7\%$; enumerating the subsets $\zmilp = 49$. The heuristic gap with respect to the optimum ($2.0\%$) is another measure; dividing by $\lb$ is a mistake.
Example 2 · mip-g-zaino-ottimo-gap · level complessa · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack: values $p = (12, 16, 4, 12, 20)$, weights $w = (8, 7, 7, 3, 2)$, capacity $C = 10$. The heuristic by decreasing $p_j/w_j$ (ties: smallest index) gives $\lb$; the dual of the relaxation with $\bar v = \max_j p_j/w_j$ gives $\ub = C\bar v$. What are the optimum $\zmilp$ and the certified gap $(\ub - \lb)/\ub$ (one decimal)?
  1. $\zmilp = 36$, gap $68.0\%$
  2. $\zmilp = 36$, gap $11.1\%$
  3. $\zmilp = 32$, gap $68.0\%$
  4. $\zmilp = 36$, gap $212.5\%$
Explanation. $\lb = 32$, $\ub = 100$, so the certified gap is $\approx 68.0\%$; enumerating the subsets $\zmilp = 36$. The heuristic gap with respect to the optimum ($11.1\%$) is another measure; dividing by $\lb$ is a mistake.

3. From the model to Python/Gurobi (5)

Example 1 · mip-g-gurobi-conta · level facile · notes
In gurobipy (the Python interface of Gurobi) the makespan on identical machines is written with these statements, applied to an empty model `m`: `x = m.addVars(6, 5, vtype=GRB.BINARY)`; `z = m.addVar()`; `m.addConstrs(x.sum(j, '*') == 1 for j in range(6))`; `m.addConstrs(gp.quicksum(d[j] * x[j, i] for j in range(6)) <= z for i in range(5))`. How many constraints does the model have (domains excluded)?
  1. $11$
  2. $30$
  3. $6$
  4. $12$
Explanation. `addVars(a, b)` creates $a \cdot b$ variables, `addVar()` a single one; `addConstrs` adds one constraint per element of the generator, so two nested `for` give one constraint per pair. The total is $11$.
Example 2 · mip-g-gurobi-conta · level facile · notes
In gurobipy (the Python interface of Gurobi) the makespan on identical machines is written with these statements, applied to an empty model `m`: `x = m.addVars(8, 3, vtype=GRB.BINARY)`; `z = m.addVar()`; `m.addConstrs(x.sum(j, '*') == 1 for j in range(8))`; `m.addConstrs(gp.quicksum(d[j] * x[j, i] for j in range(8)) <= z for i in range(3))`. How many variables does the model have?
  1. $25$
  2. $24$
  3. $27$
  4. $12$
Explanation. `addVars(a, b)` creates $a \cdot b$ variables, `addVar()` a single one; `addConstrs` adds one constraint per element of the generator, so two nested `for` give one constraint per pair. The total is $25$.
Example 1 · mip-g-gurobi-gap · level media · notes
Gurobi, **maximisation** problem. The solver stops with `Status = TIME_LIMIT` (time limit reached) after finding integer solutions: `ObjVal` $= 137$ is the value of the best integer solution, `ObjBound` $= 161$ the dual bound. The reported gap is `MIPGap` $= |\text{ObjVal} - \text{ObjBound}| / |\text{ObjVal}|$. What can be stated about the integer optimum $\zmilp$?
  1. $137 \le \zmilp \le 161$, gap $17.5\%$
  2. $161 \le \zmilp \le 137$, gap $17.5\%$
  3. $\zmilp = 137$, gap $0\%$
  4. $137 \le \zmilp \le 161$, gap $14.9\%$
Explanation. Without `OPTIMAL` the two bounds remain: the integer solution is the pessimistic side, the dual bound the optimistic one, so $137 \le \zmilp \le 161$, and the gap is $24/137 \approx 17.5\%$. Dividing by `ObjBound` is a mistake; $\zmilp = \text{ObjVal}$ can be written only with `Status = OPTIMAL`.
Example 2 · mip-g-gurobi-gap · level media · notes
Gurobi, **minimisation** problem. The solver stops with `Status = TIME_LIMIT` (time limit reached) after finding integer solutions: `ObjVal` $= 177$ is the value of the best integer solution, `ObjBound` $= 167$ the dual bound. The reported gap is `MIPGap` $= |\text{ObjVal} - \text{ObjBound}| / |\text{ObjVal}|$. What can be stated about the integer optimum $\zmilp$?
  1. $167 \le \zmilp \le 177$, gap $5.6\%$
  2. $177 \le \zmilp \le 167$, gap $5.6\%$
  3. $\zmilp = 177$, gap $0\%$
  4. $167 \le \zmilp \le 177$, gap $6.0\%$
Explanation. Without `OPTIMAL` the two bounds remain: the integer solution is the pessimistic side, the dual bound the optimistic one, so $167 \le \zmilp \le 177$, and the gap is $10/177 \approx 5.6\%$. Dividing by `ObjBound` is a mistake; $\zmilp = \text{ObjVal}$ can be written only with `Status = OPTIMAL`.
Example 1 · mip-g-mipgap-soglia · level facile · notes
Gurobi, **maximisation** problem, parameter `MIPGap` $= 5\%$: the solver stops with `OPTIMAL` as soon as $|\text{ObjVal} - \text{ObjBound}| / |\text{ObjVal}| \le$ `MIPGap`. The best solution is worth `ObjVal` $= 4800$. What is the largest value of `ObjBound` at which the solver stops?
  1. $5040$
  2. $4805$
  3. $7200$
  4. $4560$
Explanation. One needs $|4800 - \text{ObjBound}| \le 4800 \cdot 5\% = 240$; in a maximisation the bound lies above the solution, so `ObjBound` $= 5040$. The percentage must not be subtracted as an absolute number, and the bound does not lie on the solution's side.
Example 2 · mip-g-mipgap-soglia · level facile · notes
Gurobi, **minimisation** problem, parameter `MIPGap` $= 1\%$: the solver stops with `OPTIMAL` as soon as $|\text{ObjVal} - \text{ObjBound}| / |\text{ObjVal}| \le$ `MIPGap`. The best solution is worth `ObjVal` $= 6800$. What is the smallest value of `ObjBound` at which the solver stops?
  1. $6732$
  2. $6799$
  3. $6120$
  4. $6868$
Explanation. One needs $|6800 - \text{ObjBound}| \le 6800 \cdot 1\% = 68$; in a minimisation the bound lies below the solution, so `ObjBound` $= 6732$. The percentage must not be subtracted as an absolute number, and the bound does not lie on the solution's side.
Example 1 · mip-g-cmax-lb · level facile · notes
Makespan on $k = 3$ identical machines (minimum load of the busiest machine), jobs of duration $t = (10, 11, 3, 10, 8, 8)$. What is the better of the two elementary lower bounds, $\max_j t_j$ and $\sum_j t_j / k$?
  1. $\tfrac{50}{3}$
  2. $11$
  3. $50$
  4. $6$
Explanation. $\max_j t_j = 11$ and $\sum_j t_j / k = \tfrac{50}{3}$: in a minimisation the better bound is the larger one, $\tfrac{50}{3}$. $\sum_j t_j$ is an upper bound (everything on one machine), not a lower bound.
Example 2 · mip-g-cmax-lb · level facile · notes
Makespan on $k = 3$ identical machines (minimum load of the busiest machine), jobs of duration $t = (5, 3, 6, 11, 10, 9)$. What is the better of the two elementary lower bounds, $\max_j t_j$ and $\sum_j t_j / k$?
  1. $\tfrac{44}{3}$
  2. $11$
  3. $44$
  4. $6$
Explanation. $\max_j t_j = 11$ and $\sum_j t_j / k = \tfrac{44}{3}$: in a minimisation the better bound is the larger one, $\tfrac{44}{3}$. $\sum_j t_j$ is an upper bound (everything on one machine), not a lower bound.
Example 1 · mip-g-bpp-ffd · level complessa · notes
Bin packing: items of weight $w = (9, 10, 3, 10, 8, 8, 10, 2)$, containers of capacity $C = 13$. First-fit decreasing sorts the items by decreasing weight (ties: smallest index) and puts each in the first open container where it fits, otherwise opens a new one. With $\ub$ the number of containers of first-fit decreasing and the lower bound $\lb = \lceil \sum_j w_j / C \rceil$, what are $(\ub, \lb)$?
  1. $(6, 5)$
  2. $(7, 5)$
  3. $(6, 4)$
  4. $(5, 6)$
Explanation. First-fit decreasing uses $6$ containers (upper bound); $\sum_j w_j = 60$, so $\lb = \lceil 60/13 \rceil = 5$. First-fit in the given order uses $6$ containers.
Example 2 · mip-g-bpp-ffd · level complessa · notes
Bin packing: items of weight $w = (5, 3, 6, 12, 11, 9, 13, 11)$, containers of capacity $C = 15$. First-fit decreasing sorts the items by decreasing weight (ties: smallest index) and puts each in the first open container where it fits, otherwise opens a new one. With $\ub$ the number of containers of first-fit decreasing and the lower bound $\lb = \lceil \sum_j w_j / C \rceil$, what are $(\ub, \lb)$?
  1. $(6, 5)$
  2. $(7, 5)$
  3. $(6, 4)$
  4. $(5, 6)$
Explanation. First-fit decreasing uses $6$ containers (upper bound); $\sum_j w_j = 70$, so $\lb = \lceil 70/15 \rceil = 5$. First-fit in the given order uses $6$ containers.

4. Constructive heuristics (7)

Example 1 · mip-g-lpt · level facile · notes
Makespan on identical machines: jobs of duration $t = (3, 4, 2, 2, 4, 6)$ go on $k = 3$ identical machines, minimising the makespan. What value does LPT return (jobs by decreasing duration, each on the least loaded machine, ties broken by the smallest index)?
  1. $8$
  2. $10$
  3. $7$
  4. $6$
Explanation. LPT sorts the jobs by decreasing duration and puts each on the least loaded machine; the makespan is the largest final load, $8$. It is an upper bound, since the problem is a minimisation; $\max(\max_j t_j, \lceil \sum_j t_j / k \rceil)$ is instead a lower bound.
Example 2 · mip-g-lpt · level facile · notes
Makespan on identical machines: jobs of duration $t = (4, 6, 8, 7, 2, 8, 4)$ go on $k = 3$ identical machines, minimising the makespan. What value does LPT return (jobs by decreasing duration, each on the least loaded machine, ties broken by the smallest index)?
  1. $14$
  2. $16$
  3. $13$
  4. $12$
Explanation. LPT sorts the jobs by decreasing duration and puts each on the least loaded machine; the makespan is the largest final load, $14$. It is an upper bound, since the problem is a minimisation; $\max(\max_j t_j, \lceil \sum_j t_j / k \rceil)$ is instead a lower bound.
Example 1 · mip-g-bin-packing · level facile · notes
Bin packing: items of weight $w = (5, 6, 2, 6, 5, 5, 6)$, in the given order, go into bins of capacity $C = 9$. **next-fit** puts each item into the current bin if it fits, otherwise opens a new bin which becomes the current one (it never goes back). How many bins does it use?
  1. $6$
  2. $4$
  3. $7$
  4. $5$
Explanation. Running the rule item by item opens $6$ bins. Next-fit looks only at the current bin, first-fit at the first bin where the item fits, best-fit at the one left fullest; $\lceil \sum_j w_j / C \rceil$ is the elementary lower bound.
Example 2 · mip-g-bin-packing · level facile · notes
Bin packing: items of weight $w = (5, 4, 5, 5, 5, 4, 2, 4)$, in the given order, go into bins of capacity $C = 8$. **next-fit** puts each item into the current bin if it fits, otherwise opens a new bin which becomes the current one (it never goes back). How many bins does it use?
  1. $7$
  2. $6$
  3. $5$
  4. $8$
Explanation. Running the rule item by item opens $7$ bins. Next-fit looks only at the current bin, first-fit at the first bin where the item fits, best-fit at the one left fullest; $\lceil \sum_j w_j / C \rceil$ is the elementary lower bound.
Example 1 · mip-g-tsp-vicino · level media · notes
Travelling salesman (TSP): five cities with symmetric distances, row by row: 1: (-, 2, 3, 1, 1); 2: (2, -, 3, 5, 2); 3: (3, 3, -, 4, 5); 4: (1, 5, 4, -, 7); 5: (1, 2, 5, 7, -). Starting from city 1, nearest neighbour always moves to the closest unvisited city (ties broken by the smallest index) and finally returns to the start. How long is the tour?
  1. $11$
  2. $10$
  3. $14$
  4. $12$
Explanation. The tour is $1 \to 4 \to 3 \to 2 \to 5 \to 1$, of length $11$, including the return arc (often the most expensive one). It is a feasible solution, hence an upper bound: it says nothing about the distance from the optimum.
Example 2 · mip-g-tsp-vicino · level media · notes
Travelling salesman (TSP): five cities with symmetric distances, row by row: 1: (-, 8, 8, 2, 9); 2: (8, -, 9, 7, 7); 3: (8, 9, -, 3, 6); 4: (2, 7, 3, -, 5); 5: (9, 7, 6, 5, -). Starting from city 1, nearest neighbour always moves to the closest unvisited city (ties broken by the smallest index) and finally returns to the start. How long is the tour?
  1. $26$
  2. $18$
  3. $30$
  4. $27$
Explanation. The tour is $1 \to 4 \to 3 \to 5 \to 2 \to 1$, of length $26$, including the return arc (often the most expensive one). It is a feasible solution, hence an upper bound: it says nothing about the distance from the optimum.
Example 1 · mip-g-copertura-bound · level media · notes
Minimum-cost covering: four teams (one per district) of cost $c = (6, 6, 8, 8)$ must cover six zones, each covered by the teams of the two districts it borders: in order $\{1,2\}, \{2,3\}, \{1,3\}, \{1,4\}, \{2,4\}, \{3,4\}$. The heuristic always picks the team of minimum cost per newly covered zone (equal ratios: the smallest index). In the dual of the relaxation ($\pi_i \ge 0$ per zone, $\sum_{i \text{ covered by } j} \pi_i \le c_j$ per team) the zones are scanned in order, $\pi_i$ is raised until the first dual constraint is saturated and the residuals are updated. What are $(\ub, \lb)$?
  1. $(20, 14)$
  2. $(14, 20)$
  3. $(20, 38)$
  4. $(20, 46)$
Explanation. The heuristic gives a feasible cover of cost $\ub = 20$; the dual recipe gives $\lb = 14$, feasible because every residual stays non-negative. Without updating the residuals the dual solution is infeasible and its value is not a bound.
Example 2 · mip-g-copertura-bound · level media · notes
Minimum-cost covering: four teams (one per district) of cost $c = (5, 8, 4, 2)$ must cover six zones, each covered by the teams of the two districts it borders: in order $\{1,2\}, \{2,3\}, \{1,3\}, \{1,4\}, \{2,4\}, \{3,4\}$. The heuristic always picks the team of minimum cost per newly covered zone (equal ratios: the smallest index).   4. $(11, 38)$
Explanation. The heuristic gives a feasible cover of cost $\ub = 11$; the dual recipe gives $\lb = 9$, feasible because every residual stays non-negative. Without updating the residuals the dual solution is infeasible and its value is not a bound.
Example 1 · mip-g-zaino-rapporto · level facile · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack: values $p = (7, 4, 12, 10, 16)$, weights $w = (3, 2, 4, 3, 5)$, capacity $C = 10$. The heuristic scans the items by decreasing $p_j/w_j$ (ties: smallest index) and takes **every** item that fits in the residual, continuing the scan even after a rejection. What value does it obtain?
  1. $30$
  2. $28$
  3. $21$
  4. $26$
Explanation. With the ratio order the rule obtains $30$, a lower bound because the problem is a maximisation. Sorting by value or by weight gives other results; stopping at the first item that does not fit gives $26$.
Example 2 · mip-g-zaino-rapporto · level facile · notes
Binary maximisation knapsack: values $p = (17, 11, 9, 16, 4)$, weights $w = (7, 5, 5, 6, 4)$, capacity $C = 10$.   3. $15$
  4. $16$

Example 1 · mip-g-ricerca-locale · level media · notes
Makespan on $3$ identical machines: jobs of duration $t = (6, 5, 7, 7, 9, 4)$, assigned to machines $(1, 2, 1, 1, 1, 1)$ (in job order), loads $(33, 5, 0)$. All moves «move one job to another machine» are evaluated and the best one is applied, only if it reduces the makespan. What makespan is obtained after one step (if no move improves, the current one)?
  1. $24$
  2. $33$
  3. $13$
  4. $23$
Explanation. The current makespan is $33$; the best move leads to $24$. The optimum is $13$ and the bound $\lceil \sum t / k \rceil = 13$: a local move does not guarantee reaching them.
Example 2 · mip-g-ricerca-locale · level media · notes
Makespan on $3$ identical machines: jobs of duration $t = (8, 2, 8, 7, 8, 8)$, assigned to machines $(1, 2, 3, 3, 2, 3)$ (in job order), loads $(8, 10, 23)$. All moves «move one job to another machine» are evaluated and the best one is applied, only if it reduces the makespan. What makespan is obtained after one step (if no move improves, the current one)?
  1. $16$
  2. $23$
  3. $14$
  4. $15$
Explanation. The current makespan is $23$; the best move leads to $16$. The optimum is $16$ and the bound $\lceil \sum t / k \rceil = 14$: a local move does not guarantee reaching them.
Example 1 · mip-g-lpt-bound · level complessa · notes
Makespan on $k = 3$ identical machines, jobs of duration $t = (8, 8, 8, 5, 2, 4, 7, 10)$. LPT sorts the jobs by decreasing duration (ties: smallest index) and puts each on the least loaded machine (ties: machine of smallest index). The lower bound is $\max\{\max_j t_j, \lceil \sum_j t_j / k \rceil\}$ (the makespan is integer). What are $(\ub, \lb)$, with $\ub$ the LPT makespan?
  1. $(19, 18)$
  2. $(18, 19)$
  3. $(22, 18)$
  4. $(19, 17)$
Explanation. LPT gives makespan $19$, an upper bound (minimisation); the bound is $\max\{10, \lceil 52/3 \rceil\} = 18$. Without sorting (list scheduling in the given order) one gets $22$.
Example 2 · mip-g-lpt-bound · level complessa · notes

  3. $(21, 21)$
  4. $(22, 20)$

5. Logic and binary variables (5)

Example 1 · mip-g-implicazione · level facile · notes
Boolean logic with binary variables: $x_{6}, x_{9}, x_{8}$ equal $1$ if the corresponding proposition is true. Which set of linear constraints translates **exactly** the implication $x_{6} \Rightarrow x_{9} \lor x_{8}$, i.e. admits all and only the assignments that make it true?
  1. $x_{9} + x_{8} - x_{6} \ge 0$
  2. $x_{9} - x_{6} \ge 0$ and $x_{8} - x_{6} \ge 0$
  3. $x_{6} - x_{9} - x_{8} \ge 0$
  4. $x_{9} + x_{8} - x_{6} \ge 1$
Explanation. Bring the implication to CNF ($p \Rightarrow q$ is $\lnot p \lor q$), then every clause becomes a constraint $\ge 1$ with $1 - x$ for the negative literals: this gives $x_{9} + x_{8} - x_{6} \ge 0$. A disjunction in the antecedent or a conjunction in the consequent costs two constraints; splitting a conjunctive antecedent is not allowed.
Example 2 · mip-g-implicazione · level facile · notes
Boolean logic with binary variables: $x_{3}, x_{8}, x_{1}$ equal $1$ if the corresponding proposition is true. Which set of linear constraints translates **exactly** the implication $x_{3} \Rightarrow x_{8} \land x_{1}$, i.e. admits all and only the assignments that make it true?
  1. $x_{8} - x_{3} \ge 0$ and $x_{1} - x_{3} \ge 0$
  2. $x_{8} + x_{1} - x_{3} \ge 0$
  3. $x_{3} - x_{8} \ge 0$ and $x_{3} - x_{1} \ge 0$
  4. $x_{8} + x_{1} - x_{3} \ge 1$
Explanation. Bring the implication to CNF ($p \Rightarrow q$ is $\lnot p \lor q$), then every clause becomes a constraint $\ge 1$ with $1 - x$ for the negative literals: this gives $x_{8} - x_{3} \ge 0$ and $x_{1} - x_{3} \ge 0$. A disjunction in the antecedent or a conjunction in the consequent costs two constraints; splitting a conjunctive antecedent is not allowed.
Example 1 · mip-g-progetti-logici · level complessa · notes
Six projects with revenues $r = (7, 12, 9, 6, 10, 11)$ and costs $c = (4, 3, 6, 1, 3, 1)$, budget $b = 14$; $x_p = 1$ if project $p$ is funded ($1$ = true in the implications), the cost of the chosen projects does not exceed $b$ and revenue is maximised. The implications $x_{4} \land x_{2} \Rightarrow x_{1}$ and $x_{5} \Rightarrow \lnot x_{1}$ hold, translated exactly into linear constraints. What is $\zmilp$?
  1. $42$
  2. $39$
  3. $48$
  4. $41$
Explanation. Enumerate the subsets within budget that respect the two implications (the first becomes a single constraint, $x_a + x_b - x_c \le 1$): the optimum is $42$. Without logical constraints it would be $48$; splitting the conjunctive antecedent would cut off allowed solutions.
Example 2 · mip-g-progetti-logici · level complessa · notes
Six projects with revenues $r = (12, 5, 5, 6, 3, 12)$ and costs $c = (4, 4, 1, 2, 4, 1)$, budget $b = 10$; $x_p = 1$ if project $p$ is funded ($1$ = true in the implications), the cost of the chosen projects does not exceed $b$ and revenue is maximised. The implications $x_{4} \land x_{3} \Rightarrow x_{5}$ and $x_{5} \Rightarrow \lnot x_{6}$ hold, translated exactly into linear constraints. What is $\zmilp$?
  1. $34$
  2. $29$
  3. $35$
  4. $33$
Explanation. Enumerate the subsets within budget that respect the two implications (the first becomes a single constraint, $x_a + x_b - x_c \le 1$): the optimum is $34$. Without logical constraints it would be $35$; splitting the conjunctive antecedent would cut off allowed solutions.
Example 1 · mip-g-verifica-conta · level media · notes
A Boolean expression in three binary variables $x_1, x_2, x_3$ ($1$ = true) has been translated into the constraints $x_1 - x_3 \ge 0$ and $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 \le 2$. To check the translation the $8$ assignments are enumerated. How many of them satisfy both constraints?
  1. $5$
  2. $3$
  3. $6$
  4. $4$
Explanation. Enumerating the $8$ assignments, those satisfying both constraints are $5$: they must coincide with those making the expression true. $3$ are the excluded ones.
Example 2 · mip-g-verifica-conta · level media · notes
A Boolean expression in three binary variables $x_1, x_2, x_3$ ($1$ = true) has been translated into the constraints $x_1 + x_2 - x_3 \le 1$ and $x_1 - x_2 - x_3 \ge -1$. To check the translation the $8$ assignments are enumerated. How many of them satisfy both constraints?
  1. $6$
  2. $2$
  3. $7$
  4. $5$
Explanation. Enumerating the $8$ assignments, those satisfying both constraints are $6$: they must coincide with those making the expression true. $2$ are the excluded ones.
Example 1 · mip-g-clausola-rhs · level facile · notes
Binary variables ($1$ = true). The clause $\lnot x_{2} \lor x_{5} \lor \lnot x_{6} \lor x_{8}$ is translated with the rule «positive literal $x$, negated $1 - x$, sum $\ge 1$», then moving the constants to the right: $-x_{2} + x_{5} - x_{6} + x_{8} \ge \beta$. What is $\beta$?
  1. $\beta = -1$
  2. $\beta = 1$
  3. $\beta = -2$
  4. $\beta = 3$
Explanation. Every negated literal brings a $1$ to the left: with $2$ negated literals the right-hand side becomes $1 - 2 = -1$. Thus the only excluded assignment is the one making all literals false.
Example 2 · mip-g-clausola-rhs · level facile · notes
Binary variables ($1$ = true). The clause $x_{1} \lor x_{6} \lor x_{7} \lor \lnot x_{8} \lor x_{9}$ is translated with the rule «positive literal $x$, negated $1 - x$, sum $\ge 1$», then moving the constants to the right: $x_{1} + x_{6} + x_{7} - x_{8} + x_{9} \ge \beta$. What is $\beta$?
  1. $\beta = 0$
  2. $\beta = 1$
  3. $\beta = -1$
  4. $\beta = 2$
Explanation. Every negated literal brings a $1$ to the left: with $1$ negated literals the right-hand side becomes $1 - 1 = 0$. Thus the only excluded assignment is the one making all literals false.
Example 1 · mip-g-cardinalita-clausole · level facile · notes
Binary variables $x_1, \dots, x_{6}$. The condition «at most 3 of the 6» is written with a single cardinality constraint $\sum_i x_i \le 3$. How many clauses would be needed to write it in CNF?
  1. $15$
  2. $20$
  3. $21$
  4. $6$
Explanation. «At most $p$» requires every group of $p + 1$ variables to contain a false one: $\binom{6}{4} = 15$ clauses, against a single cardinality constraint (also stronger in the relaxation).
Example 2 · mip-g-cardinalita-clausole · level facile · notes
Binary variables $x_1, \dots, x_{6}$. The condition «at least 3 of the 6» is written with a single cardinality constraint $\sum_i x_i \ge 3$. How many clauses would be needed to write it in CNF?
  1. $15$
  2. $20$
  3. $21$
  4. $6$
Explanation. «At least $p$» requires every group of $n - p + 1$ variables to contain a true one: $\binom{6}{4} = 15$ clauses, against a single cardinality constraint (also stronger in the relaxation).

6. Links between variables (12)

Example 1 · mip-g-bigm-minimo · level facile · notes
Big-M conditional constraint. The variables $x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3}, x_{4}$ are integer with $0 \le x_j \le u_j$ and $u = (3, 3, 2, 1)$. The constraint $8x_{1} + 2x_{2} + 6x_{3} + 9x_{4} \le 43$ must hold only when the binary $y$ equals $1$, and it is written $8x_{1} + 2x_{2} + 6x_{3} + 9x_{4} \le 43 + M(1 - y)$: with $y = 0$ it must be satisfied by every $x$ within the bounds. What is the smallest valid $M$, computed from the variable bounds alone?
  1. $8$
  2. $51$
  3. $2$
  4. $94$
Explanation. With $y = 0$ the constraint is switched off only if $M$ is at least the maximum of the left-hand side minus the right-hand side $43$. The maximum of the left-hand side over the bounds is $51$, so $M = 51 - 43 = 8$. Forgetting to subtract the right-hand side gives a valid but improvable $M$; a smaller value cuts off solutions.
Example 2 · mip-g-bigm-minimo · level facile · notes
Big-M conditional constraint. The variables $x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3}$ are integer with $0 \le x_j \le u_j$ and $u = (1, 3, 2)$. The constraint $4x_{1} + 5x_{2} + 8x_{3} \le 30$ must hold only when the binary $y$ equals $1$, and it is written $4x_{1} + 5x_{2} + 8x_{3} \le 30 + M(1 - y)$: with $y = 0$ it must be satisfied by every $x$ within the bounds. What is the smallest valid $M$, computed from the variable bounds alone?
  1. $5$
  2. $35$
  3. $1$
  4. $65$
Explanation. With $y = 0$ the constraint is switched off only if $M$ is at least the maximum of the left-hand side minus the right-hand side $30$. The maximum of the left-hand side over the bounds is $35$, so $M = 35 - 30 = 5$. Forgetting to subtract the right-hand side gives a valid but improvable $M$; a smaller value cuts off solutions.
Example 1 · mip-g-costo-fisso-valore · level facile · notes
Fixed cost: three plants with fixed costs $f = (14, 18, 14)$, unit costs $c = (4, 1, 4)$ and capacities $C = (9, 9, 10)$; $q_j \ge 0$ is the quantity produced by plant $j$ and $y_j = 1$ if the plant is open. The model minimises $\sum_j (c_j q_j + f_j y_j)$ subject to $q_j \le C_j y_j$ and $y_j \in \{0,1\}$. With the quantities fixed at $q = (0, 8, 2)$, what is the objective value under the best choice of the $y_j$ compatible with the constraints?
  1. $48$
  2. $16$
  3. $62$
  4. $188$
Explanation. A plant that produces must be open ($q_j > 0 \Rightarrow y_j = 1$, imposed by the constraint); one that does not produce is closed, since $f_j > 0$. So one pays the variable cost plus only the fixed costs of the plants with $q_j > 0$: $48$. The fixed cost is not multiplied by the quantity.
Example 2 · mip-g-costo-fisso-valore · level facile · notes
Fixed cost: three plants with fixed costs $f = (11, 6, 17)$, unit costs $c = (6, 3, 4)$ and capacities $C = (6, 10, 10)$; $q_j \ge 0$ is the quantity produced by plant $j$ and $y_j = 1$ if the plant is open. The model minimises $\sum_j (c_j q_j + f_j y_j)$ subject to $q_j \le C_j y_j$ and $y_j \in \{0,1\}$. With the quantities fixed at $q = (4, 9, 0)$, what is the objective value under the best choice of the $y_j$ compatible with the constraints?
  1. $68$
  2. $51$
  3. $85$
  4. $149$
Explanation. A plant that produces must be open ($q_j > 0 \Rightarrow y_j = 1$, imposed by the constraint); one that does not produce is closed, since $f_j > 0$. So one pays the variable cost plus only the fixed costs of the plants with $q_j > 0$: $68$. The fixed cost is not multiplied by the quantity.
Example 1 · mip-g-conteggio-intero · level facile · notes
Integer counting: items of size $a = (6, 8, 8, 2, 8)$ must all be shipped in boxes of capacity $c = 12$, and $w$ is the number of boxes. The model minimises $w$ subject to $\sum_i a_i \le c\, w$ and $w \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$. What is the value of $w$ at the optimum?
  1. $3$
  2. $2$
  3. $\tfrac{8}{3}$
  4. $4$
Explanation. The constraint gives $w \ge 32/12$ and integrality raises it to $\lceil 32/12 \rceil = 3$: the ceiling is not written, the declaration of $w$ as integer realises it. The fractional value is that of the relaxation.
Example 2 · mip-g-conteggio-intero · level facile · notes
Integer counting: items of size $a = (8, 4, 2, 5, 8)$ must all be shipped in boxes of capacity $c = 13$, and $w$ is the number of boxes. The model minimises $w$ subject to $\sum_i a_i \le c\, w$ and $w \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$. What is the value of $w$ at the optimum?

  3. $\tfrac{27}{13}$
  4. $4$

Example 1 · mip-g-assoluto-bigm · level facile · notes
Absolute value as a constraint: the continuous variables $u, v$ lie in $[0, 19]$ and we want $|u - v| \ge 11$. With a binary $b$ (choosing which of the two directions holds) one writes $u - v \ge 11 - M(1 - b)$ and $v - u \ge 11 - M\, b$. What is the smallest $M$ valid for both constraints, that is, such that the switched-off constraint is satisfied by every $u, v$ within the bounds?
  1. $30$
  2. $41$
  3. $49$
  4. $19$
Explanation. When a constraint is switched off it must hold for every $u, v$: the first needs $11 - M \le \min (u - v) = -19$, that is $M \ge 11 + 19 = 30$. The $\ge$ case of the absolute value is not convex: it is a disjunction, and the binary is necessary.
Example 2 · mip-g-assoluto-bigm · level facile · notes
Absolute value as a constraint: the continuous variables $u, v$ lie in $[0, 14]$ and we want $|u - v| \ge 11$. With a binary $b$ (choosing which of the two directions holds) one writes $u - v \ge 11 - M(1 - b)$ and $v - u \ge 11 - M\, b$. What is the smallest $M$ valid for both constraints, that is, such that the switched-off constraint is satisfied by every $u, v$ within the bounds?
  1. $25$
  2. $11$
  3. $14$
  4. $28$
Explanation. When a constraint is switched off it must hold for every $u, v$: the first needs $11 - M \le \min (u - v) = -14$, that is $M \ge 11 + 14 = 25$. The $\ge$ case of the absolute value is not convex: it is a disjunction, and the binary is necessary.
Example 1 · mip-g-precedenze-M · level facile · notes
Precedences on one machine: jobs of duration $t = (6, 8, 8, 2)$, $\kappa_i$ is the completion time of job $i$, $s_{ij} = 1$ if $j$ precedes $i$, with $s_{ij} + s_{ji} = 1$, $\kappa_i \ge \kappa_j + t_i - M(1 - s_{ij})$ and $\kappa_j \ge t_j$. The optimum is sought among sequences without idle time. What is the smallest $M$ for which the switched-off constraint ($s_{ij} = 0$) is satisfied by all these sequences?
  1. $24$
  2. $8$
  3. $22$
  4. $16$
Explanation. Without idle time $\kappa_j \le \sum_h t_h$, and with $M = \sum_h t_h = 24$ one has $\kappa_j + t_i - M \le t_i \le \kappa_i$: the switched-off constraint cuts nothing. A smaller value fails when $j$ is the last job and $i$ the first. The horizon must be declared.
Example 2 · mip-g-precedenze-M · level facile · notes
Precedences on one machine: jobs of duration $t = (8, 4, 2, 5)$, $\kappa_i$ is the completion time of job $i$, $s_{ij} = 1$ if $j$ precedes $i$, with $s_{ij} + s_{ji} = 1$, $\kappa_i \ge \kappa_j + t_i - M(1 - s_{ij})$ and $\kappa_j \ge t_j$. The optimum is sought among sequences without idle time. What is the smallest $M$ for which the switched-off constraint ($s_{ij} = 0$) is satisfied by all these sequences?
  1. $19$
  2. $8$
  3. $17$
  4. $11$
Explanation. Without idle time $\kappa_j \le \sum_h t_h$, and with $M = \sum_h t_h = 19$ one has $\kappa_j + t_i - M \le t_i \le \kappa_i$: the switched-off constraint cuts nothing. A smaller value fails when $j$ is the last job and $i$ the first. The horizon must be declared.
Example 1 · mip-g-tratti · level facile · notes
The cost $g(q)$ is continuous and piecewise linear, with breakpoints $(0, 4, 8, 13)$ and values $(0, 24, 32, 37)$ (quantity discounts: it is not convex). The model writes $q = \sum_k q_k \lambda_k$ and $g = \sum_k g_k \lambda_k$ with breakpoint weights $\lambda_k \ge 0$ summing to $1$, one binary $w_t$ per segment ($\sum_t w_t = 1$) and adjacency: only the $\lambda$ of the two breakpoints of the chosen segment are positive. $q = 11$ is fixed. What is $g$?
  1. $35$
  2. $\tfrac{407}{13}$
  3. $38$
  4. $37$
Explanation. With adjacency only the $\lambda$ of the two breakpoints of the segment containing $q$ are positive, and $(q, g)$ lies on the graph: $g(11) = 35$. Without adjacency the model would mix the extreme breakpoints and drop to the lower convex envelope, $\tfrac{407}{13}$.
Example 2 · mip-g-tratti · level facile · notes
The cost $g(q)$ is continuous and piecewise linear, with breakpoints $(0, 3, 8, 11)$ and values $(0, 12, 22, 25)$ (quantity discounts: it is not convex). The model writes $q = \sum_k q_k \lambda_k$ and $g = \sum_k g_k \lambda_k$ with breakpoint weights $\lambda_k \ge 0$ summing to $1$, one binary $w_t$ per segment ($\sum_t w_t = 1$) and adjacency: only the $\lambda$ of the two breakpoints of the chosen segment are positive. $q = 10$ is fixed. What is $g$?
  1. $24$
  2. $\tfrac{250}{11}$
  3. $26$
  4. $25$
Explanation. With adjacency only the $\lambda$ of the two breakpoints of the segment containing $q$ are positive, and $(q, g)$ lies on the graph: $g(10) = 24$. Without adjacency the model would mix the extreme breakpoints and drop to the lower convex envelope, $\tfrac{250}{11}$.
Example 1 · mip-g-se-e-solo-se · level facile · notes
If and only if (product of binaries): the variables $x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3}, y$ are binary and we want $y = 1$ **if and only if** $x_{1} = x_{2} = x_{3} = 1$ (i.e. $y$ is the product of the $x$). The constraints $y \le x_j$, $j \in \{1, \dots, 3\}$, are already in the model. Which constraint must be added to have the link in both directions in every feasible solution?
  1. $y \ge x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3} - 2$
  2. $y \ge x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3} - 3$
  3. $3y \ge x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3}$
  4. $y \le x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3} - 2$
Explanation. If all $x_j$ equal $1$ the right-hand side of $y \ge x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3} - 2$ is $1$ and forces $y = 1$; if at least one is zero it is at most $0$ and says nothing. With $-3$ instead of $-2$ the constraint never bites.
Example 2 · mip-g-se-e-solo-se · level facile · notes
If and only if (product of binaries): the variables $x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3}, y$ are binary and we want $y = 1$ **if and only if** $x_{1} = x_{2} = x_{3} = 1$ (i.e. $y$ is the product of the $x$). The constraints $y \le x_j$, $j \in \{1, \dots, 3\}$, are already in the model. Which constraint must be added to have the link in both directions in every feasible solution?
  1. $y \ge x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3} - 2$
  2. $y \ge x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3} - 3$
  3. $3y \ge x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3}$
  4. $y \le x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3} - 2$
Explanation. If all $x_j$ equal $1$ the right-hand side of $y \ge x_{1} + x_{2} + x_{3} - 2$ is $1$ and forces $y = 1$; if at least one is zero it is at most $0$ and says nothing. With $-3$ instead of $-2$ the constraint never bites.
Example 1 · mip-g-disgiunzione · level media · notes
Disjunction with big-M: the variables are integer with $0 \le x_1 \le 4$ and $0 \le x_2 \le 4$, and **at least one** of the constraints $5x_1 + 6x_2 \le 27$ or $x_1 + 5x_2 \le 15$ must hold. With a binary $y$ one writes $5x_1 + 6x_2 \le 27 + M_1(1 - y)$ and $x_1 + 5x_2 \le 15 + M_2\, y$. What are the smallest valid values $(M_1, M_2)$, derived from the bounds?
  1. $(17, 9)$
  2. $(9, 17)$
  3. $(44, 24)$
  4. $(17, 17)$
Explanation. Each constraint, when switched off, must be satisfied by every point of the bounds: $M_1 = 44 - 27 = 17$ and $M_2 = 24 - 15 = 9$. Each constraint has its own $M$; using the larger one for both is valid but weakens the relaxation.
Example 2 · mip-g-disgiunzione · level media · notes
Disjunction with big-M: the variables are integer with $0 \le x_1 \le 3$ and $0 \le x_2 \le 6$, and **at least one** of the constraints $2x_1 + x_2 \le 9$ or $3x_1 + 6x_2 \le 29$ must hold. With a binary $y$ one writes $2x_1 + x_2 \le 9 + M_1(1 - y)$ and $3x_1 + 6x_2 \le 29 + M_2\, y$. What are the smallest valid values $(M_1, M_2)$, derived from the bounds?
  1. $(3, 16)$
  2. $(16, 3)$
  3. $(12, 45)$
  4. $(16, 16)$
Explanation. Each constraint, when switched off, must be satisfied by every point of the bounds: $M_1 = 12 - 9 = 3$ and $M_2 = 45 - 29 = 16$. Each constraint has its own $M$; using the larger one for both is valid but weakens the relaxation.
Example 1 · mip-g-equita · level media · notes
Five jobs of duration $p = (8, 6, 6, 9, 2)$ are split between two workers, each job wholly to one of them; $\ell_k$ is the load of worker $k$. Three models are compared: min-max ($\min z$ with $z \ge \ell_k$ for every $k$), max-min ($\max u$ with $u \le \ell_k$) and range ($\min z - u$ with both groups of constraints). What are the three optimal values, in order?
  1. $(16, 15, 1)$
  2. $(\tfrac{31}{2}, \tfrac{31}{2}, 0)$
  3. $(15, 16, 1)$
  4. $(16, 16, 0)$
Explanation. The total is $31$, odd: the best split is $(16, 15)$, and with two workers all three models choose it. Their values differ, though, $16$, $15$ and $1$: they describe the same solution and are not comparable. $\tfrac{31}{2}$ is the relaxation.
Example 2 · mip-g-equita · level media · notes
Five jobs of duration $p = (7, 8, 8, 3, 9)$ are split between two workers, each job wholly to one of them; $\ell_k$ is the load of worker $k$. Three models are compared: min-max ($\min z$ with $z \ge \ell_k$ for every $k$), max-min ($\max u$ with $u \le \ell_k$) and range ($\min z - u$ with both groups of constraints). What are the three optimal values, in order?
  1. $(18, 17, 1)$
  2. $(\tfrac{35}{2}, \tfrac{35}{2}, 0)$
  3. $(17, 18, 1)$
  4. $(18, 18, 0)$
Explanation. The total is $35$, odd: the best split is $(18, 17)$, and with two workers all three models choose it. Their values differ, though, $18$, $17$ and $1$: they describe the same solution and are not comparable. $\tfrac{35}{2}$ is the relaxation.
Example 1 · mip-g-costo-fisso-ottimo · level complessa · notes
Fixed cost with demand: three plants with fixed costs $f = (14, 18, 15)$, unit costs $c = (3, 1, 4)$, capacities $C = (8, 8, 9)$; the demand $D = 10$ must be met; $q_j$ is the quantity produced by plant $j$ and $y_j = 1$ if it is open. The model is $\min \sum_j (f_j y_j + c_j q_j)$ subject to $\sum_j q_j = D$, $q_j \le C_j y_j$, $q_j \ge 0$, $y_j \in \{0,1\}$. What is $\zmilp$?
  1. $46$
  2. $\tfrac{71}{2}$
  3. $61$
  4. $14$
Explanation. For every set of open plants with enough capacity, fill the cheapest per unit first and add the fixed costs: the best is $46$. The fractional value $\tfrac{71}{2}$ is $\zlpp$, where every plant costs $c_j + f_j/C_j$ per unit.
Example 2 · mip-g-costo-fisso-ottimo · level complessa · notes
Fixed cost with demand: three plants with fixed costs $f = (11, 7, 17)$, unit costs $c = (5, 2, 4)$, capacities $C = (5, 9, 9)$; the demand $D = 19$ must be met; $q_j$ is the quantity produced by plant $j$ and $y_j = 1$ if it is open. The model is $\min \sum_j (f_j y_j + c_j q_j)$ subject to $\sum_j q_j = D$, $q_j \le C_j y_j$, $q_j \ge 0$, $y_j \in \{0,1\}$. What is $\zmilp$?
  1. $94$
  2. $\tfrac{426}{5}$
  3. $59$
  4. $95$
Explanation. For every set of open plants with enough capacity, fill the cheapest per unit first and add the fixed costs: the best is $94$. The fractional value $\tfrac{426}{5}$ is $\zlpp$, where every plant costs $c_j + f_j/C_j$ per unit.
Example 1 · mip-g-deviazioni · level media · notes
Soft constraints: three targets $\beta = (40, 90, 60)$ are written $a_t'x + s_t^- - s_t^+ = \beta_t$ with $s_t^-, s_t^+ \ge 0$; a penalty $\pi^- = 6$ per unit below the target and $\pi^+ = 2$ per unit above, in a minimisation objective. A plan gives $a'x = (30, 100, 75)$. What is the total penalty, with the deviations at their optimal value?
  1. $110$
  2. $170$
  3. $210$
  4. $10$
Explanation. For each target only one deviation is used: $s^- = \max(0, \beta - a'x)$ paid $6$, $s^+ = \max(0, a'x - \beta)$ paid $2$. Total $110$. Swapping the penalties, or offsetting deviations of opposite sign, gives wrong values.
Example 2 · mip-g-deviazioni · level media · notes
Soft constraints: three targets $\beta = (50, 50, 50)$ are written $a_t'x + s_t^- - s_t^+ = \beta_t$ with $s_t^-, s_t^+ \ge 0$; a penalty $\pi^- = 5$ per unit below the target and $\pi^+ = 4$ per unit above, in a minimisation objective. A plan gives $a'x = (60, 35, 65)$. What is the total penalty, with the deviations at their optimal value?
  1. $175$
  2. $185$
  3. $200$
  4. $-25$
Explanation. For each target only one deviation is used: $s^- = \max(0, \beta - a'x)$ paid $5$, $s^+ = \max(0, a'x - \beta)$ paid $4$. Total $175$. Swapping the penalties, or offsetting deviations of opposite sign, gives wrong values.
Example 1 · mip-g-violabili-ottimo · level complessa · notes
One decides the integer production $q \in \{0, \dots, 20\}$ of a period. A fixed cost $f = 60$ is paid if $q > 0$ (link $q \le 20\,y$, $y$ binary) and $c = 5$ per unit. The demand $D = 15$ is a soft constraint: each missing unit costs $\pi^- = 13$; the regular capacity $K = 5$ can be exceeded with overtime at $\pi^+ = 2$ per unit beyond $K$. What is the minimum cost?
  1. $155$
  2. $95$
  3. $135$
  4. $195$
Explanation. The cost is $f[q > 0] + 5q + 13\max(0, 15 - q) + 2\max(0, q - 5)$; enumerating $q$ (it suffices to look at $q \in \{0, 5, 15\}$, the breakpoints) the minimum is $155$, with $q = 15$. Forgetting the fixed cost gives $95$, forgetting overtime $135$.
Example 2 · mip-g-violabili-ottimo · level complessa · notes
One decides the integer production $q \in \{0, \dots, 20\}$ of a period. A fixed cost $f = 70$ is paid if $q > 0$ (link $q \le 20\,y$, $y$ binary) and $c = 5$ per unit. The demand $D = 16$ is a soft constraint: each missing unit costs $\pi^- = 11$; the regular capacity $K = 13$ can be exceeded with overtime at $\pi^+ = 7$ per unit beyond $K$. What is the minimum cost?
  1. $168$
  2. $98$
  3. $150$
  4. $171$
Explanation. The cost is $f[q > 0] + 5q + 11\max(0, 16 - q) + 7\max(0, q - 13)$; enumerating $q$ (it suffices to look at $q \in \{0, 13, 16\}$, the breakpoints) the minimum is $168$, with $q = 13$. Forgetting the fixed cost gives $98$, forgetting overtime $150$.

Fifteen numerical models (4)

Example 1 · mip-g-turni-costo · level facile · notes
Emergency room shifts: the pattern starting on Tuesday works full time on Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday, half time on Saturday and rests on Sunday and Monday. A full day costs $120$ euro on weekdays, $130$ on Saturday and $160$ on Sunday; at half time the day's cost is halved. How much does the pattern cost per worker, in one week?
  1. $545$
  2. $610$
  3. $480$
  4. $560$
Explanation. Add the four full days, $480$, and half of the half-time day, $65$: total $545$. The typical mistakes are paying the half shift in full, forgetting it, or halving the wrong day.
Example 2 · mip-g-turni-costo · level facile · notes
Emergency room shifts: the pattern starting on Monday works full time on Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, half time on Friday and rests on Saturday and Sunday. A full day costs $90$ euro on weekdays, $120$ on Saturday and $130$ on Sunday; at half time the day's cost is halved. How much does the pattern cost per worker, in one week?
  1. $405$
  2. $450$
  3. $360$
  4. $420$
Explanation. Add the four full days, $360$, and half of the half-time day, $45$: total $405$. The typical mistakes are paying the half shift in full, forgetting it, or halving the wrong day.
Example 1 · mip-g-furgone · level complessa · notes
A van with $7$ seats. Some groups offer a price to board, all together or none (group 1: $3$ people, $60$ euro; group 2: $2$ people, $50$ euro; group 3: $3$ people, $70$ euro; group 4: $2$ people, $70$ euro). At most $2$ groups are accepted and, if group 4 is accepted, group 1 must be accepted too. With $x_j = 1$ if group $j$ is accepted and the constraints translated exactly, what is the maximum revenue $\zmilp$?
  1. $130$
  2. $140$
  3. $180$
  4. $120$
Explanation. Enumerate the subsets of at most $2$ groups that fit in the seats and respect $x_{4} \le x_{1}$: the best earns $130$. Forgetting the implication gives $140$, forgetting the limit on the number of groups gives $180$.
Example 2 · mip-g-furgone · level complessa · notes
A van with $10$ seats. Some groups offer a price to board, all together or none (group 1: $5$ people, $30$ euro; group 2: $5$ people, $80$ euro; group 3: $4$ people, $70$ euro; group 4: $2$ people, $80$ euro; group 5: $4$ people, $40$ euro). At most $2$ groups are accepted and, if group 2 is accepted, group 3 must be accepted too. With $x_j = 1$ if group $j$ is accepted and the constraints translated exactly, what is the maximum revenue $\zmilp$?
  1. $150$
  2. $160$
  3. $190$
  4. $140$
Explanation. Enumerate the subsets of at most $2$ groups that fit in the seats and respect $x_{2} \le x_{3}$: the best earns $150$. Forgetting the implication gives $160$, forgetting the limit on the number of groups gives $190$.
Example 1 · mip-g-staffetta-ottimo · level media · notes
Three tasks to be given to three different resources chosen among four, at minimum total time (assignment with more resources than tasks). Times, rows per task: $(36, 37, 41, 43)$, $(27, 41, 38, 38)$, $(43, 25, 43, 28)$. The dual of the relaxation with the resource variables at zero gives each task its minimum time: its value is $\lb$. What are $(\zmilp, \lb)$, with $\zmilp$ the optimal total time?
  1. $(92, 88)$
  2. $(88, 92)$
  3. $(99, 88)$
  4. $(92, 87)$
Explanation. Enumerating the assignments with distinct resources the optimum is $92$; the sum of the row minima gives $\lb = 88$, lower when two tasks share the same best resource. The task-by-task heuristic would give $99$.
Example 2 · mip-g-staffetta-ottimo · level media · notes
Three tasks to be given to three different resources chosen among four, at minimum total time (assignment with more resources than tasks). Times, rows per task: $(40, 31, 30, 29)$, $(45, 27, 44, 35)$, $(40, 40, 41, 26)$. The dual of the relaxation with the resource variables at zero gives each task its minimum time: its value is $\lb$. What are $(\zmilp, \lb)$, with $\zmilp$ the optimal total time?
  1. $(83, 82)$
  2. $(82, 83)$
  3. $(96, 82)$
  4. $(83, 81)$
Explanation. Enumerating the assignments with distinct resources the optimum is $83$; the sum of the row minima gives $\lb = 82$, lower when two tasks share the same best resource. The task-by-task heuristic would give $96$.
Example 1 · mip-g-fondi-rendimento · level facile · notes
Funds in lots (EX 5): indivisible lots of $12$, $15$, $18$, $25$ million with annual return $1/6$, $1/3$, $1/9$ and $16\%$ of the capital invested; budget $100$; the lots of the second fund do not exceed $50\%$ of the total lots. The portfolio $x = (0, 2, 1, 1)$ (lots per fund) is feasible. What is its annual return, in millions?
  1. $16$
  2. $73$
  3. $\tfrac{211}{225}$
  4. $43$
Explanation. One lot yields $(2, 5, 2, 4)$ million: $16$. The expense ($73$) is not the return, and summing rates times lots forgets the capital of each lot.
Example 2 · mip-g-fondi-rendimento · level facile · notes
Funds in lots (EX 5): indivisible lots of $12$, $15$, $18$, $25$ million with annual return $1/6$, $1/3$, $1/9$ and $16\%$ of the capital invested; budget $100$; the lots of the second fund do not exceed $50\%$ of the total lots. The portfolio $x = (2, 2, 0, 1)$ (lots per fund) is feasible. What is its annual return, in millions?
  1. $18$
  2. $79$
  3. $\tfrac{29}{25}$
  4. $39$
Explanation. One lot yields $(2, 5, 2, 4)$ million: $18$. The expense ($79$) is not the return, and summing rates times lots forgets the capital of each lot.

7. Assignment and scheduling (3)

Example 1 · mip-g-conteggio-scheduling · level facile · notes
Assignment of jobs to machines with a fixed cost: $x_{jm} = 1$ if job $j$ goes on machine $m$, $y_m = 1$ if machine $m$ is used, $t_{jm}$ is the time of job $j$ on $m$ and $a_m$ the availability of $m$. The constraint families are $\sum_{m} x_{jm} = 1\ \forall j$, $\ \sum_{j} t_{jm} x_{jm} \le a_m y_m\ \forall m$; the variables are $x_{jm}$ and $y_m$ binary. With $n = 7$ jobs and $k = 3$ machines, how many linear constraints does the model have, domain constraints excluded?
  1. $10$
  2. $24$
  3. $7$
  4. $11$
Explanation. Count family by family, following the quantifiers: "$\forall j$" gives one row (or variable) per element, "$\forall j, m$" one per pair, a constraint without quantifier just one. The total is $10$. The typical mistakes are counting ordered pairs instead of unordered ones and forgetting a family (or an auxiliary variable).
Example 2 · mip-g-conteggio-scheduling · level facile · notes
Parallel jobs on machines: $x_{jm} = 1$ if job $j$ goes on machine $m$, $y_m$ is the time of machine $m$ (the maximum of the times $t_{jm}$ of its jobs), $p_m$ the maximum number of jobs on $m$. The constraint families are $\sum_{m} x_{jm} = 1\ \forall j$, $\ \sum_{j} x_{jm} \le p_m\ \forall m$, $\ y_m \ge t_{jm} x_{jm}\ \forall j, m$; the variables are $x_{jm}$ binary and $y_m \ge 0$. With $n = 7$ jobs and $k = 2$ machines, how many variables does the model have?
  1. $16$
  2. $14$
  3. $21$
  4. $9$
Explanation. Count family by family, following the quantifiers: "$\forall j$" gives one row (or variable) per element, "$\forall j, m$" one per pair, a constraint without quantifier just one. The total is $16$. The typical mistakes are counting ordered pairs instead of unordered ones and forgetting a family (or an auxiliary variable).
Example 1 · mip-g-ritardo-totale · level complessa · notes
Total tardiness on one machine: four jobs with durations $t = (7, 2, 7, 3)$ and due dates $d = (6, 2, 3, 7)$, processed one at a time without interruption from time $0$. $\kappa_j$ is the completion time of job $j$ and the model minimises $\sum_j \tau_j$ subject to $\tau_j \ge \kappa_j - d_j$, $\tau_j \ge 0$ and the big-M precedence constraints. What is $\zmilp$, the minimum total tardiness?
  1. $22$
  2. $28$
  3. $33$
  4. $16$
Explanation. At the optimum $\tau_j = \max\{0, \kappa_j - d_j\}$ and a sequence without idle time is best: it suffices to compare the $24$ sequences. The minimum total tardiness is $22$; ordering by due date or by duration is only a heuristic and may give more.
Example 2 · mip-g-ritardo-totale · level complessa · notes
Total tardiness on one machine: four jobs with durations $t = (7, 6, 5, 7)$ and due dates $d = (19, 4, 23, 22)$, processed one at a time without interruption from time $0$. $\kappa_j$ is the completion time of job $j$ and the model minimises $\sum_j \tau_j$ subject to $\tau_j \ge \kappa_j - d_j$, $\tau_j \ge 0$ and the big-M precedence constraints. What is $\zmilp$, the minimum total tardiness?
  1. $4$
  2. $10$
  3. $12$
  4. $2$
Explanation. At the optimum $\tau_j = \max\{0, \kappa_j - d_j\}$ and a sequence without idle time is best: it suffices to compare the $24$ sequences. The minimum total tardiness is $4$; ordering by due date or by duration is only a heuristic and may give more.
Example 1 · mip-g-ritardo-sequenza · level facile · notes
One machine, four jobs with durations $t = (6, 8, 3, 6)$ and due dates $d = (12, 7, 9, 9)$, processed without interruption from $0$ in the order $3, 2, 1, 4$. The tardiness of job $j$ is $\tau_j = \max\{0, \kappa_j - d_j\}$, with $\kappa_j$ the completion time. What is the total tardiness $\sum_j \tau_j$?
  1. $23$
  2. $17$
  3. $14$
  4. $8$
Explanation. Compute the completions in the given order and add only the positive parts of $\kappa_j - d_j$: $23$. Adding the (negative) earliness too gives $17$; the maximum tardiness is another objective; using start times instead of completions is a mistake.
Example 2 · mip-g-ritardo-sequenza · level facile · notes
One machine, four jobs with durations $t = (4, 4, 4, 7)$ and due dates $d = (14, 4, 15, 12)$, processed without interruption from $0$ in the order $4, 3, 2, 1$. The tardiness of job $j$ is $\tau_j = \max\{0, \kappa_j - d_j\}$, with $\kappa_j$ the completion time. What is the total tardiness $\sum_j \tau_j$?
  1. $16$
  2. $7$
  3. $11$
  4. $8$
Explanation. Compute the completions in the given order and add only the positive parts of $\kappa_j - d_j$: $16$. Adding the (negative) earliness too gives $7$; the maximum tardiness is another objective; using start times instead of completions is a mistake.

8. Location and covering (4)

Example 1 · mip-g-conteggio-localizzazione · level facile · notes
p-median: $x_l = 1$ if site $l$ is open, $y_{lc} = 1$ if customer $c$ is served by site $l$, at most $k$ open sites. The constraint families are $\sum_{l} y_{lc} = 1\ \forall c$, $\ \sum_{l} x_l \le k$, $\ x_l \ge y_{lc}\ \forall l, c$; the variables are $x_l$ and $y_{lc}$ binary. With $m = 5$ candidate sites and $n = 4$ customers, how many linear constraints does the model have, domain constraints excluded?
  1. $25$
  2. $10$
  3. $24$
  4. $26$
Explanation. Count family by family, following the quantifiers: "$\forall j$" gives one row (or variable) per element, "$\forall j, m$" one per pair, a constraint without quantifier just one. The total is $25$. The typical mistakes are counting ordered pairs instead of unordered ones and forgetting a family (or an auxiliary variable).
Example 2 · mip-g-conteggio-localizzazione · level facile · notes
p-median: $x_l = 1$ if site $l$ is open, $y_{lc} = 1$ if customer $c$ is served by site $l$, at most $k$ open sites. The constraint families are $\sum_{l} y_{lc} = 1\ \forall c$, $\ \sum_{l} x_l \le k$, $\ x_l \ge y_{lc}\ \forall l, c$; the variables are $x_l$ and $y_{lc}$ binary. With $m = 7$ candidate sites and $n = 2$ customers, how many variables does the model have?
  1. $21$
  2. $14$
  3. $16$
  4. $9$
Explanation. Count family by family, following the quantifiers: "$\forall j$" gives one row (or variable) per element, "$\forall j, m$" one per pair, a constraint without quantifier just one. The total is $21$. The typical mistakes are counting ordered pairs instead of unordered ones and forgetting a family (or an auxiliary variable).
Example 1 · mip-g-pmediana-bound · level media · notes
p-median (minimisation) with $3$ candidate sites, $4$ customers and at most $k = 2$ open sites; every customer goes to the nearest open site and the sum of the distances $d_{lc}$ is minimised. Rows per site: site 1 $(7, 7, 10, 11)$, site 2 $(2, 10, 8, 8)$, site 3 $(11, 1, 11, 3)$. A heuristic opens sites 1 and 2; in the dual of the relaxation every customer gets $\bar\mu_c = \min_l d_{lc}$, the distance from the nearest site of all, which is feasible. What are $(\ub, \lb)$?
  1. $(25, 14)$
  2. $(14, 25)$
  3. $(38, 14)$
  4. $(25, 4)$
Explanation. With the open sites every customer takes the nearer of the two: $\ub = 25$. The dual sums the column minima over all sites: $\lb = 14$. Assigning the customer to the farther open site, or using a single minimum for everyone, does not give correct bounds.
Example 2 · mip-g-pmediana-bound · level media · notes
p-median (minimisation) with $3$ candidate sites, $4$ customers and at most $k = 2$ open sites; every customer goes to the nearest open site and the sum of the distances $d_{lc}$ is minimised. Rows per site: site 1 $(4, 7, 10, 9)$, site 2 $(1, 10, 4, 1)$, site 3 $(2, 12, 11, 10)$. A heuristic opens sites 1 and 3; in the dual of the relaxation every customer gets $\bar\mu_c = \min_l d_{lc}$, the distance from the nearest site of all, which is feasible. What are $(\ub, \lb)$?
  1. $(28, 13)$
  2. $(13, 28)$
  3. $(37, 13)$
  4. $(28, 4)$
Explanation. With the open sites every customer takes the nearer of the two: $\ub = 28$. The dual sums the column minima over all sites: $\lb = 13$. Assigning the customer to the farther open site, or using a single minimum for everyone, does not give correct bounds.
Example 1 · mip-g-ufl-costo · level facile · notes
Location (minimum cost): $3$ candidate sites with installation cost $i = (60, 60, 80)$, $4$ customers with demand $d = (8, 2, 8, 6)$ litres, transport cost per litre $t_{lc}$ (rows per site): site 1 $(4, 6, 1, 6)$, site 2 $(2, 2, 1, 1)$, site 3 $(2, 4, 1, 3)$. Capacities are sufficient and each customer is supplied by the cheapest open site. Sites 2 and 3 are installed. What does the solution cost?
  1. $174$
  2. $34$
  3. $190$
  4. $146$
Explanation. Installation $140$ plus transport $\sum_c d_c \min_{l \text{ open}} t_{lc} = 34$: total $174$. Forgetting installation, using the dearer site or not multiplying by demand are the typical mistakes.
Example 2 · mip-g-ufl-costo · level facile · notes
Location (minimum cost): $3$ candidate sites with installation cost $i = (50, 80, 40)$, $4$ customers with demand $d = (2, 5, 8, 8)$ litres, transport cost per litre $t_{lc}$ (rows per site): site 1 $(4, 6, 5, 4)$, site 2 $(6, 5, 1, 6)$, site 3 $(6, 5, 5, 2)$. Capacities are sufficient and each customer is supplied by the cheapest open site. Sites 2 and 3 are installed. What does the solution cost?
  1. $181$
  2. $61$
  3. $245$
  4. $134$
Explanation. Installation $120$ plus transport $\sum_c d_c \min_{l \text{ open}} t_{lc} = 61$: total $181$. Forgetting installation, using the dearer site or not multiplying by demand are the typical mistakes.
Example 1 · mip-g-ufl-ottimo · level complessa · notes
Location (minimum cost): $3$ candidate sites with installation cost $i = (60, 60, 80)$, $4$ customers with demand $d = (8, 2, 8, 6)$ litres, transport cost per litre $t_{lc}$ (rows per site): site 1 $(4, 6, 1, 6)$, site 2 $(2, 2, 1, 1)$, site 3 $(2, 4, 1, 3)$. Sufficient capacities; at least one site must be installed and each customer is supplied by an installed site. What is the minimum cost $\zmilp$?
  1. $94$
  2. $234$
  3. $34$
  4. $104$
Explanation. Enumerate the $7$ non-empty subsets of sites; for each the cost is installation plus $\sum_c d_c \min_l t_{lc}$ over the open sites. The minimum is $94$. Transport alone with all sites free ($34$) is a lower bound, not the optimum.
Example 2 · mip-g-ufl-ottimo · level complessa · notes
Location (minimum cost): $3$ candidate sites with installation cost $i = (50, 80, 40)$, $4$ customers with demand $d = (2, 5, 8, 8)$ litres, transport cost per litre $t_{lc}$ (rows per site): site 1 $(4, 6, 5, 4)$, site 2 $(6, 5, 1, 6)$, site 3 $(6, 5, 5, 2)$. Sufficient capacities; at least one site must be installed and each customer is supplied by an installed site. What is the minimum cost $\zmilp$?
  1. $133$
  2. $227$
  3. $57$
  4. $143$
Explanation. Enumerate the $7$ non-empty subsets of sites; for each the cost is installation plus $\sum_c d_c \min_l t_{lc}$ over the open sites. The minimum is $133$. Transport alone with all sites free ($57$) is a lower bound, not the optimum.

9. Production planning (4)

Example 1 · mip-g-lotti-bigM · level facile · notes
Lot sizing with a fixed setup cost over $n = 4$ periods: demands $d = (15, 35, 30, 35)$, initial stock $r_0 = 10$, required final stock $r_n = 15$, positive holding costs. $x_t$ is the production of period $t$, $y_t = 1$ if production is launched in the period, and the setup is modelled with $x_t \le M_t\, y_t$. What is the smallest $M_{3}$, read from the data, that cuts off no optimal solution?
  1. $80$
  2. $70$
  3. $65$
  4. $130$
Explanation. At an optimum one never produces more than the demand still to be covered: $M_t = \sum_{\tau \ge t} d_\tau + r_n$; only in the first period is the initial stock, which is a datum, subtracted. Here $M_{3} = 80$. A larger value is valid but weakens the relaxation.
Example 2 · mip-g-lotti-bigM · level facile · notes
Lot sizing with a fixed setup cost over $n = 4$ periods: demands $d = (35, 15, 5, 20)$, initial stock $r_0 = 15$, required final stock $r_n = 15$, positive holding costs. $x_t$ is the production of period $t$, $y_t = 1$ if production is launched in the period, and the setup is modelled with $x_t \le M_t\, y_t$. What is the smallest $M_{3}$, read from the data, that cuts off no optimal solution?
  1. $40$
  2. $25$
  3. $90$
  4. $20$
Explanation. At an optimum one never produces more than the demand still to be covered: $M_t = \sum_{\tau \ge t} d_\tau + r_n$; only in the first period is the initial stock, which is a datum, subtracted. Here $M_{3} = 40$. A larger value is valid but weakens the relaxation.
Example 1 · mip-g-lotti-luc · level media · notes
Uncapacitated lot sizing over $4$ periods: demands $d = (40, 20, 20, 10)$, no initial stock, setup cost $f = 40$ for every period with production, holding cost $h = 2$ per unit and period. The least unit cost rule starts from the first uncovered period, chooses to cover $1, 2, \dots$ periods with the lowest average cost per unit (setup plus holding, divided by the units) — ties: the smallest number — and restarts from the next period. What total setup and holding cost does it obtain?
  1. $160$
  2. $140$
  3. $220$
  4. $200$
Explanation. The rule launches in periods 1, 2, 3, 4 and costs $160$: it is an upper bound. The optimum (Wagner–Whitin) is $140$, so here the heuristic is not optimal: it looks at one launch at a time and never goes back.
Example 2 · mip-g-lotti-luc · level media · notes
Uncapacitated lot sizing over $4$ periods: demands $d = (20, 35, 20, 10)$, no initial stock, setup cost $f = 50$ for every period with production, holding cost $h = 2$ per unit and period. The least unit cost rule starts from the first uncovered period, chooses to cover $1, 2, \dots$ periods with the lowest average cost per unit (setup plus holding, divided by the units) — ties: the smallest number — and restarts from the next period. What total setup and holding cost does it obtain?
  1. $190$
  2. $170$
  3. $200$
  4. $260$
Explanation. The rule launches in periods 1, 3 and costs $190$: it is an upper bound. The optimum (Wagner–Whitin) is $170$, so here the heuristic is not optimal: it looks at one launch at a time and never goes back.
Example 1 · mip-g-lot-bilancio · level facile · notes
Lot sizing over $4$ periods: initial stock $r_0 = 10$, production $x = (35, 35, 50, 15)$, demand $d = (25, 10, 15, 20)$. The balance is $r_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + r_t$, with $r_t$ the stock at the end of period $t$. What is $r_3$?
  1. $80$
  2. $95$
  3. $130$
  4. $75$
Explanation. $r_3 = r_0 + x_1 + x_2 + x_3 - d_1 - d_2 - d_3 = 80$. Forgetting the demand of period 3, or all demands, are the typical mistakes; $75$ is the stock at the end of period 4.
Example 2 · mip-g-lot-bilancio · level facile · notes
Lot sizing over $4$ periods: initial stock $r_0 = 15$, production $x = (45, 5, 50, 35)$, demand $d = (30, 35, 40, 30)$. The balance is $r_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + r_t$, with $r_t$ the stock at the end of period $t$. What is $r_3$?
  1. $10$
  2. $50$
  3. $115$
  4. $15$
Explanation. $r_3 = r_0 + x_1 + x_2 + x_3 - d_1 - d_2 - d_3 = 10$. Forgetting the demand of period 3, or all demands, are the typical mistakes; $15$ is the stock at the end of period 4.
Example 1 · mip-g-lotto-costo · level facile · notes
Uncapacitated lot sizing over $4$ periods, no initial or final stock: demand $d = (25, 30, 35, 40)$, production plan $x = (130, 0, 0, 0)$. Every period with $x_t > 0$ pays the setup $f = 80$ ($x_t \le M y_t$) and each unit in stock at the end of a period costs $h = 2$. What does the plan cost?
  1. $520$
  2. $80$
  3. $600$
  4. $960$
Explanation. Setups: $1 \cdot 80 = 80$; end-of-period stocks from $r_t = r_{t-1} + x_t - d_t$, summed and multiplied by $2$: $440$. Total $520$.
Example 2 · mip-g-lotto-costo · level facile · notes
Uncapacitated lot sizing over $4$ periods, no initial or final stock: demand $d = (20, 35, 20, 10)$, production plan $x = (75, 0, 0, 10)$. Every period with $x_t > 0$ pays the setup $f = 80$ ($x_t \le M y_t$) and each unit in stock at the end of a period costs $h = 2$. What does the plan cost?
  1. $310$
  2. $160$
  3. $390$
  4. $460$
Explanation. Setups: $2 \cdot 80 = 160$; end-of-period stocks from $r_t = r_{t-1} + x_t - d_t$, summed and multiplied by $2$: $150$. Total $310$.

10. Mixed problems (4)

Example 1 · mip-g-scaffali-valore · level facile · notes
Books on shelves: books of height $h = (31, 31, 33, 18, 17, 17)$ and width $w = (6, 5, 2, 3, 3, 4)$, $m = 3$ shelves of width $c = 14$; $x_{bs} = 1$ if book $b$ goes on shelf $s$, $y_s \ge 0$ is the height of the shelf, and the model minimises $\sum_s y_s$ subject to $y_s \ge h_b\, x_{bs}$ for every pair. The shelves assigned to the books, in order, are $(1, 2, 3, 1, 2, 2)$ (a feasible assignment). What is the objective value with the best $y_s$ for this assignment?
  1. $95$
  2. $147$
  3. $99$
  4. $68$
Explanation. The constraints give $y_s \ge \max_{b : x_{bs} = 1} h_b$ and minimisation brings each $y_s$ down to that maximum: the height of a shelf is that of its tallest book. The sum is $95$.
Example 2 · mip-g-scaffali-valore · level facile · notes
Books on shelves: books of height $h = (21, 33, 34, 33, 35)$ and width $w = (2, 5, 5, 5, 6)$, $m = 3$ shelves of width $c = 14$; $x_{bs} = 1$ if book $b$ goes on shelf $s$, $y_s \ge 0$ is the height of the shelf, and the model minimises $\sum_s y_s$ subject to $y_s \ge h_b\, x_{bs}$ for every pair. The shelves assigned to the books, in order, are $(2, 2, 2, 1, 3)$ (a feasible assignment). What is the objective value with the best $y_s$ for this assignment?
  1. $102$
  2. $156$
  3. $105$
  4. $89$
Explanation. The constraints give $y_s \ge \max_{b : x_{bs} = 1} h_b$ and minimisation brings each $y_s$ down to that maximum: the height of a shelf is that of its tallest book. The sum is $102$.
Example 1 · mip-g-asta-bound · level media · notes
Combinatorial auction (maximisation): items $1, \dots, 4$, each sold at most once; every bid buys a set $B_j$ of items at price $p_j$: $\{3,4\}$: $4$; $\{2,3,4\}$: $3$; $\{1,2,3\}$: $4$; $\{3\}$: $4$; $\{2,4\}$: $3$. The heuristic sorts the bids by decreasing $p_j / |B_j|$ (equal ratios in the listed order) and accepts those whose items are still free. In the dual of the relaxation ($\lambda_i \ge 0$ price of item $i$, $\sum_{i \in B_j} \lambda_i \ge p_j$) one sets $\bar\lambda_i = \max_{j : i \in B_j} p_j / |B_j|$ ($0$ if no bid contains $i$). What are $(\lb, \ub)$?
  1. $(7, \tfrac{53}{6})$
  2. $(\tfrac{53}{6}, 7)$
  3. $(7, 16)$
  4. $(4, \tfrac{53}{6})$
Explanation. The heuristic accepts compatible bids: $\lb = 7$. The dual sums the prices $\bar\lambda_i$: $\ub = \tfrac{53}{6}$, feasible because every bid receives at least $|B_j| \cdot p_j/|B_j| = p_j$. Without dividing by $|B_j|$ the bound rises to $16$; sorting by price instead of ratio gives $4$.
Example 2 · mip-g-asta-bound · level media · notes
Combinatorial auction (maximisation): items $1, \dots, 4$, each sold at most once; every bid buys a set $B_j$ of items at price $p_j$: $\{1,2\}$: $6$; $\{1,2,4\}$: $4$; $\{3\}$: $15$; $\{2,4\}$: $15$; $\{4\}$: $12$. The heuristic sorts the bids by decreasing $p_j / |B_j|$ (equal ratios in the listed order) and accepts those whose items are still free. In the dual of the relaxation ($\lambda_i \ge 0$ price of item $i$, $\sum_{i \in B_j} \lambda_i \ge p_j$) one sets $\bar\lambda_i = \max_{j : i \in B_j} p_j / |B_j|$ ($0$ if no bid contains $i$). What are $(\lb, \ub)$?
  1. $(33, \tfrac{75}{2})$
  2. $(\tfrac{75}{2}, 33)$
  3. $(33, 51)$
  4. $(30, \tfrac{75}{2})$
Explanation. The heuristic accepts compatible bids: $\lb = 33$. The dual sums the prices $\bar\lambda_i$: $\ub = \tfrac{75}{2}$, feasible because every bid receives at least $|B_j| \cdot p_j/|B_j| = p_j$. Without dividing by $|B_j|$ the bound rises to $51$; sorting by price instead of ratio gives $30$.
Example 1 · mip-g-cd-differenza · level facile · notes
Tracks of duration $d = (6, 8, 8, 6, 2, 3)$ minutes distributed over two CDs (minimum difference between the duration of the longest and the shortest CD, $z_{\max} - z_{\min}$ with $z_{\max} \ge L_j$ and $z_{\min} \le L_j$). The tracks go, in order, onto CDs $(2, 1, 2, 2, 2, 1)$. What is the objective for this assignment?
  1. $11$
  2. $22$
  3. $1$
  4. $33$
Explanation. The CD durations are $11$ and $22$; at the optimum for this assignment $z_{\max} = 22$ and $z_{\min} = 11$, difference $11$. $z_{\max}$ alone is the makespan, not the spread.
Example 2 · mip-g-cd-differenza · level facile · notes
Tracks of duration $d = (8, 2, 8, 7, 8, 8)$ minutes distributed over two CDs (minimum difference between the duration of the longest and the shortest CD, $z_{\max} - z_{\min}$ with $z_{\max} \ge L_j$ and $z_{\min} \le L_j$). The tracks go, in order, onto CDs $(1, 1, 2, 2, 2, 2)$. What is the objective for this assignment?
  1. $21$
  2. $31$
  3. $1$
  4. $41$
Explanation. The CD durations are $10$ and $31$; at the optimum for this assignment $z_{\max} = 31$ and $z_{\min} = 10$, difference $21$. $z_{\max}$ alone is the makespan, not the spread.
Example 1 · mip-g-premi-punti · level facile · notes
Loyalty programme: reward $i$ is obtained in mode A with $a_i$ points, or in mode B with $b_i$ points plus $c_i$ euro ($x_i + y_i \le 1$). Data $a = (60, 30, 90, 40, 60)$, $b = (40, 10, 80, 30, 50)$, $c = (5, 4, 6, 1, 3)$. The customer chooses, reward by reward, the modes $(B, A, –, –, A)$ («–» = reward not taken). How many points are spent?
  1. $130$
  2. $150$
  3. $100$
  4. $135$
Explanation. Points $\sum_i (a_i x_i + b_i y_i) = 130$; the euros ($5$) are not points. Counting all taken rewards with $a_i$, or all with $b_i$, ignores the chosen mode.
Example 2 · mip-g-premi-punti · level facile · notes

Data $a = (50, 50, 90, 80, 70)$, $b = (30, 30, 70, 70, 50)$, $c = (2, 5, 4, 6, 2)$. The customer chooses, reward by reward, the modes $(–, A, B, B, A)$ («–» = reward not taken). How many points are spent?
  1. $260$
  2. $290$
  3. $220$
  4. $270$
Explanation. Points $\sum_i (a_i x_i + b_i y_i) = 260$; the euros ($10$) are not points. Counting all taken rewards with $a_i$, or all with $b_i$, ignores the chosen mode.
